- **Renewals, the reaper and fencing are unchanged.** The reaper still ends a claim whose `expires_at` is not after the store's time, and an epoch still rises with each new holder; no comparison site changed, since the store's time is what they all read. `claim.expire` keeps naming the lease it ended, which is now the extended one. Tests take a claim through a takeover with the clock stepped back, forward and both ways at each point: no second holder, no write by the stale one, and the epoch only rises.
- **Across a restart** there is no monotonic clock to compare with, so a lease is measured by the wall clock, as before: a daemon that comes back after a lease ran out, or with its clock set forward, ends the claim. A machine paused in a way that leaves the monotonic clock running is the same case.
- **At start** the daemon compares the clock with the latest event of the control store and of every active project, which it opens then anyway, so the check costs nothing more with hundreds of projects; archived projects are not compared. Behind by more than `clock_behind_tolerance` (2 minutes, above the 60 seconds at which systemd-timesyncd saves the clock it restores at boot), it refuses to start with exit status 75, which the unit does not restart, or warns (decision 37). `ariesd upgrade` asks the same question before it swaps the binary.
- **The server's own timers** (the reaper's tick, the idle close of stores and connections, the write buckets, the refusal sampler, uptime) already ran on the monotonic readings of `time.Now` and are left as they were, with tests that say what each does when given a wall clock that steps.
- **Protocol 5, no bump.** `status` gains result fields (`server.clock`), and `clock.step` is an event op, a string in the log. A usage record's time may be 5 minutes past the server's clock, not an hour (`clock_client_tolerance`). Two instruments join §16.4's, `aries.clock.behind` (a gauge, seconds) and `aries.clock.steps` (a counter, by `direction`), recorded at the reaper's tick; `/readyz` does not answer for a clock behind history, since the daemon serves correctly and an unready daemon may be taken out of service.
- **The soak** (§12) now sets the clock forward by 5 to 30 minutes and back by seconds while the sessions work, and restarts the daemon with its clock set back 10 to 15 minutes, which must be refused and is then corrected or accepted for one start. A claim with lease left when the clock jumps must not be reaped for it. It found two faults in the first build: a time finer than a microsecond while a step was unsettled, pushed to a watch as one instant and stored as another; and the stores of one server going on from different marks. Five minutes with `-race` (seed 1359954275010811867): 48,888 requests, 38,979 events in 20,505 transactions across three projects, 4,810 claims taken, 1,884 reaped and 2,837 released; the clock set forward 66 times and back 27 while running, 2,432 claims with lease left checked across the jumps and none reaped for one, 158 `clock.step` events in the projects' logs extending 2,699 leases and 66 in the control log; 2 restarts with the clock set back, both refused, one then corrected and one accepted; every invariant held.
- **Run under systemd, 12 Oct 2026**, on the guide's virtual machine (Ubuntu 24.04.5 on arm64, kernel 6.8.0-142, systemd 255.4, Dolt 2.4.2, the AppArmor profiles enforced, the unit `Type=notify`); the guide's clock section has the figures. The refusal exits with status 75, which systemd shows as `status=75/TEMPFAIL`, 0.13 s after the start, and with `RestartPreventExitStatus=75` is not retried (one start in 20 s, against 7 in 32 s without the line). Each way on works as the guide gives it: the corrected clock, `--accept-clock-behind` in a drop-in (inert for a later history, and for a later start), and `warn`. Set back seven minutes under a running daemon, the clock was caught up with in fourteen, at 30 seconds a minute, with the error line again at ten minutes, `/readyz` 200 throughout and no watchdog restart in sixteen. Set forward ten minutes with four claims held in two projects, each lease moved on by the jump, each log gained its `clock.step`, and the three short leases were reaped 270, 330 and 390 s after they were taken. On stock Ubuntu `time-sync.target` is reached 1.7 s into the boot, before any synchronization, so the unit's ordering waits for nothing there; with `systemd-time-wait-sync` enabled ariesd served at 33.5 s instead of 9.7 s, and with no time server answering at 91.6 s, the wait unit's timeout being a drop-in the guide gives, since as shipped it is infinite and the start then never comes. `systemd-timesyncd` saves the clock every 60 to 70 s, synchronized or not, and restores it at its start when the clock is behind it, which is what the two-minute tolerance rests on. The run changed three things: the refusal is printed a line at a time, since `systemctl status` cut the one long line before its fix; the report of a clock behind history lasts until the clock has caught up, not until it is within the tolerance, and the gauge is the lag to the second; and a lease's extension is kept to the microsecond. No denial in the kernel's log, and the earlier runs' 76 checks passed afterwards. Not run: amd64, a machine with a real-time clock of its own, a boot with a wrong clock (the hypervisor sets this guest's at every boot), a paused or snapshotted machine (Lima has no pause, and stopping the hypervisor's process ended the guest), a provider's time service, chrony.
- **Not built, and still exposed:** a jump forward while the daemon is down, or under a pause the monotonic clock does not see, ends leases as an outage of that length would; a clock that ran ahead for long leaves history in the future, which the next start refuses and `--accept-clock-behind` then runs at half speed until the clock passes it; the idempotency day and the inbox and registry pruning roll forward with a forward jump; and the similar-titles cache keeps its minute by the store's time.
- **13 Oct 2026, a claim the soak called reaped for a jump.** A two-minute soak (seed 264949260793903814) failed about one run in seven on a loaded machine with "the reaper ended … claim … after the clock jumped forward". The store was right and the check wrong. A store settles a jump with its first write after it, and until then its time runs on as if the clock had not moved; a lease that runs out in between had all of its time, so the settle leaves it and the reaper ends it as granted. The check allowed 30 seconds of the store's time for that, a quarter of a second at the soak's speed, and on a loaded machine the settle came later: in each of three failures reproduced with the log printed, the lease had 29 to 35 seconds left at the jump and the store's `clock.step` is dated 1 to 3 seconds after the lease's end. The check now reads the project's log and reckons on the store's time alone (`jumpFaults`, with a table test of its own): no event is dated after the jump until a `clock.step`, a claim reaped after a `clock.step` that found its lease live has a lease extended by it, and by the whole jump when the store did not run slow. `TestLeaseThatRunsOutBeforeAJumpIsSettled` pins what the store does. Reading for other ways a lease could miss its extension found a fault in how a store that opens a database again decides which steps it has settled: it counted as settled every step the clock dated before the database's latest event, and that event is dated on the store's time, which is not always the clock's. Two tests failed on it. After a step back the event is dated ahead of the clock, so a project closed for idleness while the clock was behind lost its leases to a jump forward made before it was next used, which the soak cannot reach because it does not check a jump made while a store runs slow. And two jumps settled together are one `clock.step` dated before the clock's reading at the second, so a store that closed again with that as its latest event settled the second jump a second time at its next opening, with a second event and the leases extended twice. The process's clock now remembers what each database's store had settled, and a store that opens the database again goes on from that; the dates still decide for a database the process has not had open. Left as they were: a jump made while a store runs slow is still not checked by the soak; and a write that was already waiting for the writer when the clock jumped is dated on the store's time before the jump, though it may record something the control store dates after it, such as a claim released for a membership removed, which the soak's check of those releases takes for a release without a cause when the machine is loaded enough for that wait to be long (2 runs in 24 with eight soaks at once, the order read from both).
- **13 Oct 2026, what may be compared across two logs, and two soak checks that compared more.** The first note above says a server's stores keep one time, so that an event in one log can be placed against an event in another. That holds with one exception, which is by design and is now stated: each store settles a forward jump by itself, with a write of its own, and a write that had passed its store's settle before the clock jumped (it was waiting for the writer, or is a retry) is dated on the time before the jump, though it runs after it. The control store does this as a project's store does. So an effect can be dated before its cause by the size of the jump: a `claim.release` for `membership removed` at 18:19:29 in a project whose `clock.step` is at 18:19:31, for a `member.remove` the control log dates 18:31:19, after its own `clock.step` at 18:19:04. Neither date is wrong, and the logs say which case it is without any allowance for how long something took: the control log dates nothing between a `clock.step`'s time and where it went, so a project's event dated there, before that project's own `clock.step` for the jump, was written on the time before it, and happened at most the step later. Nothing the server decides compares times across stores: a lease is judged on its project's time, a claim is released for lost access on what the control store's memory holds and not on when, the audit line carries its event's own time, and `digest` reads one project. So this is a matter for whoever reads two logs side by side, not a fault in what the server did. **Left open, not built:** a write could look for an unsettled step once more when its transaction has begun, and settle before it goes on, which would date every event after a jump on the time after it; it touches every write, and is for the maintainer to decide (built since, and this exception closed: the last note of this date, below). **The soak.** Two of its checks failed on loaded machines for comparing times that are not one clock, and a third did in CI on the short run. (1) *Claims released for lost access* took the release above for one without a cause (3 runs in 48 at eight two-minute soaks at once). It now allows the jump to a release that its log's and the control log's `clock.step` events place before a settle (`behindJump`), and to nothing else. (2) *Handshakes* (`was let into project … when the control log gives it no access`, and its converse) dated each handshake by the run's own reckoning of the stores' time and each access change by the control log. The reckoning is not the control store's time. After a restart with the clock set back it is ahead by as long as the daemon took to stop and start: measured at each of 172 restarts of the short run on a loaded machine, a median of 86 seconds of the stores' time and up to 172, against the 120 the check allowed; and a jump forward that lands while an admin's request waits for the control store's writer dates the change on the time before the jump and every handshake after it on the time after. Either way a session let in just before a principal was disabled or a project archived was dated after it, and one refused just before a membership came back was dated after that. Both directions occurred (3 runs in 48: two refused, one let in; and CI's short run, twelve sessions let in to a project about to be archived). No one was let in who should not have been: in every case reproduced with the control log's sequence recorded beside the handshake, the log did not yet hold the change when the handshake ended, or gained it while the handshake was under way; and of 131,828 handshakes in 48 runs, the 44 whose answer was not what the log held as committed when they began each had one change commit during them, and took 7 to 46 ms. The check now goes by the control log's order and no clock (`dialFault`): the server's memory, which answers a handshake, held every event it had answered an admin for before the handshake began and none the log gained after it ended, so the answer must be right after one of the events between. It is stricter than before where it could be: a session let in half a second after the server answered for its removal passed the old check and fails this one. (3) *A restart with the clock set back must be refused* failed once, with eighteen soaks at once: the clock gains on the history while the daemon stops and opens its databases, and when that takes longer than the clock was set back less the tolerance, a daemon that starts is right. A start is now faulted only if the clock is still past the tolerance behind when the daemon has started; the cause of that one failure was inferred from the code and not observed again. A jump made while a store's time runs slow is now checked too, as far as a log can say: a claim reaped after it must have the extension its log's `clock.step` events give (33 such jumps in 48 runs). After the changes, 64 two-minute runs at eight at once and 60 short runs of CI's seed at ten at once passed; before, 6 of 48 two-minute runs failed. Pull request #82 does not bear on any of this: the control store is opened once in a process, so what the process's clock remembers of a database's settles never changes its time, and the same failures occur without it.
- **13 Oct 2026, a database the process opens for the first time.** The note above left the dates deciding for a database the process had not had open, and they are no sounder there. Two faults, each with a test that failed first. With the latest event dated ahead of the clock (the clock set back since the daemon started, or one that ran fast under the daemon before and was corrected), a jump forward made before the database was first opened was taken as settled, and its live claims ended at opening without their extension; in the control store, which has no leases, the jump was followed with no `clock.step` in the log. And the opening read the clock and then the list of its steps under two holds of the clock's lock, so a step another goroutine saw between them was counted twice: with the opening stopped between the two by a test, a ten-minute jump was pending as twenty, the lease extended by twenty, and the event's `step_us` twenty. The rule is now what the process knows, and no date enters it. A process has written nothing to a database it has not had open, so the claims in it were made before the clock's first reading, and every forward step seen since then took its size from their leases: none is settled, and the first write settles them all with one `clock.step`. A database with no event holds no claim and starts with nothing to settle, so a new project's log does not begin with a jump made before it. The reading, the answer and its record are one hold of the lock (`Clock.open`), and the list of steps with their wall-clock readings, and its limit of 64, are gone: the clock keeps only the sum. A daemon opens every active project once at start, so this is the path of a project whose opening failed then, of one added or restored since, and of a step during the start itself. **The line** between a step and an outage is the clock's first reading, which the daemon makes as it starts: a jump before it, the daemon down, still ends leases as an outage of that length would. **Still exposed, in the safe direction:** a database that another process wrote after a jump (a restore, `ariesd import-bd`) and that the daemon then opens for the first time has claims that lost nothing to the jump, and they are extended anyway, by the jump or by the time since the database's latest event if that is less, since a store's time never goes before that event; a lease may live that much too long, none ends early, and the log gains a `clock.step` that says so.
- **13 Oct 2026, no write that begins after a jump is dated before it.** This corrects the note above on what may be compared across two logs: the exception it calls by design is closed, and what it left open is built. Six tests failed first, on a clock the test sets and with the write stopped where it waits for the writer by the store's tracer, no sleep deciding any order: a write that had settled and was waiting when the clock jumped twelve minutes was dated on the time before, with no `clock.step` ahead of it and the leases not extended, in a project's store and in the control store; so were two such writes at once, and a write run again after losing to a concurrent transaction. **The rule.** An attempt of a write takes its time once it holds the writer, as it did. If a forward step is pending then, it rolls back, settles and begins again (`writeOnce`); the settle is the one there was (`SettleClock`: a write of its own, under the store's settle lock, with one `clock.step`), and the settle a write starts with stays too, because it gives the leases the step before the wait for the writer and not after it. Considered and not done: settling inside the write's own transaction, since a `clock.step` is the server's event on the time before the step and a write's events carry one actor and one time, and since a transaction that then lost would leave what the store remembers as settled to be undone; and holding the settle lock across every write, a second lock on every write for something that happens when a clock is set. **Once for each jump.** The settle lock and the sum the store has settled are unchanged: of two writes that find a step, the second waits on the lock, holding no transaction, and then finds nothing pending. The attempt gives up the writer before it settles, so the settle's write is never behind the write that asked for it. **The bound.** An attempt settles once. If the clock has stepped again by the time it has begun again, it ends as an attempt that lost to a concurrent transaction does: the write backs off and is run again, and after `MaxAttempts` (5) it is refused with `conflict`, whose fix is to retry. It never goes on with a step pending, so a clock that jumps at every attempt costs a refusal and not an event dated before a jump; a test jumps the clock at every wait for the writer and reads the log for that, and for every jump settled once. **Nothing is slower when nothing is pending:** the attempt already read the store's time there, and now reads from the same reading whether a step is pending. A transaction that begins and commits on a development machine under load (`BenchmarkWrite/empty`, the binaries of before and after run in turn, twelve runs of 3,000 each): 43.8 µs before and 40.1 µs after by the median, 38.6 to 47.2 and 38.3 to 45.5, which is no difference. **Leases** are as they were: the settle still adds the whole jump to every lease live on the store's own time before any time after the jump is given out, and a lease that ran out before the settle still had all of its time and is not extended, so `TestLeaseThatRunsOutBeforeAJumpIsSettled` stands unchanged; a settle now comes sooner than it did for a write that was waiting, and never later. **What may now be relied on.** In each log the `clock.step` stands between the events dated before a jump and those after it. A store's time is never behind the latest time any store of the server has given out once nothing is pending for it, and no attempt goes on with anything pending, so an event one store records because of an event in another is never dated before it. **What remains:** an event is dated when its transaction begins, so the one write in each database whose transaction had begun when the clock jumped is dated before the jump and commits after it, by no more than that write took; it is before its log's `clock.step`, and nothing it causes elsewhere is dated before it. A reading of the store's time that is not a write (a list of who is active, a report's window) is on the time before a jump until the store settles, as before; none of them dates an event. **The soak.** The allowance its check of claims released for lost access made for this (`behindJump`) is removed, not kept: with it a release dated before its cause passed, and the store no longer writes one. The check places a release against the control log by time and gives it only the soak's second; its table test now fails a release, and an admin's override, dated on the time before a jump for a cause dated after it, which it passed before. The check of handshakes stays on the control log's order (`dialFault`): a handshake's times are the run's reckoning and no store's, so no time could be stricter than the order is. With the check strict, 32 two-minute runs of seed 264949260793903814 with `-race`, eight at once on a 16-core machine whose load average reached 85, all passed: 767 jumps forward, 646 claims released for lost access and 67,659 handshakes; with the allowance, and before it, the note above counts 3 runs in 48 failing on this check.
- **16 Oct 2026, slack forgiven only when something read the store's time.** A partition soak on arm64 (seed 7307281696249292707) ended a claim 0.6 s short of the lease it was owed after a jump of 14 minutes. After a step back, a store's time runs slow, and what its leases gain meanwhile is its slack, which a later jump takes back before any is added; once the clock has caught up the slack is forgiven. It was forgiven only by a reading of the store's time with no jump pending. A store that nothing read as the clock caught up, as a project's is while its agents are cut off, still held its slack when the clock jumped, and the reading that settles a jump has one pending, so the settle took the stale slack from every lease. A reading that finds the clock, less any jump pending, caught up with the store's time now forgives the slack whether a jump is pending or not (`TestSlackIsForgivenWithoutAReadOnceCaughtUp`).

## 8. Offline and conflicts

The client keeps a SQLite read cache of everything you can see, plus an outbox of operations. Each operation carries the `rev` it was based on, a hybrid logical clock stamp and an idempotency key. On reconnect the outbox replays in order; the server applies each operation or parks it as a conflict. Sync never blocks on a conflict.

| Change | Rule |
|---|---|
| Comments, labels, deps, new issues, memories with new keys | Merge (set-like, never conflict) |
| Different fields of one issue | Both apply (per-field three-way merge) |
| Same scalar field, both changed | Conflict, parked with base, mine and theirs; the server value stays until resolved |
| Long text (body, design, notes) | diff3 merge; overlapping hunks park a conflict |
| Status and claim | Server wins. Offline claims are *provisional*: if someone else claimed it, your claim loses, you get an inbox item, and your offline changes are kept as a comment |
| Close vs. edit | Close wins; the edit is kept as a comment |
| Delete vs. edit | Delete wins; the edit is kept on the tombstone and is restorable |

- `aries away 4h` extends your leases before you go offline, so your claims hold.
- Conflicts show in `aries conflicts` and in the MCP `conflicts` tool. Each one lists three choices: mine, theirs, or merged text. Resolving one is a normal operation, so it is audited too.
- A recommendation comes with each conflict. For example: "theirs is newer and from the claim holder; take theirs".

## 9. Bandwidth and verbosity

- Lists return `id, title, status, priority` plus only the fields asked for. `show` defaults to compact, and full text comes on request.
- Write results return `{id, rev}` only.
- Results are paged with a cursor; the default limit is 10 for agents.
- Sync is incremental by event sequence, compressed, and sends deltas, not rows.
- Errors take one line, with a `fix:` hint.
- Cap the prime payload and the MCP tool schemas; CI fails on a budget breach.

## 10. Search and vectors (later)

A design that builds this in slices, with an endpoint in place of a model on the server and the vectors kept outside Dolt, is proposed in §19.

- An `embeddings` table keyed by (kind, id, model), filled when an issue, comment or memory changes, and skipped when its content hash is unchanged.
- The model runs locally on the server (nomic-embed-text by default), configurable per deployment to a hosted model. Store and model trade-offs: [database.md](database.md#vectors-later).
- Below about 50k rows, brute-force cosine in Go is fast enough. Above that, a Dolt vector index, or pgvector on the Postgres fallback.
- Uses:
  - `search --similar`;
  - the duplicate check at create;
  - ranking memories for prime.

## 11. Versions and upgrades

Dolt stays the backend (decided 6 Oct 2026). Aries and ariesd ship as one signed release: cosign keyless signature on the checksums, plus a build provenance attestation.

**`aries upgrade [--check] [--rollback]`** (each developer machine)
- Reads the latest release, verifies the signature and checksum, and replaces the binary atomically. The previous binary is kept for `--rollback`.
- `--check` prints one line and changes nothing.
- Nothing upgrades itself without the command.

**`ariesd upgrade [--check] [--to vX.Y.Z] [--rollback]`** (on the server, as admin, or remotely as `aries admin server upgrade`)
1. Verify the release, as above.
2. Back up first: a SQL dump and a Dolt tag `aries-<old version>`.
3. Drain: refuse new writes, flush the commit batcher.
4. Swap the binary, run schema migrations, restart through systemd.
5. Health check. On failure, restore the old binary and report. Migrations are additive for one release, so the old binary still runs against the new schema.

**Dolt version.** Each Aries release pins the Dolt version it was tested with. `ariesd upgrade` installs that version (checksum verified), not simply the newest Dolt, because Dolt releases about weekly and an untested server/client mix is how bd lost data. `ariesd check` warns when the running Dolt differs from the pin.

**Version handshake.** The first exchange on every connection carries the client's version and protocol version, and the server's version, its protocol range and the latest release it knows of. ariesd checks for releases at most once a day; this can be turned off for air-gapped servers, where the admin sets the latest version by hand.

| Situation | Who is told | How |
|---|---|---|
| Server behind the latest release | every client and the admin | CLI: one stderr line, at most once a day per machine. MCP: one line in prime, never in tool results. `ariesd check` fails with a `fix:` line |
| Server behind a security release | everyone | as above, but on every CLI command and every session start until upgraded |
| Client behind the server | that client | one line: `aries upgrade` |
| Client outside the server's protocol range | that client | refused, with a typed exit code and a `fix:` line (fail closed) |

The protocol supports one version back and one forward (principle 9), so the server and clients can be upgraded independently.

**As built (stage 2, 7 Oct 2026).** Where the build differs from the above:
- **Signing.** Changed on 7 Oct from cosign keyless: `checksums.txt.sig` is an Ed25519 signature over `checksums.txt`, made in the release workflow with a key held as a GitHub environment secret (`release`). Only release runs the maintainer approves, on `v*` tags only the maintainer can push, can use it; GitHub and organization owners can still reach it, which is the accepted trade-off. The public keys are compiled in (`internal/release/keys.go`, a list so a rotation can overlap); with none, every upgrade fails closed. GitHub build provenance (`actions/attest-build-provenance`) is kept as an independent check. `RELEASING.md` has the workflow, setup and rotation.
- **`aries upgrade`** verifies the signature and the archive's sha256, runs the staged binary's `version` to confirm it, keeps the old binary as `<exe>.prev` and renames the new one into place (on Windows the running exe is first renamed to `.old`). It refuses dev builds and ignores prereleases. `--rollback` consumes `.prev`, so a second rollback reports there is nothing to restore.
- **`ariesd upgrade`** runs on Linux as the daemon's user, never root. The backup is the Dolt tag `aries-<old version>` (`store.BackupTag`, after a commit of the working set; a later upgrade from the same version adds `aries-<old>-<time>` rather than moving it). There is no SQL dump yet: for one, run `dolt dump` in the server's data directory before upgrading. There is no drain op: the restart relies on systemd's stop, which cancels the daemon's context, closes connections (an in-flight write is rolled back and the client told) and makes the final Dolt commit in `Store.Close`. Migrations run when the new daemon opens the store. It restarts only when `systemd_unit:` is set or `--restart` is given, through `sudo -n systemctl restart`; the health check is a handshake over the socket, wanting the new version within 30 seconds, and a failure restores the old binary and restarts it. `--to` refuses to go back a version (migrations only go forward); `--rollback` is the way back. `aries admin server upgrade` is not built.
- **Dolt version.** `version.Dolt` records the pin (2.4.2), a test keeps CI's `DOLT_VERSION` equal to it, and the release notes name it. `ariesd upgrade` does not install Dolt yet, and `ariesd check` does not exist yet.
- **Latest release in the handshake.** The welcome still carries only the hand-set `latest:`; ariesd does not check GitHub for releases yet, so the "server behind" notices depend on that setting. The CLI prints only the "client behind" line, on every command that connects, and not the daily "server behind" line in the table above; that notice appears only in `prime`, for agents and people alike.

**As built (releases from a static host, 13 Oct 2026).** The maintainer decided that the repository becomes private and that releases are then published to a storage bucket; this is the client's half, built before either. Where the build differs from the above:
- **Two sources, one check.** `internal/release` reads a release from GitHub's API or from a static tree: `index.json` at a base URL and a directory for each tag holding the archives, `checksums.txt` and `checksums.txt.sig`. The tree may be on any host that serves files over HTTPS, or in a directory, which is how a site with no network installs a release carried in on media. What decides whether a release is accepted is above both and unchanged: the Ed25519 signature on `checksums.txt` against the compiled-in keys, and the archive's sha256.
- **The host is transport.** `index.json` only chooses which tag to ask for. It is refused whole when it is over 1 MiB or 4,096 releases, of another format, or names anything but a release version; the latest release is the highest version that is not a prerelease, by the tag itself, whatever the file says of order or of prereleases. No redirect from a static host is followed, plain HTTP is accepted only to a loopback address, and a tag is checked before it is put in a URL. A host can still withhold: one that serves an old index keeps clients where they are, as GitHub could. Neither binary installs a release older than the one running.
- **Which source.** One constant, `release.DefaultSource`, which is GitHub until the release that precedes the change of visibility; `ARIES_RELEASES` overrides it for `aries`, `ariesd` and `install.sh` alike, and `releases:` in ariesd's config file does for the server. `RELEASING.md` has the layout, the tool that writes it (`internal/tools/releaseindex`) and the order in which the repository may be made private.

**As built (one protocol for everything unreleased, 12 Oct 2026).** Decision 25 (§14). Where an earlier note in this document says "protocol 6", read 5: the key ops, `principal.add`'s keys and `status` belong to protocol 5, with the admin ops. Where one says `ProtoMin` stays 2 or that v0.2.x to v0.4.x clients keep working, that held until this change. `internal/proto/version.go` has the history.
- **The numbers.** `Proto`, `ProtoMin` and `ProtoMax` are 5. `aries version` prints `(protocol 5)`, `ariesd version` `(protocol 5-5)`, and `aries admin status` `protocol 5 to 5`.
- **"One version back and one forward" is suspended for this one step**, and the table above still describes every later one. Until both sides are upgraded, the side upgraded first cannot talk to the other.
- **What an older client is told** is the server's to choose, because a released client prints the error of the welcome as it is. A v0.4.0 `aries` prints `aries: aries speaks protocol 4; the server (v0.5.0) needs protocol 5` and `fix: upgrade this client: run `aries upgrade`, or install the latest release (docs/install.md), then restart any agent that runs `aries mcp``, and exits 3. The fix named only `aries upgrade` before; it gained the installer, for a client too old or too broken to upgrade itself, and the restart, because the MCP server in an agent's harness is the old `aries` until the harness starts it again. An agent on the old `aries mcp` gets code `version` on every tool call, with that fix quoted for the user. `aries upgrade` reads the release and asks the server nothing, so the refusal does not stop it.
- **What this client is told by an older server** is unchanged in substance: it speaks 5, the server is older and accepts protocols 2 to 4, and the fix is to have the server upgraded.
- **The upgrade's health check crosses the boundary.** `ariesd upgrade` is run by the old binary, whose check is a handshake in the old protocol with the new daemon. Refused, a v0.4.0 ariesd would have taken the new daemon for dead and put itself back, at every attempt. So the daemon answers the upgrade's probe whatever protocol it speaks. The probe is a reserved principal that only the daemon's own user can claim on the socket, sends no request, and was already answered for the server before any project is looked up. Rollback is as before: a daemon that does not answer is replaced by the old binary, which serves the old clients, provided the new one migrated nothing (§11).
- **The order for an operator:** tell people; upgrade the server; everyone runs `aries upgrade`; everyone restarts their agents. The server guide's Upgrades section, the install guide and `RELEASING.md` say so.
- **Removed, because only older clients needed it.** No code in the server branched on a client's protocol version, and none does now: what older clients relied on was optional arguments and ignored result fields, which rule 10 still requires. What went is tests that drove protocols 2 to 4, and the protocol 5 of main before the key ops, against this server and expected a welcome; they now expect the refusal, or were folded together. `TestReleasedClients` pins the refusal and its words for each release. In the client, `mcpserver` no longer allows for a server that returns a page with no total, which protocol 1 did.
- **Kept.** Clients ignore result fields and frame types they do not know, and the server refuses argument fields it does not know (rule 10). The client's allowances for a server that lacks an op (the inbox and memory reads of `prime`, the board without `claims`, cost without its newer fields) are not reachable from a released server any more, since each is refused at the handshake; they were left, as they also cover a server built from main between two additions to an unreleased version.

**As built (requests carry trace context, 12 Oct 2026).** Decision 27 (§14). It rides protocol 5, with no further bump: frames are decoded leniently, so a server built from main before it ignores the fields.
- **Wire.** Two optional fields of a `req` frame, `tp` (the `traceparent`) and `ts` (the `tracestate`), in the style of the frame's other short keys (`internal/proto/doc.go`). They are on the request and not on the hello, because one `aries mcp` connection serves many agent turns.
- **Validation** is `proto.ParseTraceContext`, used by both sides. `traceparent` must be exactly `00-`, 32 lowercase hex digits, `-`, 16, `-`, 2, with neither id all zeros; a later version's value is not read as a version-00 one. `tracestate` is one line of printable ASCII of at most 512 bytes, and one that is not is dropped while its `traceparent` is kept, which the recommendation allows; a `tracestate` with no valid `traceparent` is nothing. A table test and a fuzz target pin it.
- **Server.** The request loop parses the two fields and, when they are valid, puts the context on the request's `context.Context` (`server.TraceFrom` reads it), where a later span would take its parent from. The request's log line, at debug for a success and sampled at info for a refusal as before, gains `trace_id` and `span_id`, the caller's span, read back from that context. Nothing else reads it: no spans, no export, no dependency, and no change to any answer. An invalid context is ignored without a log line of its own, and no `tracestate` is ever logged, since it is the caller's text.
- **Client.** `client.TraceFromEnv` reads `TRACEPARENT` and `TRACESTATE`, the environment carrier, through the `Getenv` the process's `cli.Env` supplies, validates them the same way and gives `client.Options.Trace`, which the connection sends on every request. `aries mcp` therefore sends the context it was started with on every tool call; a context for each tool call, from the MCP request's metadata, is left to the tracing work. No MCP tool or schema changed.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (no `latest`, decision 88).** The hand-set `latest:` setting is removed (the surface review's question 11): it went stale silently, and `aries upgrade --check` and `ariesd upgrade --check` ask the release source itself. So the "server behind the latest release" row of the table above has no source: no client notes it, in `prime` or on the command line. A config file that still sets `latest:` is refused as an unknown key. The server sends no `latest` in the welcome and the client no longer reads it; the frame's field and `proto.OlderServerWarning` go with the protocol's settling after the Postgres port's P1. A check of the release source by the daemon itself, the table's plan, may come in 1.x.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (the protocol settled at 8, decisions 78 and 88).** After the Postgres port's P1, the protocol is settled for v1.0.0 at 8, spoken alone: `Proto`, `ProtoMin` and `ProtoMax` are 8 (the surface review's question 5). Where an earlier note in this document describes protocols 5, 6 or 7, a client stepping down to an older server, or a server answering an older client otherwise, read it as history: no release spoke 5 to 7, and none of it is built now.
- **Refused at the handshake.** A client of any other version is refused with code `version`, its fix saying which side to upgrade (`proto.CheckProto`), and `aries` exits with code 3. A client refused so says no second hello: `client.Dial` takes the refusal as it is. `TestReleasedClients` (`internal/proto`) pins what each of v0.1.x to v0.4.x, and a build of 5, 6 or 7, is told.
- **Deleted:** `proto.Speak`, `ForProtocol`, `Keyed`, `ProtoKeys`, `ProtoConflicts` and `ProtoLate`; the server's `codeFor`, which sent `stale` to a client of 5 as `conflict`; the client's `stepDown` and second hello, and `Conn.Proto`; the outbox's protocol checks before it queues and before it replays (`f1Proto`, `speaks`), since every op it queues takes a key in 8; the welcome frame's `latest` and `proto.OlderServerWarning`; and the version history in `internal/proto/version.go`, with the "(protocol N)" notes on the types. `seen.json` keeps `proto` as a record, which nothing reads.
- **Kept:** the welcome's `min` and `max`, the status report's `proto_min` and `proto_max`, `CheckProto` and the server's `ProtoMin`/`ProtoMax` settings, since rule 10's range is how 1.x adds ops; and the upgrade's health check, which the daemon answers whatever it speaks.
- **`dep.rm` honors its key.** It took `idem` and ignored it; it now applies a repeat once, as `dep.add` does, so a remove sent again after someone put the edge back leaves the edge. `aries dep remove` and the MCP `dep` tool send one. It is still never queued (§18.3).
- **From v0.5.0 on** (decision 93; v1.0.0 before it), rule 10's bump applies to every change: `Proto` and `ProtoMax` go up, and `ProtoMin` stays at the version before.

**As built, 17 Oct 2026 (protocol 9: the key proof bound to the TLS session).** Protocol 9 is v0.6.0's one bump (one per release, from 18 Oct 2026: the first change after a release raises the protocol and later ones join it, `ProtoMin` staying at the last released version). `handoff`'s `ticked` and `waived` made it (§21, As built); this joins it. `Proto` and `ProtoMax` are 9, `ProtoMin` stays 8, so a v0.6.0 server serves v0.5.0's clients, and a v0.6.0 client is refused by a v0.5.0 server with code `version` and the fix to have the server upgraded. What it changes here is the HTTPS key proof (finding K-2 of [security-review-v1.md](security-review-v1.md), `aries-tl2cc2df`): where ariesd terminates TLS its challenge asks for RFC 9266's `tls-exporter`, and a client of 9 signs a v2 transcript bound to its TLS session, so that a proof relayed between two sessions fails; behind a proxy nothing is bound. A client of 9 never signs v1, so nothing it signs can be relayed to a server that still takes v1; the server takes v1 from 8's clients until `ProtoMin` moves to 9, or until `https: refuse_key_proof_v1` is set. [transport.md, As built 17 Oct 2026](transport.md#as-built-17-oct-2026-the-key-proof-bound-to-the-tls-session) has the design and why the rule is the client's; `TestReleasedClients` (`internal/proto`) holds a v0.5.0 client served.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (the protocol's names, decision 88).** Inside protocol 8, before any release carried it (decision 78), so with no bump: the surface review's §4 renames (questions 6 and 8). `internal/proto/doc.go` now states the rule they follow, and `TestWireNames` (`internal/proto`) pins each.
- **Ops.** `resolve` is `conflict.resolve`, as its event is. The CLI command stays `aries resolve`.
- **Fields.** An issue's `parent_id` is `parent`, as every argument already was; its `due_at`, and a summary's, is `due`, as `create` and `update` take it. A claim's and an overlap's `id` is `issue`, and so is `hours.log`'s argument. The principal a result of `principal.add`, `principal.disable`, `principal.enable`, `member.set` and `member.remove` names is `name`, and the key of `key.add` and `key.remove` is `fingerprint`. `lock.release`'s `released` is `epoch`, as `lock.leave`'s is.
- **Removed.** An issue's `expires_at`, `ephemeral`, `pinned`, `template` and `metadata`, which only `import-bd` set and no client read: the store keeps them for `export-bd`. `IDArgs`, which no op took. `aries create --id` stays, for scripts that import, and no usage line or guide names it.
- **Inbox kinds.** `assigned`, `mention`, `handoff` and `conflict` are `issue.assigned`, `issue.mentioned`, `issue.handoff` and `conflict.parked`. Migration 0002 renames the items already written.
- **Not renamed.** A conflict's `field` and an event's before and after states name an issue's fields as the event log records them (`parent_id`, `due_at`): they are history, and the merge reads them from the log. A gate names its issues `blocks` and `subject`.
- **The client's cache** is at entry version 2, so an answer kept under the names before is read as absent.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (the names of `limits:`, decision 88).** The surface review's question 3: one rule for every key of `limits:`, with no aliases, since no release has carried any of them on 1.0. A count is `THING_per_SCOPE`, a size ends in `_bytes`, a rate names its unit, a burst ends in `_burst`, and a duration says what the time is for. `TestLimitNames` (`internal/server`) holds the keys to the rule, and `TestLimitsTable` the Limits table of docs/server.md to the keys (rule 18).
- **Renamed:** `acceptance_items` → `acceptance_items_per_issue`; `inbox_unread` → `unread_per_principal`; `usage_records` → `records_per_usage_call`; `memory_body` → `memory_body_bytes`; `memory_tags` → `tags_per_memory`; `memory_tag_length` → `memory_tag_bytes`; `memory_key_length` → `memory_key_bytes`; `prices` → `prices_per_project`; `hours_note` → `hours_note_bytes`; `plans` → `plans_per_project`; `plan_principals` → `principals_per_plan`; `gate_approvers` → `approvers_per_gate`; `open_gates` → `open_gates_per_project`; `gate_checks` → `checks_per_issue`; `gate_evidence` → `gate_evidence_bytes`; `locks` → `locks_per_project`; `lock_capacity` → `holders_per_lock`; `lock_waiters` → `waiters_per_lock`; `projects` → `projects_per_server`; `principals` → `principals_per_server`; `conns` → `conns_per_server`; `write_rate` → `writes_per_second`; `refusal_logs` → `refusal_logs_per_minute`; `open_stores` → `open_stores_per_server`; `control_write_rate` → `control_writes_per_second`.
- **Moved:** `conflict_age` and `due_soon`, which bound nothing, are settings at the top of the config file, with the same defaults; `telemetry_requests` is `telemetry.max_requests`, beside the listener it bounds. Under `limits:` each is refused as an unknown key.
- **Kept:** every key that already followed the rule, P2's `db_conns_per_server` and `db_conns_per_project` among them; and `notices_per_minute`, `usage_per_day` and `hours_per_day`, which name their unit.
- **Telemetry.** The `limit` attribute of `aries.limit.refusals` names the setting, so its values follow: `conns_per_server`, `writes_per_second`, `control_writes_per_second`, `open_stores_per_server`, `telemetry.max_requests`, `projects_per_server`, `principals_per_server`, `prices_per_project`, `plans_per_project`, `locks_per_project` and `waiters_per_lock`; the alert rules in `deploy/observability` match them.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (the naming promise, decision 88).** The surface review's question 13, written once the names above settled. The promise is in docs/server.md, Observability, "Names that are kept": from v1.0.0 and through 1.x, an instrument's name and Prometheus name, an attribute's key and listed values, a span's name and keys, and a log event's and field's names are never renamed or removed, and a value never changes meaning; only additions are made. Two golden files hold the code to it, `internal/telemetry/testdata/names.golden` and `internal/logs/testdata/names.golden` (`TestNamesKept`): a name that goes fails, and a new one is recorded with `-update`, after which it is kept too. A log line's message and the text of `ariesd status` are for people and are not promised. The same commit made the MCP `dep` and `label` tools' `action` `add` or `remove`, as the CLI spells it (`rm` is refused there; the CLI keeps `rm` as a hidden alias); the tool schemas measure about 2,773 estimated tokens (8,318 bytes).

## 12. Developer experience

Agreed 6 Oct 2026; each item lands in the stage shown in §13.

| # | Feature | Agent or person sees |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **`start` and `finish`** | `start` claims the top ready issue (or a named one) and returns it with acceptance criteria, the last handoff and the relevant memories. `finish` closes it, writes the handoff and files discovered work linked `discovered-from`. A typical session is two calls, not six. |
| 2 | **Git awareness** | `claim`/`start` can create the branch or worktree (`<type>/<id>-<slug>`). Commits and PRs link to the issue by branch name or an `Aries:` trailer. A merged PR closes the issue when the CI check reports `landed` (decision 67), or for a server CI cannot reach through `aries land`, which git's post-merge hook runs only if the person asks (`aries setup post-merge`). No git hook is installed unasked. |
| 3 | **Files to issues** | The server records which paths each issue's commits touched. `ready` ranks down work that overlaps paths held by another claim or reservation; `show` lists likely files. |
| 4 | **Acceptance checklist** | Acceptance criteria are items, not prose. The agent ticks them; `close` refuses until all are ticked or waived with a reason. |
| 5 | **Errors that say what to do next** | Every refusal names the cause and the next action, for example `ar-a1b2 claimed by alice/codex 3m ago; next ready: ar-c3d4`. Typed error codes for programs. |
| 6 | **Similar closed issues** | `show` and `create` list up to three similar closed issues: full-text first, vectors when §10 lands. |
| 7 | **Live board** | `aries tui` (terminal), driven by the event stream. A web view served by `ariesd` is dropped for now (7 Oct 2026): ariesd opens no port, and a TUI over the existing SSH connection keeps it that way. |
| 8 | **`ariesd --dev`** | A throwaway local server with a temporary Dolt database and seeded sample data, for trying Aries, demos and agent tests. |
| 9 | **Token budget in CI** | Scripted agent sessions measure tokens for each tool schema, each result shape and prime; CI fails when one exceeds its budget. |
| 10 | **Time and token reporting** | Optional: the client records wall time per claim and, where the harness exposes it, tokens per issue. Shown in `show` and `digest`. |

**As built, 8 Oct 2026 (files to issues).** Item 3, decided with the maintainer: an issue's likely files are the paths its own commits touched, from any holder, plus paths people declare. Nothing is predicted, but each row carries a `source` so a later source can join. Where the build differs from, or fills in, the above:
- **Storage.** Migration 0012 adds `issue_paths`: issue, path (binary collation), source (`commit` or `declared`), the principal and session that last sent it, first and last time, `rev` and `write_id`, keyed by (issue, path, source). A path is 1 to 1,024 bytes of printable UTF-8, relative to the repository root with forward slashes: no leading `/`, no `\`, and no empty, `.` or `..` segment (`proto.CheckPath`, fuzzed). A declared path ending in `/` is a directory prefix; a commit path never is.
- **Protocol 3, no bump.** `renew` (paths per issue), `finish` and `handoff` take commit `paths`; `create` and `update` take declared `paths`; `ready` summaries gain `overlaps` and `show` gains `files`. Protocol 3 has not been released (v0.2.2 speaks 2), so the fields join it, as `version.go` records. A protocol 2 client omits them and is served as before.
- **Capture is client-side** (`gitx.ReadChanges`, `gitx.IssuePaths`). `aries` runs `git log` and `git status` without optional locks, bounded at 5 s and 500 commits. An issue's paths are those of the commits since the default branch whose `Aries:` trailer names it; on the issue's own branch, every commit on it and the uncommitted files (staged, changed, untracked, not ignored), newest first. The log follows first parents with merge diffs off (git 2.31 or later), so commits a merge brought in, such as `main` merged into the branch, are not the branch's work. The default branch is `origin/HEAD`, then `origin/main` or `origin/master`, then local `main` or `master`, which may be stale, then the branch's upstream last, since a feature branch's upstream is usually its own remote copy. With none of them only uncommitted files count. A detached HEAD, no repository or no git sends nothing. `aries mcp` sends the paths of each held issue with a renewal at most every five minutes per issue, for at most 50 issues a renewal, and with `finish` and `handoff`, so no tool schema changed. The CLI has no `renew`; `aries away` is its renewal: it renews first, without paths, then sends each returned claim's paths in a renew of its own, so the server refusing one issue's paths loses only those. `aries finish` and `handoff` send their issue's. Paths the server would refuse are left out, and any request whose paths are refused anyway is sent again without them (`proto.RetryWithoutPaths`, shared by the CLI and `aries mcp`), so a hint never costs a claim or a finish. `aries create` and `update --paths` take paths relative to the current directory, like any path on a command line, and send them relative to the repository root (`git rev-parse --show-prefix`).
- **Writes.** Commit paths add rows and refresh `last_at` on known ones. Each path's `last_at` is the write's time less its index in the request in microseconds, so the client's most-recent-first order survives within one request. The refresh runs through `wtx.touch`, which exempts only its own statement from the event check (rule 7 now lists it): a finish or handoff that refreshes paths still needs an event for anything else it changes. A call that adds paths appends one `issue.paths` event naming up to 20 added and removed paths, with counts beyond that. Declared paths replace the declared set, with an event when it changes, and raise the issue's revision, writing the issue row with a new `write_id`: the paths live in their own table, so without that two replaces made from one revision would both commit and leave their union. The guard applies to `update`, `finish` and `handoff`; `renew` records only on claims the caller holds and ignores other ids. `finish` leaves its paths out of the idempotency hash, so a retry from a tree that has moved on is still the same finish. One request carries at most 200 paths for an issue, counted before repeats are dropped, and a renew carries paths for at most 50 issues (`proto.MaxPaths`, `proto.MaxPathIssues`); past either it is refused as `invalid`.
- **Cap** (`paths_per_issue`, default 200, both sources together). Commit paths over the cap are trimmed, keeping the most recent, because they arrive on a renewal no one is watching and a refusal would only drop them; declared paths take precedence over commit paths. More declared paths than the cap are refused (`invalid`), naming a directory prefix as the fix, since a person typed them.
- **Ranking.** An issue overlaps a held one when a path is equal, or a declared directory of either covers a path of the other. Held means a live claim of any session but the caller's own; another session of the same principal counts. A held path that more than 10 held issues share, such as `go.mod`, is left out: most work touches it, so it says nothing about which work collides. `ready` keeps its order, and moves every issue that overlaps after all those that do not, with `overlaps: [held ids]`. `start` with no id takes the first that overlaps nothing, if any, and a `start` refused because someone else holds the issue names the next ready issue by the caller's own ranking. Only the first max(limit × 5, 500) ready issues are inspected, so the cost under the writer (start ranks there) is bounded however long the backlog; those past them are never reached, since at least `limit` come first. Dolt does not use a secondary index for string prefix ranges, so the held paths (live claims times the cap) are loaded once and indexed in Go, and the ready ids are walked in batches of 200 with primary-key lookups; with no held paths the query is the one it was.
- **Show** lists declared paths in path order, then commit paths by recency, 20 in all with a count of the rest, and each overlapping held issue with its holder and session. MCP `show` gives `files`, `files_more` and `overlaps`, dropping the files first when over its token budget; MCP `ready` passes `overlaps` through.
- **Reservations** (stage 6) are not built. The seam is the held-path index (`loadHeld`): a reservation's glob would join the held set, and ranking and `show` would then cover it unchanged.

**As built, 8 Oct 2026 (live board).** Item 7 is `aries tui`, package `internal/board`. Where the build differs from, or fills in, the above:
- **The event stream.** Stage 3's push carried only inbox items, so the board needed more from the server. `watch` takes `events: true`, and that connection is then also pushed each event committed on an issue, as `{"t":"evt","op":"event","e":{seq, at, principal, session, machine, op, issue}}`, without the before and after states (`history` has them). The store hands them to a watch through the same queue of 64 as inbox items, after the commit, so a rolled-back write pushes nothing and a slow reader overflows into a `resync` rather than holding up the writer; usage events, which are no issue's, are not pushed. The store counts its open event watches, and a write builds and publishes issue events only while there is one. An event is an issue's when its target is shaped like an issue id (`IssueID.Validate`), so the targets of stage 4's memory events must never look like issue ids, or every board is pushed them. Watching again with the other setting starts the watch afresh.
- **Who holds what.** No read listed every live claim with when it was taken, so the board would have needed a `show` per held issue on every refresh. The read op `claims` lists them all, longest held first, with `claimed_at` (now on every wire `Claim`), the server's `now`, and `more` past its limit (100 by default, at most 500). It writes nothing, so it is not rate limited. It is not an MCP tool.
- **Protocol 3, no bump.** `watch`'s `events`, the `event` push and `claims` join protocol 3, which has not been released (v0.2.2 speaks 2), as `version.go` records. A server built from `main` before them refuses `events` as a bad argument; the board then shows the refusal and its fix in its header rather than redialing.
- **Reads.** The board watches first, then reads `ready` and `blocked` (100 each), `claims`, and `list` of `in_progress` issues for the held issues' titles: four requests, about as cheap as `aries ready`. It reads them again 250 ms after the first event of a burst that can change them (anything but comments, labels, acceptance items and an admin's override note, and any op it does not know), after a `resync`, after its own queue of 256 pushed events overflows, and on `r`. An open issue's detail is read again when an event names it. It never polls the server; the only timer redraws relative times each second, which on Windows, where there is no SIGWINCH, also notices a resize. A lost connection is redialed after a random wait (full jitter) up to a ceiling that starts at 1 s and doubles to 30 s, and the board watches and reads afresh; the ceiling starts over only once a new connection has read the lists, so a server that accepts and drops every connection is not dialed once a second. Widths are terminal cells, not runes: East Asian wide and fullwidth characters take two, combining marks and format characters none, from a table of Unicode 17.0.0 in `width.go`, and a wide character that would straddle the edge becomes a space. Lease ages are on the server's clock, from the `now` in `claims`.
- **The terminal.** Raw mode and the size come from `golang.org/x/term`, the one dependency added; the board is drawn with hand-written ANSI sequences on the alternate screen, with escape sequences turned on for Windows consoles through `golang.org/x/sys/windows`. The model draws pure frames, golden-tested, separate from the loop, which writes a frame only when it changed. The terminal is restored on every way out, panics in the loop and in its goroutines included. Raw mode turns Ctrl-Z into a plain byte, but a board stopped from outside may come back to a terminal the shell reset, so on Unix a SIGCONT puts it back in raw mode on the alternate screen and repaints the whole frame. The goroutine reading keys cannot be interrupted portably, so it ends at its next read after the board closes, or with the process.
- **Read-only, as any command.** The board changes no issue. Like every connection, its handshake touches the agents registry, so it shows in `who` as the person's `cli` session.

**As built, 8 Oct 2026 (multi-session soak).** Stage 3's gate (§13) is `TestSoak` in `internal/e2e`; [CONTRIBUTING.md](../../CONTRIBUTING.md#the-soak-test) says how to run it. Where it fills in the plan:
- **The stack is the real one.** Four principals (one an admin) with five sessions each reach a real daemon and Dolt through the in-process SSH server: per principal, one session runs `aries mcp`'s tools over an in-memory MCP transport, with its own renewals and redials, and the others use the client library, one of them watching with `events` as a live board does. Each session creates, starts the top ready issue or a named one, renews (sending paths), finishes with items ticked and work discovered, hands off with and without release, takes over issues other sessions hold, links, comments, labels, updates at fresh and stale revisions, closes, reopens, ticks items and reports usage, at random from a printed seed.
- **Failure is injected throughout.** The answer to a request the server applied is dropped, every connection is dropped every few seconds, the daemon restarts (once in the short run, about every two minutes in a long one), sessions vanish without a word, and others stall past their leases and then carry on with the claims they believe they hold. After a lost answer a session resends only what is safe to resend: a request with its idempotency key, a start or renewal of its own, or a read.
- **Leases are short by the clock, not by the limits.** The store's clock (`Options.Now`) runs 120 times fast, so a one-minute lease, still the minimum, lapses in half a second, and the reaper runs every 100 ms. Nothing in the store or server changed for the test, beyond the e2e harness learning to restart its daemon.
- **Invariants are checked three ways.** Each acknowledged answer is checked as it arrives (an epoch is never acknowledged to two sessions, a revision never to two writes). Every three seconds one read-only transaction reads the new events and the tables; a replay of the log checks each transaction (gap-free seq, monotonic time, takes, expiries and releases that name the claim they end, no other principal taking a live claim, a same-principal takeover only when asked) and rebuilds issues, labels, edges and claims, which must equal the tables, and rule 18's caps are checked. At the end every request's unique marker must be in the log once if acknowledged, at most once if its answer was lost and never if refused; fencing, guards, renewals, `claim.lost` items, inbox and event pushes, `ready` and `blocked`, usage and its attribution, and `claims` are checked against the log and the tables; and no daemon goroutine or SSH connection may outlive the sessions.
- **Pushes are owed from the watch's answer.** An inbox item or issue event committed in a transaction that began after the client received its `watch` answer must reach that watch, in any order, unless a `resync` came first; one committed earlier may or may not. Owed pushes are awaited when a session drops its connection on purpose and at the end.
- **Every claim ends with an event of its own.** The replay changes a claim only on `claim.take`, `claim.expire` and `claim.release`, and fails a close of an issue still held, an update other than start's that changes a held issue's status or assignee, a release that names another claim than the one held, and a take that does not name a holder the log says is still there. Every release by another session than the holder's, of a live or a lapsed claim, must leave the holder a `claim.lost` item. The first version of the soak found releases with no event (§7, As built, claim.release); a table test keeps a silent release failing the replay.
- **First long run.** Ten minutes with `-race` (seed 588816792842611115): 49,086 requests, 28,493 events in 16,142 transactions, 2,284 issues, 4,095 claims taken and 2,514 reaped, 50 takeovers of a live claim, 2,408 dropped answers, 281 connection drops, 5 daemon restarts, 103 sessions vanished and 52 stalled; every invariant held. p50 per write was 110 to 160 ms (renew, which sends paths, 280 ms) and p99 under 1.3 s on a shared machine, the daemon's peak goroutines 486.
- **After claim.release.** Ten minutes with `-race` (seed 7700901455356988733), the replay strict about how claims end: 50,078 requests, 30,653 events in 16,491 transactions, 2,389 issues, 4,167 claims taken, 2,534 reaped and 1,561 released, 59 takeovers of a live claim, 2,395 dropped answers, 281 connection drops, 5 daemon restarts, 86 sessions vanished and 67 stalled; 3,202 issue events owed to 52 event watches, all delivered; every invariant held. Latencies were as in the first run.

### 12.1 Token and cost tracking

Agreed 6 Oct 2026. Extends item 10.

- **Account.** Every project has an `account`: a client's engagement code name or an internal department. It defaults to `internal`. An epic or issue can override it, and children inherit it. Code names only; the map to real clients lives outside Aries.
- **What is recorded, per issue and per session:** model, input tokens, output tokens, cache-write tokens, cache-read tokens, wall time, harness. Cache tokens are separate because they are priced differently.
- **Where the numbers come from:** the harness, never the model's own report. The client reads whatever the harness exposes (for example Claude Code's OpenTelemetry usage metrics or hook payloads, and Codex's token-usage log) and attributes each delta to the issue the session held at that moment. When a session holds several issues, the delta is split by time held, and the record says it was split. A harness that exposes nothing records wall time only, marked as such.
- **Prices.** A `prices` table keyed by (model, effective date) with input, output, cache-write and cache-read rates. Cost is computed when a report runs, never stored, so a price change never rewrites history. Admins update prices with `aries admin prices set`.
- **Subscriptions.** Reports always show the **list-price equivalent** (tokens at API rates). For a flat-rate plan, an admin records the plan's monthly fee and its seats; reports then also show the **amortized cost**: the month's fee split across all issues in proportion to their tokens.
- **Human time.** `aries log 1.5h <id>` records a person's hours against the same account.
- **Reports.** `aries cost --by account|issue|epic|person|model --since <date>` on the CLI, the MCP tool `cost` (below), and a cost line in `digest`. Agents see their current issue's running total in `show`, so they can notice when an issue gets expensive.
- **Limits.** Attribution is approximate when a session switches issues, and harnesses differ in what they expose. Invoicing stays outside Aries.

**As built, 8 Oct 2026 (server side, PR 1).** Client capture (`aries usage --hook=AGENT`, reading transcripts and hook payloads) follows in PR 2; prices, `aries cost`, `aries log` and the cost line stay in stage 4. Where the build differs from the above:
- **Storage.** Migration 0011 adds `token_usage` (`usage` is a reserved word in SQL): one row per harness request, keyed by (principal, session, request id), with model, harness, the source's time, a granularity (`request`, `turn` or `session`), a span start for turns and sessions, and five counts: input, output, cache write, `cache_write_1h` (the part of cache write with a one-hour lifetime, priced apart) and cache read. A NULL count is unknown, not 0. The principal is in the key because sessions are client-chosen and the CLI's `cli` session is shared. `issues.account` holds an issue's own account; NULL inherits up the parent chain, then the `account:` server setting (default `internal`). Names are code names: lowercase letters, digits and inner hyphens.
- **Protocol 3.** The `usage` op takes a batch of records and returns `{added, duplicates}`; a resent record is ignored, so a lost client offset cannot double-count. `create` and `update` take `account`. ProtoMin is 2: v0.1.x clients are refused.
- **Events.** Each `usage` call that stores rows appends one `usage.add` event (target `usage`, which no issue id can be), so the log does not grow by a row per API request. The rows are history in their own table.
- **Attribution** is computed on read from the events that already record claims: `claim.take` opens a hold and ends the previous holder's, at that claim's lease expiry if it lapsed first (the take's before state now records the claim it replaced: holder, epoch and expiry; a take recorded earlier has no before state and ends the hold at the take); `claim.expire` ends it at the lease expiry its before state records; `issue.close` ends it; so does an `issue.update` or `issue.import` that moves the issue out of `in_progress`, which is how a releasing handoff shows (a claimed issue is always `in_progress`), and an `issue.import` whose after state has `claim_released`, which covers a reassignment. An open hold ends now, or at its lease's expiry if the reaper has not run. No new event was needed, so claims taken before this change attribute too. A request record goes to the issues its session held at its time, shared evenly if several; a turn or session record is cut at hold boundaries and each piece shared among the issues held then; unheld pieces are unattributed. Each count is divided in whole tokens by the largest-remainder method, leftover tokens going to the largest remainders and ties in issue id order, so a record's parts sum to it exactly and totals are integer sums, never float. Wall time is the sum of an issue's holds, so a harness with no tokens still has time. Dividing loads only the holds of the records' sessions that overlap those records, in batched queries, indexes them once per session, and sweeps each record once over the k holds it overlaps, O(k log k), checking the context as it goes.
- **Amended 8 Oct 2026 (claim.release, §7).** A new event was needed after all: every claim now ends with an event of its own, so a hold ends at `claim.take`, `claim.expire` or the new `claim.release`, each at its before state's `expires_at` if the lease ran out first. A lapsed hold that is then closed, finished, handed off or moved out of progress by an update ends at the lapse, not at that change. `issue.close`, `issue.update` and `claim_released` still end holds in logs written before `claim.release`, so claims taken earlier still attribute.
- **Reads.** `show` returns `usage`: the resolved account and where it comes from, seconds held, tokens by model and whether any were split. `digest` returns the window's seconds held, tokens by model, the unattributed part and whether any record was split (`split`, a result field, so no protocol bump), filtered by `--by` and `--label`. Both list at most 20 models, summing the rest as `(other)`. MCP `show` and `digest` carry the account and a one-line summary; no MCP tool sets an account. The CLI sets it with `create`/`update --account`, not a separate command.
- **Limits.** `usage_records` (500 a call, `invalid`) and `usage_per_day` (50,000 rows a principal in 24 hours by server receipt time, `busy`). The daily cap is per principal, not per session, since a client can name any number of sessions. Each count is at most 10¹², a record's time at most an hour ahead of the server, and a span at most 7 days (`MaxUsageSpan`), so a read finds the spans reaching into a stretch within a bounded range (index `token_usage_span`). `show` reads an issue's records stretch by stretch of its holds, and each read attributes at most 100,000 records, marking the result partial only when a record that counts was left out.

**As built, 20 Oct 2026 (prices by prompt length, aries-itlyj3yw).** Claude Haiku 5.5 bills a request whose prompt is over 100,000 tokens at five times the rates, so a price may carry a tier: a threshold and a second set of rates (migration 3, `price.set`'s `tier`, protocol 10). Cost stays computed on read; each request record is priced by its own prompt (input, cache writes, cache reads), a record that sums a span is priced at the base rates, and a part of a shared record at its whole record's tier. Claude Code's usage hook already sends one record per response, so no capture change was needed. `inference_geo`'s 1.1x is not built.

**As built, 8 Oct 2026 (client capture, Claude Code).** `aries usage --hook` reads Claude Code's local transcripts; the other harnesses follow once their formats are checked against real files, and until then report time only. Where the build differs from, or fills in, the above:
- **Source.** Transcripts, not OpenTelemetry (which needs user-level settings that `setup` does not touch) and not hook payloads (which carry no counts). `aries setup claude-code` installs `aries usage --hook` on Stop, SubagentStop and SessionEnd beside the SessionStart hook, Stop and SubagentStop with `async: true` and a 30 s timeout, so a turn never waits on the server. SessionEnd runs synchronously with a 10 s timeout, and aries gives up at 9 s: it sends the session's last responses, and it is unverified that an async hook outlives Claude Code's exit, while a synchronous one is waited for. Claude Code 2.1.294 bounds that wait at max(1.5 s, min(the largest SessionEnd hook timeout, 60 s)). `--check` reports an async SessionEnd hook, from the first release, as needing `--write`. Each run reads the hook's `transcript_path`, the subagent transcripts in `<dir>/<session_id>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`, and SubagentStop's `agent_transcript_path` if it is elsewhere. Package `internal/capture`.
- **Records.** One per API response, granularity `request`: request id `message.id` (unique per response; `requestId` is missing from some lines, such as a `remote_projects` session's), `message.model`, the line's `timestamp`, and `input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `cache_creation_input_tokens`, `cache_creation.ephemeral_1h_input_tokens` and `cache_read_input_tokens`; a missing count is NULL. Thinking tokens are already in output and `iterations` already summed at the top level, so neither is added; service tier and speed are not recorded (stage 4). Each response is on consecutive lines, one per content block. Their counts repeat except `output_tokens`, which grows to the last line's final value (checked against a live transcript on 8 Oct 2026), so records are deduplicated by message id, keeping the largest output. Since the server keeps the first count it gets for a request, a response is sent only once finished: once an assistant line of another message follows it in its file, since the next request starts only after it ends. Tools run while a response streams, so tool results (user lines) and attachments can fall among its lines, and they do not finish it. A file's last response is held back, the offset stopping on the line boundary before its first line, until such a line arrives; at SubagentStop the stopped subagent's file (`agent_transcript_path`), and at SessionEnd every file, is read whole. Lines of another `sessionId` (history copied into a forked session) and `<synthetic>` messages are skipped. Lines are decoded into a struct of these fields only, and lines over 4 MiB are passed over.
- **Unknown formats.** A file whose new response lines are there but none can be read sends nothing, and the hook prints one note naming the Claude Code version; its offset stays, so an upgraded `aries` picks it up. Unreadable lines among readable ones are skipped and the offset moves past them. Counts are never guessed.
- **Refusals.** The server refuses a whole batch, as `invalid`, for one record it refuses, such as one whose time is more than an hour past its clock, which the client cannot check. The client halves such a batch until each refused record stands alone, drops it, and notes how many were dropped, so one record cannot hold back every later run. Any other failure, such as `unavailable` or `busy`, drops nothing and is retried by the next hook. A server with a smaller `usage_records` limit is met the same way, in smaller batches.
- **Files.** Only regular files are read: a FIFO or device named as a transcript would block the open past any deadline.
- **Offsets.** In `aries/usage-offsets.json` under the user cache directory (0600, directory 0700), keyed by absolute path: the offset past the last complete line, and the SHA-256 of the file's first 4 KiB as its identity, which works on every platform where an inode does not. A shorter file, or a different head, is read from 0. The offset moves only once the server accepted every record read from the file (batches of 500); concurrent hooks write a temp file and rename it, and the server's dedupe makes any resend harmless. Entries for files that no longer exist are pruned.
- **Validation.** The request id and model patterns moved from the store to `proto`, and the client drops a record the server would refuse, since one bad record refuses its whole batch.
- **Session.** The hook's `session_id`, which is the `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` `aries mcp` claims under, unless `ARIES_SESSION` is set, as for `prime --hook`.

**As built, 9 Oct 2026 (stage 4, prices and cost).** Prices, `aries cost`, the MCP `cost` tool and the cost in `show` and `digest`; `aries log`, subscription plans and amortized cost follow in later work. Where the build differs from, or fills in, the above:
- **Prices.** Migration 0014 adds `prices`, keyed by (model, `effective_at`), with input, output, cache-write (five minutes), `cache_write_1h` and cache-read rates. A rate is an integer of micro-dollars (10⁻⁶ USD) per million tokens, at most 10¹², so rate × tokens is an exact count of picodollars. The model compares byte for byte, as the harness reports it; there is no aliasing of model names. The effective time is a date (midnight UTC) or an RFC 3339 time, at most a year ahead. Setting a (model, time) that exists replaces its rates; setting it to what it is writes nothing. Each change appends a `price.set` event, target `prices` (a model name can look like an issue id), its states holding the model, time and rates, and a replace's the rates replaced. Only admins (the `admins:` setting, as for `close --force`) set prices, over the `price.set` op from `aries admin prices set`; anyone lists them (`prices`). No MCP tool sets one. The table is capped by `limits: prices` (1,000 rows, `invalid`). No price ships with Aries.
- **Pricing.** A record is priced by the newest price for its model whose effective time is no later than its time. A model with none is unpriced: its tokens are shown, and every figure they touch says so, rather than borrowing a later or another model's price. The five-minute part of a cache write is the write less its one-hour part, and all of it when the one-hour part is unknown, five minutes being the default lifetime. Tokens are summed per price row and multiplied once, with `math/big`, so no float or overflow enters a cost.
- **Attribution is reused.** A record divided among issues is priced part by part, as the tokens it gives each (the same largest-remainder parts `show` reports), so the issues' costs add up to the record's exactly. `show`'s cost covers the tokens it attributes to the issue, and `digest`'s the window's tokens, filtered as its tokens are.
- **Reports.** `cost` reads the records whose time is in [since, until), until defaulting to now, in one snapshot, grouped by `account` (an issue's resolved account, the server's default for none), `issue`, `epic` (the issue, or its nearest ancestor, of type epic), `person` (the reporting principal) or `model`.
