Account, issue and epic divide records as `show` does; person and model take them whole. Tokens no issue was held for are the group `(unattributed)`, issues under no epic `(no epic)`. A group is `split` when a record's parts fell in more than one group, so two issues of one account sharing a record do not mark it. Groups are by cost, then tokens, then key; past the limit (50, at most 500) the rest are summed into `(other)`, so the listed groups add up to the total. A report reads at most 100,000 records (`truncated` past that) over at most 366 days, and loads accounts and epics a parent generation at a time, in chunks.
- **Wire.** A cost travels as `cost_usd`, an exact decimal string of US dollars, since a JSON number rounds through a float in many readers and an int64 of picodollars overflows past about 9.2 million dollars; rates travel as integer micro-dollars. Only display rounds, to the cent, halves up; a cost above zero that rounds to nothing reads `<$0.01`, and one with no priced token reads `unpriced`, never $0.00. `show` and `digest` gain `cost_usd` and `unpriced` in their usage, result fields, sent only once the server has a price, so a server that prices nothing shows no cost rather than calling every token unpriced. A report names at most 20 unpriced models, with `unpriced_more` counting the rest. `price.set`, `prices` and `cost` join protocol 4, which had not shipped (v0.3.0 speaks 3), so with no bump; ProtoMin stays 2.
- **MCP.** The `cost` tool was added on the maintainer's decision (9 Oct 2026). It takes `by`, `since` and `until`, asks for 20 groups in one call and, when the result would pass 2,000 tokens, sums the last groups into `(other)` itself, exactly, until it fits, and returns a line per group. The tool set is 24 tools at about 2,674 estimated tokens; the budget rose from 2,600 to 2,700.

**As built, 9 Oct 2026 (stage 4, hours and plans).** `aries log`, subscription plans and amortized cost. Where the build differs from, or fills in, the above:
- **Hours.** Migration 0015 adds `hours`, one row per entry: id, issue, principal, day, seconds, note and when it was logged. `aries log DURATION ID [--on DATE] [--note TEXT]` logs the caller's own time, as `1.5h`, `90m` or `1h30m`, from a minute to 24 hours in whole seconds, for a day at most a year back and at most one past the server's today (UTC). The day is the logger's calendar day: the CLI sends its local date unless `--on` gives one, and a client that sends none gets the server's today. A window that reaches now counts the entries for tomorrow (UTC) too. A principal's entries on one day sum to at most 24 hours and number at most `limits: hours_per_day` (50, `invalid`); a note is one line of at most `limits: hours_note` bytes (500). The account is the issue's, resolved when read, as for tokens, and hours carry no money. Each log and undo is an event on the issue (`hours.log`, `hours.delete`) whose state is the entry, so `history` shows them and an undone entry stays on the record. A log and an undo each take an idempotency key: a retried undo returns the entry it removed rather than `not_found`, and a retried log whose entry was since undone returns it marked `undone`. `aries log --undo ENTRY` deletes one's own entry, and an admin may delete anyone's. Bare `aries log` lists entries, newest day first, by `--issue` and `--by`. No MCP tool logs hours.
- **Hours in reports.** `show` gives an issue's hours in total and by principal (20 named, then `(other)`). `digest` and `cost` give the hours of the days their window overlaps: a day counts whole when the window touches it, so a 24-hour digest at a morning standup counts yesterday, and windows from midnight to midnight count each day once. In `cost`, by account, issue and epic hours go with their issue, by person with who logged them, and by model they are the group `(human)`, so under every grouping the groups' hours sum to the total. The digest's `--by` and `--label` filter them by who logged them and by their issue.
- **Plans.** Migration 0015 also adds `plans`, keyed by (name, month), with the fee a seat a month in integer micro-dollars and the seats, and `plan_principals`, the principals whose usage each row pays for. Terms hold from their month until a later month of the same name; no fee or no seats ends the plan. Only admins set plans, over `plan.set` from `aries admin plans set`, and anyone lists them (`plans`). Replacing a row, setting it to what it is, and the `plan.set` event (target `plans`) work as for prices. A fee is at most 1,000,000 US dollars and a plan at most 100,000 seats; `limits: plans` (1,000 rows) and `plan_principals` (100 a row) bound the table.
- **Amortized cost.** The month's fee is split per plan, across its principals' work: for each calendar month (UTC) a report touches, each plan's monthly total, fee × seats, is split across the report's groups in proportion to the tokens, of all kinds (input, output, cache write and cache read), that its principals' records gave each group that month, a shared record's parts as attribution divides it. Tokens no issue was held for take their share into `(unattributed)`, so a whole month sums to the total. The month's tokens outside the window take their share out of the report, which prorates a month the window covers in part by its tokens. A month whose principals reported no tokens is prorated by time instead, into `(unattributed)`, since its fee was paid all the same. A fee accrues by time: the month that holds now is split for only the part of its total that has passed, rounded down to the picodollar, and the window ends at now for plans, so a report reaching into the future charges no month that has not happened. A principal on no plan adds nothing, and one on two plans carries both. The split is exact, in `math/big` by the largest remainder with ties to the group first by key, so the parts sum to the total to the picodollar. A month's totals are one SQL sum per month, outside the window's 100,000-record cap; a window past that cap assigns its groups less, and says `truncated`. Only `cost`, on the CLI and over MCP, shows amortized cost, beside the list price and never instead of it: `show` and `digest` would have to read whole months. `amortized_usd` is sent on every group once the server has a plan, so a server with none shows no amortized column.
- **Wire.** `hours.log`, `hours.delete`, `hours`, `plan.set` and `plans` join protocol 4 with no bump, which had still not shipped, as do the result fields `logged_seconds` and `logged` in show's usage, `logged_seconds` in digest's, and `logged_seconds` and `amortized_usd` in each cost group. ProtoMin stays 2.
- **MCP.** No tool was added; the `cost` tool's description now reads "Token cost, plan share and hours.", which brings the tool set to about 2,676 estimated tokens. The `show`, `digest` and `cost` lines carry `logged 1.5h` and `amortized $20.00`.

**As built, 19 Oct 2026 (cost per outcome, F3 of [productivity-metrics.md](productivity-metrics.md)).** `aries cost --group-by outcome` groups an issue's attributed usage by how the issue stood at the window's end: `landed` (its last close in or before the window gave the reason `landed`), `closed` (any other reason), `open` (never closed, or reopened since) and `(unattributed)`. Records are divided among issues as for `--group-by issue`, so the groups add up to the total to the picodollar; each group carries the issues it counted, its **rework** (the part of its tokens after the first close of the issue they went to; a reopened issue is one issue, not two) and, for `landed`, the cost per landed issue, by issue type, and a list of the landed issues (at most `-n`, dearest first) with held time over cycle time and the acceptance items and gates waived. Everything is computed on read from the event log, the usage records and the prices then in effect; nothing is stored and no instrument is added (the maintainer's answer of 19 Oct 2026: per-issue values belong in reports, not telemetry). Close reasons stay free text: only `landed` is told apart (`store.ReasonLanded`), and every other reason is `closed`, so the forced and other of F1's counter are not split here. **Protocol:** a new `by` value rather than new fields on the existing groups, because the groups are different ones (the issue's end state, not its id) and the extra fields are meaningful only for them; an older server refuses `outcome` as `invalid` naming the groupings it has. That is a protocol change after v0.5.0 (rule 10), and it joins protocol 9, v0.6.0's, with no further bump; `CostArgs` and `CostGroup` gain only optional fields. Unpriced models still make a group's cost `unpriced`, never $0. A held time counts every hold of the issue, and a landed issue's cost counts only tokens inside the window.

**As built, 15 Oct 2026 (the CLI's names, decision 88).** The surface review's question 4 renamed what `aries` takes before v1.0.0 froze it; the wire's ops and fields are unchanged. Removing is `remove` (`dep add|remove`, `label add|remove`), with `rm` a hidden alias; `aries log` is `aries hours`, with `log` an alias kept through 1.x; `admin prices` and `admin plans` are `admin price list|set` and `admin plan list|set`; `start --branch` is `start --new-branch`; `cost --by` is `cost --group-by`; `gate add --subject` is `gate add --issue`; and `gate add --by` and `dep -t` are gone, `--approver` and `--type` remaining. Except for the two aliases, the old spellings are refused as unknown flags or commands. The server's fix lines name the new spellings. `pin` and `unpin` stay at the top level.

## 13. Plan

| Stage | Delivers | Gate |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | One-day Dolt spike: 20–50 concurrent claimers with compare-and-swap; commits per request vs. batched | **Done:** Dolt OK with conditions (`write_id`, serialized claims, batched commits) |
| 1 | Schema, `ariesd` core, SSH transport, issues/deps/labels/comments, ready via CTE, events, CLI CRUD, bd JSONL import, version handshake | Real bd backlogs imported and round-tripped |
| 2 | MCP server (work and issue tools), `start`/`finish`, git awareness, next-step errors, `ariesd --dev`, token budget in CI, `digest` (MCP and CLI), prime, `aries upgrade` and `ariesd upgrade`, `setup` for Claude Code, Codex, Gemini, Cursor, VS Code | Agents use it daily on a real project |
| 3 | Claims with leases, epochs, reaper, agents registry, inbox, event push, handoff, idempotency, files to issues, acceptance checklist, similar closed issues (full-text), live board, time and token reporting, `account` and token capture (§12.1) | Multi-session soak test |
| 3a | Projects and access (§15): many projects on one server with a database and a writer each, principals, keys and memberships in a control database, server and project admins, `aries admin` | Two projects on one server; a principal refused in a project it is not a member of; the soak test run across both. **Met in the test stack** (§15.4, As built, status and the soak), against Aries's stand-in for sshd; what no real machine has run yet is listed there |
| 3b | Observability (§16): OpenTelemetry traces and metrics from ariesd, a loopback endpoint for metrics and health, a log schema and audit stream, Dolt's metrics, and a Collector harness | The Docker harness receives every signal from ariesd and Dolt; the soak's counters equal its event logs; a dead collector changes no request |
| 4 | Memory with scopes and tags, migrated from bd `kv.memory.*`; prices, `aries cost` and `aries log` (§12.1) | |
| 5 | Offline cache, outbox, conflict parking and resolution (§8; the design, in slices, is §18) | Partition tests. **Met in the test stack** (§18, As built, F5): the soak with agents cut from the server for minutes at a time holds every write they queued to their outboxes and the event logs. Not verified: any of it on Windows, a real network partition rather than cut dials, and the cost of comparing a field against an old rev on a long history |
| 6 | Locks, reservations, gates, molecules/formulas, swarm, cross-project (a design in slices, proposed and not decided, is §20) | |
| 7 | Scheduled digests (draft only), GitHub sync, compaction, duplicate check, vectors, other trackers | |

bd stays in use until stage 2 passes on a real project.

**v1.0.0** (decisions 81 and 83). The next release is v1.0.0, narrowed by decisions 90 and 91 and tagged v0.5.0 by decision 93, which holds v1.0.0 as long as possible. It holds the rename to Aries; the Postgres port, slices P0, P1, P2, P4 and P5 of decision 79 (`spike/postgres/RESULTS.md`, section 5); stage 6's G0 to G2 (§20); sign-in with OpenID Connect (Google Workspace, Microsoft Entra ID, Okta, Keycloak) and GitHub, with expiring keys and the identity of §17's I2, service principals that sign in with their platform's workload identity, and a Claude Desktop extension (decision 83); and a hardening pass: a review of every public name, flag, variable and setting, the protocol settled at one version (decision 78), and the open follow-ups. G3 to G5 come in 1.x as compatible additions. In P1, Postgres comes from the PGDG apt repository on servers and in CI, and on developers' Macs from a pinned, checksummed build of the official source tarball; the minimum supported version is Postgres 16, and the tests pin 18.

## 14. Decisions

Maintainer, 6 Oct 2026:

1. The four Replace rows in §4 are accepted.
2. Memory defaults to `project` scope; the agent decides when a memory is a personal preference (`user`) and asks when borderline.
3. Embeddings: open; recommendation is a local model on the server (see §10).
4. License: Apache-2.0. Superseded by decision 82 (AGPL-3.0-only).
5. Dolt stays the backend. `aries upgrade` and `ariesd upgrade` exist, and clients are warned when the server is out of date (§11).
6. All ten developer-experience features in §12 are in scope, and `digest` is an agent tool.
7. Track input and output tokens per issue, with cost estimates even on subscription plans (§12.1); accounts default to `internal`.

Maintainer, 7 Oct 2026, from the unattended-operation review ([bearings.md §3.14](bearings.md#314-unattended-operation)):

8. ariesd gains an autonomy window, narrower default rights, distinct agent identities, parking of a person's lapsed claim during a window, and git push fencing by claim epoch. They are staged after the security review, before any release that enables unattended work.
9. SSH stays the only transport; an HTTPS transport waits. Superseded by decision 86 (HTTPS primary, SSH kept).
10. WireGuard is a documented, optional layer in front of the server's SSH port; nothing in Aries requires it.

Maintainer, 9 Oct 2026, on projects and access (§15):

11. One server serves many projects. Each project has its own Dolt database and its own writer; no writer is shared between projects.
12. Access is managed with `aries admin` only. No admin tool over MCP and no web page for now.
13. Public keys move into the store, in this stage, and must work on AWS, DigitalOcean and Azure as well as Google Cloud.
14. Two admin tiers: server admins with total control, and project admins, a role a principal may hold on one or more projects.
15. A project admin may add new people.

Maintainer, 11 Oct 2026, after the measurements of §15.5:

16. A project's database is created by `aries admin project add`, from a block of database names the operator grants ariesd's account at setup. No command on the server is needed for each project.

Maintainer, 11 Oct 2026, on the control store as built (§15.1):

17. A project admin may add to their project only a principal that already belongs to a project or that they created. Any other principal in no project is placed by a server admin.
18. An archived project can be reactivated by a server admin.

Maintainer, 11 Oct 2026, on keys in the store and on access that ends (§15.2):

19. Nobody changes the keys of their own account. Adding and removing a key is refused when the actor is the key's principal, for a server admin too. A server admin's keys are changed by another server admin, or in `authorized_keys` on the server. The operator's `ariesd control init` is exempt, as it is from the role rules.
20. Disabled wins. A principal whose row is disabled is refused everywhere, even when the config file's `admins:` names it: it is admitted to nothing and has no admin rights in the control store or in any project until it is enabled. A config admin with no row is not disabled. Only a server admin disables or enables a server admin, and the last server admin who is not disabled cannot be disabled, so a server is never left with nobody able to enable anyone remotely.
21. Losing access releases claims at once. When a membership is removed, the principal's claims in that project end; when a principal is disabled, its claims in every project end; when a project is archived, every claim in it ends. Each ends with its own `claim.release` event naming the admin and the reason, and the issue is ready again.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, after keys from the store were run on a real sshd (§15.2):

22. Keys reach sshd through a file that ariesd writes. The daemon writes an `authorized_keys`-format file from the control store, one line for each key of an active principal, and sshd reads it with `AuthorizedKeysFile`, beside the hand-maintained `authorized_keys`, which stays the way in for the first admin and for break-glass: `AuthorizedKeysFile .ssh/authorized_keys .ssh/aries_keys`, in the `Match User aries` block. `ariesd keys`, the daemon's `keys` first frame and the `key_lookups` limit are removed, not kept as an option: one mechanism to test, document and secure. The file is a cache and the store decides: every line carries its key's fingerprint, and the daemon refuses, at the handshake, a key the store does not hold for that principal, and a disabled principal. A write of the file that fails after the start does not fail or undo the control write: it is logged, retried, shown in `aries admin status` and returned to the admin as a warning.
23. The supported platform for an Aries server is Ubuntu LTS; the server guide is written for and tested on 24.04. Ubuntu's mandatory access control is AppArmor, and an AppArmor profile for ariesd will ship for it. Servers on SELinux distributions (RHEL, Rocky Linux, AlmaLinux, Fedora) are not supported yet. SELinux support, meaning labels and a policy module if one is needed, will follow once there is a test harness that boots such a VM.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on what the soak left open (§15.4):

24. The daemon ends claims in archived projects when it starts. Decision 21 says an archive ends every claim at once, and a daemon that stopped between an archive's control write and its release left them until the project was activated, since nobody can connect to an archived project and so nothing opened its store. Before it listens, a server of many projects ends every claim in every archived project, each with its own `claim.release`, for `project archived`, recorded as the server's as the sweep's are, and the holder's `claim.lost`. It does not migrate an archived project's database, and a failure for one project is logged and stops nothing.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on protocol versions while nothing is in production (§11):

25. One protocol version for everything unreleased, and it is the only one accepted. Protocol 4 is what the last release, v0.4.0, speaks; 5 and 6 were both added since and neither shipped, so 6 is folded into 5 and every op, argument and result that joined at 6 belongs to 5. The minimum is raised to 5 as well: `Proto`, `ProtoMin` and `ProtoMax` are all 5, and every released client is refused at the handshake and told to upgrade. The reason is that nobody is using Aries in production yet and it is under active development, so some breakage between versions is expected at this point. This is a reset made once, before anyone depends on the range, and not a change to principle 9 or to rule 10 of `AGENTS.md`: from the next bump on, `ProtoMin` again stays one version back, so that a new server serves the clients already installed.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on the order of the two key files (§15.2):

26. sshd reads the generated keys file first: `AuthorizedKeysFile .ssh/aries_keys .ssh/authorized_keys`, which replaces the order written in decision 22. sshd takes the first line that matches a key, so with the hand-maintained file first a key in both, which is every key `control init` imports, came in by the line with no fingerprint: `aries admin key remove` neither closed its connections nor showed. With the generated file first it comes in by the line that names it, its connections are closed by the removal, and the daemon's check at the handshake applies to it. The hand-maintained file is still read, so the removed key gets in again through it until its line is deleted, and the warning says exactly that. The price is that a stale line of the generated file is matched before the same key's hand-maintained line and refused by the daemon; for a removed key that is the intended result.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, while protocol 5 is unreleased (an observability design, §16, is written separately and builds on this):

27. Requests carry W3C trace context from the client: the `traceparent` value and, optionally, `tracestate`, on each request frame. Adding the field after protocol 5 ships would cost a protocol bump, and adding it now is free. Only the carrying is built, not tracing: the server validates the context, carries it with the request and names its ids in its debug log, and creates and exports no spans. A trace context that is not valid is ignored and never fails a request.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on observability (§16). Decision 27, that requests carry W3C trace context in protocol 5, is recorded with the change that adds the field:

28. ariesd is instrumented with the OpenTelemetry Go SDK, for traces and metrics, exported with OTLP over HTTP. The maintainer chose the SDK over open formats written by hand, accepting what it costs (§16.2), for a standard, complete implementation. The SDK is ariesd's alone: `aries` passes trace context on and links none of it.
29. ariesd may open one HTTP listener, on a loopback address only, off by default, for metrics and health. This narrows the earlier position that ariesd opens no port: it still opens none that another machine can reach.
30. The first version includes metrics broken down by project, an audit stream of access changes in the logs, Dolt's own figures in `aries admin status`, and an OpenTelemetry Collector configuration, alert rules and a dashboard in the repository, exercised by the Docker harness.
31. Metrics are both pushed (OTLP) and offered for pull (Prometheus), so that whoever deploys Aries chooses.
32. A setting decides how a person appears in telemetry: by name, by a pseudonym, or not at all (§16.4). How people are identified at all, beyond one SSH key at a time, is under investigation (§15.6).
33. A dependency for the Go runtime's metrics is acceptable. The cost of the standard library's own was measured first (§16.4).
34. A token for a hosted backend is allowed where one is needed, and the guide gives sending to a collector on the machine as the way to avoid holding one.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on two findings of the [security review of stage 3a](security-review-stage-3a.md) (F-1 and F-3; §15.4, As built, the review's fixes):

35. A configured key is the only key offered; with none configured, `aries` does as `ssh` does. When `aries` is told which key to use, by `ARIES_KEY_FILE` in the environment (the key file's path) or by `key:` in `.aries.yaml`, it offers the server that key and no other, as OpenSSH's `IdentitiesOnly` does. ssh-agent may sign for that one key, found by its public half, when the file is passphrase-protected or only its `.pub` is there; no other key the agent holds is offered. So a process given a narrow principal's key acts as that principal, whoever's home directory and agent socket are in its environment. With no key configured, `aries` offers the default identity files of `~/.ssh` that exist, in `ssh`'s order, and then the keys ssh-agent holds, each key once, capped below sshd's `MaxAuthTries` so that the server can still answer; the first key the server knows decides the principal. The order is one list: `ARIES_KEY_FILE`, `key:`, the default identities, ssh-agent. `aries whoami` and `prime` say which principal the server took a connection for, which key signed, and where the key came from. The variable holds a path and is never trusted to: a value that is plainly a key is refused unrepeated, and `aries` reads no variable named `ARIES_KEY`, which earlier CI instructions filled with a private key. The maintainer chose the name `ARIES_KEY_FILE` and asked for the default-identity rule on 12 Oct 2026.
36. Joining a project shows a person's keys and needs acceptance. `member add` of a principal who has any key does not make the membership on the first request: the server answers with every key the principal has, who added each and when, and whether that was an admin of the project being joined, and makes the membership only when the request is repeated with an explicit acceptance. This holds for every caller, a server admin included, so that the habit is the same for all; a principal with no key is added at once, and a role change needs none. The admin is shown the keys whether or not they have reach over the principal, and an admin of a project may list the keys of its members for the same reason: every one of those keys signs in to their project. Who may add or remove a key is unchanged (the reach rule, and decision 19).

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on the server's clock. Asked whether a person adjusting the time on their own computer is a worry for certificate revocation (§17), the answer was that a client's clock decides nothing on the server and that the exposure is the server's own; they replied: "record the clock section in the identity design now and let's start reinforcing against clock manipulation." The section is §17.14; what was built is in §7 (As built, the server's clock). Numbers 35 and 36 are taken by decisions on a branch not yet merged:

37. The server's clock is relied on and not trusted to be right.
    - **Time in a log never goes backward.** A store's time is the later of the clock and its high-water mark, which starts at the latest event of its database; the stores of one server share the highest, so their logs keep one time. While the clock is behind, that time runs on at half speed until the clock has caught up.
    - **A step is detected and reported.** Elapsed wall-clock time is compared with elapsed monotonic time on every reading; a difference past `clock_step_tolerance` (5 seconds), in one reading or added up over several, is a step, logged and counted in `aries admin status`.
    - **A clock jump does not by itself end every claim.** A jump forward under a running daemon is added to every live lease, with one `clock.step` event in each log. Across a restart a lease is measured by the wall clock, as before.
    - **A daemon that finds its clock behind its own history stops or warns, as the server admin chooses.** The maintainer: "We should give the server admin the option to stop or warn if the ariesd server clock is behind its own history." The setting is `clock_behind: stop | warn`, and on the default, the maintainer: "make stop the default for clock_behind". (Coordinator's note, not the maintainer's words: an unexpected rollback of the clock is worth a person's look before the server carries on.) `stop` applies at start only. A clock that goes back under a running daemon is held and reported, never turned into an exit, so that spoofed network time is not a way to take a server down; the report says that the server will not start again until the clock is corrected. `ariesd serve --accept-clock-behind TIME` starts once for a history no later than TIME, for a database written by a clock that ran ahead.
    - **The tolerances are settings with defaults** (approved by the maintainer the same day): `clock_step_tolerance` 5s, `clock_behind_tolerance` 2m and `clock_client_tolerance` 5m, each refused at start when zero, negative or past its ceiling.
    - **A clock that ran ahead and was corrected is what later shows as "behind its history",** so the forward and the backward case are linked in practice, though only the second is what `clock_behind` decides.

Maintainer, 12 Oct 2026, on what fixing F-2 of that review found. It amends decision 19, whose text above is left as the history it is:

38. Nobody adds a key to their own account; anyone may remove one of their own. Removing only ever reduces access, so an agent holding a person's key cannot use removal to persist or to gain anything; and without it the victim of a planted key cannot clean their own account, which for the last server admin meant that only the operator could. The rule is the control store's, in the write's transaction, for a member, a project admin and a server admin alike; removal of someone else's key follows the reach rule as before. The daemon refuses removing the key the request's own connection signed in with, so that nobody locks themselves out by accident.

Maintainer, 13 Oct 2026, on the seven questions of the stage 5 design (§18.11). The answers are quoted; the rest of each entry is the proposal it answers:

39. No claims are made offline in the first cut. "A session finishes what it holds and files new work, but starts nothing new without the server."
40. The cache holds what a session read, in plain files, with no embedded store in `aries`. "Only what a session reads."
41. The idempotency window is 7 days by default and a server setting. "7 days is a good default and a server setting."
42. An agent may propose how to resolve a conflict, and a person approves. "Have the agent recommend a solution to MCP conflicts", and, asked whether the agent then applies it: "I want the agent to be able to propose a solution but have the person approve." (Coordinator's note, not the maintainer's words: the proposal is stored with the conflict and applied only by a person's `aries resolve ID --accept`; agents get no `resolve` tool. Approval is kept from agents the way admin commands are, by being absent from MCP (rule 12), which does not stop an agent that runs `aries` in a shell with a person's key; whether the server should tell the two apart is identity's question, §17. How an agent reads the two sides, and the shape of the proposing tool, are F4's to settle.)
43. Long text in conflict parks whole, with no automatic merge of hunks. "Makes sense."
44. A queued write exits 0, with `--no-queue` for scripts. "Ok."
45. There is no hybrid logical clock. "Ok."
46. Where vectors live is decided after V0 measures both: files beside the database, or a table in Dolt (§19.4). Asked what the difference would be, and told that search costs the same either way and that the two differ in loading, in writes through a project's single writer, in Dolt's history and memory, and in what a restore brings back: "Decide after measuring."
47. V0, the measurement of whether any model beats word matching on real data, runs now; what it finds decides whether the rest of §19 is built, and the build waits (§19.7). "Measure now, build later."
48. A server may use an embedding endpoint on another machine, behind an explicit setting, with a warning at start naming what is sent. "Allowed, opt-in."
49. Agents get no search tool over MCP at first. "Not at first."
50. No embedding model is named as the default until V0. "None until V0."
51. Embedding is set for the server, and a project can opt out. "Per server, opt-out."
52. Aries signs people in itself, as a way in that a server turns on, beside keys and §17's certificates and never required ([access-research.md](access-research.md) §11). "Yes, opt-in per server."
53. A sign-in records an expiring key in the file sshd already reads; Aries signs no certificates and holds no signing key. "Expiring keys."
54. The path a machine not yet known takes to hand over its sign-in is chosen by a spike of both candidates under AppArmor, the published enrollment key first, the key command for enrollment second. "Spike both, published key first."
55. The device-code flow is a server setting, off by default, with tokens accepted once and only fresh. "Server option, off."
56. Generic OpenID Connect comes first, then workload tokens for CI, then GitHub's own adapter. "Generic OIDC first."
57. New people are invited; a principal is not made at first sign-in from an allowed domain. "Invitation only."
58. A sign-in lasts a day by default, a server setting with a ceiling, and does not renew silently. "A day, no silent renewal."
59. When a signed-in key expires, the server closes the connections made with it, decided together with §17.12's decision 8 for certificates. "Yes, close them."
60. An external identity is a table of provider, subject and invited address, decided before §17's slice I2 is built. "Yes, a table."
61. A person may approve a new machine of their own only from a session that has just signed in at the provider, never by a key alone; the details follow the spike. "Only after a fresh sign-in."
62. The client's pinned host key becomes a list now, independent of sign-in. "Yes, now."
63. `opkssh` is tried privately in the spike and not yet documented as a way in. "Not yet, trial it."
64. A server installer comes before sign-in; `ariesd doctor` after it. "Installer before."
65. Tunnels other than IAP are added when someone asks, as IAP was; Tailscale and WireGuard are documented now as network layers. "On request; document now."
66. Gates are issues of a new type, with a side table (§20.3). "Gates are issues."
67. CI reaches the server by running `aries` as a principal of its own; ariesd neither polls a CI host nor receives webhooks (§20.4). "CI runs aries."
68. A human gate is passed by the principals it names, or by the project's admins when it names none, only from the session `cli`, and by its creator only when named. "Named people, else admins."
69. There are no timer, issue or gh gate kinds: a timer is `defer_until`, an issue gate a `blocks` edge, gh the `ci` kind. "Yes, no extra kinds."
70. Locks are per project, first in first out with waiter leases, over the CLI and the protocol only; an agent does not take one itself. "Per project, CLI only."
71. Formulas are files in the repository that `aries` reads; the server gets `graph.create` with run keys and the `graph` read. "Files in the repository."
72. Wisps are dropped, with squash and burn. "Drop them."
73. Swarm is computed by the client over `graph`. "In the client."
74. Cross-project dependencies include edges to another project's issues, not only capabilities. Changed from the proposal, which deferred issue edges (§20.7). "Edges to issues too."
75. Reservations wait for evidence of rework at merge, and are built then as §20.8 has them. "Wait for evidence."
76. The MCP tool-schema budget rises from about 2,700 to about 3,000 estimated tokens, for: a gate tool for agents (add a gate and read gates; passing stays a person's, decision 68), `defer_until` and `due` on the create and update tools, linking an issue to an issue or capability in another project, and, once reservations are built, `start`'s reserve. Changed from the proposal, which kept the budget. Asked which tools the room pays for: "A gate tool for agents, Dates on the MCP tools, A cross-project dependency tool, Reservations for agents"; and the size: "3,000 tokens."
77. Each slice that adds ops raises the protocol by one, keeping `ProtoMin` one back. "One bump per slice."
78. Protocol changes that no release has carried join the current unreleased version rather than raising it; at the next release the protocol is settled to one version above any released one (v0.4.0 spoke at most 4, so it does not restart at 1), and the step-down code for versions no release spoke is removed. The protocol number stays out of routine output: it shows in `--json`, the status report's JSON and a refusal at the handshake for a version mismatch. Supersedes decision 77 for unreleased work. Asked whether to reset the numbering: "fold G0 into version 8. Let's keep the protocol out of the user's face."
79. The backend moves from Dolt to Postgres, and only to Postgres: one backend, a store interface in front of it for the server's sake, and nothing of Dolt kept beside it. On the Postgres spike (`spike/postgres/RESULTS.md`): the store ports with about 130 lines changed; Postgres needs about a tenth of Dolt's memory per project and gives it back; it answers the recursive reads 2.7 to 3.9 times faster at 10,000 issues; its replication carries roles and grants, and a standby with failover can be bought from any cloud or run with Patroni. Projects become a schema each in one database per server, the control database a schema beside them. The port is slices P0 to P5 less P3 (about 6 to 8 weeks), after stage 6's G2 and before G3, so that no later stage writes migrations for Dolt. Asked whether to switch: "Ok, let's make a switch to PostGres." The next release ships after the port, so it is the first on Postgres, and the protocol is settled then (decision 78). Asked when to release: "Next release ships after the port." No server runs on Dolt, so none is moved: there is no `ariesd migrate-from-dolt` (the spike's P3 is dropped), and the port removes Dolt rather than leaving it behind. Both migration sets start again at 0001 in Postgres's dialect, each squashed to the schema as it then stands, and rule 9's append-only rule applies from there; the Dolt sets are deleted. Removed with them: rule 6's `write_id` (row locks make `rev = ?` an exact compare-and-swap), the commit-a-second loop and the tag before upgrade, `internal/dolttest`, `scripts/install-dolt.sh`, the Dolt pin and its checksums, `internal/doltmetrics` and Dolt's figures in the status report, and Dolt in the installer, the AppArmor profile, the Docker test server, CI and the guides. The measurements of `spike/dolt` and `spike/multidb` are summarized in `spike/postgres/RESULTS.md` and the design, and their code is removed; git history keeps it. Asked about Dolt's remains: "We do not have any active Dolt servers so let's avoid leaving too many Dolt artifacts and migrations."
80. The names (15 Oct 2026). The project is Aries: the module `github.com/ariesworx/aries`, the client `aries` (with `aries mcp` for agents) and the daemon `ariesd`, with no short alias, and every file, variable, metric, path and account named to match, with nothing that reads another name. Bearings stays the orchestrator's name. Sextant is reserved for future observability (metrics, monitoring, tracing, cost and usage reporting); it is a name only, and nothing moves under it now.
81. The next release is v1.0.0 (15 Oct 2026; widened by decision 83 to sign-in and service principals, and narrowed again by decision 90). It holds the rename (decision 80); the Postgres port, slices P0, P1, P2, P4 and P5 (decision 79); stage 6's G0 to G2; and a hardening pass: a review of every public name, flag, variable and setting, the protocol settled at one version (decision 78), and the open follow-ups. G3 to G5 come in 1.x as compatible additions. In P1, Postgres's binaries come from the PGDG apt repository for servers and CI, and from a pinned, checksummed build of the official source tarball for developers' Macs; the minimum supported version is Postgres 16, and the tests pin 18.
82. The license is the GNU Affero General Public License, version 3 only (AGPL-3.0-only), from 15 Oct 2026, in place of Apache-2.0 (decision 4), copyright Ariesworx LLC. Releases up to v0.4.0 stay under Apache-2.0, as published. Contributions are accepted under a contributor license agreement that lets the maintainer offer the code under other terms too, so that a commercial license can be sold beside the AGPL; until that agreement and the means to sign it are in place, outside contributions are not merged. The dependencies are under Apache-2.0, BSD and MIT licenses, and one, the MySQL driver, under MPL-2.0, which permits combining with the AGPL (it is not marked incompatible with secondary licenses) and leaves with Dolt in the Postgres port. The AGPL is a known obstacle in some enterprises' procurement, which the commercial license is there to answer. Asked: "AGPL-3.0-only"; the holder: "Let's make the copyright Ariesworx LLC."; and whether to keep the option of a commercial license: "Yes, add a contributor license agreement."
83. Sign-in and service principals come in v1.0.0 (15 Oct 2026), widening decision 81's scope; GitHub's adapter, the device grant and the Desktop extension move to 1.x by decision 90. People: §17's identity (slice I2: a handle, a display name, and an external identity of provider, issuer and subject with the invited address, decision 60), expiring keys (decision 53), and `aries login` with OpenID Connect for Google Workspace, Microsoft Entra ID, Okta and Keycloak, and GitHub through its own adapter (access-research.md, §4.3 and §5), all at once rather than in decision 56's order; a person signs in through the browser where one is at hand, or by opening a URL `aries login` prints, in any browser, where none is (a remote or headless machine). Service principals: principals that are not people (a CI job, an unattended agent, a cloud workload) sign in with their platform's workload identity, such as GitHub Actions' OIDC token, a Google Cloud service account or an Azure service principal or managed identity, instead of holding a stored key (decision 56's workload tokens; decision 8's agent identities; access-research.md §7). And a Claude Desktop extension (an MCP bundle) that carries `aries` for macOS and Windows and signs its user in the same way, beside `aries setup claude-desktop`. They follow the Postgres port's store slices (P1, P2), so their tables are written once, in the new migration sets; the provider spikes of access-research.md §1 come first, on real tenants of each provider. Asked whether sign-in is in 1.0: "Yes, in v1.0.0"; which providers: "Google Workspace, Microsoft Entra ID (M365), GitHub, Okta or Keycloak"; "We will have to allow service principal access as well."; and: "We want to include the Claude Desktop plug-in as well and ensure developers can leverage SSO or URL to sign in."
84. The contributor license agreement, `CLA.md`, is in effect from 15 Oct 2026, version 1.0, adopted without counsel's review. It is governed by the laws of the State of Wisconsin, United States, Ariesworx LLC's state of organization, with no venue clause. Contributors sign it with CLA Assistant Lite (the `cla` workflow; replaced by a check of the repository's own by decision 94), which records each signature on the `cla-signatures` branch; the maintainer and bots are on its allowlist. The draft's notes for counsel are removed and its choices accepted as written: a license rather than an assignment, grants that run to those who receive Aries from Ariesworx, the patent termination, the representation about generated content, and one document for individuals and entities. The notes stay in git history (4212240), and a reviewed agreement would come as version 1.1. Asked the governing law: "Wisconsin, USA"; how contributors sign: "CLA Assistant Lite"; and whether to adopt before counsel's review: "Adopt now".
85. The design of sign-in, service principals and the Claude Desktop extension ([identity.md](identity.md)) is accepted with its twelve recommendations (its §15), from 15 Oct 2026: the published enrollment key as the way in; the URL path (code with PKCE, the address pasted back) on by default and the device grant off (decision 55 stands); the server exchanges the code; `prompt=login` on every sign-in with `auth_time` checked; a sign-in lasts 24 hours by default and 7 days at most; new-machine approval built only if a tenant blocks both the URL path and the device grant; no Kubernetes in v1.0.0; AppArmor allows the outbound rules always; one project per Desktop extension; the bundle's signing deferred; service principals have a member's rights; the display name on `principals`. All of it stays in v1.0.0, slices N0 to N8 after P1 and P2, with N1 to N7 beside P4 and P5 where their files do not overlap. Asked: "Accept all twelve", and "Keep it all in v1.0.0".
86. HTTPS is the primary transport from v1.0.0, with tokens from a sign-in at the company's identity provider (SSO), and SSH a second transport, off by default, that an operator turns on at installation or in the settings (15 Oct 2026). It supersedes decision 9 and answers [enterprise-review.md](enterprise-review.md)'s E14. Air-gapped sites are a first-class case: SSO through an identity provider inside the network, with its keys from an internal URL or a file and nothing reached outside unless configured; and SSH for teams that sign in with keys alone. An Entra ID provider takes its cloud's authority from configuration, with no default; Microsoft's GCC High, DoD, Secret and Top Secret clouds are configurable in 1.0 and not tested. The design is [transport.md](transport.md), proposed, with the evidence of `spike/https`; its recommendations (its §16) wait on the maintainer. Asked what the transport should be: "HTTPS primary, SSH kept"; the question that led to it: "If we are using SSO, shouldn't we use HTTPS as our primary means of transport?"; on SSH: "We need to keep SSH for teams working in air-gapped environments", and "SSH should be disabled by default. It must be explicitly enabled during setup or installation."; on priority: "I would be more concerned with SSO in an air-gapped environment."; on Microsoft's clouds: "We will skip GCC High and classified testing for now."
87. The transport design ([transport.md](transport.md)) is accepted with its eighteen recommendations (its §16), from 15 Oct 2026, and all of it stays in v1.0.0 (until decision 90 moved T3, N6 and N7 to 1.x): transport and identity together are about 16.5 weeks after P1 and P2, the remote MCP endpoint and the GitHub adapter included. Asked: "Accept all 18", and whether to move anything to 1.x: "Keep all in 1.0".
88. The review of the public surface before v1.0.0 ([surface-review.md](surface-review.md)) is accepted with its fifteen recommendations, from 15 Oct 2026. (1) A new project's default issue id prefix is `ar`, not `sf`. (2) `aries` warns once of an unknown key in `.aries.yaml` and goes on, while `ariesd.yaml` stays strict. (3) `limits:` takes one naming rule: `THING_per_SCOPE` for counts, `_bytes` for sizes, `_per_minute` or `_rate` (a second) for rates, and `conflict_age`, `due_soon` and `telemetry_requests` move out of `limits:`. (4) The CLI spells removal `remove`, with `rm` a hidden alias; says `admin price` and `admin plan`; names hours `aries hours`, with `log` an alias; keeps `pin` and `unpin` at the top level; and has `start --new-branch`, `cost --group-by` and `gate add --issue`, without `gate add --by` or `dep -t`. (5) The protocol settles at 8, spoken alone, its step-down code deleted, and `dep.rm` takes its idempotency key as `dep.add` does. (6) The protocol says `id` for the issue an op is about and `issue` where the record is something else, renaming `parent_id`, `Claim.id`, `Overlap.id`, `HoursLogArgs.id`, the `id` of three admin results, `LockReleaseResult.released` and `due_at`, and the inbox kinds `assigned`, `mention`, `handoff` and `conflict` become `issue.assigned`, `issue.mentioned`, `issue.handoff` and `conflict.parked`. (7) `install.sh` loses `ARIES_PUBKEY_PEM`, and its tests inject their key another way. (8) `create --id` is hidden, and `Issue`'s import-only fields leave the wire. (9) `lock run` keeps passing its command's exit code through, and 130 on an interrupt, and the guide says so. (10) `aries upgrade --to` comes in 1.x, as a compatible addition. (11) The `latest` setting is removed, and the welcome frame's `latest` with it. (12) The MCP `cost` tool is removed for 1.0. (13) `ariesd`'s log event names, metric names and their attribute values are part of 1.0's promise: a name is kept and fields may be added. (14) The branch prefixes keep their default, documented, without the unreachable `docs` entry, and a per-project setting may come in 1.x. (15) `aries --json` prints an error as `{"error":{"code","message","fix","req"}}`, and a JSON document only gains fields within 1.x. The changes that touch neither the store, the protocol nor the server's dispatch are made now; the rest (the prefix, the protocol's settling and names, the welcome frame's `latest`, the import-only fields, `create --id`, the `limits:` names and the naming promise that follows them) wait until the Postgres port's slice P1 has landed, so that they are made once, against the new store. Asked: "Accept all 15"; and when to make the changes: "Now where safe, rest after P1".
89. The proof by SSH key over HTTPS, which slice T1 built as the way an HTTPS connection authenticates before sign-in exists (transport.md, As built for T1), stays after T2, beside the tokens a sign-in gives, from 16 Oct 2026: a person or a service principal with a key the server holds may sign a connection's challenge instead of signing in, for automation and for air-gapped teams with keys and no identity provider. It is held to the same rules as a token: the challenge names the server's configured URL, never `Host`; a key's principal is the one the control store (or the hand-kept `authorized_keys`) gives it; and a key that `expires_at` has passed proves nothing. Asked: "keep the SSH-key proof after T2".
90. v1.0.0 is cut back so that Aries can be used to build Aries sooner, from 16 Oct 2026, narrowing decisions 83 and 87. It holds what has merged (the rename, the Postgres port's P0 to P4, G0 to G2, the surface review, T1's HTTPS, S1's expiring keys, N1's identities and invitations, T2's sign-in at any OpenID Connect provider with Aries's own tokens bound by DPoP), and adds only N5 (service principals and workload token exchange), P5 (the soaks on Linux, the installer on amd64, a machine with the AppArmor profiles enforced, ACME against a real CA), N8 (the guides and identity's security review, identity.md §12.4) and N0 against one real provider. T3 (the remote MCP endpoint, bearer tokens, refresh, client registration), N6 (GitHub's adapter and the device grant), N7 (the Claude Desktop extension) and N0's other providers come in 1.x, each a compatible addition; once v1.0.0 ships, each protocol change among them takes rule 10's bump. Bearings, the supply chain slices after SC1, and G3 to G5 wait until v1.0.0 ships. The work first is what makes Aries usable on its own development (dogfooding), before the further enterprise and security features. Asked whether 1.0 had drifted, and offered this cut: "Yes, cut 1.0 back as you recommend and lets focus on rounding out aries and ariesd so we can start eating our own dogfood".
91. v1.0.0 authenticates by keys alone, from 16 Oct 2026, narrowing decision 90 (and so decisions 83 and 87): over HTTPS by the key proof (decision 89) or over SSH, a person or a service with a key the server knows. Sign-in at an identity provider (T2) and service principals by workload identity (N5) stay on `main`, built and tested against the in-process stand-in and Keycloak, but off unless an operator configures `login: providers` or `workload: providers`, and they are not part of what v1.0.0 promises: neither has had a security review, and neither has run against a real provider. N0 (sign-in against a real provider), the security review of sign-in, tokens, DPoP and the workload exchange (identity.md §12.4), and the guides for each provider move to 1.x, tracked in Aries as one epic (aries-sqjvkqdz), each a compatible addition; the protocol change rule 10 requires applies to them once v1.0.0 ships. The rest of decision 90 stands: P5 (the soaks on Linux, the installer on amd64, a machine with the AppArmor profiles enforced, ACME against a real CA), the hardening pass and the security review of the HTTPS listener and the key proof are in v1.0.0. Asked, when the maintainer wanted to "keep aries SSH for now to round out aries and ariesd", which to do about sign-in: "Keys only; defer SSO work".
92. Business metrics that count are ariesd's, from 16 Oct 2026, narrowing Sextant's recommendation 1 ([sextant.md](sextant.md) §6) to cost: a token count is a fact the server holds the moment it stores a usage record, so ariesd counts it, as `aries.usage.tokens` by project, kind and model (§16.4, As built), and the dogfood stack's Grafana shows it before Sextant exists. Dollars and windows stay with `aries cost` and Sextant: a cost is computed on read from prices that change, so it is no counter, and recommendation 2's gauges over a window are unchanged. A model keeps its name as a label only if the server's price table has a price for it, and any other model is counted as `(other)`, since a model name is text a client chose (rule 2). Asked: "make token usage by model visible over time in ariesd's own monitoring".
93. v1.0.0, the production release, is held as long as possible, and until then releases are plain minor versions, from 16 Oct 2026, narrowing decisions 78, 81, 88, 90 and 91. The release those decisions call v1.0.0 is tagged v0.5.0, with what they put in it and nothing more; then v0.6.0 and on, which `aries upgrade` installs as it does any release, not `v1.0.0-beta.N` or `-rc.N`. Two features are pulled forward into those minor releases: the project workflow setting (aries-3yxddgjk) and organization memory (aries-iakie56e), under the held v1.0.0 epic (aries-foz66bi6). Everything else decisions 90 and 91 put in 1.x waits for it, behind v1.0.0 (aries-vojgudc4), and so do Bearings and Sextant. The protocol: decision 78's joining of an unreleased version without a bump ends with v0.5.0, which is settled at 8, spoken alone, as decision 88 has it; from v0.5.0 on every protocol change, a new op or a new argument field, takes rule 10's bump (`Proto` and `ProtoMax` up, `ProtoMin` kept at the version before, the server working when an older client omits the new field), since a released client must keep working against a newer server. New result fields and frame types still need none. Where decisions 90 and 91 say a change takes rule 10's bump "once v1.0.0 ships", read v0.5.0. The release notes and the upgrade guide are v0.5.0's (`docs/releases/v0.5.0.md`, `docs/upgrade-to-0.5.md`). Decision 88's promise that the names ariesd reports are kept (docs/server.md, Names that are kept; `TestNamesKept`) starts at v0.5.0 too, not v1.0.0, as the maintainer decided on 16 Oct 2026 (aries-77qnkejf): "Let's freeze them from v0.5.0". The maintainer: "Let's hold off on issuing a production v1.0.0 as long as possible and continue to use the pre-release versioning as we add these critical features." Asked how to tag them, they chose "v0.5.0, v0.6.0, …" over v1.0.0-beta.N or -rc.N; and on the protocol: "Let's apply the usual protocol bump".
94. Contributors sign the CLA through a check of this repository's own, `internal/tools/cla`, which `cla.yml` runs from the base branch, from 16 Oct 2026, narrowing decision 84, whose CLA Assistant Lite it replaces. That action (`contributor-assistant/github-action` v2.6.1) had had no release since September 2024, declared Node.js 20, which GitHub deprecated and runs on Node.js 24 in its place, and ran with write access on `pull_request_target`: unmaintained code of a third party with write access, which docs/design/supply-chain.md is about. The check keeps CLA.md's flow (section 13's sentence, and `recheck`), the allowlist, and the signatures file on the `cla-signatures` branch in the shape the action wrote, so no one signs twice; it adds that every commit's author must have signed, not only the pull request's, and that a commit whose author email belongs to no GitHub account fails until rewritten. It uses the standard library and the workflow's token alone, and its `deps.allow` line is gone. Offered keeping the action, forking it, this check, or the hosted CLA Assistant app, and asked whether we would maintain it: "Let's do a small check in the repo".
95. The project workflow setting of §21 is accepted with its fourteen recommendations (§21.12), from 16 Oct 2026, and pulled forward into the releases before v1.0.0 (decision 93). A project's workflow is a document in the project's `settings` table, kept as a file in the repository and applied by `aries admin workflow set`, never over MCP, by project and server admins (a `policy` role later, with enterprise review E6). Its default acceptance items are appended to an issue's text at create, and on a change of type; an item leaves only by waiver; a create queued offline gets the defaults in force when it is sent; nothing is retroactive. Close means landed: `finish` and `close` are refused while a required check has not passed, the passing report closes the issue, and a later failure reopens it; an item bound to a check is ticked by its pass, and an agent may still tick it. For a server CI cannot reach, as this repository's dogfood server, `aries land` reports `landed` from a person's checkout of the default branch. Review before close is per type and off by default; approval before start is per type, for epics. The close-out checklist is shown, not tracked. A server's default is a floor projects add to and do not loosen, built with organization memory (aries-iakie56e) and sharing its scope; Bearings P0 builds on `workflow.set`. Read-only work gets a type of its own (`chore`). W0's two ops are one protocol bump (`ProtoMin` kept), under decision 93; W1 to W4 add behavior and result fields only. The slices are W0 to W5 of §21.11. Asked to accept: "Accept all 14 as recommended".
96. The client-side dispatcher ([dispatcher.md](dispatcher.md)) is accepted with the answers to its §14, from 19 Oct 2026: an internal, experimental loop, `aries dispatch run|status|stop`, CLI only, on macOS and Linux, removed when Bearings' B0 takes it over. What it calls is built as permanent parts of Aries that Bearings reuses: `aries watch --events`; the locks `dispatch/runs`, `dispatch/pause` and `pgtest/HOST`, the last in the first slice; claim before launch; a credential store (the keychain on macOS, the Secret Service on Linux or else a 0600 file in the data directory, Credential Manager on Windows when Bearings needs it), holding an API key; and notices on a standing issue that mentions the maintainer, until a notification design (aries-teizxh5h) reaches people away from every machine. It skips issues labeled `waiting:maintainer`, and reworks a PR its own runs did not open only when it carries `dispatch:rework`. Claude Code first. The maintainer weighed it as a product feature of `aries` and as Bearings' first slice, and chose: "Go with A: an internal experimental loop over permanent, reusable parts, removed when Bearings B0 takes over."
97. An agent is a principal per role, owned by a person or a service, with a grant bound to each worker's session, from 19 Oct 2026, narrowing decision 8's "distinct agent identities": the session tells workers apart and makes each accountable, and principals do not pile up, since none can be removed while its name is in every log (§15, on removing a principal). Names are pseudonymous before v1.0.0: an opaque handle in every log, a display name shown to those who may list principals (§15.6, depth 2; aries-k723g26u). Counts of a person's interventions are kept per issue and per session, never per person, until then. Asked for "the option that gives us the most accountability without the pile-up", and "Build pseudos." Answers enterprise review questions 4 and 10.
98. Sextant lives in this repository under AGPL-3.0-only, a binary of its own that `aries` does not link, from 19 Oct 2026, departing from sextant.md's recommendation (c); its v0 runs beside one `ariesd` and is proved there before it reads several servers or is hosted, shows no per-person spend, and keeps budgets as alert rules. Answers sextant.md §7.
99. Aries accounts for spend and alerts on it; hard stops are set at each model provider, from 19 Oct 2026, and later managed for every provider in one place by Bearings' gateway, with one dashboard and an API for real-time reporting (bearings.md §8, question 16, provisional). A self-hosted model costs zero until a cost per token from hardware and power is designed (aries-ntxxdf2f). The first product is Aries with budgets, policy, audit and feedback, and Sextant, with Bearings in 1.x; the predecessor tool's feature list is no longer a commitment. Answers enterprise review questions 3, 5, 6 and 8.
100. Sextant starts before v1.0.0, from 19 Oct 2026, narrowing decision 90 (by which sextant.md started it after v1.0.0) and decision 93 (which held it behind v1.0.0) for Sextant alone: its slices ([sextant.md](sextant.md) §5) are built against a released server, v0.5.0 on, through the ops `aries` already sends, so they need no protocol change and wait for no release; X0 is built in the same change. Decision 99 makes Sextant part of the first product while decision 93 holds v1.0.0 as long as possible, so waiting for 1.0 would have held it indefinitely. A slice that needs a new op (X4's stand-in for a principal, or an event feed) still takes rule 10's bump in the release it joins, and X5's packaging is the maintainer's, as every release's is. Bearings still waits for v1.0.0. Asked whether X0 starts before v1.0.0: "Let's start sextant before v1.0.0."
101. Lessons are a first-class field on `handoff` and `finish`, from 19 Oct 2026 ([lessons.md](lessons.md), L2 of the learning loop, aries-l436tgkj): a lesson is about the agentic dark factory's workflow, never the repository's content, and carries `lesson`, `stage` (a closed list), `scope` (`factory` or `project`) and `recommend`, required, with `worked`, `failed`, `fix`, free tags and typed evidence links. It is append-only, written with the handoff or finish it rides on, with a `lesson.create` event; the issue's outcome and cost are joined at read time, never stored. `aries lessons` queries them and `--export` gives the distillation each lesson with its outcome, its session and its principal, while counts stay per session (decision 97). The MCP `handoff` and `finish` take a structured lesson and an MCP `lessons` tool reads them, so the tool schema budget of decision 76 rises from 3,000 to 3,200. The change joins protocol 9, and v0.6.0 waits for it. L1's `lesson:` comments are imported once from a file a person checks; a project admin may redact a lesson, CLI only; raw lessons reach organization memory only through the distillation and the maintainer's approval; a kept counter counts lessons by stage and scope. Across projects (lessons.md §10, asked the same day): raw lessons never reach another project's members; server admins and the organization-memory role read `factory` lessons across projects with `aries admin lessons --all-projects`; what the distillation proposes and that role approves becomes an organization lesson, shown in every project's prime and start within caps and exported as the playbook a new project is created with. An organization lesson is an organization memory of a kind `lesson`, with its provenance as a field, and each cross-project read is recorded as an event in the control store. Asked to answer lessons.md §14, the maintainer accepted nine recommendations, chose to build the MCP read, a structured MCP write with the budget raised, and to name the principal in the export. Revised the same day, when the maintainer adopted a critical review of the learning loop: "Adopt the review's revised sequence: per-run record primary, lessons as annotation; v0.6.0 ships without lessons (protocol 10); pre-registered rules, one change at a time; defaults from one project, checks after two; open stages, optional recommend, no org lessons in prime." So the loop's primary signal is a per-run outcome record captured after the outcome, and a lesson is an annotation beside it. Lessons take protocol 10, and v0.6.0 does not wait. `stage` is open for at least a month, `recommend` is optional, and `lessons_none` counts. The export names the session and the principal's kind only. Evidence types are not specific to one forge. There are no organization lessons in prime or start, and the organization tier stays a design until a second project and an injection test exist. Redaction, the import of L1's comments, the per-field limits and the kept counter are deferred. Each rule is pre-registered with one metric, a predicted direction, a minimum number of runs and a kill criterion, and evaluated one at a time with the memory `workflow` frozen. A rule ships as a default with a setting on one project's evidence, and becomes a check only after a second project (lessons.md §12). lessons.md §14 keeps each superseded answer beside its revision.

**As built (the Postgres port, slice P1, 15 Oct 2026).** Decision 79's slice P1 is built: the store, the daemon and every test run on Postgres alone, and nothing of Dolt is left in the code. Where the build fills in or departs from the decision:
- **Layout: a schema for each project, in one database.** A DSN is a `postgres://` URL whose `search_path` names the schema; a server of many projects derives each project's DSN, and the control store's, from its one DSN by replacing the schema (`store.SchemaDSN`). The control store is a schema beside the projects'. `Control.HeldClaims` reads every project's claims in one query, the schemas named. The names stay as they were on the wire and in settings for now (`control_db`, `project_dbs`, a project's `db_name`, the `Database` of a DSN), and now name schemas; `ariesd control init` makes the control schema, and `aries admin project add` makes a project's with `CREATE SCHEMA`, refusing with the `GRANT` to run when another role made it, and with `GRANT CREATE ON DATABASE` when ariesd's role may not make schemas. Slice P2 owes: the daemon naming schemas itself rather than taking them from a block an operator sets aside, the renames, an advisory lock for each project's single writer, one shared connection pool, and Postgres's own figures in the status report where Dolt's were (`pg_stat_database`, `pg_stat_activity`); the report has a gap there now.
- **Migrations** start again at `0001_init.sql` in each set, in Postgres's dialect, squashed from the two Dolt sets as they stood on `main`, without `write_id`. Each migration runs in one transaction with the row that records it, which Postgres's transactional DDL allows. A few columns whose order or equality must be byte for byte, whatever the database's locale, are `COLLATE "C"`: file paths, memory keys, tags, model names, and the control store's fingerprints and keys. The rest follow the database's default collation, which the tests make `C`; a database made with another may order ids and titles differently in lists, so P4's setup must make it with `C` too.
- **Removed:** rule 6's `write_id` (an update names the rev it read, and the row lock makes that compare exact: a second writer at repeatable read fails with 40001 and is retried, `TestCASNeedsNoWriteID`); the commit-a-second loop with `Store.Flush`, `Options.CommitInterval`, `Observer.Committed`, the `store.commit` span, the `aries.store.commit.duration` instrument and the `store.commit_failed` event; the Dolt tag before `ariesd upgrade` (`BackupTag`), which is not replaced: a migration that fails rolls back whole, and backups (`pg_dump`, a standby) are the operator's, in P4's guide; `internal/dolttest`, `scripts/install-dolt.sh`, the Dolt pin and `dolt.sha256`; `internal/doltmetrics`, the daemon's reader of it, `telemetry: dolt_metrics_url` (now refused as an unknown setting), Dolt's lines in the status report and `proto.ServerStatus.Dolt` (an unknown result field to an old client, so no protocol change), and the Dolt job, alert rules and panels of `deploy/observability`; the driver log hook, the full-text probe and the Dolt-only tests. `--allow-unsafe-dolt` is `--allow-unsafe-account`. The MySQL driver is gone from `go.mod`, and pgx v5, through `database/sql`, is its one replacement.
- **Errors.** A write is retried from the start on SQLSTATE 40001 (serialization) and 40P01 (deadlock), and on 23505 (unique violation), which is how a race for a new key ends: two stores that insert the same event seq, claim, memory key or idempotency key. The rerun reads what the other committed and answers from it: a fresh seq, the held claim, a conflict, the replayed answer. A unique violation that is the caller's own, an issue id already taken, is `exists` and is not retried. 42501 (insufficient privilege) is `DatabaseDenied`, which the daemon turns into the operator's `GRANT`.
- **The role check** (S-2's equivalent) reads `pg_roles`: `Open` refuses a superuser, and a role with `CREATEROLE`, `CREATEDB`, `REPLICATION` or `BYPASSRLS`, or a member of `pg_read_all_data`, `pg_write_all_data`, `pg_read_server_files`, `pg_write_server_files` or `pg_execute_server_program`, with one message that names each and the SQL that fixes it. Rights on other databases are not checked: every role may connect to every database through `PUBLIC` by default, and what it may do there rests on what was granted in it.
- **The version check.** `Open` reads `server_version_num` and refuses a Postgres older than 16 (`version.PostgresMin`) with `ServerVersionError`, whose fix names the version the tests pin.
- **Tests and CI.** `internal/pgtest` starts a throwaway Postgres in a temporary directory on a unix socket, with `fsync`, `synchronous_commit` and `full_page_writes` off, a superuser only the harness uses and the locked-down role `aries` the tests connect as, and a watcher that kills it, removes its files and frees its System V shared memory when the test binary dies. `ARIES_REQUIRE_POSTGRES=1` replaces `ARIES_REQUIRE_DOLT=1`. CI installs `postgresql-18` from apt.postgresql.org, with the repository's key checked against its fingerprint first; `scripts/install-postgres.sh` gives contributors the packaged one on Linux, or builds the pinned release (18.6) from its checksummed source archive into the cache. The eight tests that used the store's barrier to stage a lost race now race many stores, each with its own writer, as rivals on one database, with no hook: what each proved (one winner of a compare-and-swap, of a start, of a remember, of a control write; that the loser retries and gets a fresh read) is asserted of whatever interleaving the run gets. Postgres refuses to run as root, so the peer check CI runs under `sudo` uses the server's fake stores.
- **Not done in P1:** `docs/server.md`, the security model's account section, `install-server.sh`, the AppArmor profile, the Docker test server and the operations skill still describe and install Dolt; they are P4's, and the Docker test server does not work with this ariesd until then. `spike/postgres` no longer builds, since it imports `internal/dolttest`; it is kept as evidence, unmaintained.

**As built (the Postgres port, slice P2, 15 Oct 2026).** Decision 79's slice P2 is built: the daemon names its projects' schemas, serves them all through one pool, and is the only writer of each, however many processes share the database. Where the build fills in or departs from the decision and P1's note:
- **The schema rule.** `aries admin project add NAME` makes the schema `project_` and the name with each hyphen an underscore (`store.ProjectSchema`): a code name has no underscore, so two names never share one, and Postgres's 63 bytes leave a name 55 characters. A schema of that name another project has, or that is already in the database whoever made it (another daemon's project, an earlier one, a person's), is refused with `exists` before anything is written, and never taken over; the operator drops or renames it, or the admin picks another name. `control_schema` may not start with `project_`, so the control store is never taken for a project. The project `ariesd control init` adopts keeps the schema its data was in. `project_dbs`, the block of names an operator set aside, is gone. No command removes a project and ariesd never drops a schema: archiving closes the project's store, which gives its writer's lock up, and keeps the schema as it is.
- **The renames**, with no alias, since no release carried them: the setting `control_db` and `ARIESD_CONTROL_DB` are `control_schema` and `ARIESD_CONTROL_SCHEMA`; the control table's `projects.db_name` is `schema_name` (control migration `0002_schema_name.sql`, since `0001` has merged); `store.Project.DBName` and `NewProject.DBName` are `Schema`, and the JSON key in the event log `schema`; the wire's `database` on `project.add`'s result and on each row of `projects` is `schema` (a result field, so no protocol change, and no release spoke it); `ValidDBName`, `TakenDBName`, `CreateDatabase` and `DatabaseDenied` are `ValidSchemaName`, `TakenSchema`, `CreateSchema` and `SchemaDenied`; and the log field `database` is `schema`, with ariesd's start line saying `control_schema`. `store.DSN.Database` keeps its name: it is the database a DSN names, in which the schemas are.
- **One pool** (`store.DB`, `internal/store/db.go`): one pgxpool for the server's database, of at most `db_conns_per_server` (32), through which every store's writer and readers take a connection for each transaction, or each read outside one, and give it back. database/sql reaches it through a connector that sets the connection's `search_path` to the store's schema when it last worked in another, which a session setting keeps, so a connection that stays with one project sets nothing. Postgres plans a prepared statement again when the `search_path` it was planned under changes, and the schemas of one binary have the same tables, so a statement's result has the same columns in any of them. A store's writer is still one at a time (database/sql's limit of one connection for it) and its reads take at most `db_conns_per_project` (8). A store opened alone (`store.Open`, as `ariesd import-bd` does) has a DB of its own. A read inside a write's closure would wait for the pool while holding a connection of it; none does, and a pool of one runs the store's tests that race many schemas (`TestPoolOfOneServesEveryStore`).
- **Memory per project**, measured on this Mac (Apple M4 Max, macOS 27, Postgres 18.6 with pgtest's settings) as spike section 3.5 did: each store loaded with 1,200 writes and one ready query, the footprint of Postgres's processes together (and the sum of their resident sizes):

  | Stores open | One pool: footprint (RSS sum), processes | A pool each: footprint (RSS sum), processes |
  |---|---|---|
  | 0 | 50 MiB (72), 10 | 47 MiB (63), 9 |
  | 1 | 60 MiB (100), 11 | 60 MiB (100), 11 |
  | 20 | 108 MiB (152), 11 | 272 MiB (744), 49 |
  | 50 | | 608 MiB (1,825), 109 |
  | 200 | 228 MiB (418), 11 | (about 600 connections: past the server's 300) |
  | then 32 readers through every store at once | 949 MiB (3,805), 43 | 1,248 MiB (3,559), 227, at 50 |

  With a pool each, a project costs about 11 MiB, as the spike found. With one pool, a project costs about 2.5 MiB up to 20 and 0.6 MiB more each up to 200, nearly all of it the catalog and plan caches Postgres keeps in each connection for every schema it touched: the one connection that served all 200 projects held 194 MiB. The worst case is every connection of the pool having touched every schema, 4.7 MiB a project at 200 with all 32 in use. A connection keeps its caches until pgxpool retires it, idle for 30 minutes or an hour old, so closing stores gives the memory back then, not at once.
- **The writer's lock** (`store/fence.go`). Each DB holds, on one connection beside the pool (`ariesd writer locks` in `pg_stat_activity`), a session advisory lock on a key made from each schema it writes, taken before a store migrates or writes and given up when its last store of the schema closes; Postgres gives them up when that connection ends. Each write checks it in its transaction with one statement: it takes a transaction lock on a second key of the schema (the barrier), then tries to take, shared, a random key the lock connection holds as its own; refused, that session is alive and holds every lock it took. A process that takes a schema over waits on the barrier for the other's write in flight, and every later write of the other fails the check, so two processes never write a schema at once, not even for one transaction. Reading `pg_locks` instead cost 60µs a write; this costs about 20µs (an empty write 44µs against 25µs on P1, a comment 153µs against 133µs, with Postgres's `fsync` off, so most of a durable write's time is not in either). A write that finds its lock connection gone connects again, takes back every lock, and tries again; a schema another process took meanwhile refuses its writes with a `*store.ServedError`, which the server sends as `unavailable` with the fix, and its reads go on until the store closes. The control store's writer takes its lock too, so a second ariesd on one control store refuses to start; the operator's `ariesd control init` takes none, as it ran beside the daemon before. A store opened `ReadOnly` takes none and migrates and writes nothing: `ariesd export-bd`, `import-bd --dry-run` and the clock check of `ariesd upgrade` open the daemon's schemas so, and the admin commands read the control store so. `ariesd import-bd` writes, and is refused while ariesd serves the project, with a fix that says to stop it first; before P2 it wrote beside the daemon as a second writer, which rule 5 forbade and nothing enforced. Tested with two DBs, and two daemons, on one database, with the lock's connection ended by `pg_terminate_backend`.
- **Postgres's figures** in the status report (`proto.ServerStatus.Postgres` and `ProjectStatus.SchemaBytes`, new result fields): the version; client connections on the whole server against `max_connections`, and those in ariesd's database; the pool's connections held and in use against its cap, and how often a request waited for one; the database's commits, rollbacks, deadlocks, conflicts and temporary bytes since its statistics were reset, and its size; and each project's schema's size, open or not. They come from `pg_stat_activity`, `pg_stat_database`, `pg_database_size` and `pg_total_relation_size`, under the report's 2-second bound, with ariesd's own role: none needs `pg_monitor`, which would add only other roles' queries and wait events, so the setup grants it nothing. When Postgres does not answer the report says `postgres: unknown` and why, with the pool's figures from memory.
- **Metrics**: `aries.db.pool.conns` and `aries.db.pool.in_use` (gauges) and `aries.db.pool.waits` (a counter), watched from the pool with no attribute, so rule 2 holds; a wait counts an acquire that found the pool at its cap, pgxpool's empty acquires less the connections it made. `deploy/observability` has an alert on waits and a panel. Postgres's own figures stay in the report: an operator who wants them as metrics runs Postgres's own exporter beside ariesd's.
- **Limits**: `db_conns_per_server` (32) and `db_conns_per_project` (8, at most the first). The pool and the open stores now bound everything a principal could make the database hold; a project a server admin adds is bounded by `projects`, as before.
- **Tests and time.** The store's suite takes as long as on P1: 17.0 and 17.4 seconds against 17.3, and 19.6 and 20.3 with `-race` against 20.4. The tests open their stores from one DB for each test database, as the daemon does, so that the tests that race many stores of one schema stay one process's rivals.
