- **`ariesd upgrade`'s clock check** opened the control store as the new binary, which migrated it under the old daemon still serving it (`0002` renames a column the old one reads). It now opens it to read only, and passes over one that is behind the new binary, which cannot read it with its own SQL; the restarted daemon migrates it and checks the clock itself (`TestUpgradeClockCheckMigratesNothing`).
- **Owed to P4:** `install-server.sh` and the Docker test server still write `control_db` and `project_dbs`, which ariesd now refuses, with the rest of their Dolt; the guide's sections on Dolt, the security model's account section, and sizing Postgres's `max_connections` for the pool.

**As built (the Postgres port, slice P4, 15 Oct 2026).** Decision 79's slice P4 is built: the installer, the Docker test server, the AppArmor profiles, the server guide, backups and the operations skill are Postgres's, and none of them names Dolt (`scripts`' `TestOperationsNameNoDolt`). Where the build fills in or departs from the decision and the notes above:
- **No password on the machine.** With Postgres on the server, ariesd signs in by the unix socket as the role of its Unix user's name (peer authentication, as PGDG's `pg_hba.conf` has it), with the DSN `postgres://aries@/aries?host=/var/run/postgresql`: there is no secret to keep. A password exists only for a Postgres on another machine or a managed service, in `/etc/aries/postgres/pgpass` (0600, the `aries` user's, Postgres's own format), which the DSN names with `passfile=`; pgx reads it when the store opens, so a changed password needs a restart. The first project is the DSN's `public` schema, which the database's owner owns; the projects that follow are `project_NAME`.
- **The installer** keeps eleven steps, two of them new (Install Postgres; Create the role and the database) where Dolt's two were, and the guide's step numbers 5 to 7 are unchanged. It installs `postgresql-18` from PGDG with the repository's key checked against the fingerprint CI checks, or uses the one cluster `pg_lsclusters` shows if it is 16 or newer, and refuses several; with `--dsn URL` (refused when it holds a password) and `--db-password-file FILE` it installs only `postgresql-client-18`, prints step 2's statements for the database's administrator, writes the password file, and checks the sign-in. Step 5 signs in as ariesd will, with `psql` as the `aries` user, and warns when `max_connections` leaves no room for `db_conns_per_server` and the writers' lock connection with ten to spare. Tests hold its SQL to the guide's step 2, and its `pg_major`, `pg_min` and key fingerprint to `internal/version` and `ci.yml`. The deployment profiles of transport.md §3 are not built; a comment marks step 7 as the SSH transport's, and `--dsn` is independent of them.
- **The unit** orders ariesd after `postgresql.service` and wants it, with no `Requires=`: ariesd outlives a restart of Postgres, its pool connecting again and its next write taking back the writers' locks. Run under systemd (24.04, 26.04) and in the Docker test server: the same process served a write made at once after the restart. One `agent.register_failed` (SQLSTATE 57P01, a pooled connection the restart had ended) was logged in the Docker run; the request itself was answered. A ping before a pooled connection is reused, or a retry of that one note, would remove it; owed to a later slice of `internal/`. **Done 16 Oct 2026:** pgxpool pings only a connection idle for more than a second, so one used just before the restart was handed out dead; the pool's `PrepareConn` hook (`store/idle.go`) now peeks at each connection it hands out with one non-blocking `recv` (`MSG_PEEK`), and drops one with anything to read or its end closed, since a healthy Postgres sends nothing between statements. Chosen over widening the write's retry to 57P01 and broken connections: the failure came from a read (`TouchAgent` reads first), and reads have no retry; a retry would also have to tell a commit that failed from one that may have landed. It costs a system call an acquire, not a round trip. A statement in flight at the moment of a restart still fails. Linux and macOS only; elsewhere pgxpool's ping is all the check there is (`TestPoolHandsOutNoConnectionPostgresEnded`, `TestPostgresRestartBetweenRequests`).
- **The Docker test server** is two containers: sshd and ariesd, and Docker's official `postgres:18` image pinned by digest, which ariesd reaches over the compose network as a Postgres on another machine, so the test server exercises the `--dsn` path. Its init script makes the role (with a generated password) and the database as the guide's managed-service steps do, and leaves the password in a 0600 file on a volume of its own, which the server's entrypoint copies into the password file its DSN names; the superuser's password is generated and kept by nobody. A volume made on Dolt is refused with the fix to remove it. `smoke.sh` checks Postgres's figures in the status report, `/readyz` and the report with Postgres's container frozen, that Postgres publishes no port, and that the config holds no password: 155 checks plain, 212 with the telemetry harness, 189 with the identity provider.
- **AppArmor.** `ariesd-serve` and `ariesd-admin` may use Postgres's socket (`@{ariesd_pg_socket}/.s.PGSQL.*`) and read `@{ariesd_db_files}` (`/etc/aries/postgres`); `ariesd-serve` now allows DNS and `abstractions/ssl_certs` always, which a managed Postgres by name with `sslmode=verify-full` needs, as identity.md §6.4's recommendation (accepted in decision 85) has it for sign-in, so slice N3 need not add them. Parsed under AppArmor 4.0.1 and 5.0.2; not run under an enforcing kernel.
- **Backups** are a whole-database `pg_dump` in the custom format by a systemd timer (`aries-backup.service` and `.timer`), one file a weekday, as `postgres`; a restore is `dropdb` and `pg_restore --create`, which brings back the database's owner, collation and ACL. Restoring one project from a whole dump needs its schema made again first (`CREATE SCHEMA … AUTHORIZATION aries`): `pg_restore --schema` restores what is in a schema and not the schema, which the first run of the guide's text showed. Point-in-time recovery is described, not run. `ariesd upgrade` takes no copy; the guide has the operator start the dump service and keep its file before each upgrade.
- **Managed services.** On Postgres 16 and later an administrator with `CREATEROLE` and `CREATEDB` but no superuser must hold a role to give it a database (`must be able to SET ROLE "aries"`): the guide's statements grant it to `CURRENT_USER` for the `CREATE DATABASE` and revoke it after, run on Postgres 18. Where the role may not own a database, the owner makes the control schema and the first project's beforehand; `control init` adopts both (run), and `aries admin project add` is refused with the `GRANT CREATE ON DATABASE` fix, since P2's rule never takes over a schema ariesd did not make, so such a server serves one project. A pooler in transaction mode would hand the writers' locks and each connection's `search_path` to other clients: the guide says to connect directly or in session mode (not run). IAM sign-in by expiring tokens is not supported. No service was run.
- **Standby and failover** are described from spike §4.1 and the build: streaming replication, Patroni or a service's high availability promotes and fences; ariesd's advisory locks fence ariesd processes, not databases, and are free on a new primary; a multi-host DSN with `target_session_attrs=read-write` follows the primary. Not run.
- **The log schema** still lists `url`, `failures`, `after_failures` and `every`, which only the reader of Dolt's metrics logged; no line carries them, and the guide says so. **Removed 16 Oct 2026,** before v1.0.0 made the names a promise: `TestEveryFieldIsLogged` (`internal/logs`) now fails for a field of the schema that no line of the daemon gives.
- **Owed to P5:** the soaks on Linux; and, not in the decision's P5 but owed before the release, the installer and the units under an enforcing AppArmor in a virtual machine, with a cold boot, on 24.04 and 26.04, which ran only before Postgres.

**As built (the Postgres port, slice P5 on Linux, 16 Oct 2026).** Decision 79's slice P5 ran in Linux containers, against `main` at `e6e8b57` as it was: the soak gates of stages 3, 3a and 5, the telemetry soak, the installer and the peer check. Every check passed; one product fix came of it.
- **The soaks**, 10 minutes of work each (`ARIES_SOAK=10m`), under `-race`, on linux/amd64 (Docker Desktop's emulation on an Apple M4 Max, which ran the race detector at nearly native speed), in the Go 1.27.2 image with Postgres 18.6 from PGDG installed as CI installs it, each container with 4 processors (`GOMAXPROCS=4`), 6 GiB and CI's tmpfs, one run at a time:

  | Run | Seed | Requests in 10 min | Result |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Over SSH | 2100977249723793866 | 176,557 (294/s) | passed, 758 s in all |
  | Over HTTPS (`ARIES_E2E_TRANSPORT=https`) | 4307015050330002229 | 129,720 (216/s) | passed, 707 s |
  | Over HTTPS, the seed of the failed run below | 13266722811648902734 | 122,741 (203/s) | passed, 771 s |
  | Partition (`ARIES_SOAK_PARTITION=1`) | 5651590347421124218 | 166,287 (277/s) | passed, 754 s: 224 cuts, 9,175 writes queued, 8,303 replays (5 refused, 459 lost and sent again, 132 kept as a comment, none parked) |
  | Telemetry (`ARIES_SOAK_TELEMETRY=check`) | 16135190713759795944 | 183,473 (306/s) | passed, 748 s: 164 series of 28 instruments and 567,688 spans held to the event logs |

  Each had 5 daemon restarts, about 2,000 dropped answers and 270 dropped connections. The latest a lapsed lock hold was ended was 3.1 s of real time after its lease over SSH and HTTPS, against the check's 7.2 s; over HTTPS the p99 was 1.4 s against SSH's 0.35 s, and throughput a quarter lower, which T1's run on macOS did not show (198,510 requests). Peak heap was 530 to 800 MiB, and the container held about 4.5 GiB at its peak, 3 GiB of it the test binary's: CONTRIBUTING.md now says so.
- **The first attempt** ran the SSH, HTTPS and partition soaks at once, each in 3 GiB of a VM of 8 GiB: SSH and partition were killed for memory, and the HTTPS run (seed 13266722811648902734, 118,513 requests) failed one check, a hold of `train/main` ended 9.7 s of real time after its lease, past the bound, while the three thrashed (about 9 GB read from disk by each). Its seed passed alone (above). Not a fault of the code: the check bounds real time, and the machine was starved.
- **The installer** ran on Ubuntu 24.04.5 (systemd 255) and 26.04.1 (systemd 259) in containers booted by systemd, from a checkout, with `aries` and `ariesd` built from `main` into a static release tree signed by a throwaway key that the checkout's `install.sh` was edited to trust, as the installer's tests do. For each of `--profile private` (`--cert` and `--key` from a test CA, `--url https://localhost:18443`) and `--profile ssh-only`, on each release, in a fresh container: a dry run, a clean run, a rerun ("Nothing to change"), and a dry run again ("0 changes"), each exiting 0; then `aries` from macOS, with only a test key (`ARIES_KEY_FILE`) and a data directory of its own, ran `whoami`, `create`, `list` and `admin status` over HTTPS with `server.ca`, and over SSH with the host key pinned; over SSH the `private` profile's server accepted no key, as a default install should. `internet` was only planned (`--plan`): ACME needs a public name. They ran on arm64: under amd64 emulation systemd's own journald failed to start, since a filter of system calls by architecture (`SystemCallArchitectures=native`) sees the emulator's native calls, and the units of `docs/server.md` have such filters too, so the installer on amd64 is still not run. A dry run counted one or two changes fewer than the clean run then made. One is the container's: PGDG's package made its cluster and did not start it (Docker's `policy-rc.d`), so the installer started it; the rest was not traced, and is a count printed, not a step skipped.
- **The peer check** ran as CI's step does, the test binary built by an unprivileged user and run as root under `scripts/leftovers.sh`: `TestCheckPeerOtherUID` and `TestCheckPeerKeepsStatusFromOtherUsers` passed, none skipped (linux/amd64).
- **Fixed:** with SSH off, every clean run and rerun of the installer printed, from `ariesd control init`, that sshd reads `/etc/aries/admin.keys` and that `aries admin key remove` would not keep the admin's key out until its line was deleted. Nothing reads that file but `control init`. The line is now printed only when something still lets a key in by the file: sshd with SSH on, and with SSH off ariesd's key proof over HTTPS, when the file is ariesd's own `authorized_keys` (decision 89), which it then names (`TestControlInitSaysWhoReadsTheFile`, seen failing first). Run again in the 24.04 container with the fix: no such line, and the rest as before.
- **Still owed before the release:** the installer and the units under an enforcing AppArmor in a virtual machine, with a cold boot, on 24.04 and 26.04; the installer on amd64; a long soak on a Linux machine that is not emulated (**done 16 Oct 2026**, below).

**As built (the Postgres port, slice P5 on GitHub's runners, 16 Oct 2026).** The long soaks ran on Linux machines that are not emulated: GitHub's hosted runners, `ubuntu-24.04` (amd64) and `ubuntu-24.04-arm` (arm64), each 4 processors and 16 GiB, one soak to a runner, by a workflow a person starts (`.github/workflows/soak.yml`; CONTRIBUTING.md, Run the soaks on GitHub). Each job installs Go and Postgres 18 from PGDG as CI's test job does, through composite actions both workflows now use (`.github/actions`), and runs `TestSoak` with `ARIES_SOAK=10m`, `-race`, `ARIES_REQUIRE_POSTGRES=1` and the temporary files on `/dev/shm`, under `scripts/leftovers.sh`. Run [38956156535](https://github.com/ariesworx/aries/actions/runs/38956156535), at `19eac45` (`main` at `d90c92d` with the workflow): all eight passed, with nothing left behind, and no fix was needed.

  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | Over SSH | amd64 | 8144634381899110909 | 129,272 (215/s) | 1.5 s | 731 s |
  | Over SSH | arm64 | 17519127777524288142 | 168,503 (281/s) | 0.6 s | 771 s |

  | Partition | arm64 | 3520707629322557325 | 161,751 (269/s): 219 cuts, 9,554 queued, 8,869 replays (6 refused, 503 sent again, 157 kept, none parked) | 2.5 s | 760 s |

HTTPS was not slower than SSH here: on amd64 it served a fifth more requests, on arm64 4% fewer, so the quarter less that the emulated run above showed was the emulation's, not the transport's. The arm64 runners did more work than the amd64 ones in every mode, from 5% more over HTTPS to 39% more cut off. Each job took about 14 minutes, under its `timeout-minutes` of 60.

## 15. Projects and access

Designed 9 Oct 2026. What is built is in the As built notes of §15.1, §15.2 and §15.4: a server may now hold many projects, with membership checked at connection, projects, principals, members and keys managed with `aries admin`, keys written from the control store to a file sshd reads (decision 22, which replaced the command described in the plan of §15.2 and in the notes before 12 Oct 2026), and `aries admin status` to watch it by. The stage's gate, the soak across two projects, passes. The sshd setup has run on a real sshd in Docker; running it under systemd and on each provider's machines remains, with the rest of what the last note of §15.4 lists as unverified.

**Goal.** One server, many projects. A project has one or more developers, remote or not, each running from one to hundreds of agent sessions. A principal has access to zero or more projects.

### 15.1 Databases

- **One Dolt database per project**, all in the one `dolt sql-server`, on the one machine. A project's database is exactly today's schema, so the migrations and the store's queries do not change, and the `project` column §3 planned for `issues` is not needed.
- **One `Store` per project** inside the one `ariesd`: its own writer connection, its own readers, its own event log, reaper and event push. A busy project does not queue another's writes; they still share the machine's CPU, memory and disk. A project's store opens on first use and closes when idle, so connections follow the projects in use, not the projects that exist. Dolt's memory does not: it follows the data written and is not given back when a store closes (§15.5, 2). *Measured 12 Oct 2026, on Linux:* more exactly, it follows what was written since Dolt started and how many projects that was spread over, and a restart of Dolt gives most of it back (§15.5, 2).
- **A control database** holds what is not a project's: `projects`, `principals`, `principal_keys`, `members`, and its own event log, so that every grant, revocation and key change is history (rule 7). It has its own writer.
- **Routing.** The hello already names the project (`proj`), so a current client and its `.aries.yaml` work unchanged: the daemon looks the project up instead of comparing it to the one it was started with. `aries admin` opens a control connection, which names no project; that, and the admin ops, raise the protocol version (rule 10).
- **Limits** become per principal per project where they protect a project (connections, registry rows, write rate, inbox), with the server totals kept. New caps (rule 18): projects, open stores, principals, keys per principal, members per project.

| Table | Columns |
|---|---|
| `projects` | `id` (UUID), `name`, `prefix`, `account`, `db_name`, `state` (`creating` / `active` / `archived`), `rev` |
| `principals` | `name`, `state` (`active` / `disabled`), `created_by`, `rev` |
| `principal_keys` | `fingerprint` (SHA-256), `principal`, `key` (the public key line), `comment`, `added_by`, `added_at` |
| `members` | (`principal`, `project`), `role` (`member` / `admin`), `added_by`, `rev` |

**As built (stage 3a, control store, 11 Oct 2026).** The control store exists as a library, `store.Control` (`internal/store/control*.go`); nothing calls it yet, so a server still holds one project, principals are still lines in `authorized_keys`, and admins and their rights are as before. The daemon, the protocol, `ariesd keys` and `aries admin` come in later slices. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:
- `Control` is a `Store` over a second database: the same single writer, retry loop, `write_id` check, event log and committer, with its own migration set, `internal/store/controlmigrations`, to which rule 9 applies from here on. Its versions are recorded in a table of its own, `control_migrations`. Each kind of store refuses a database that holds the other's version table, before changing anything, so two DSNs swapped in a config file do not run one schema over the other.
- Columns beyond the table above: `projects` has `created_by`; `projects`, `principals` and `members` have their times and a `write_id`; `projects.name` and `projects.db_name` are unique. A key is added or removed and never changed, so `principal_keys` has no `rev`.
- A project's `name` and `account` are code names as accounts are (§12.1), its `prefix` an issue id prefix, and its `db_name` a lowercase letter followed by lowercase letters, digits and underscores, 64 at most: narrower than Dolt allows, because the name is written into `CREATE DATABASE`.
- `AddProject` writes the row as `creating` and may be run again with the same id and fields, which returns the row and writes nothing; `ActivateProject` and `ArchiveProject` likewise return a project already in that state. A project that was never activated can be archived. **An archived project can be activated again**, by a server admin (decision 18); the `project.activate` event's before state shows that it was archived.
- A key is parsed with `golang.org/x/crypto/ssh` from one line, `TYPE BASE64 [COMMENT]`. Options, a second line, a certificate and a comment with a control or bidirectional character are refused, without repeating the line, which may be a private key pasted by mistake. What is stored as `key` is the type and base64 the parser writes back, never the caller's text; the comment is a column of its own, for display.
- The fingerprint index of §15.2 is built: `AuthorizedKey` reads memory only and answers only for a key of an active principal. It is loaded at open and changed by each write, in commit order. A write that fails rather than being refused may have committed, so the index is then read again from the database. It does not see a change made by another process until the next open.
- Roles are enforced inside each write's transaction, so a refusal records nothing. Three rules go beyond §15.3, each closing a way for a project admin to gain rights: **a server admin's principal is outside every project admin's reach**, whatever projects it is in, and a project admin may not create a principal with a server admin's name (a server admin named in the config file need not have a row yet); **a principal in no project is outside every project admin's reach**; and a role counts only while its holder is active, so a disabled project admin administers nothing. A project admin may also enable a principal within reach, not only disable it.
- **Adding to reach is restricted (decision 17).** A project admin may add to their project only a principal that already belongs to some project, theirs or not, or that they created. A principal in no project that someone else created is placed by a server admin; the refusal says so. Otherwise a project admin could bring an unassigned principal within reach and then manage its keys. Changing a member's role and removing a member are as in §15.3. What remains is by design: a principal whose only projects are ones an admin administers is within that admin's reach, so one who leaves their other projects comes within it, by the write of whoever removes them. `aries admin` should say so at that step.
- Events: `project.add`, `project.activate`, `project.archive`, `principal.add`, `principal.disable`, `principal.enable`, `key.add`, `key.remove`, `member.set` and `member.remove`. A target is a project id, a principal name, a key fingerprint, or `PROJECT/PRINCIPAL` for a membership, so the control database's `events.target` is 128 wide.
- Caps (rule 18), with defaults: 500 projects, 5,000 principals, 20 keys a principal and 1,000 members a project. They are `store.ControlLimits`, apart from `store.Limits`. The `limits:` settings and their rows in the server guide come with the slice that lets a client reach them, as does the cap on open stores.
- Reads are not guarded in the store: the daemon decides who may list what.

**As built (stage 3a, the daemon serves many projects, 11 Oct 2026).** `ariesd` serves many projects when its settings name a control database (`control_db`); without one it is the server of one project it was, on the wire and in its log. The protocol did not change: the hello already named the project. `aries admin`, the control connection, `ariesd keys` and keys served from the store are still to come, so the only project a server can hold is the one it adopts, and its members are those `ariesd control init` imports from `authorized_keys`. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:
- **Settings.** `control_db` names the control database on the Dolt server and account of `dsn`; the daemon reaches it, and each project's database (`projects.db_name`), by replacing the database name in `dsn`. `project`, `prefix` and `account` stay in the config file and describe the project to adopt, which `import-bd`, `export-bd` (both take `--project UUID`) and the health check of `upgrade` use by default.
- **`ariesd control init --name NAME`** is the command of §15.2 that imports `authorized_keys`, and also the move of §15.5, 3: it records the database in `dsn` as a project with the config file's UUID, prefix and account, activates it, and for each line whose forced command is `ariesd stdio --principal NAME` adds the principal, the key and a membership. A server admin gets no membership. It is safe to run again, and then adds only what is new: it does not change a role, activate an archived project or remove anything. Its writes are recorded under the reserved principal `import`, which a control store opened as the operator's (`ControlOptions.Operator`) lets write as a server admin; the daemon never opens its store that way, and no key can be that principal.
- **The check at connection** is one read of memory. The control store's index, which held the keys, now also holds the projects and the roles, kept current by each write in commit order as before, and `Control.Access` answers from it: whether the project is active, and the principal's role. An unknown, archived or unfinished project is refused as not served, in the words a server of one project uses; an active one is served to a server admin and to an active member, and anyone else gets `forbidden`, with a fix that says whom to ask and names no command, since none exists. The upgrade's probe, a reserved principal no key can be, is let into any active project.
- **Another process's writes.** The index follows the daemon's own writes, and `control init` is another process, so the daemon reads the index again on every reaper tick (30 seconds). The same will hold for any operator command until the daemon makes every control write itself.
- **Open connections are not dropped** when a membership ends, a principal is disabled or a project is archived (§15.2 says they are): the next handshake is refused. Nothing over the protocol can end access yet; dropping connections comes with the ops that can.
- **The registry of stores** (`internal/server/registry.go`). A project's store is opened by its first handshake, and handshakes that arrive meanwhile wait for that one opening. It is closed after `store_idle` (10 minutes) with no connection, and at most `open_stores` (64) are open: at the cap the store longest without a connection is closed to make room, and if every store has one the handshake is refused with `busy`. A project being closed is waited for, never opened beside its closing store, so a project never has two writers. A store that fails to open is refused with `unavailable` and tried again by the next handshake.
- **Background work** runs for every open store on each tick, and once as a store opens, before its first request: a closed store has no reaper, so claims that expired while it was closed are ended then.
- **Admins in a project.** `store.Options.IsAdmin` is a function the project's store asks, beside `Options.Admins`, on every write and admin-only request; the daemon answers it from the control store's index, so a project admin's role counts, and stops counting, on the next request with the store still open. A project admin has every right an admin had in a server of one project, in that project: overriding a held issue, a forced close, prices, plans and undoing another's hours.
- **Limits.** What the daemon counts per principal (connections, the write bucket, refusal log lines) it counts per principal per project; the cap on all connections stays the server's. What the store counts per principal was per project already, each project having its own database. `open_stores`, `store_idle` and the control store's four caps are `limits:` settings.
- **Upgrade.** The backup tag is made in the control database and in the database of every project that has one, archived ones included, before the binary is swapped. The health check opens the config file's project, which shows the control database and that one project migrated. **Not built:** the other projects' databases are migrated when their stores next open, and a failure there is not seen by the upgrade, only by that project's connections (`unavailable`) and the log.
- **Memory scopes** are unchanged: each project's memories, `team` ones included, are in its own database.

### 15.2 Keys

A key names a principal and nothing else. What a principal may reach is in `members`, so no table ties a key to a project. A key that should reach fewer projects than its owner (CI, a cloud sandbox) gets a principal of its own, as the server guide already advises. Only public keys are stored.

sshd still authenticates, and the forced command still names the principal:

```text
Match User aries
    AuthorizedKeysCommand /usr/local/bin/ariesd keys %t %k
    AuthorizedKeysCommandUser aries
```

- `ariesd keys` asks the daemon over its socket for the offered key and prints, for a key of an active principal, the line used today: `restrict,command="ariesd stdio --principal NAME" KEY`. For any other key, and on any error, it prints nothing, and sshd refuses the key.
- It runs before authentication, for every key a stranger offers. So the lookup is an in-memory index by fingerprint, reads no database, changes nothing, and is bounded by sshd's `MaxStartups`.
- `~aries/.ssh/authorized_keys` stays, read by sshd as before, for the first server admin and as the way in when the daemon is down. `ariesd` gets a command that imports its lines into the store.
- Disabling a principal, removing a key or ending a membership drops the open connections it allowed. Today that takes `pkill` on the server.
- The `Match User` block matters: where the image already sets a global `AuthorizedKeysCommand` for its own login service, the block overrides it for the `aries` account alone.

**Providers.** `AuthorizedKeysCommand` is OpenSSH's, so nothing here belongs to a cloud. From general knowledge of the products, to be checked on a VM of each before the stage's gate:

| Provider | Note |
|---|---|
| Google Cloud | OS Login sets a global `AuthorizedKeysCommand`; the `Match` block overrides it for `aries` |
| AWS | EC2 Instance Connect, on Ubuntu and Amazon Linux images, does the same |
| Azure | Plain VMs set none; the Entra ID login extension sets one |
| DigitalOcean | Droplets set none |

Open, to check on those VMs: sshd runs the command only if the file and every directory above it are owned by root and not writable by others, which the binary swap of `ariesd upgrade` must keep true; SELinux in enforcing mode on RHEL-family images (the guide is tested on Ubuntu 24.04 only). The private-network transport (§2, IAP) is Google Cloud's alone; on the other three a server is reached on a public SSH port with the pinned host key, optionally behind WireGuard (decision 10).

**As built (stage 3a, keys from the store, 11 Oct 2026).** Public keys come from the control store: an admin adds and removes a person's keys with `aries admin`, sshd asks `ariesd keys` whose a key is, and removing a key or disabling a principal takes effect at once. Onboarding someone needs no shell on the server. Decisions 19 to 21 (§14) are built here. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:
- **`ariesd keys TYPE BASE64`** takes sshd's `%t` and `%k` and computes the SHA-256 fingerprint itself; it does not use `%f`, whose form depends on sshd's configuration. It asks the running daemon over its socket and, for a key of an active principal, prints exactly `restrict,command="/usr/local/bin/ariesd stdio --principal NAME --key FINGERPRINT" TYPE BASE64`: the line of the plan, with the key's fingerprint added to the forced command. For anything else (an unknown key, a disabled principal, the daemon down or from before key lookups, a server of one project, arguments that are not one key's type and base64, an answer it does not trust) it prints nothing on standard output and exits 0, with one line on standard error saying why, which never repeats its arguments. The line holds nothing a stranger chose: the principal is the daemon's and must be a principal name that is not reserved, and the key printed is the stored one, which must have the fingerprint that was asked about. `--bridge PATH` changes the path in the forced command, and must be absolute with no character that needs quoting.
- **The daemon's side** is a first frame of its own on the socket, `{"t":"keys","k":"SHA256:…"}`, answered with `{"t":"keys","pr":NAME,"pk":KEY}` or an empty keys frame, from `Control.AuthorizedKey`, which reads memory only. It takes no connection slot, opens no store and writes nothing. The plan bounded it by sshd's `MaxStartups` alone; the daemon also caps the lookups in flight (`key_lookups`, 64), closes one past the cap unanswered, and gives each asker two seconds to take its answer. It is logged at debug only, sampled as refusals are, by fingerprint and never by key. The socket's peer check admits only the daemon's own user, hence `AuthorizedKeysCommandUser aries`.
- **Not a client protocol.** The keys frame and the bridge frame pass between two `ariesd` processes on one machine and carry no version. Both are read leniently and only ever gain fields, so the bridges of sessions open across a binary swap keep working in either direction: an old bridge sends no fingerprint, which a new daemon takes as a line of `authorized_keys`; a new bridge's fingerprint is ignored by an old daemon; and an old daemon closes a keys lookup unanswered, which is no key.
- **The fingerprint travels with the connection.** `ariesd stdio --principal NAME --key FINGERPRINT` puts it in the bridge frame, and the daemon records it beside the principal and the project. When `key.remove` commits, the daemon closes the connections that key authenticated. A connection that came in by a line of the `authorized_keys` file names no key and is not closed by a key's removal. A handshake that names a key the store does not hold for that principal is refused with `auth`, at the handshake and again as the connection is recorded, which closes the gap between sshd's question and the handshake.
- **`authorized_keys` stays a way in**, as planned, and `control init` still imports it and is safe to run again. The consequence is spelled out in the server guide: a key that is in both the file and the store is not revoked by `aries admin key remove`, because sshd falls back to the file, so moving a server's keys into the store means deleting their lines from the file. The daemon's reload of the index every 30 seconds stays, for `control init` run beside it.
- **Commands.** `aries admin key add NAME FILE` (`-` reads standard input), `key list NAME`, `key remove FINGERPRINT`, and `principal add NAME --key FILE`, which may be repeated and adds the principal and its keys in one write. The client reads the file, refuses anything that is not one public key before it connects (a file that holds a private key is named, never quoted), and sends the key as the parser writes it back. Who may write is the control store's rule; the daemon applies the read rule, so `key list` is for a server admin, an admin with reach over the principal, and the principal itself, and anyone else is refused the same way whether or not the principal exists. A project admin's refused `key remove` names no principal and is the same for a key that does not exist. **Protocol 6** adds the three ops and `principal.add`'s keys. Protocol 5 had not shipped in a release, so by the practice `internal/proto/version.go` records they could have joined it; they were made a bump so that a client with the key commands is refused at the handshake by a server built before them. Nothing was added to MCP.
- **Decision 19** is enforced inside the write's transaction, with an `AccessReason` of its own, for `key.add`, `key.remove` and `principal.add` with keys: the last so that a server admin the config file names, who has no row yet, cannot create their own principal with a key. `control init` is not refused, since the operator's principal is no one's account.
- **Decision 20.** The control store kept two questions apart that the config list had answered together: whether the config file names a principal a server admin, which is what puts it beyond every project admin's reach, disabled or not; and whether it has a server admin's rights now, which needs its row, if it has one, not to be disabled. `Control.Access`, the handshake, each project store's live admin check and every control write ask the second, the writes inside their transaction. A disabled principal's handshake is refused as disabled, with the command that enables it. Disabling the last server admin who is not disabled is refused with `invalid`.
- **Decision 21.** After the control write commits, beside the connection drop, the daemon ends the claims in the stores that are open (`Store.EndClaims`): for each, a `claim.release` event by the admin whose after state gives the reason (`membership removed`, `principal disabled` or `project archived`), a `claim.lost` inbox item for the session that held it, and the issue back to open and unassigned if it was in progress under the holder. Like the reaper, it does not pass `wtx.guard` and records no `admin.override`: it is the daemon ending a hold, not an admin changing an issue. The results of the three commands count the claims released. **A project whose store is closed is not opened to release:** its claims end at its next open, in a sweep beside the reap-at-open and before the first request, which ends every claim whose holder no longer has access to the project. Those are recorded under the server's own actor (`ariesd`, session `access`) with the same reasons, since by then the admin is not at hand; the control store's event log says who ended the access. The same sweep runs on every reaper tick for the stores that are open, so a daemon that stopped between the control write and the release ends the claims after it restarts. A demotion releases nothing.
- **The Docker test server** opts in: its entrypoint creates the control database and a block of eight database names at first start, runs `control init` at every start, and its sshd has the `AuthorizedKeysCommand`. Its smoke test adds a second project, a principal whose key only the store holds, removes the key, and checks a non-member is refused.
- **The binary's ownership.** sshd runs an `AuthorizedKeysCommand` only if the file and every directory above it are owned by root and not writable by group or others. The documented install does not satisfy that: `aries` owns `/opt/aries/bin` and the binary in it, so that `ariesd upgrade`, which refuses to run as root, can swap it, and `/usr/local/bin/ariesd` is a link to it. So the server guide gives sshd a root-owned copy, `/usr/local/sbin/ariesd-keys`, which only asks the daemon and prints its answer; the forced command it prints still names `/usr/local/bin/ariesd`, the upgraded binary. The upgrade does not refresh the copy, and need not for it to work, since the keys frame is kept compatible; root copies it again after an upgrade. The Docker image's binary is root's, so its sshd runs it directly.
- **Unverified.** Everything above is tested against Aries's own stand-in for sshd (`internal/e2e`), which asks the same code `ariesd keys` runs and reads its line as sshd would. No real sshd has run the command: not the ownership check, not the `Match` block overriding a provider's global command, not SELinux. The Docker image and its smoke test were changed on a machine with no Docker and have not been run. The provider table above is still from general knowledge. All of that is for the VM checks before the stage's gate.
- **Checked in Docker, 12 Oct 2026.** `deploy/docker/smoke.sh` was run for the first time, unchanged, and passes (65 checks): OpenSSH 9.6p1 on Ubuntu 24.04 runs `ariesd keys` as its `AuthorizedKeysCommand` under the `Match User` block, a principal whose key only the control store holds signs in, and removing the key locks it out. Two variants of the image, with ariesd installed as the server guide installs it (owned by `aries` in `/opt/aries/bin`, linked from `/usr/local/bin`), settle the ownership question: with the link as the command, sshd refuses it (`Unsafe AuthorizedKeysCommand "/usr/local/bin/ariesd": bad ownership or modes for file /opt/aries/bin/ariesd`), resolving the link as expected, and falls back to `authorized_keys`, so the smoke test fails at the first key from the store; with the guide's root-owned copy, `/usr/local/sbin/ariesd-keys`, all 65 checks pass. Still unchecked: a provider's global command, SELinux (Docker Desktop's kernel has none), and the fallback with the daemon stopped.
- **Left for the last slice:** `aries admin status`, the soak across two projects, and those VM checks. The first two are built (§15.4, the last As built note); the VM checks are not done.

**As built (stage 3a, keys through a file, 12 Oct 2026).** Decision 22 (§14) replaces the mechanism of the plan above and of the note before this one: sshd no longer asks ariesd about a key. ariesd writes the keys to a file, and sshd reads it. The notes above are left as the history they are; where they speak of `ariesd keys`, the keys frame, `key_lookups`, `--bridge`, the root-owned copy or "sshd asks", this note is what is built.
- **Why.** Running the command on a real sshd (the note above, Checked in Docker) showed its cost. sshd refuses an `AuthorizedKeysCommand` unless root owns the binary and every directory above it, and the guide's binary belongs to `aries` so that `ariesd upgrade` can swap it, so the guide had to install a root-owned copy that root refreshed by hand. Under SELinux, sshd's domain would need a policy to connect to the daemon's socket. Cloud images set a global `AuthorizedKeysCommand` of their own, which ours had to override. It was a path into the daemon that ran before authentication. And keys from the store stopped working while the daemon was down.
- **The file.** `~aries/.ssh/aries_keys` by default (`keys_file`, `ARIESD_KEYS_FILE`; a relative path is under the home directory of the user ariesd runs as, taken from the account database). Its first line is a comment saying that ariesd generates it and overwrites edits. Then one line for each key of an active principal, exactly the line `ariesd keys` printed, `restrict,command="BRIDGE stdio --principal NAME --key FINGERPRINT" TYPE BASE64`, by principal and then fingerprint, so an unchanged store gives identical bytes. Each line is built only from what the store holds, checked again as it is written: the principal matches the principal pattern and is not reserved, the fingerprint is the stored key's own, and the key is written back by the parser. The path of ariesd in the forced command was `ariesd keys --bridge` and is now the setting `bridge` (`ARIESD_BRIDGE`), `/usr/local/bin/ariesd` by default, refused at start unless it is absolute and needs no quoting. Only a server of many projects writes the file.
- **How it is written** (`Server.WriteKeys`, `internal/server/keys.go`). A temporary file in the same directory, mode 0600, written and synced, renamed over the target, and the directory synced. It refuses a directory that group or others may write to, a target that is a symbolic link or not a regular file, and, beyond the decision, **a target that exists and does not start with ariesd's own first line**: a `keys_file` pointed at `authorized_keys` would otherwise replace the first admin's way in. The settings refuse the two being the same path. It compares what it would write with what the file holds and writes nothing when they are equal, so nothing is rewritten when nothing changed, and an edit to the file is undone at the next tick.
- **When.** At start, after the control store opens and before the daemon listens; a failure there fails the start, and the error names its fix. After `key.add`, `key.remove`, `principal.add` with keys, `principal.disable` and `principal.enable`, before the admin is answered. And on the reaper's tick, after the control index is reloaded, which is how an `ariesd control init` run beside the daemon reaches the file, and how a failed write is tried again. A control write that fails without being refused may have committed; its file write is left to the tick.
- **A failed write after the start** does not fail or undo the control write. It is logged at error on each attempt; `aries admin status` gained a keys file line for server admins (path, keys, when it was last written, and whether the latest write failed); and the result of each of the five ops carries a `warning` saying that sshd may still be reading the old file.
- **Why a stale file is not a hole.** Nothing in the handshake changed: a bridge frame that names a key the store does not hold for that principal is refused with `auth`, and a disabled principal with `forbidden`, both before a connection slot or a store is taken, and the first again as the connection is recorded. `TestStaleKeysFileIsNotAHole` (`internal/e2e`) states the property: it removes a key and disables a principal while the daemon cannot write its file, shows that the stale file still lets both keys past the stand-in for sshd and that the daemon refuses both, then puts the old file back by hand after a good write and shows the same.
- **Which file sshd takes.** sshd uses the first line that matches, in the order of `AuthorizedKeysFile`, so with the order of decision 22 a key in both files is let in by its line of `authorized_keys`, which names no fingerprint. Measured on OpenSSH 9.6: the connection of a key in both files reached the daemon with no key named. So such a key's connections are not closed by `key remove`, where the earlier notes have the store's line win. The order is the decision's and is the safer one for break-glass: a stale line of the generated file cannot shadow the hand-maintained line of the same key, which would turn a way in into a refusal. The stand-in for sshd in `internal/e2e` reads the two in that order, which is a change from "the store first" in the tests of the earlier note.
- **A precise warning for a key still in `authorized_keys`.** The daemon runs as the account whose file it is, so it reads the file (`authorized_keys`, `ARIESD_AUTHORIZED_KEYS`), leniently: a line that is not a key is skipped, only the first mebibyte is read, a missing or unreadable file marks nothing, and the answer is kept until the file's size or time changes. It never writes to it, and nothing read from it is logged or returned but the fact that a key is in it. `key.remove` of a key whose material is also there returns `in_authorized_keys` and a warning that sshd still accepts it, by a line whose forced command names no fingerprint, and that the line must be deleted on the server; each key of `keys` carries the same mark. This replaces the warning of the note in §15.4, which counted the principal's open connections that named no key and could not fire for a key in both places while the store's line won. `control init`'s closing line stays.
- **The counts that stay, and the one that went.** `file_conns` on `key.remove`'s result is removed: the mark above says what it hinted at. `file_conns` on `keys` and on each project of `status` stay, and are still true: a connection that names no key came in by the hand-maintained file. They show what the mark cannot, a line of that file whose key was never in the store. The CLI's column is now BY HAND.
- **Removed.** `ariesd keys` and `cmd/ariesd/keys.go`; `server.AuthorizedKey`, the socket client; the keys frame, its handling, its in-flight counter and `proto.FrameKeys`; the `pk` field of a frame; the `key_lookups` limit, its setting and its row; the key lookups figure of `status`; `--bridge`; the guide's root-owned copy; the Docker image's `AuthorizedKeysCommand`. A config file that still sets `key_lookups` is refused as an unknown key. Kept: the fingerprint in the bridge frame, the connection's recorded fingerprint, the drop of a removed key's connections, the handshake's refusal, and `Control.AuthorizedKey`, which the handshake asks; `Control.AuthorizedKeys` is new, the list the file is written from.
- **No protocol bump.** Protocol 6 has not shipped in a release, and the changes are to results, which clients read leniently; `internal/proto/version.go` records them. The keys frame was never client protocol. A daemon of this version closes a connection that opens with one, unanswered and logged at debug only: a root-owned `ariesd-keys` from an unreleased build, still configured in some sshd while its server is upgraded, reads that as no key, as it read a daemon from before the frame.
- **Checked in Docker, 12 Oct 2026** (OpenSSH 9.6p1 on Ubuntu 24.04; `docs/deploy/docker.md` has the detail). The smoke test passes with 84 checks, among them the file's mode and owner in the container, a key added with `aries admin` signing in with no restart, its removal locking it out, and no failed write in the daemon's log. Three variants of the image, built for the purpose and not kept: (1) ariesd installed as the guide installs it, owned by `aries` and linked, with no root-owned copy: all 84 pass, where the command was refused. (2) A stand-in for a provider's agent, a global `AuthorizedKeysCommand` outside any `Match` block, run as `nobody`, that prints nothing: all 84 pass with no override. sshd ran that command twice for each sign-in by `aries`, whichever file held the key; `AuthorizedKeysCommand none` in the `Match` block stopped it, and the guide says when to add it. (3) ariesd killed and sshd left running: sshd accepts a key the store alone holds (`Accepted publickey for aries`), and the client is told `ariesd is not running on the server`, code `unavailable`, the refusal the bridge already gave. The guide's `Match` block and the image set `AuthorizedKeysCommand none`: without it a provider's global command is still run for the `aries` account and a key it printed would be accepted with no forced command; with it the stand-in was not run and both files still worked.
- **A globally trusted certificate authority, 12 Oct 2026.** Found while investigating certificates (§15.6) and confirmed separately: where the machine's sshd trusts a certificate authority for every account (`TrustedUserCAKeys` outside any `Match` block, as some companies' images have), a certificate from it for the principal `aries` was let in with no forced command and ran a command as the account. The guide's first `Match` block, the one in the keys section and the image now set `TrustedUserCAKeys none` and `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile none` beside `AuthorizedKeysCommand none`, and the same certificate is refused. The first block had none of the three until now, so a server of one project was open to both.
- **Unverified.** systemd: the guide's unit has `ProtectSystem=strict`, and the guide now adds `ReadWritePaths=/var/lib/aries/.ssh`; that line, and that `ProtectHome=yes` does not cover `/var/lib/aries`, are from systemd's documentation and were not run. The real provider agents, on real machines. SELinux, which is not supported yet (decision 23); from general knowledge, unverified, sshd there reads keys only from files with the label for it, which a home directory outside `/home` does not give its `.ssh` by default, as true of the hand-maintained file as of this one. AppArmor: Docker Desktop's kernel loads neither, so nothing was tested under either.

**As built (stage 3a, the generated file first, 12 Oct 2026).** Decision 26 (§14) reverses the order of the two files that decision 22 and the note above give. Where that note says "Which file sshd takes" and "the safer one for break-glass", this note is what is built; the rest of it stands.
- **What changed.** One line of the guide's and the image's `Match` block, `AuthorizedKeysFile .ssh/aries_keys .ssh/authorized_keys`, and no code of the daemon: it never knew the order. A key in both files now reaches the daemon with its fingerprint, so `key remove` closes its connections (`dropped` counts them) and the handshake's check of the key against the store applies. The stand-in for sshd in `internal/e2e` reads the daemon's file first and its own keys second.
- **After `key remove`** the generated file has no line for the key and sshd finds the hand-maintained one, so the key signs in again, with no fingerprint, as the principal that line names. The mark (`in_authorized_keys`, ALSO IN) and the warning therefore stay, reworded: the connections were closed, and sshd will still accept the key from `authorized_keys` until its line is deleted on the server. `file_conns` and BY HAND are unchanged and still true: a connection that names no key came in by the hand-maintained file, which now means its key is not in the store, or was removed from it.
- **The store wins for a key in both.** It signs in as the store's principal, whatever its hand-maintained line names. The hand-maintained file lets in only a key the store does not hold, or holds for a disabled principal, which the daemon then refuses as disabled.
- **The cases where the new order could shut out someone who should get in:**
  1. *A write that failed, leaving a stale line for a key the store no longer holds, with the hand-maintained line still there.* sshd matches the stale line, the daemon refuses it with `auth`, and the person is shut out though the hand-maintained line would have let them in. When the write succeeds (it is retried every 30 seconds, and `status` shows the failure) the stale line goes and the hand-maintained line lets them in. For a removed key this is right, and stricter than the files in step: the admin removed the key. It is the one place where a failure makes the server stricter than its own settled state. `TestStaleKeysFileIsNotAHole` now pins it, with a key in both files. An operator who needs the hand-maintained line to work meanwhile moves the generated file aside.
  2. *The first server admin.* Before `control init` the server writes no file. After it, the admin's key is in both and comes in by the generated line, as the same principal. Nobody removes a key of their own account (decision 19), so a server admin's key leaves the store only by another server admin's hand, who can still connect; the last server admin who is not disabled cannot be disabled (decision 20). So no sequence of `aries admin` commands by the first admin, or by anyone when there is one server admin, leaves that admin with a stale or missing generated line and no way in. Right.
  3. *A principal disabled in the store, with a hand-maintained line.* The generated file has no line for a disabled principal, so sshd takes the hand-maintained one, and the daemon refuses the principal as disabled (decision 20), as before the change. With a stale generated line the answer is the same refusal. Right: disabled wins, and the order does not matter.
  4. *The daemon stopped.* sshd accepts a key by either file and the bridge answers `ariesd is not running on the server`, whatever the order. The daemon writes the file before it listens and does not start if it cannot, so a file that went stale while it was stopped (a control database restored from a backup, a `control init` run meanwhile) is replaced before anyone is answered. Right.
  5. *The first admin's key claimed by someone else.* **This was wrong, and is fixed.** It was found while working through these cases and, separately, by the security review of 12 Oct (its F-5). The store refuses a key another principal has, and knew only its own keys. A key added to `authorized_keys` after `control init` (which is how decision 19 has a sole server admin replace their own key) is in no principal's name in the store. Anyone who may add a key could add that public key to a principal within their reach, a project admin included (decision 15), and the generated line, read first, signed the owner in as that principal: tried in `internal/e2e`, a project admin's `principal add mallory --key` with the only server admin's public key turned that admin's next command into mallory's, refused every admin op. The project admin gains nothing of the owner's, holding no private key, but the owner is shut out of their own identity, and with one server admin nobody could remove the key over the protocol. With the old order the hand-maintained line won, and this could not happen. What is built:
     - *The rule that a key belongs to one principal covers the hand-maintained file.* The daemon, which already read that file for the mark, keeps the principal each line's forced command names (`Server.handOwner`) and gives the control store a function to ask (`Control.KeysElsewhere`). `key.add` and `principal.add` refuse a key whose line names another principal, with the `TakenError` of a key another principal has, **inside the write's transaction** and after the checks of who may add a key, so it cannot be skipped by another handler or raced by a second request, and it records nothing. A project admin is told only what the store's own refusal says, that the key belongs to another principal; a server admin, who can read the file on the server, is told that it is a line of `authorized_keys` for another principal, and to add it to that principal or have the line deleted. A line with no Aries forced command names no principal and reserves nothing.
     - *`control init` is not refused.* It is the operator's, opens its own store with no such function, and imports each key for the principal its line names, which is the rule itself.
     - *A hand-maintained line added after the store had the key* cannot be refused, since the operator's edit is not a request. So the daemon **holds that key back from the generated file**: `keyLines` leaves out a key of the store whose fingerprint the hand-maintained file gives to another principal, sshd then reaches the operator's line, and the key signs in as the principal that line names. Until the file is rewritten, which is at the next key change or the next tick, the handshake refuses a connection that named that key for the store's principal (`keyHolds`), as it refuses any stale line. The daemon logs each key held back at warn when the set changes, and `status` counts them (`held_back`, "keys held back from it"). Deleting the line puts the store's line back. This also closes the race the check alone leaves, where someone adds a key to the store just before the operator adds its line.
     - *What is left.* The daemon knows the hand-maintained file by its size and modification time, reads its first mebibyte, and takes the first line for a key, as sshd does. A line beyond the first mebibyte is not seen: its key can be claimed, and is not held back. An edit that keeps both the size and the time is not seen until one of them changes.
     - *Tests.* `TestKeyOfAuthorizedKeysKeepsItsPrincipal` and `TestHandLineAddedLaterHoldsTheStoreKeyBack` (`internal/server`), and on a real sshd, below.
- **Checked in Docker, 12 Oct 2026** (OpenSSH 9.6p1 on Ubuntu 24.04). The smoke test passes with 97 checks, thirteen more than before. With the key in both files, neither the admin's own connection nor the member's is counted as let in by `authorized_keys`, which shows sshd took the generated line, where the same check under the old order counted one; after `key remove`, the same key signs in again and its own connection is counted as by hand. For case 5: a second key of the server admin, added to `authorized_keys` by hand in the container, signs in as the admin; `key add` and `principal add --key` of it for anyone else are refused and the generated file gains no line for it; it still signs in as the admin afterward. And for the other order: a key the store holds for a member, then given to the admin by a line added by hand, leaves the generated file within a tick, signs in as the admin, and `status` counts one key held back.
- **The soak.** The principal whose key is in both places (`erin`) now comes in by the generated line. The check of her key's removal gained two assertions and lost none: a connection opened while the store held the key is closed by the removal, and one opened after it, by the hand-maintained line, is left open by the next removal.

**As built (the guide on Ubuntu under systemd, and AppArmor profiles, 12 Oct 2026).** Decision 23's promise is kept: AppArmor profiles for ariesd ship in `deploy/apparmor`, as an optional step of the server guide. They were written while the guide was followed, step by step, for the first time on a real Ubuntu system with systemd: Ubuntu 24.04.5 (kernel 6.8.0-142, systemd 255, OpenSSH 9.6p1, AppArmor 4.0.1), arm64, in a Lima virtual machine, with `ariesd` built from source and put where the installer puts a release. This settles what the note above lists as unverified for systemd and AppArmor, on that one machine; amd64, the providers' images and SELinux are as they were.
- **The units work as written.** Without the `ReadWritePaths` drop-in a server of many projects does not start, and its error names the line (`read-only file system`); with it the keys file is written, and `ProtectHome=yes` does not get in the way. `systemd-analyze security` scores ariesd's unit 7.2 and Dolt's 7.1. Not changed here: the units limit no system calls, capabilities or namespaces.
- **What the guide had wrong or missing**, each now in `docs/server.md`: (1) at boot ariesd's first start fails, because systemd counts Dolt as started about a second before it listens, and `Restart=on-failure` brings it up five seconds later; (2) `AuthorizedKeysCommand none` in the `Match` block makes sshd log `error: No user for AuthorizedKeysCommand specified, skipping` for every key that is in neither file; (3) `ariesd upgrade --restart` with no `systemd_unit` asks sudo for `systemctl restart ariesd`, which the guide's rule for `ariesd.service` does not cover; (4) the Dolt download had no checksum. Confirmed as written: the sshd `Match` block on stock Ubuntu, which listens through `ssh.socket`; that a `Match` block ends with its drop-in file; the forced command, no terminal and no forwarding; `upgrade --check` and `--rollback` as the `aries` user, with the sudo rule.
- **Stock Ubuntu confines neither sshd nor ariesd.** `aa-status` listed 117 profiles, none for sshd; sshd, its children and ariesd ran `unconfined`. Ubuntu 24.04 restricts unprivileged user namespaces through AppArmor (`kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_userns = 1`); neither ariesd nor Dolt asked for one.
- **Three profiles, not one.** AppArmor attaches by path, never by argument, and the one binary is the bridge, the daemon and the operator's tool. The path carries the bridge's profile, the smallest (the config file and the daemon's socket), because sshd cannot ask for another and the bridge runs outside the unit's sandbox. The daemon's (`ariesd-serve`: Dolt over TCP, the keys file) is named by the unit's `AppArmorProfile=`. The operator's (`ariesd-admin`: the network, the binary, `sudo`) is entered with `aa-exec`, so `control init`, `import-bd`, `export-bd` and `upgrade` are run through it where the profiles are installed. With one profile the bridge could reach Dolt and write the keys file, and everything that listens could replace its own binary.
- **What only a real system showed.** The daemon's socket is bound inside the unit's mount namespace, so AppArmor sees its path as disconnected and refuses the bridge's connection, in complain mode too, until the profiles carry `attach_disconnected`. sudo cannot run inside a profile that holds no capabilities, so the operator's profile runs it unconfined, bounded by sudoers.
- **Tested.** Enforced: 76 checks (issues, a second project, keys from the store, lockout, the operator's commands, restarts) with nothing in the kernel log, before and after a reboot; then what the profiles forbid, each refused and logged. Not tested: an upgrade to a newer release, which a build from source refuses, so the download, the signature check and the swap to a new binary were not run; amd64.
- **A limit.** The daemon writes the file sshd reads keys from, so a daemon that has been taken over can let a key in, and no profile changes that. The profiles keep the bridge and the operator's commands from that file, and every process from the config file, `authorized_keys` and, but for `upgrade`, the binary, all of which the `aries` user owns.
- **CI** checks the profiles' syntax (`apparmor_parser -Q -K`) on its Ubuntu runner. It cannot check that ariesd works when confined; that needs a machine with AppArmor and systemd, which CI's jobs do not set up.

**As built (Ubuntu 26.04 LTS, 14 Oct 2026).** Decision 23 now names two releases: the supported platforms are Ubuntu 24.04 LTS and 26.04 LTS, with one server guide and one `install-server.sh` for both, which accepts those two and refuses the rest. 26.04.1 was set up by the installer for real, in a systemd container and in a fresh Lima virtual machine (kernel 7.0, systemd 259, OpenSSH 10.2p1, AppArmor 5.0.2, sudo-rs, Rust coreutils), and driven with `aries`; 24.04 was run again the same way with the changes. What 26.04 changed, each in `docs/server.md` where it applies:
- **sudo-rs and Rust coreutils** ran every command the guide and the installer use. sudo-rs's `visudo -c -q` prints `parsed OK`, so the installer silences it. Its path, `/usr/lib/cargo/bin/sudo`, is what AppArmor sees, so the operator's profile refused it and `ariesd upgrade` could not restart the unit; the profile now names it and `sudo.ws`.
- **AppArmor stacks a profile changed to by a process that is not root** (`kernel.apparmor_restrict_unprivileged_unconfined=1`, 0 on 24.04), so the daemon runs as `ariesd-serve//&unconfined` and the operator's commands as `ariesd-admin//&unconfined`. Confinement holds, since a stack allows what each profile allows, but the base abstraction's self-signal rule no longer matched, and the kernel refused Go's `SIGURG`; the two profiles now allow their stacked label.
- **OpenSSH 10.2 turns away an address** that fails to log in or ends connections before logging in (`PerSourcePenalties`, new in 9.8, on by default): measured, one removed key retried four times locked the admin out of the same address for about 25 seconds, and clients refusing a changed host key did the same. The guide says so and points to `PerSourcePenaltyExemptList`, which 24.04's sshd refuses, so the installer's drop-in does not set it. The `Match` block, the forced command and the refusals of a terminal, a tunnel and a certificate behaved as on 9.6. **Closed, 15 Oct 2026:** a turned-away client saw only `ssh handshake with HOST:PORT failed: ssh: handshake failed: EOF`, with a fix to check `server.host`. `aries` now tells that case apart (`client.ClosedBeforeHandshake`): a server that accepted the TCP connection and closed or reset it before showing its host key, which no refusal of a host key or of a key looks like. It says `HOST:PORT closed the connection before the ssh handshake`, names penalties, `MaxStartups` and a firewall or proxy as causes, and its fix is to wait a minute and try once, check `aries whoami`, and have the operator look for `penalty` in sshd's log. It stays `unavailable`, so a queueable write is queued, as for any outage. `aries mcp` tells the agent to tell the user and not to loop, and dials again no sooner than 30 seconds after such a refusal, as it waits 5 after one at the connection limit.
- **chrony replaces systemd-timesyncd**, with NTS by default; `time-sync.target` still waits for nothing, and `chrony-wait.service` takes the place of `systemd-time-wait-sync.service`, which 26.04 lacks.
- **Unchanged:** the units (`systemd-analyze verify` and `security` as on 255), the installer's steps and the files it writes, and the Docker test server's image, which stays on 24.04. CI checks the profiles' syntax under 26.04's parser too, in a job of its own on GitHub's `ubuntu-26.04` runner. Not run: amd64, a cloud provider's image of 26.04, and an upgrade from 24.04 to 26.04 in place.

### 15.3 Roles

| Role | Where | May |
|---|---|---|
| Server admin | The whole server | Create and archive projects; create and disable any principal; add and remove any key; set any membership and role; act as admin in every project |
| Project admin | Each project where `members.role` is `admin` | Add and remove the project's members and set their roles; create a principal and its keys; override a held issue and `close --force`, as an admin does today |
| Member | Each project it belongs to | Work |

- Server admins stay in the config file (`admins:`), read at start, so nothing over the protocol makes one.
- A handshake for a project the principal is not a member of is refused with `forbidden` and a fix naming the admin command. The store's admin check (`wtx.guard`) reads the project's admins and the server's, live.
- **A project admin's reach over keys is bounded.** A principal a project admin creates starts as a member of that project only. A project admin may add or remove a principal's keys, or disable it, only while every project that principal belongs to is one they administer. Otherwise a project admin could add a key of their own to someone with access elsewhere and act as them there; past that line it takes a server admin.
- Principal names are one namespace for the server, so a project admin's new name can collide with another project's person. The refusal says the name is taken, which reveals that it exists.

### 15.4 `aries admin`

CLI only (rule 12). Sketch:

```text
aries admin project add NAME [--prefix P] [--account A]     server admin (§15.5, 1)
aries admin project list | archive PROJECT
aries admin status                                          server admin: every project; project admin: theirs
aries admin principal add NAME --key FILE [--project P]     project admin: --project is theirs
aries admin principal list | disable NAME
aries admin key add NAME FILE | list NAME | remove FINGERPRINT
aries admin member add NAME [--project P] [--role admin]    default project: this repository's
aries admin member list | remove NAME
```

`project add` prints the UUID and the `.aries.yaml` to commit.

`status` reports, per project: whether its store is open, connections, sessions seen, writes a minute, write latency and the database's size on disk; and for the server: ariesd's own memory and goroutines, the machine's free memory and disk, and Dolt's memory where ariesd can read it. Dolt is one process, so its memory cannot be split by project. (As built, below: the size on disk, the machine's figures and Dolt's memory are left out.)

**As built (stage 3a, `aries admin` for projects, principals and members, 11 Oct 2026).** Admins manage a server of many projects from anywhere, over the protocol: `aries admin project add | list | archive | activate`, `principal add | list | disable | enable` and `member add | list | remove`. The key commands, `ariesd keys`, keys served from the store and `aries admin status` are still to come, so a new person still needs a line in `authorized_keys`. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:
- **No control connection.** §15.1 says `aries admin` opens a connection that names no project. It does not: the admin ops ride an ordinary connection to the project in the repository's `.aries.yaml`, which the caller must be admitted to as for any other command (a server admin is admitted to every active project). An op that acts on a project takes it as an argument, a UUID or the project's name, and defaults to the connection's. So there is one handshake, one way in and one set of limits. The cost is that an admin needs an active project to connect to: the server already refuses to start without one, and `project archive` refuses the server's last active project, since nobody could then connect to activate another. Archiving the project one is connected to answers, then closes that connection.
- **Protocol 5** adds the eleven ops (`internal/proto/admin.go`); `ProtoMin` stays 2. A server of one project refuses them all with `invalid`, and its fix names the opt-in section of the server guide. Nothing was added to MCP.
- **Who may** is the control store's rule for every write: the handler passes the connection's principal as the actor and words the refusal (`mapErr`), as `forbidden` with a fix that says who can. Three ops are checked for a server admin before anything is read, with the store's own refusal, so that the answer does not depend on what exists. The store does not guard reads, so the daemon does: `project list` shows a server admin every project, and anyone else the active projects they belong to; `principal list` is for server admins; `member list` for the project's admins and server admins. A caller who is no server admin gets the same refusal for a project that does not exist as for one they have no role in. Lists are by name, 200 rows or up to 1,000, with a count of the rest and `--after NAME` to continue.
- **`principal add` puts the principal in the connection's project** unless `--project` names another; `--no-project`, which only a server admin may use, adds it to none. The sketch's `--key FILE` waits for the key commands.
- **`project add`** follows §15.5, 1. The block of names is the setting `project_dbs: {prefix, count}`: the prefix and a number of three digits, 001 to at most 999, each granted by root by exact name at setup. The daemon takes the lowest name no project row has, writes the row as `creating` with a new random UUID, runs `CREATE DATABASE IF NOT EXISTS` on the control store's writer connection (`Control.CreateDatabase`; like the backup tag it is outside `Store.write`, since it changes no table), opens the project's store through the registry, which migrates it, and activates it. The same command run again for a project still `creating` resumes it with the row's id, prefix and database; for any other state the name is taken. A name root never granted is created and cannot be used: Dolt answers the open with "Access denied", the refusal gives the `GRANT` statement for that name and account, and the row stays `creating`. A full block is refused with `invalid`, and its fix says to grant more and raise `count`. The issue prefix defaults to the project's name and the account to `internal`. `project activate` refuses a project still `creating`, which may have no database, and opens an archived one's store before activating it, so an active project is always one this ariesd has opened.
- **The reply's config.** The CLI prints the new project's id and the `.aries.yaml` for its repository: the server block of the repository it was run in, with the project swapped, without the key path and without values left to their defaults (`client.Config.ForProject`).
- **Open connections are dropped when access ends** (§15.2). The daemon records, for each connection past its handshake, its principal and project. After the write has committed it closes: on `member remove`, that principal's connections to that project, unless the principal is a server admin; on `principal disable`, all of that principal's; on `project archive`, all of the project's, and the project's store once they have ended. A demotion from admin to member closes nothing. The connection that made the request is answered first and closed after. A handshake that was admitted before the write and recorded after it is caught by a second check as it is recorded. A disabled server admin's connections are closed too, and the next is let in: server admins are the config file's.
- **Two things the admin is told.** `member remove` warns when project admins gained reach over the principal: when, after the removal, the principal is no server admin, still belongs to a project, and some active project admin administers every project it still belongs to and did not administer the one it left (`Control.ReachGained`). The warning counts those admins and does not name them, since they may be admins of projects the caller knows nothing of. `member add` that gives a principal a second or later project lists the fingerprints of the keys recorded for it, when the caller is a server admin or a project admin with reach over it (`Control.Reaches`).
- **Limits.** Control writes have a token bucket of their own, per principal on the whole server (`control_write_rate` 1 a second, `control_write_burst` 30): the write bucket is per principal per project, and the control store is no project's. The lists are not counted.
- **Upgrade.** The gap the last note left is closed at daemon start, not in `ariesd upgrade`: the command that runs an upgrade is the old binary, which cannot apply the new one's migrations. A daemon of many projects opens every active project's store once before it listens (`Server.OpenProjects`), which migrates each. A project that cannot be opened is logged and held against the upgrade's health probe, which is refused, naming it, until the project opens or is archived; the others are served. So a failed migration fails the upgrade, which puts the old binary back, and a plain restart with one broken database does not take the server down. The probe is answered for the server before any project is looked up, so the health check no longer depends on the config file's project being active. As before, the old binary cannot open a database the new one migrated.
- **The reload every 30 seconds** stays, for `control init` run beside the daemon; the daemon's own writes update the index as they commit.
- **Left for the next slices:** `aries admin key add | list | remove` and dropping connections when a key is removed; `ariesd keys` as sshd's `AuthorizedKeysCommand`, answered by the daemon from the index; `aries admin status`; and the Docker test server opting in.

**As built (stage 3a, `aries admin status`, the file warning and the soak across two projects, 11 Oct 2026).** The last slice of the stage: an admin can watch a server of many projects, the daemon says what it can see of a key that is still in `authorized_keys`, and the stage's gate (§13) is run. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:
- **`aries admin status`** is the op `status`, a read. It **joins protocol 6**, which has not shipped in a release, as `version.go` records; a server built between the key ops and it refuses it as an unknown operation. A server admin gets the server's figures and a row for every project in every state; a project admin gets rows for the active projects they administer, as `project list` shows them, and nothing of the server; anyone else is refused with `forbidden`. A disabled server admin on a connection still open is refused too. Projects are paged by name like the admin lists (200, at most 1,000). Nothing was added to MCP, and rule 12 now names it.
- **Where the figures come from.** Everything is the daemon's memory, apart from one `COUNT` on the registry's `last_seen` index for each open store listed. It opens no store, reads no control table and writes nothing. The projects and the counts by state come from the control store's index (`Control.KnownProjects`, `Control.Counts`); connections from the daemon's own record of whose each is; open stores, connections and key lookups stand beside their limits.
- **Per project:** whether its store is open; connections past their handshake, and how many of them named no key of the store; and, only while the store is open, the sessions seen in the last five minutes (`who`'s window), the writes, and when it last answered a request. **A write** is a transaction that changed a row and committed (`store.WriteStats`): a refused request is not one, nor is a reap or a sweep that found nothing; a lease renewal and a registry touch are. The report gives how many committed in the last 60 seconds, and the **median and 99th percentile, by nearest rank, of the latency of the latest 256 writes**, however old: from the request reaching the store to its commit, so the wait for the single writer and any retry are in it. The counters are a ring of 256 durations and 60 one-second counts for each open store, on the machine's clock, and start from zero each time a store opens.
