- **For the server:** version and protocol range, uptime, open stores, connections and key lookups in flight against their caps, ariesd's Go heap in use and its memory from the operating system (`runtime.MemStats` `HeapAlloc` and `Sys`), goroutines, and projects and principals by state.
- **Left out, against the sketch above.** *Dolt's memory:* Dolt is another process under another user, and the daemon has no portable way to read it; the server guide says to read it from the machine, and that it follows the data written (§15.5, 2). *A database's size on disk:* at Dolt 2.4.2 the least-privileged account is offered no table or function for it. `information_schema.tables` gives an estimate of the current rows that leaves out the history, which is most of a Dolt database, and reading the files belongs to Dolt's user, so the field does not exist. *The machine's free memory and disk:* not read; the provider's metrics have them.
- **The file warning.** The daemon knows whether a connection named a key of the store (the bridge frame's fingerprint) or not, and so came in by a line of `authorized_keys`; it does not know which line. So `key remove` carries `file_conns` and a warning when the key's principal has such connections open, `key list` gives the count for its principal, and `status` gives it for each project. The wording says that much and no more. **A limit worth knowing:** sshd asks the store first, so while a key is in both places every connection it makes names the key, and the first `key remove` after `control init` warns of nothing. The count appears once the key is out of the store and its owner connects again. What does speak at the moment of the import is `control init` itself, which now ends by saying how many keys are in both the store and the file it read, and that removing one from the store does not remove its line. It never edits the file.
- **A defect the soak found: a claim taken as its project was archived.** `project archive` closes the project's connections and ends its claims, but a request already read from a connection is still answered, and a `start` among those could take a claim after the admin's release. The store then closed, no tick sweeps a closed store, and the claim stayed in an archived project's table until it was activated (seed 15536472690001368833: one claim left of twenty). Now a store closed because its project was archived is **swept once more as it closes**, when nothing uses it and so no request can be in flight (`registry.retiring`); the claim ends with a `claim.release` of the server's own, for `project archived`. A server test pins it (`TestArchiveSweepsAsTheStoreCloses`).
- **Decision 21, as measured.** Another short run (seed 16755269619008477475) showed the closed-store rule at its widest, which is by design and is now said in the server guide: a daemon that restarts between an archive's control write and its release has no store open for a project nobody may connect to, and does not open one, so those claims end when the project is activated, before its first request. For a membership or a disabled principal the store is open or soon is, and the sweep on the next tick, or at the next open, ends them.
- **The gate: `TestSoak` across two projects.** The one soak (§12) now runs on a server of many projects whose keys the in-process SSH server asks the daemon for. Five principals with five sessions each work two projects: one in both, one in the first only, one in the second only, the first project's admin, who is a plain member of the second, and a server admin; two of them sign in with keys only the store holds, one with a key in both places. Each project has its own replay of its own event log, checked against its own tables as before, with every earlier invariant kept and applied per project. A second server admin, the conductor, changes access over the protocol under the same dropped answers, dropped connections and restarts: a membership, a principal, a key, the project admin's role, the second project as a whole, and a membership of a third, quiet project while its store is closed.
- **What it adds to the invariants.** (1) *Isolation:* no answer and no push to a connection names an issue of another project (the projects' id prefixes differ, so every result and push is searched for them); a `show` or `start` of another project's issue is refused; and every marker and usage record in a project's log and tables was sent by one of that project's own sessions. (2) *Handshakes:* every handshake a session tried is judged against the control log: none was let in while the log gave it no access, none was refused access it had. One that overlaps a change, give or take a second, proves nothing, since an event carries the time its transaction began; sshd's own refusals are not judged, because it finds no key for a principal of the store while the daemon restarts. (3) *Access that ends:* the connections open before a change are closed after it, the next handshake is refused with the code the rule gives, and the one after the access returns is let in. (4) *Claims:* the replay knows a `claim.release` with a reason. The issue must be open and unassigned again if it was in progress under the holder, and untouched otherwise; the reason must be true by the control log at some moment between the claim's take and its release; and the releaser is the admin whose change it was, or the server. Whoever lost access holds nothing shortly after, and nothing again when the access is about to return. A demotion releases nothing, and a connection open across it still answers. (5) *Project admins:* an `admin.override` by anyone but a server admin is checked against the control log, so the role counts while it is held and not after. (6) *The control store:* its own log is replayed: no gap, every event following from the rows as the log left them, and the tables equal to the replay in the same snapshot; at the end the daemon's index answers `Access` and `AuthorizedKey` as the tables say for every principal, project and key, and `status` counts the same. (7) *The closed store:* a member is removed while the quiet project's store is closed; the removal releases nothing and does not open the store, the claim is still in the table, and the first request after the store next opens no longer lists it: it ended with the server's own release, for `membership removed`. Each check has table tests that feed it logs breaking one rule each.
- **What the soak does not show.** That a demotion closes no connection is checked only by one probe connection for each demotion, when the chaos left it alone. "Within one reaper tick" is not asserted as a time: under load a tick's writes queue behind others, so the test waits up to 20 seconds and reports the slowest release it saw. The run uses the stand-in for sshd, as every test of keys does.
- **Timing.** The short run is still 12 seconds of work, with every kind of change made once (the first round is finished even if the work has stopped). The whole test takes about 23 seconds on the machine the earlier notes used, where it took 21.5 before: the 2 seconds are two more projects and six principals made through the admin ops before the work starts, and three projects checked at the end. Nine short runs with different seeds passed after the fix above (among them 8748412614808947511, 15429299472522359386, 5725929588000663177, 14495164100753390935, 2803297220170075094 and 14786954930934344576); of the five before it, two are the runs that found the claim and the closed-store case.
- **First long run across two projects.** Ten minutes with `-race` (seed 18365330744792630486), every invariant held: 125,980 requests; in the first project 52,662 events in 27,413 transactions, 4,543 issues, 6,452 claims taken, 2,175 reaped and 4,213 released; in the second 52,205 events in 27,226 transactions, 3,460 issues, 6,317 claims taken, 2,062 reaped and 4,184 released; in the quiet project 275 events and 55 claims, each ended at its store's opening. The control log has 898 events: 131 memberships removed and added again, 53 principals disabled and enabled, 51 keys removed and added, 25 removals of a key still in `authorized_keys` (24 warned; the other's answer was lost, or its probe connection dropped first), 45 demotions and promotions, and 51 archives and activations, 7 of which left the claims to the activation. 1,977 claims ended for lost access (811 for a membership, 420 for a disabled principal, 746 for an archive), 474 of them by the server's sweep and the rest by the admin's own release; the slowest took 396 ms after the change was acknowledged. 764 connections were seen to close as their access ended, 355 handshakes were refused and 374 admitted right after a change, and of 19,392 handshakes by sessions, 10,274 let in, none broke the control log. Chaos: 2,394 dropped answers, 282 connection drops, 5 daemon restarts, 294 sessions vanished and 205 stalled; 15,830 issue events owed to 237 event watches, all delivered. p50 per project write was 35 to 60 ms (renew 91 ms) and p99 under 0.4 s (renew 0.51 s) on a shared machine, with two writers at work; the test process, daemon and sessions together, peaked at 615 goroutines.
- **Claims in archived projects end at start (decision 24, 12 Oct 2026).** This changes two notes above: "A project whose store is closed is not opened to release" (§15.2, decision 21) no longer holds for an archive, and "Decision 21, as measured" in this note described the gap that is now closed. `Server.ReleaseArchived` runs once in `ariesd serve`, after `OpenProjects` and before the daemon listens, where nothing else can have the project's store open. What the decision left open, as built:
  - *Cost.* For each archived project it first asks, through the control store's own connection, how many claims the project's database holds (`Control.HeldClaims`, one `COUNT`), and opens a store only for one that holds any, or whose read failed. Measured with ten archived projects holding nothing: 0.2 to 0.3 ms a project, where opening each store took 8 ms. With nothing to end it writes nothing, so it is safe to repeat.
  - *No migration.* The store is opened with `store.Options.NoMigrate`, which refuses, with `ErrSchemaOld`, a database this binary's migrations have not all been applied to, and changes nothing in it. Such a project is logged at info and left as it is, and any claim in it ends when the project is activated, which already migrates the database and sweeps it before the first request. The other choice, migrating at start, would have ended those claims too, at the price of an upgrade changing databases nobody can use, of a rollback finding them too new for the old binary, and of contradicting `TestServeMigratesEveryProject`, which already pinned that an archived project's database is not migrated at start. **What that leaves:** a daemon stopped between an archive and its release, and started again as a newer binary that carries a project migration, leaves that project's claims until it is activated. Both halves have to happen in the same restart.
  - *Failures.* A project whose database cannot be opened, or whose schema is newer, is logged at error and does not stop the others or the start; it is not held against the upgrade's health probe, which is about active projects.
  - *Not on the reaper tick.* A running daemon makes this state only at an archive, so the archive is where it is prevented: `project archive` now opens a closed store through the registry (so the project never has two writers), which ends the claims as every opening does, under the server's name, and closes it again; and the release no longer stops when the admin's connection drops, since the archive has committed. One store is opened for one admin's request, which the control write bucket bounds. A tick would instead read every archived project every 30 seconds, up to the cap of 500, to find a state that can then arise only when the release itself failed, which is logged and is ended by the next start or the activation. The archive's `released` count is of the admin's own releases, so it is 0 for a store that was closed.
  - *Two archives at once.* The check that an archive does not take the server's last active project was made before the control write and outside it, so two archives sent together could each see the other's project still active and leave none: nobody could then connect, and the daemon would not start (the security review of 12 Oct). The check and the write are now one step under a mutex of the daemon's (`Server.archiving`), which is enough because the daemon is the only writer that archives. `TestArchivesTogetherLeaveOneProject` states the property; it did not fail without the mutex in fifteen tries, the window being a few microseconds, so it guards the property and not this fix.
  - *An answer from a stopping daemon.* Two short runs failed on a conductor's op answered `unavailable` (`the server could not complete project.archive`, seed 15006515746959414509 on this branch; `member.set`, seed 14245368110280542215 on main), with `err=project …: context canceled` in the daemon's log. That is the daemon stopping for the run's restart: its context cancels the requests in flight, and one of them was answered before its connection closed. Both were canceled reading the project, before any write, so nothing was lost or half done; and where a write is cut off at its commit, `unavailable` with a fix to retry is the right answer, since the daemon does not know, and every admin op is safe to send again. The fault was the soak's: the conductor took that answer for a refusal. It now takes `unavailable` for a lost answer, and sends the op again, only while a restart is under way; at any other time it still fails the run. Neither seed reproduces the timing; the six short runs and the long run after the change passed.
  - *The soak.* The archive check was lenient when the daemon restarted meanwhile, the answer was lost and the archive resent, or the store was closed or had no connection: it then asked only that no claim outlive the activation. It now asks in every case that the project's table hold no claim once the archive is answered and the store reported closed, with the project still archived. The count once reported as "left to the activation" is now of those harder cases, held to the same rule.
- **What stage 3a leaves unverified** (as of 12 Oct 2026, after decisions 22 and 23). The code is built and its gate passes in the test stack. Done since this note was written (§15.2, the two notes of 12 Oct): the Docker image and its smoke test run and pass on a real sshd, with keys read from the file ariesd writes; the guide's install, with no root-owned copy, works; and sshd accepts a store key with the daemon stopped, the client then being told the daemon is not running. Done on 12 Oct 2026 on one Ubuntu 24.04 virtual machine, arm64 (§15.2, the last note): (1) systemd: the units, with `ReadWritePaths` for the keys file, were run and work, and AppArmor profiles for ariesd were written and run enforced. Not done: (2) the providers: a stand-in for a global `AuthorizedKeysCommand` was tested, and the real agents (OS Login, EC2 Instance Connect, the Entra ID extension) on real VMs were not, so the provider table of §15.2, for Google Cloud, AWS, Azure and DigitalOcean, is still from general knowledge; (3) SELinux is no longer an open check: it is not supported yet (decision 23); amd64 was run under neither systemd nor AppArmor; (4) the memory items of §15.5, 2 are unmeasured: what a restart of Dolt gives back, a memory limit set with room to spare, projects written to at the same time, and Linux. The soak's two projects were written to at once, which says the writers do not corrupt or starve each other in a 10-minute run on one Mac; it measured no memory. Item (4) was measured later that day, on Linux in a container (§15.5, 2).

**As built (stage 3a, the security review's fixes, 12 Oct 2026).** The [security review of stage 3a](security-review-stage-3a.md) found nine things at the edges of the access rules; its Disposition section says what became of each. Seven are fixed here, with decisions 35 and 36 (§14). Where the build now differs from the notes above:
- **Which key `aries` offers (decision 35, F-1).** One place decides (`client.loadIdentity`). A configured key is the only key offered: `ARIES_KEY_FILE` in the environment, any path, before `key:` in `.aries.yaml`. The file signs when it is a private key that needs no passphrase; ssh-agent signs for that one key, found by its public half, when the file is passphrase-protected, is the `.pub`, or is missing with its `.pub` beside it, which is how OpenSSH treats an `IdentityFile` whose key is in the agent. With no key configured, `aries` tries `~/.ssh/id_rsa`, `id_ecdsa`, `id_ecdsa_sk`, `id_ed25519` and `id_ed25519_sk` (ssh_config(5)'s default `IdentityFile` list, less the retired DSA and XMSS names), only those that exist, and then the agent's keys that are not among them, and offers five keys at most (`maxOffered`), one below sshd's default `MaxAuthTries` of six; the keys left out are counted in a refusal. A default file that is passphrase-protected is used through the agent when the agent holds its key, matched by public key, and is otherwise skipped; `aries` never prompts. An `_sk` file holds a handle to a hardware token, so it too is used only through the agent, found by its `.pub`. A certificate beside a key is not read (§17). The home directory is `os.UserHomeDir` (`HOME`; `USERPROFILE` on Windows), which nothing in a repository sets. `ARIES_KEY_FILE` is checked before it is used as a path (`notAPath`: more than one line, a private key's opening, or over 4,096 bytes), and the refusal does not repeat it; the same check guards `key:`. `ARIES_KEY` is never read: only whether it is set is noted, and a refusal at sign-in then says once that it is not read. `aries setup --global --key-file PATH`, and the same for a desktop app, writes the variable into the person's own MCP entry, the one variable beside `ARIES_HARNESS` an entry may hold, and never into a project's committed files. Tests cannot reach the developer's `~/.ssh` or agent: `clienttest.Isolate`, called from `TestMain` of every package that connects, points `HOME` at an empty directory and unsets `SSH_AUTH_SOCK` and both variables. **A committed `key:` stays in the repository:** a relative path that, with symbolic links resolved, is under the root; an absolute path, one under `~` and one that climbs or links out are refused, since a cloned repository's file would otherwise choose which of a person's key files is read and shown to the server the same file names. This changes what a `key: ~/.ssh/…` written before does. `aries whoami` prints the welcome's principal, the project, the server and the fingerprint of the key that signed, with its source and any default file skipped; it is no new op and no MCP tool, and `prime` names the principal on its first line and the key on its second. A server that accepts no key, and one that hangs up after too many (an sshd whose `MaxAuthTries` is below the default), are each an `auth` refusal that lists the keys offered, by fingerprint and file.
- **Joining a project (decision 36, F-3).** `member.set` gains `accept_keys` and its result `carried` and the change `pending`, in protocol 5 without a bump (it was protocol 6 when this was built, since folded into 5 by decision 25), which `version.go` records. The decision is the store's, in the write's transaction (`Control.ProposeMember`), after it has decided that the caller may add the principal at all, so a caller who may not is shown no key. The answer to an accepted add lists the keys again, read after the write, so one added between the question and the answer is seen; `aries` warns of it. **The read rule for `keys` changed with it:** an admin of an active project the principal belongs to may list its keys, where before only an admin with reach could. The write rules (reach, and decision 19) did not change. A key `ariesd control init` imported is shown as added by an admin of every project, since the operator is beyond one.
- **What an account added outlives it (F-2).** Decision 19 stops an account's holder from giving that account another key. It does not stop a server admin from adding a key to another server admin, or from making a principal an admin everywhere, and by decision 14 nothing should: so disabling an account does not end what it set up, and the security model no longer says it does. `principal.disable` now counts what the account left, as much as the caller may list, and warns; `keys`, `principals` and `members` gained `added_by` and `created_by` filters over columns the control store already had (`trail.go`). A server admin sees all of it, a project admin the keys and principals of their projects' members and their projects' memberships. Nothing is removed automatically. **Found while fixing:** decision 19 refused removal as well as adding, so a server admin could not remove a key planted on their own account, and with one server admin left only the operator could. **Decision 38 changed that the same day:** `Control.RemoveKey` allows the key's own principal, of any kind, before it asks about reach, and tells anyone else what it told them before. The daemon adds the guard (`keyRemove`): a request that names the fingerprint its own connection came in by is refused with `invalid`, naming the fingerprint, since the handshake checked that fingerprint against the store. A connection that came in by `authorized_keys` names none, so the daemon cannot tell which key it used; the removal goes ahead and the result warns that it could not check, which loses nothing, because sshd reads the generated file first (decision 26) and so that connection's key is not a store key that was offered. There is no guard for the last store key of the last server admin: the remover keeps the way in they are using, and what the store holds does not say whether an admin can sign in, as with decision 20's rule. The refusal for adding one's own now reads "nobody adds a key to their own account".
- **`control init`, run again (F-7).** It asks the control log before it adds (`Control.LastEvent`, one indexed read for each key and each principal): a key whose newest event is `key.remove`, a membership of the adopted project whose newest event is `member.remove`, and a principal that is disabled are left as they are and reported with the line number. What an admin added again is in the store and is found there. `--restore-removed-access` puts all three back, as events of the operator's. The forced command of a line is now parsed by the code that parses `ariesd stdio`'s arguments, so the two cannot disagree on a line with `--principal` twice.
- **Before the hello (F-4).** The bridge frame names the principal, so from there a connection is somebody's. A server of many projects refuses at once a principal that is disabled, or that has no row and is no configured admin (`Control.Standing`); the health probe and a server of one project are not affected. Any server counts a principal's connections between bridge frame and hello and refuses past `handshakes_per_principal` (16). The hello's deadline is 10 seconds, not 30: the client gives its whole connection 15, and the hello trails the bridge frame by one round trip (the arithmetic is on `helloTimeout`). At twice `conns` sockets, the one that has waited longest without a hello is closed, not the one that just arrived. Membership and the key's standing are still decided at the hello.
- **Smaller ones.** A principal who is no server admin may create at most `principals_per_creator` principals (500), since none is ever deleted (F-8). For a caller who is no server admin, `key add`, `principal disable` and `principal enable` refuse a name that does not exist as they refuse one out of reach, and `key add` refuses a key that some principal has the same way whoever has it (F-6). That a key is on the server can still be inferred by someone who may add keys, since a new key is added and a held one is not. ariesd warns at start of a configured admin whose row or key was set up by someone who is no server admin.
- **Left as it is.** The last-admin rule (decision 20) counts every name under `admins:`, with or without a key: ariesd does not judge `authorized_keys`, where an admin's only key may be, so the guide now says to name only admins who can sign in. Removing a principal is not built: a principal's name is written into every project's event log and into `added_by` and `created_by` of the control store, so a name freed for reuse would let a new person inherit an old one's history; a safe removal needs either names that are never reused (a tombstone that keeps the name taken, which frees nothing of the cap) or the opaque handle of §15.6, 2.
- **F-5 and F-9** were fixed on `main` in pull request 67, with the change to the order sshd reads the two files in (decisions 24 and 26). Merged with this work, `AddKey` decides who the actor is and whether the principal is within reach first, then looks for the key in the store and in the hand-maintained file inside the same write; a project admin gets one refusal for either, so F-5's check reveals nothing F-6 hid. The review's two remaining tests were removed as duplicates of `main`'s. Checked after the merge, 12 Oct 2026: the gate passes, the Docker smoke test passes with 116 checks (97 on `main`, 102 on this branch before the merge), five short soaks and one of five minutes with `-race` (seed 9728677840777988629: 39,427 requests, 376 control events, 42 members added only once their keys were shown and accepted) held every invariant.

**As built (the client's 15 seconds, from the key's acceptance to the welcome, 13 Oct 2026).** The 15 seconds the client gives a connection (`client.Options.Timeout`, and the caller's context) cover the TCP connection or IAP tunnel, the SSH handshake, the opening of the session, the request to run the bridge, the hello and the welcome. They end each of those the same way, by closing the transport, which for IAP kills and reaps `gcloud`; no step has a deadline of its own. A connection that runs out of time after the key was accepted is now refused as `unavailable` with the step it waited for (`timed out after 15s waiting for … to open an ssh session`, `to start ariesd`, `to answer the hello`), where it had said the server closed the connection, or blamed the `authorized_keys` line. One wait had no end at all: `golang.org/x/crypto/ssh` waits forever for the answer to a channel it opened just after the connection ended, whatever is closed afterward ([golang/go#82009](https://github.com/golang/go/issues/82009), open; the code is the same at v0.55.0, v0.57.0 and v0.58.0), and a test in CI waited ten minutes on it. The client now wraps the transport (`client.transport`): it opens no session once a read has failed, and keeps a failed read from the SSH package while the session is being opened until the request to open it is written, by when the package closes that channel with the rest; and it waits for the opening on a goroutine it can stop waiting for. The wrapper sees a connection end as a transport ends (closed, reset, cut, or the client's own timeout), not a server's SSH disconnect message: there the fault's window of a few instructions is still open, the client then gives up at its 15 seconds as for any quiet server, and the package's goroutine stays, holding memory and no connection, until the package is fixed. **Left as it is:** a request on an established connection has no deadline of the client's own. It ends when the caller's context does, when the server answers (its request timeout is a minute) or when the connection is seen to be lost, which for a TCP path that went silent is the operating system's keep-alive, some minutes; `aries mcp` holds its one connection for that long, renewals included. **Closed, 14 Oct 2026:** `Conn.Call` now gives each request 75 seconds from sending it (`client.RequestTimeout`): the server's request timeout, a minute, which is not a setting and after which the server answers with a refusal of its own, and 15 seconds for that answer to cross the network, so a slow server that works is never cut off first. The welcome does not carry the server's timeout, and needs not while it is fixed; no protocol change. When the time passes the client closes the connection, which ends a write that was waiting as well as the wait for the answer, and the request fails `unavailable`, naming the op and the wait (`the server did not answer show within 1m15s, so aries closed the connection`), with a fix to retry and what the admin checks; the `Conn` is broken from then on, since an unanswered request leaves the stream unusable. Being `unavailable`, it is what `client.Unreachable` counts as not reached, as a lost connection is: the server may have applied it, a queueable write is queued, and `aries mcp`'s link treats it as a drop, closing the connection, retrying a read or a write safe to repeat once on a new one, and letting the renewal that waited behind it through. Only the control writes (`project.add`, `.archive`, `.activate`, `principal.add`, `.disable`, `.enable`, `member.set`, `.remove`, `key.add`, `.remove`) are waited on for as long as the caller's context allows: the server answers them after a read of the control store's log for the audit stream, which has a timeout of its own behind a lock the reaper takes too, and an archive also opens the project's store to end its claims; only `aries admin` sends them, for a person who can interrupt. A test in `internal/server` holds that list to the server's `controlWrites`. No other op waits on the server longer: `watch` is answered at once and its pushes follow outside any request, and import, export and upgrade are not requests on a `Conn`. `Conn.Close` also had no bound: it ends the session with two writes before it closes the connection, and those wait behind a write already waiting on a server that takes nothing; it now closes the connection under them after a second.

### 15.5 Open questions

1. **Creating a database.** Settled (decision 16). Measured 11 Oct 2026 at Dolt 2.4.2 ([spike/multidb](https://github.com/ariesworx/aries/blob/fbbbf4655665845db81a72d2bbd1845472fd1c5a/spike/multidb/RESULTS.md)), which corrects the test of 9 Oct: ariesd's account, which holds nothing on `*.*` (§7, S-2), can `CREATE DATABASE`, because Dolt checks that statement against the connection's current database. It can use the new database only if root granted it that exact name, which root may do before the database exists; a grant on a name pattern does nothing. So at setup the operator grants ariesd's account a block of database names (`aries_p001` to `aries_p100`, say), and `aries admin project add` creates the next free one from anywhere, with no shell on the server. A server that runs out is granted more, and the refusal says so. Creating is safe to repeat: the project's row is written first, then `CREATE DATABASE IF NOT EXISTS`, then the migrations, then the row is marked active, so a run that failed halfway is run again.
2. **Memory.** Measured the same day. Dolt's memory follows the data written across all databases, not their number: an open or existing database adds nothing measurable, and one database given five projects' writes costs what five databases do. A project of 300 issues cost about 100 MiB, or 50 MiB with Dolt under a Go memory limit, at four times the write latency when the limit is too low. Closing stores returns none of it, so the idle close of §15.1 saves connections only. Still to measure, on Linux: what a restart gives back, a memory limit set with room to spare, and projects written to at the same time. `aries admin status` (§15.4) shows an admin ariesd's side of it, the stores, connections and writes; Dolt's memory itself is read from the machine.

   **Measured 12 Oct 2026, on Linux** ([results](https://github.com/ariesworx/aries/blob/fbbbf4655665845db81a72d2bbd1845472fd1c5a/spike/multidb/RESULTS.md), sections 3 to 8): arm64, in a container on a laptop that was doing other work, with synthetic data of one shape. It answers the three questions above and corrects the first sentence.
   - *Linux agrees with macOS* to within 8% on the same loads.
   - *The memory is what Dolt accumulates while it writes, since it started.* A restart on the same data gave back 58 to 86% at rest, and a half to three quarters once the projects were open, read and written to again. Dolt's own `dolt gc` and then a restart left it at 110 to 200 MiB at rest for every load measured, up to one project of 30,000 issues.
   - *The number of projects written to does matter.* 15,000 issues cost 1.6 GiB in one project and 5.1 GiB in fifty. One project cost 1.1 GiB at 3,000 issues and 2.1 GiB at 30,000; each further project of 300 issues added 95 to 180 MiB.
   - *A memory limit has a cliff.* `GOMEMLIMIT` at 0.6 of `go_memstats_next_gc_bytes`, read from a Dolt running without a limit, cost at most twice Dolt's CPU in three loads; at 0.5 it cost up to 25 times, and lower up to 100 times, with Dolt still serving and still over the limit. What Dolt holds grows as it writes, so a limit with room today is at the cliff later.
   - *Projects written to at the same time* slow each other a little: a write's median was 2.0 ms alone, 2.9 ms among 5 writers, 4.3 ms among 10 and 7.1 ms among 20, with none starved. Throughput stopped growing between 5 and 10 writers, at 2,400 to 2,800 writes a second. These timings are one quiet run's; on the shared machine others varied fivefold.
   - *Still not measured:* another machine, traffic at a real server's few writes a second, `dolt gc` and a restart of Dolt under a serving ariesd, and what sets off Dolt's own garbage collection, which once held a write for 18.7 seconds.
   - *For the design:* a restart of Dolt, and `dolt gc` before it, are the operator's way to get memory back, so they belong in the server guide's routine. Whether ariesd should run them or say when they are due is open.
3. **Moving an existing server.** Its database becomes the first project's, unchanged; the control database is created beside it; the config file's `project:` names the project to adopt.
4. **A principal's own keys.** Settled (decision 19): nobody adds or removes a key of their own account, since an agent holding the developer's key could add one of its own. A new laptop's key is added by another admin.
5. **Stage 6's cross-project edges** become reads across databases, checked against the reader's memberships.

### 15.6 Identity beyond one key at a time (investigation, 12 Oct 2026)

Not designed and not decided: what was looked at, what was tried, and what would have to be chosen. **Since designed in §17,** which tried what this section lists as not tried and puts the choices as questions (§17.12). This section is left as the investigation it was; where §17 measured something differently, §17 is right. In particular, "forgetting a person is then deleting one row" below is not true of a Dolt table as it stands, since Dolt keeps the deleted row (§17.1, §17.7).

**The concern.** A person is a principal, and gets in by a public key that an admin adds for each of their machines (decision 19 keeps people from adding their own). That is right for a team. In a company of hundreds of developers, each with several machines and agents, it means: every joiner, leaver and new laptop is an admin's manual step; a key never expires; nothing ties Aries to the company's single sign-on, its second factor or its groups; and a leaver keeps access until someone remembers. The name of a principal is also the name everywhere: in every event of every project's log, which Dolt keeps forever, in claims, in `who`, and now in telemetry.

**SSH certificates fit what is built, and were tried.** Many companies already issue short-lived SSH certificates from a single sign-on login, through a certificate authority (several open-source and commercial ones exist, and cloud providers have their own). sshd can admit an account by certificate, and map each certificate principal to a forced command, from a file: `TrustedUserCAKeys` and `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile`, whose lines take the same options as `authorized_keys`. That file has the shape of the keys file ariesd already writes (decision 22). Tried on 12 Oct 2026 with OpenSSH 9.6p1 on Ubuntu 24.04, in the test server's image, with a throwaway authority:

| Offered | Result |
|---|---|
| A certificate for `alice@example.com`, valid five minutes, with a line for that principal in the file | Admitted, and the line's forced command ran, not the command asked for |
| A valid certificate from the same authority for a principal with no line | Refused: `Certificate does not contain an authorized principal` |
| An expired certificate for `alice@example.com` | Refused: `Certificate invalid: expired` |
| alice's bare key, with no certificate | Refused |

So Aries could hold, for a principal, the identity the authority puts in its certificates, write a principals file beside or in place of the keys file, and hold no key at all for such a person. Joining is one `aries admin` command, or none if membership follows a group; a new laptop is nothing, since the person signs in again; leaving is the authority no longer issuing, within hours, and `principal disable` at once. Expiry and the second factor are the authority's.

Not tried, and needed before a design: that `aries`'s in-process SSH client offers a certificate (it takes its signers from ssh-agent, which can hold certificates; a `key:` file would need its `-cert.pub` read beside it); a real authority; what replaces the key's fingerprint on a connection (the certificate's serial or key id), so that revoking and dropping still work; and groups in a certificate mapped to memberships.

**Other routes.** Provisioning principals and memberships from a directory (SCIM, or a job that reads the directory and runs `aries admin`) is complementary: it answers who is a member, not how they prove who they are. Signing in to Aries itself with OpenID Connect needs an HTTPS transport, which decision 9 defers.

**Hiding who a person is.** Three depths, each a different amount of work:

1. **In telemetry only:** the stand-ins of §16.4. Designed there.
2. **A name that is not a person's name.** Nothing stops an operator naming principals `u-7f3a2c` today, but then every list shows only that. A display name and an external identity (the single sign-on subject) on the principal's row in the control store, shown by `aries` to those who may list principals, would let the name in every project's log be an opaque handle while people still see who is who. Forgetting a person is then deleting one row. This is a small change to the control store and the clients, and none to a project's database.
3. **Renaming, or erasing a name already written.** A project's event log is its history and Dolt keeps it; a principal's name is in it as text. Changing that means an id in place of the name throughout a project's schema, and a migration of every log. Large, and only worth it if 2 does not satisfy a legal requirement.

**What would have to be chosen:** whether certificates become a second way in beside keys or the way for servers above some size; whether membership may follow a group; and which of the depths above is wanted. Until then, keys and names work as §15.2 and §15.3 describe.

## 16. Observability

Designed 12 Oct 2026. Slices O1, O2 and O3 of §16.8 are built, and the **As built** notes at the end of this section say how; so are slice O4, whose note is at the end of §16.5, and slice O5, the harness, whose note is at the end of §16.7. When this was written ariesd had structured logs (text or JSON) on stderr, `aries admin status`, and nothing else: no metrics a monitor can collect, no traces, no health check, and no way to read status on the server without an admin's SSH key.

**Goal.** An operator can wire ariesd, and the Dolt behind it, into whatever they already run, on any cloud or none, by configuration alone. Telemetry must never slow, block or fail a request.

### 16.1 Principle

ariesd speaks open formats on the machine it runs on, and **a collector on the same machine is the vendor adapter**. The OpenTelemetry Collector, and every vendor's and cloud's own agent, read these formats and forward to their backend. Nothing in ariesd names a vendor, and ariesd sends nothing anywhere unless it is configured to.

| Signal | From ariesd | From Dolt |
|---|---|---|
| Metrics | OTLP push to a collector, and the same instruments on a Prometheus endpoint for pull | Its own Prometheus endpoint (§16.5) |
| Traces | OTLP push | None: Dolt has no tracing |
| Logs | JSON lines on stderr, to the journal, as now, with a stable schema; OTLP push as an option | JSON lines, to the journal |
| Health | `/healthz` and `/readyz` on the loopback listener; systemd readiness and watchdog | The unit's state; its metrics endpoint answering |

Both pull and push are offered because operators differ: a Prometheus shop scrapes and runs no collector, an OTLP shop does not scrape, and Dolt can only be scraped, so most servers will have something that scrapes anyway.

### 16.2 The SDK, and what it costs

Measured 12 Oct 2026 with the release build flags (`CGO_ENABLED=0 -trimpath -ldflags "-s -w"`), in a program of a few lines, against a baseline of `log/slog` and `net/http`:

| Linked | Binary | Third-party modules |
|---|---|---|
| Baseline | 6.3 MB | 0 |
| SDK, traces and metrics, OTLP over HTTP (`otel` v1.47.0) | +9.3 MB | 24, among them `google.golang.org/grpc` and `google.golang.org/protobuf` |
| The above, the SDK's Prometheus exporter, OTLP logs and the `slog` bridge | +10.6 MB | 34, adding `prometheus/client_golang` and its own |

ariesd is 10.1 MB today with about 11 modules, so it roughly doubles. That is against rule 4's grain, and decision 28 accepts it for ariesd. To keep the cost where it was accepted:

- **`aries` links none of it.** The client is on every developer's machine and inside every agent's harness. It passes trace context on (decision 27) and starts no span. A test fails if `cmd/aries`, or any package it imports, imports `go.opentelemetry.io`.
- **One package owns it.** `internal/telemetry` is the only importer of the SDK and exporters. The server, the store and the commands see a small interface of Aries's own (start a span, record a measurement), so the SDK can be replaced, or compiled to nothing for a test, in one place.
- **OTLP over HTTP only.** No gRPC exporter: it would add more of gRPC than the HTTP exporter already drags in.
- **Each module added is named, with its reason, in the pull request** that adds it (rule 4), and the weekly `govulncheck` covers them.
- **The logs exporter is experimental** upstream (the Logs API and SDK reached release candidate in 2026; the exporters are outside that). So OTLP logs are an option, off by default, and the journal stays the logs' home.

### 16.3 Configuration

- **Off by default.** With nothing configured ariesd exports nothing, listens on nothing new and behaves as now.
- **The standard `OTEL_*` environment variables** configure export, as for any OpenTelemetry program, so an operator's existing knowledge and tooling apply: `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (a collector on the machine, `http://127.0.0.1:4318`, is the documented setup), `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` (default `ariesd`), `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES`, `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`, `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED`. The resource carries `service.name`, `service.version` and `service.instance.id`.
- **`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` can hold a token** for a hosted backend. It is a secret (rule 2): it comes from the unit's `EnvironmentFile`, mode 0600, never from argv, and ariesd never logs it. Sending to a collector on the machine needs none, which is one more reason to prefer that.
- **`telemetry:` in ariesd's settings** holds what is Aries's own: `listen` (the loopback listener, below), `per_project` (on by default), `principals` (how a person appears, §16.4), `audit_log` (on by default), `dolt_metrics_url` (§16.5).
- **The listener** (decision 29): `telemetry.listen: 127.0.0.1:9464`, say. An address that is not loopback is refused at start with a fix. It serves `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz` and nothing else, with a cap on requests in flight and a deadline on each (rule 18). It has no authentication: any user on the machine can read it, and it carries project ids, names and counts, and no secret, principal name or issue text. A failure to bind at start fails the start, with a fix, as a keys file that cannot be written does.

### 16.4 What is measured

**Metrics.** Named in OpenTelemetry's style; on the Prometheus endpoint the exporter writes them with underscores and unit suffixes.

| Instrument | Kind | Attributes |
|---|---|---|
| `aries.request.duration` | histogram, seconds | `op`, `result` |
| `aries.project.requests` | counter | `project`, `result_class` (ok, refused, error) |
| `aries.connections` | up-down counter | `project` |
| `aries.handshakes` | counter | `result` (the refusal's code, or ok) |
| `aries.connections.dropped` | counter | `reason` (membership, disabled, key, archived, idle, limit) |
| `aries.limit.refusals` | counter | `limit` |
| `aries.store.write.duration` | histogram, seconds | `project` |
| `aries.store.write.retries` | counter | `project` |
| `aries.store.commit.duration` | histogram, seconds | none |
| `aries.stores.open` | gauge | none |
| `aries.store.open.duration` | histogram, seconds | `migrated` |
| `aries.claims` | counter | `event` (take, renew, release, expire), `reason` |
| `aries.control.writes` | counter | `op`, `result` |
| `aries.keys_file.writes` | counter | `result` |
| `aries.keys_file.last_write.time` | gauge, unix seconds | none |
| `aries.reaper.duration`, `aries.reaper.last_run.time` | histogram, gauge | none |
| `aries.telemetry.dropped` | counter | `signal` |
| Go runtime and process | from `runtime/metrics` | none |

- **Bounded (rule 18).** An attribute's values come from a fixed list in the code: ops, codes, reasons, limits. An op the server does not know is `unknown`. A principal, session, machine, issue id or anything else a client chooses is never an attribute of a metric; those are for traces and logs.
- **`project` is on few instruments, and never beside `op`.** 500 projects by 60 ops by 10 results by a histogram's buckets would be hundreds of thousands of series. So one instrument has `op` and `result`, and separate, coarser ones have `project`. `telemetry.per_project: false` drops the attribute everywhere, for a server near the project cap.
- **Histograms have a short fixed list of buckets,** 5 ms to 10 s, the same everywhere.

**Traces.**
- One server span for each request, named for its op, with the op, result code, project and protocol version, and the principal and session as `telemetry.principals` allows (below). A trace may carry them; a metric never does. Its parent is the client's trace context when the request carries a valid one (decision 27).
- Child spans where time goes: the wait for the writer, each attempt of a write (a retry is a new one), the Dolt commit, a store's open and migration.
- A span for a handshake, and root spans for the reaper, the sweep, a keys file write and a control index reload, so background work is visible.
- No span carries an issue's title or body, a memory's text, a comment, a key or a DSN. Ids only.
- Sampling is the SDK's, by `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER`; the default follows the parent and otherwise samples everything, since a tracker's request volume is small.
- `aries` reads `TRACEPARENT` and `TRACESTATE` from its environment and sends them, so a trace begun by an agent's harness continues into Aries. Per-call context from an MCP request's metadata is part of this stage.

**Logs.**
- The JSON schema becomes a documented contract: `time`, `level`, `msg`, and where they apply `event`, `project`, `principal`, `session`, `op`, `code`, `dur_ms`, `req`, `trace_id`, `span_id`.
- **`event` is a stable name** for anything worth an alert (`keys_file.write_failed`, `project.open_failed`, `conn.cap_refused`), so an alert matches a name, not a sentence that may be reworded.
- **`req`** is an id for each request, on every line about it and in the refusal the client sees, so a person's report finds the server's lines.
- **Audit stream** (decision 30): each event of the control store's log (a project added or archived, a principal added, disabled or enabled, a key added or removed, a membership set or removed) is also written as a log line with `audit=true`, the actor, the op and the target, after the write commits. Fingerprints, never keys. The event log stays the record; the line is a copy for a SIEM that reads the journal.
- With `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` the same records also go out over OTLP, through the `slog` bridge. Stderr is never turned off.

**People in telemetry** (decision 32). `telemetry.principals` is one of:

| Value | A span or an exported log record carries |
|---|---|
| `pseudonym` (default) | A stable stand-in, `p_` and 16 hex characters: an HMAC-SHA-256 of the principal's name under a key that ariesd generates and keeps in a 0600 file. The same person is the same stand-in in every trace and line, so their requests can still be followed and counted; the backend never learns who. `aries admin principal whois p_…`, for a server admin, gives the name. Deleting the key file makes every earlier stand-in unlinkable to anyone, which is what a retention rule may want. |
| `name` | The principal's name |
| `omit` | Nothing about who |

- It governs what leaves the machine through telemetry: spans, and log records exported over OTLP. Sessions are stand-ins or omitted with the principal, since a session id can name a person's machine.
- **It does not change the journal or the audit stream.** Those are the server's own records, an access change with no actor is not an audit record, and whoever can read the journal can read the databases. An operator who ships the journal to a third party filters it in the collector, and the guide shows how.
- A stand-in hides a name from the backend. It does not make a person anonymous: what they did, when and in which project is still there. The guide says so.

**The Go runtime's metrics** (decision 33). Measured 12 Oct 2026 on an Apple M4 Max with Go 1.27.1: reading the fifteen an operator wants (heap, GC, goroutines, scheduler latency, CPU, the memory limit) from the standard library's `runtime/metrics` takes 0.45 µs and allocates nothing; reading every metric the runtime has takes 1.6 µs. Done once for each export or scrape, that is nothing. The older `runtime.ReadMemStats` takes 22 µs and stops the world, and is not used. So the cost of the standard library is about sixty lines of code and names of our own choosing. The contrib module that does the same reading (`go.opentelemetry.io/contrib/instrumentation/runtime`) adds 86 KB and one module, and gives the names of OpenTelemetry's semantic conventions, which ready-made dashboards expect. ariesd uses the module, for the names; it is below version 1, so if it breaks, the sixty lines replace it.

**Health.**
- `/healthz` answers while the process serves. `/readyz` answers only when the daemon is listening, its last check of Dolt (a cheap query, cached a few seconds, never run per request) passed, and the keys file's last write did not fail; otherwise it says which, in one line.
- systemd: `Type=notify` with readiness sent once the daemon listens, and a watchdog fed by the reaper tick, so a daemon that hangs is restarted. On other systems neither is sent.
- `ariesd status [--json]`, run on the server as the daemon's user over its socket, prints what `aries admin status` prints, for a script or an agent that has no admin key.

### 16.5 Dolt

Measured 12 Oct 2026 at Dolt 2.4.2. A `metrics:` block (`host`, `port`, `labels`) in Dolt's config turns on a Prometheus endpoint at `/metrics`, with no authentication, so it is bound to loopback:

| Group | Metrics |
|---|---|
| Server | `dss_concurrent_connections`, `dss_concurrent_queries`, `dss_connects`, `dss_disconnects`, `dss_query_duration` (histogram; buckets 0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100, 1000 s), `dss_dolt_version` |
| Process | `process_resident_memory_bytes`, `process_cpu_seconds_total`, `process_open_fds`, `process_max_fds`, `process_start_time_seconds` |
| Go runtime | `go_memstats_*`, `go_gc_*` (among them `go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes`), `go_goroutines` |
| System | `sys_cpu_usage`, `sys_mem_usage`, `sys_disk_usage` |

- **Nothing is per database.** Dolt cannot tell one project from another; ariesd's own per-project instruments (§16.4) are the only such view.
- **No tracing,** in its flags, its config or its documentation. A trace ends at ariesd's span around the SQL it ran.
- **Logs** are text or JSON (`log_format`), to the journal.
- Over SQL, ariesd's least-privileged account can read `SHOW STATUS`, the process list and a table's estimated rows and size. None of it is needed for what follows.

What Aries does with it:
- **The server guide and the Docker image turn Dolt's endpoint on,** on loopback, and the collector configuration scrapes it beside ariesd's. Dolt's metrics reach the backend under Dolt's own names; ariesd does not re-export them, which would count them twice.
- **`aries admin status` shows Dolt's memory, connections and uptime** (decision 30), closing the gap §15.4 left. With `telemetry.dolt_metrics_url` set (loopback only), ariesd reads that endpoint on a timer, with a deadline and a size limit, parses the handful of lines it needs with a parser of its own, and shows when it last succeeded. A failure shows as "unknown", never as an error of ariesd's.
- **Dolt's memory limit.** `go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes` beside `process_resident_memory_bytes` is what tells an operator whether the `GOMEMLIMIT` of §15.5, 2 is set with room to spare. The alert rules include it. *Measured 12 Oct 2026 (§15.5, 2):* that pair separates a limit that is too low only roughly (resident memory was up to 4% over the limit where it cost little, and from 4.8% over where it cost nine times the CPU or more). The collector's cycles (`go_gc_duration_seconds_count`) against ariesd's writes separate it cleanly: under 3 for each 100 writes without trouble, more than 14 past the cliff.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O4: Dolt's own figures, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O4 of §16.8 is built: Dolt's endpoint on in the guide and the Docker image, `telemetry.dolt_metrics_url`, and Dolt's figures in `aries admin status` and `ariesd status`. The collector configuration that scrapes Dolt and the alert rules are slice O5's and are not. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **The port is 11228**, which the guide chose: Dolt has no default (`port: -1` is off), 11228 is free on stock Ubuntu 24.04, below the range the kernel gives outgoing connections, and is neither Dolt's 3306, the 9464 the guide gives ariesd's listener, the 4318 of OTLP, nor one of the 909x ports a Prometheus installation uses. A test holds the guide, the image and the refusals' example to it.
- **"No authentication" is how the guide configures it, not all Dolt can do.** Dolt 2.4.2's `metrics:` block also takes `tls_cert`, `tls_key`, `tls_ca` and `jwt_required_for_localhost`, and its log says `auth_enabled = false` at start. None of that was tried. ariesd reads plain HTTP on loopback and refuses anything else, so a server that turned those on would show Dolt as unknown.
- **The URL** must be `http://` with a host, a port, a path beyond `/`, and no user, query or fragment: it is logged whole, so it has no place for a secret. An IP address that is not loopback is refused when the settings are read, by every command; a name is resolved when the daemon starts and refused unless every address is loopback. Each connection is then checked again against the address it reaches (the dialer's `Control`), so a name that changes later leads nowhere else. `ARIESD_TELEMETRY_DOLT_METRICS_URL` sets it too. `telemetry.CheckAddr` was not reused: its refusals name `listen:`.
- **The reader** is `internal/doltmetrics`, on the standard library alone. One GET every 15 seconds from a goroutine `Serve` owns, started at once and stopped with it; 5 seconds for the whole read; no redirect followed, no proxy, no compression asked for, no connection kept. The parser reads at most 1 MiB, passes over a line longer than 4,096 bytes, and looks only at six series by name; a series given more than once is believed in neither line, and a value that is not a finite number in the figure's range is left out. A body with none of the six is a failed read. It has a fuzz target. Dolt 2.4.2 serves 10 KiB in lines of at most 250 bytes.
- **The figures, and why each:** the version (`dss_dolt_version`, which packs major, minor and patch into one number), uptime (`process_start_time_seconds`), connections (`dss_concurrent_connections`), resident memory (`process_resident_memory_bytes`), the memory limit (`go_gc_gomemlimit_bytes`, which is the largest int64 with none set) and the collector's cycles (`go_gc_duration_seconds_count`). Operator labels (`labels:`) attach to the `dss_` series only, and are read past. **Left out:** query latency, since `dss_query_duration`'s buckets start at 10 ms and step by tens, which says less than the report's own P50 and P99 of each project's writes; `go_memstats_next_gc_bytes`, which the measurements found did not separate a good limit from a bad one; and Dolt's CPU, which a collector has from Dolt directly.
- **Cycles for each 100 writes** is the one figure that exists nowhere else, so ariesd derives it: the difference in Dolt's cycle count against the difference in its own count of writes, between the latest read and the latest one at least ten minutes older, or the oldest it has. The writes are the project stores' (`Store.Writes`, counted as `WRITES/MIN` is), summed at each read over the stores then open; a store that closes between two reads takes with it what it wrote since the earlier one, and the control database's writes are not counted. A change of Dolt's start time, or a cycle count that went back, starts the window again. The ratio is printed from 500 writes in the window, because an idle Dolt still collects about every two minutes (measured: 3 cycles in the first 30 seconds, then one every two minutes).
- **The verdict** is the client's (`internal/statustext`), from the measurements' two numbers: under 3 with room, over 14 too low, and between them said to be between. It is given only when a limit is set. One rule was added after the run on the virtual machine below: with fewer than 500 writes, cycles past 14 for each 100 of 500 writes (more than 70 in the window) are judged too low all the same, because a Dolt under a limit of under half its resident memory made 500 cycles in 15 seconds with nothing written, and "too few writes to judge" would have been false. The status line and the guide say the thresholds come from one machine and one shape of load.
- **Unknown.** A read that fails for any reason leaves the report saying `Dolt: unknown` with the time of the last read that worked, and no figure: stale memory figures were judged worse than none. A read that works but lacks a series shows that figure alone as unknown. A failure is logged at warn when it starts, every ten minutes while it lasts with the count, and at info when it ends; it changes neither `/readyz`, which keeps its own ping of Dolt, nor any request.
- **No instrument was added** for the derived figure. `Telemetry.Watch` has no way to say "unknown", so a gauge would hold a last value through a failure, or a zero; and wherever both ariesd and Dolt are scraped, which is the documented setup, the backend can divide `go_gc_duration_seconds_count` by the sum of `aries_store_write_duration_seconds_count` itself. A server that pushes straight to a hosted backend with no collector has nothing of Dolt there, and has the status report.
- **Result fields only.** `ServerStatus.dolt` joins protocol 5, which no release speaks; an older client ignores it.
- **Found on the way, and left:** while a project's store is open, the status report makes one query for that store's sessions, so with Dolt frozen whole (`SIGSTOP`) `ariesd status` waited and gave up after its 30 seconds instead of saying `Dolt: unknown`. `/readyz` said `the last check of Dolt failed`. Bounding that query is not this slice's.
- **Run on the virtual machine of §15's notes** (Ubuntu 24.04.5 on arm64, the three AppArmor profiles enforced), set up by the guide's own block for a server set up before. The endpoint answered on 127.0.0.1 and was refused on the machine's other address. The report's memory was within a mebibyte of `VmRSS`, its start time `ps`'s, its connections the two `ss` showed ariesd holding. Over a burst the collector line gave the cycles and writes that Dolt's counter and `aries_store_write_duration_seconds_count` gave: 4 cycles for 342 writes with no limit. Under `GOMEMLIMIT=2GiB`: 7 cycles for 562 writes, 1.2 for each 100, 4.2 ms of Dolt's CPU a write. Under `GOMEMLIMIT=120MiB`, with Dolt at 265 MiB at rest: 3,441 for 562, 612 for each 100, 55 ms a write, and a median write of 20 ms against 5 to 8. With the port refused for 50 seconds: `Dolt: unknown` with the last read, `/readyz` 200, one warning, one line when it ended. A second `telemetry:` key in the config file stops the daemon (`mapping key "telemetry" already defined`), which the guide warns of. With `host: 0.0.0.0` Dolt listened on every address; Dolt's unit (`IPAddressAllow=localhost`) left a request to the other address, made on the machine, unanswered. ariesd's unit held to loopback the same way still read. No rule was added to an AppArmor profile, and the kernel log had no denial. The 76 checks passed, after their script was changed for decisions 35, 36 and 38.
- **Not run:** amd64; a real collector scraping Dolt beside ariesd; a request to Dolt's metrics from another machine; Dolt's TLS and token settings; the thresholds against real traffic or days of uptime, which is the measurements' own gap.

### 16.6 Robustness

- **A request never waits for telemetry.** Recording a measurement or ending a span is memory only. Export happens on the SDK's own goroutines, in batches, with bounded queues and a timeout; a full queue drops, and `aries.telemetry.dropped` counts it.
- **A collector that is down, slow or refusing changes nothing** but the drop counter and one sampled warning. A test points the exporter at a closed port and at a server that never answers, runs the soak's mix of work, and checks latency, goroutines and memory against a run with telemetry off.
- **Export errors go to ariesd's log, sampled,** through the SDK's error handler, never to stderr unformatted and never once a second forever.
- **Stopping flushes with a deadline,** so a stop is not held up by a dead collector.
- **Cardinality is bounded by construction** (§16.4), and a test fails if an instrument gains an attribute that is not on the list.

### 16.7 The harness

- **`deploy/observability/`**: an OpenTelemetry Collector configuration (OTLP in; Prometheus scrapes of ariesd and Dolt; out to whatever the operator names), alert rules in Prometheus's rule format, which most systems read or translate, and one dashboard. They are examples that are tested, not a product.
- **A Compose profile** beside the Docker test server runs a Collector, pinned by digest, wired to ariesd and Dolt and writing what it receives to a file. The smoke test, asked for it, then checks that each expected metric arrived from both, that a trace arrived whose root is the trace id the test put in `TRACEPARENT` with the server's spans beneath it, that an access change produced its audit line, and that `/readyz` turns unready when Dolt is stopped. A second profile adds a metrics store, a trace store and the dashboard, for a person to look at.
- **The soak checks the numbers.** It already replays every project's event log. At the end it reads the instruments in process and asserts that they say what the log says: requests by result, claims taken, released by reason and expired, connections dropped by reason, control writes. A metric that is present and wrong fails the gate.
- **Alerts shipped:** ariesd not ready; a keys file write failing; an active project that cannot be opened; the reaper not having run; refusals at a connection or store cap; write latency; Dolt's memory against its limit and against the machine's; disk.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O5: the harness, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O5 of §16.8 is built, first for metrics and traces and then, once slice O3 had merged, for logs (the last items of this note): the files of `deploy/observability`, two Compose profiles beside the Docker test server, the smoke test's checks of them, and the soak with telemetry on. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **The Collector prints what it receives; it writes no file.** Its image holds one program and no shell, runs as a user of its own, and a volume for a file would have had to be made writable for that user and read back through another container. The `debug` exporter at `detailed` writes every metric and span to the container's output, which the smoke test reads with `docker compose logs` and which is capped at 40 MB. Nothing is left on a volume.
- **Both profiles run in the server's network namespace** (`network_mode: service:aries`), as §16.7 implies and decision 29 requires: the listener, Dolt's endpoint and the OTLP receiver are all on loopback. Three things follow. The Collector starts when the server's container has started, not when it is healthy, so that it listens before ariesd, which starts after Dolt, has anything to send: started the other way round, the first spans were dropped and their alert fired at once. A restart of the server's container replaces the namespace, so the two beside it must be restarted after it, and `docker compose restart` of all three fails for them; the smoke test and the guide do it in two steps. And neither profile can publish a port, since the namespace's ports are the server's service's: Prometheus has no page a person can open, and is asked with `promtool` from inside.
- **The second profile is a Prometheus, and no more.** §16.7 has a metrics store, a trace store and the dashboard, for a person to look at. A trace store and a dashboard server would be two more images and two published ports that the shared namespace does not allow without changing the default stack. What the Prometheus is for is the rules: it loads `alerts.yaml`, evaluates every rule against a real ariesd and Dolt, and runs every query of the dashboard.
- **One switch for ariesd, one for each thing beside it.** `ARIES_TELEMETRY=1` in the container's environment has the entrypoint name the listener and the stand-ins' key in the config file and give ariesd `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`; the profiles add the Collector and the Prometheus. They are separate so that the first without the second is a server whose collector is down, which is a case worth having. Unset, the config file and ariesd's environment are as they were, and a volume that had it on has it taken out at the next start; the key stays. The test server exports metrics every 5 seconds and holds a span for at most one, which the entrypoint passes as arguments of `env`: three fixed values, none a secret (rule 2).
- **The Collector's file receives ariesd's metrics twice,** pushed and scraped, because the harness checks both paths; the file and the guide say to keep one on a real server. It names no vendor: the example of a backend is `otlp_http` to `https://otlp.example.com`, with its token from the Collector's environment, and that variant passes the Collector's own `validate`. Components are the core distribution's; none needs the contrib one.
- **Images.** `otel/opentelemetry-collector` 0.162.0 and `prom/prometheus` v3.15.0, each by digest under a comment with its version; a test fails for an image that is not. *As built (16 Oct 2026):* each is pinned as `name:tag@sha256:…`, the tag in the reference rather than a comment, so that Dependabot (the `docker-compose` ecosystem) can propose updates; the test holds that form. They are pulled only when a profile asks.
- **The alert rules** are twenty-one, in two groups. They use the names on `/metrics`, select by the scrape jobs `ariesd` and `dolt`, and each says what happened and what to do. Beyond §16.7's list: the clock behind history and a step of it (decision 37), failed pushes of metrics and dropped spans as two rules, since the first loses nothing and the second does, writes retried, writes refused at the rate limit, and Dolt's collector at both of slice O4's thresholds, only when a memory limit is set, as the status report judges it.
- **Three things §16.7 lists have no rule, because no metric says them.** *ariesd not ready*: `/readyz` is an HTTP answer and no instrument, so the rules cover its three causes from the metrics (the listener down, the reaper stalled, a keys file write failing, Dolt's endpoint down) and the guide says to probe the path. A gauge for it was not added: it belongs in `internal/server`, where slice O3 was working. *Queueing*: the wait for a project's writer is a span, not a metric; the rule on write latency includes it and one on retries stands beside it. *Dolt's memory against the machine's* and *disk* use Dolt's own `sys_mem_usage` and `sys_disk_usage`, which were measured to be percentages (0.75 where `df` showed 1%, 12.0 where `free` showed 12.5%); a server with a node exporter has better.
- **A counter that should stay at nothing needs two clauses.** The SDK's Prometheus exporter writes no series for a counter's attribute set until it first counts, and Prometheus sees no increase in a series' first sample, so `increase(m[15m]) > 0` alone misses the first drop, the first failed write and the first step of the clock. Each such rule also has `m > 0 unless m offset 15m`. The harness showed it: with the Collector started late, the rule for dropped spans fired on the second clause alone.
- **A test holds the rules and the dashboard to the instruments** (`internal/telemetry/harness_test.go`), with a reader of PromQL of its own, since Prometheus's parser would be a dependency for a test: it finds each series selector and its matchers, and fails for a series that is not an instrument's name on `/metrics` (with `_bucket`, `_sum` and `_count` for a histogram), a label the instrument does not have, or a value the label never takes. Renaming an instrument fails the build, which was tried. Dolt's series are a list in the test, each held to `job="dolt"`, and the smoke test waits for every one a rule or the dashboard reads to arrive from a real Dolt. The test also requires a rule for each value of the `signal` attribute, so the slice that adds `logs` to it must add the rule.
- **The dashboard** is one file in Grafana's format, twelve panels, with the data source as a variable. Its queries are held to the instruments as the rules are, and run in the Prometheus of the harness. It was not loaded into a Grafana.
- **What the smoke test checks, asked with `ARIES_SMOKE_TELEMETRY=1`:** the whole run with telemetry on, and then the trace of §16.7 (the server's span under the test's own, the write's attempt beneath it and the wait beneath that; a request with no context as a root); stand-ins in every span that says who, resolved by `whois` for a server admin alone, and no name, session id or title anywhere in what the Collector received; fifteen instruments pushed, the same scraped, ten of Dolt's series; every rule healthy and every dashboard query run; `/readyz` unready with Dolt frozen whole and ready again after; and the dead collector, below. Without the variable the script does what it did, in 137 checks; with it there are 194. The audit line of §16.7, and the other checks of the log, are among the last items of this note.
- **`/readyz` is checked with Dolt frozen, not stopped.** In the container the three programs stop together when one exits, as `Requires=` has them on a server. `SIGSTOP` leaves Dolt holding its port and answering nothing, which is what the check of Dolt is for.
- **A dead collector, twice over.** The same 18 requests are sent with the Collector running, stopped (its port refuses) and frozen with `docker compose pause` (its port accepts and never answers), and must answer the same and take no more than 5 seconds longer in all; an export that a request waited for would hold the frozen run for the exporter's 10 seconds at each request. ariesd counts the failed exports and the lost spans, logs one warning for each signal, the alert for dropped spans fires in Prometheus, and exports work again when the Collector is back. Then the server is restarted with the Collector frozen and Docker told to wait 30 seconds, and the restart may take 8 seconds longer than with it running: §16.6's 5, and 3 for a clock read in whole seconds. As run, on Docker Desktop on a Mac (arm64): the 18 requests took 1 or 2 seconds each way, and the restart under a second with the Collector running and 5 and 6 seconds, in two runs, with it frozen, which is the 5 seconds ariesd gives its last exports and little more.
- **The soak, with `ARIES_SOAK_TELEMETRY`** (`soak_telemetry_test.go`): off by default, so the gate's short soak is the length it was. `check` reads the instruments and the spans in process at rest. What turned out to be exact: the claims taken, expired and released, each release by its reason, are the projects' logs'; every request and every handshake has one observation and one span with one result; retries counted are spans of a retried attempt; control writes are counted the same where the admin's change is made and where its request is answered. What is a bound, and why: a control write that succeeded is at least a change in the control log, because a change asked for again, after a lost answer or because it already held, succeeds and records nothing (84 `member.set` for 56 changes in one run); a project has at least as many writes as its log has transactions, because renewals and acknowledgements commit and record nothing; the server answered at least what the sessions saw, because the setup and the checks send requests the journal does not keep; and a connection the server closed is counted at least as often as the run saw one close, and under no reason the control log has no change for. "Requests by result" against the log, as §16.7 has it, is therefore against the sessions' journal and the spans, since a read leaves nothing in an event log.
- **Spans are counted as the run goes,** through `Manual.TakeSpans`, which this slice added. Kept until the end, the 100,723 spans of a two-minute run took the test's peak heap to 226 MiB; counted as they end, a like run of 82,650 spans peaked at 98 MiB, among the runs with telemetry off (74 to 129 MiB).
- **What the runs showed.** On one machine (Apple M4 Max, shared with other test runs at the time), two minutes each: with `check`, 26,536 requests and then 21,511, every comparison holding, 2,660 claims taken, 799 expired and 1,808 released in the first, the same in the instruments as in the logs. With `refused`, 25,356 requests, 149 failed exports of metrics and 96,080 spans dropped; with `hang`, 22,006 and 27,530 requests, and 83,316 and 101,191 spans dropped; one warning for each signal in each. With telemetry off, 25,287, 29,402 and 19,926 requests. The spread between two runs of one kind is wider than any difference between the kinds, so no slowing shows; nothing finer can be said from a shared machine.
- **Two runs failed, neither on a telemetry check.** A first two-minute run with `hang`, made while the same machine compiled the test binary again, was a third slower and failed one access check (`was refused project first (forbidden) by a handshake … when the control log gives it access throughout`); the same seed passed when run again, and so did another. And a two-minute run with telemetry off, on that seed (264949260793903814), failed the clock check for two claims (`the reaper ended … claim … after the clock jumped forward 19m0s … want it extended`), and passed when run again, and five times more after slice O3 merged. That one is not this slice's, since nothing of it runs with telemetry off, and was not run down.
- **`refused` and `hang`** give the daemon a collector that is not there, through the real exporters: a closed port, and a listener that accepts and never answers. Every other check of the soak must hold; the drop counter, read from the daemon's own `/metrics`, must be above zero for each signal; each signal must be warned of once for each ten minutes; and stopping the telemetry must take no more than its 5 seconds. Latency is reported beside any run's, not asserted.
- **The test world wired one thing unlike `ariesd serve`:** it released the claims of archived projects at a daemon's start with no telemetry, so those releases went uncounted. It is given the daemon's now, as `serve` gives it.
- **Still not run:** amd64; the Collector under systemd on a server, and beside the AppArmor profiles; a hosted backend; the dashboard in a Grafana; the rules against days of real traffic, or any of them firing but the one for dropped spans; a soak of ten minutes with telemetry on.
- **Logs, added once slice O3 had merged (12 Oct 2026).** What that slice left for this one, and what the first build of this one had marked `LOGS:`:
  - **The Collector's file has a `logs` pipeline,** from the same `otlp` receiver to the same exporter, and the test that asks for a rule and a pipeline for each value of `signal` passes with `logs` among them.
  - **The test server exports its log,** only with `ARIES_TELEMETRY=1`: the entrypoint adds `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp`, which a real server has to ask for (slice O3's note), because a harness with two of three signals would test less. The guide's table of differences says so.
  - **One rule more, twenty-one in all:** `AriesTelemetryLogRecordsDropped`, on `aries_telemetry_dropped_total{signal="logs"}`, shaped as the one for spans is.
  - **No rule matches an `event`,** though slice O3's note asks for rules on `telemetry.export_failed`, `keys_file.write_failed` and `project.open_failed` by name. The file is rules over metrics, and nothing in the harness turns a log record into a metric: the Collector's core distribution has no component that counts records, and the contrib distribution was not taken for it. Each of the three already has a rule from the metric that counts the same thing (the three on `aries_telemetry_dropped_total`, `AriesKeysFileWriteFailing`, and `AriesProjectUnavailable` for a project that cannot be opened at a handshake); one that fails to open at the daemon's start is seen only in the log and in `ariesd upgrade`'s health check. The file and the guide say to match `event` where the logs are read.
  - **"Filters the journal for a third party"** (§16.4, and slice O3's note) is answered in two parts, because ariesd does not send the journal anywhere: the journal is filtered where it is read, on `audit=true` or `event`; and what the Collector receives is filtered by a `filter/audit` processor that the file gives as a comment and the guide as an example, which keeps the audit stream and drops the rest. That processor, like the backend's exporter, was accepted by the Collector's own `validate` and had no record sent through it.
