  - **What the smoke test checks of logs, with the harness.** A request the test traces and the server refuses arrives as a record in the test's trace, with the id of the server's own span for it, its code and its `req`, and the journal has that line, found by the `req`, naming the person the record names by a stand-in. A principal added with `aries admin` is an audit line in the journal with both names, and a record at the Collector with the same `seq` where both are stand-ins; `whois` resolves the one for whom it changed, and the membership it gave is the next record. Every field of every record that names a person holds a stand-in, no record has a field that holds text, and nothing the Collector received in any signal holds a name, a session id or a title. With the Collector stopped, the records lost are counted, there is one warning for logs as for the others, and the new rule fires. 194 checks in all; 137 without the harness, which is slice O3's count.
  - **A request that succeeds has no record at the default level,** since its line is at debug. The check of a record's trace therefore uses a refused request, whose line is at info.
  - **The soak holds the audit stream to the control store's log,** with `ARIES_SOAK_TELEMETRY=check`: every line copies the event of its `seq` (its op, its actor and its target), none comes twice or out of the log's order, and every event that changes access and that a request made has its line. A restart may lose the lines of changes in flight, since the stream starts again at the newest event, so the check allows eight for each restart; none was lost in the runs made, 44 lines for 44 changes in the short run and 180 for 180 in one of two minutes with a restart. It is not in the gate's short soak: it needs the daemon's lines at info, which that run does not read.
  - **Not done for logs:** the soak's `refused` and `hang` export no log records, because the soak's daemon logs to the test's own handler and not through the exporter; the dead-collector test of slice O3, at the server's level, and the smoke test cover that. The status report still says `traces:` for how people appear (slice O3's note).
  - **Still not run, for logs:** a hosted backend receiving the records; a SIEM reading the audit stream; the `filter/audit` example with records through it.

### 16.8 Plan

| Slice | Delivers |
|---|---|
| O1 | `internal/telemetry`, the SDK and OTLP export of metrics, the settings, the loopback listener with `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz`, systemd readiness and watchdog, `ariesd status`, the instruments of §16.4, and the test that keeps the SDK out of `aries` |
| O2 | Traces: the spans of §16.4 from the context decision 27 carries, per-call context in `aries mcp`, and trace ids in the logs |
| O3 | The log schema, `event` names, `req`, the audit stream, and OTLP logs as an option |
| O4 | Dolt: its endpoint on in the guide and the image, and its figures in `aries admin status` |
| O5 | The harness: Collector configuration, Compose profiles, smoke checks, alert rules, dashboard, the soak's cross-check, and the tests of §16.6. Built (§16.7's note) |

### 16.9 Settled since the first draft

1. **Push and pull for metrics** (decision 31): both, as §16.1 has it.
2. **People in traces** (decision 32): the `telemetry.principals` setting of §16.4, defaulting to a pseudonym.
3. **Go runtime metrics** (decision 33): the contrib module, after measuring the standard library (§16.4).
4. **A token for a hosted backend** (decision 34): allowed, from a 0600 environment file. The guide's first setup sends to a collector on the machine, which needs none and keeps the secret, the retries and the vendor's address out of ariesd; sending straight to a hosted backend is the second, for a server where running a collector is not wanted.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O1: metrics, the listener, health and `ariesd status`, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O1 of §16.8 is built: `internal/telemetry`, the SDK for metrics with OTLP/HTTP push and the Prometheus exporter, the `telemetry:` settings `listen` and `per_project`, the loopback listener with `/metrics`, `/healthz` and `/readyz`, systemd readiness and watchdog, `ariesd status`, the instruments of §16.4 and the tests that keep the SDK in its one package and out of `aries`. Traces, the log schema, `event`, `req` and the audit stream, Dolt's figures, `telemetry.principals`, `audit_log` and `dolt_metrics_url`, and the harness of §16.7 are not built; a config file that sets one of those three keys is refused as holding an unknown key. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** With the release flags, `ariesd` went from 10.8 MB to 20.6 MB for linux/amd64 and from 10.1 MB to 19.7 MB for darwin/arm64, and from 4 third-party modules linked to 35 (34 off Linux), counting the modules whose packages `go list -deps ./cmd/ariesd` names; §16.2's "about 11" was a looser count. `aries` is the same size to the byte and links the same 12 third-party modules. Two of those 12 rose a version, `golang.org/x/oauth2` from v0.35.0 to v0.37.0 and `golang.org/x/sync` from v0.20.0 to v0.23.0: the module has one build list, and `github.com/prometheus/common` and `golang.org/x/text`, which only `ariesd` links, require the newer ones.
- **The variables are read by Aries, not left to the SDK.** `internal/telemetry` takes the environment's lookup from the main package (rule 15) and parses the endpoint, headers, protocol, timeouts, interval, exporter, service name, resource attributes and `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED` itself, so that a test can give it an environment and a bad value is refused at start with a fix. The SDK still reads from the process environment, on its own, what Aries does not parse: the TLS files and compression of the OTLP exporter. `OTEL_TRACES_*` and `OTEL_LOGS_*` do nothing yet.
- **No retry within an interval.** The exporter's own retry is off: the totals are cumulative, so the next export carries what a failed one did, and a retry only holds the exporter's goroutine for longer. A failed export is handled in one place, a wrapper around the exporter: it counts on `aries.telemetry.dropped`, logs through a sampler of its own (the first failure, then one line every 10 minutes with the number left out, and a line when exports succeed again) and returns nothing to the SDK, whose handler would log it again each interval. The server's refusal sampler did not fit: it is per principal and per minute, and the server imports this package, not the other way round. `aries.telemetry.dropped` counts failed exports, not data points, and its `signal` is only `metrics` so far.
- **The SDK's own errors** (its process-wide error handler and internal logger) go to the same sampler, redacted, in place of stderr. Both are settings of the process, so a process makes one exporting `Telemetry`.
- **A token cannot reach the log.** Every header's value, and the password and query of the endpoint, are removed from each line before it is written, because the exporter's error does repeat the body of a backend's refusal. The start-up line gives the number of headers, not their names.
- **Recording and locks.** A measurement takes the SDK's aggregation lock for its instrument, which a collection holds only while it copies the numbers; the periodic reader exports after the collection has returned, and the Prometheus handler writes its response after gathering. `internal/telemetry` adds one lock of its own, a read lock on the set of projects seen, held for a map lookup.
- **Attribute values are enforced in `internal/telemetry`,** not left to its callers: each attribute has its list there, the server gives it the list of ops, and a value on no list is recorded as `unknown` (an op) or `other`. `project` must have a UUID's shape and be one of at most as many as the `projects` limit.
- **Instruments, where the code has what the table did not, or lacks it:**
  - `aries.handshakes` has the result `failed` for a handshake that ended with no protocol error to name: a first frame that could not be read or was not a bridge frame, a welcome that could not be written. A connection refused before its handshake (a peer that is not the daemon's user, a socket past twice `conns`) is not counted as one.
  - `aries.connections.dropped` has the reasons `membership`, `disabled`, `archived`, `key`, `idle`, `limit` and one more, `access`: a connection whose access ended while its handshake ran. `limit` is a socket closed unread past twice `conns`; a refusal at `conns` or `conns_per_principal` is a handshake answered `busy`, not a dropped connection, and is counted on `aries.handshakes` and `aries.limit.refusals`. The admin's own connection, closed after the reply to the change that ended its access, is counted with the others.
  - `aries.limit.refusals` names the limits the server enforces itself and the store's caps that have an error type of their own: `conns`, `conns_per_principal`, `handshake_sockets`, `write_rate`, `control_write_rate`, `open_stores`, `telemetry_requests`, `projects`, `principals`, `keys_per_principal`, `members_per_project`, `prices`, `plans`, `hours_per_day`, `usage_per_day`. The store refuses a request past its other limits (labels, dependencies, acceptance items, memories and the rest) as plain invalid, which nothing tells apart from any other invalid request, so those are not counted by name.
  - `aries.claims` is counted from the store, after each write commits, so it says what the event log says: `take`, `release` and `expire` are the events `claim.take`, `claim.release` and `claim.expire`. `reason` is `membership`, `disabled` or `archived` for a release whose event gives that reason (decision 21) and `none` for everything else: the store does not record whether a release came from a finish, a releasing handoff, a close or an import, and a take that replaces a lapsed holder is one `take`, not also an end. `renew` is not an event; it counts each lease a renew extended, which a renew with most of its lease left does not. A session that starts an issue it already holds extends its lease too, and is counted as nothing.
  - `aries.store.write.duration` is the measurement behind the status report's P50 and P99, taken once: a write that changed a row and committed. `aries.store.commit.duration` covers the project stores' Dolt commits, failed ones included, and not the control database's.
  - `aries.control.writes` counts each `aries admin` change asked for, by its result, refusals and rate-limited requests included.
  - `aries.stores.open` counts stores that are open, opening or closing, as the status report does.
  - `aries.keys_file.writes` has the results `ok` and `failed`; a tick that finds the file already right is not a write.
  - **Go runtime, without the process.** The contrib module at v0.72.0 gives `go.memory.used`, `go.memory.allocated`, `go.memory.allocations`, `go.memory.gc.goal`, `go.memory.limit`, `go.goroutine.count`, `go.processor.limit` and `go.config.gogc`. It has no GC pause or CPU metric. Its scheduler latency comes only as a producer that reports the runtime's own histogram, with far more buckets than the short list above and outside any view, so it is left out. No process metrics (resident memory, file descriptors) are exported.
- **The Prometheus endpoint** leaves the scope labels off each series (`otel_scope_name`), and carries the resource in `target_info`.
- **The store's hook** is `store.Options.Observer`, an interface of four methods (a write that committed, a retry, a Dolt commit, a change of a claim), called after the work and outside any transaction. With it came `Store.Migrated` (for `migrated`) and `Store.Ping` (for `/readyz`). The store imports nothing of telemetry.
- **`/readyz`.** The check of Dolt is a ping on a pooled connection to the control database, or to the one project's, with a deadline of 2 seconds. It is made when `/readyz` is asked and the last answer is 5 seconds old, never more often, and requests that arrive together share one check; nothing runs on a timer. A project that could not be opened at start does not make the server unready: `ariesd upgrade`'s health check is what holds that. One more line was added, `ariesd is starting`: the listener is opened before the database, so a monitor can watch a start that migrates many projects.
- **The listener** resolves a host name at start and binds the address it checked, not the name. Beside the cap on requests in flight (`telemetry_requests`, 8) it holds at most twice that many connections open and closes one more unanswered, since a cap on requests alone does not stop a local user from holding file descriptors with idle connections. `ARIESD_TELEMETRY_LISTEN` sets the address as well as the config file. With `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` the listener still answers health, and `/metrics` is 404.
- **What the SDK links, and what the listener serves.** The OTLP/HTTP exporter links `google.golang.org/grpc` (66 packages: the exporter's configuration code and the generated code of the OTLP protocol import it, and no gRPC connection is made) and with it `golang.org/x/net/trace`, which registers `/debug/requests` and `/debug/events` on Go's default HTTP mux, as `expvar` does `/debug/vars`. The listener serves a mux of its own with three paths, never the default one, and a test asks for those pages and gets 404. `govulncheck` still counts `html/template`, which `x/net/trace` uses, as reachable through `http.Server.Serve`: two standard-library findings more than before this slice (GO-2026-6599 and GO-2026-6600, both fixed in Go 1.27.2). `golang.org/x/net` is at v0.60.0, not the v0.59.0 the SDK asks for, which had five findings of its own.
- **The watchdog is fed by a ticker in the reaper's goroutine,** at half `WATCHDOG_USEC`, not by the reaper's tick itself, which comes every 30 seconds whatever the unit asks. A reaper that hangs, or one tick that outlasts `WatchdogSec`, stops the feeding. The guide's unit has `Type=notify`, `NotifyAccess=main`, `WatchdogSec=120` and `TimeoutStartSec=600`: with `Type=notify` systemd waits for the daemon to say it is ready, by default for 90 seconds, and a start that migrates many projects can take longer. The notifications are tested against a socket standing in for systemd's, on Linux; the unit itself was not run under a real systemd for this note, and was afterward (the next note).
- **`ariesd status`** connects as the upgrade's probe does, under a second reserved principal, `ariesd-status`. Its connection is in no project, holds no connection slot and is answered only the status report. It asks for every page, so it prints every project. It also works on a server of one project, where `aries admin status` is refused: it reports the server and that project, listed by its id, since only a control database names projects. The report's text moved to `internal/statustext`, which `aries admin status` and `ariesd status` both print with.
- **Left for the later slices, and found on the way:** the `go-sql-driver/mysql` driver logs through its own logger, unformatted, to stderr when a pooled connection to Dolt dies (`closing bad idle connection`); slice O3's log schema will want that routed through ariesd's logger. The dead-collector test of §16.6 is written, at the server's level, against a closed port and a listener that never answers; the soak's cross-check and the Collector harness of §16.7 are not.
- **13 Oct 2026: an export that fails before the instruments are made.** The SDK's periodic reader exports on a goroutine of its own from the moment it is made, and `telemetry.New` made it before it made the meter provider and the instruments. The wrapper counted a failed export on `aries.telemetry.dropped` by reading them, so an export that failed in between read what `New` was writing: a data race, and a failure that went uncounted. It needs the push interval to pass while `New` is still at work, so it bites with a very short `OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL` and a collector that is down, or a start that stalls; the tests' 20 ms interval met it on a machine with one processor or less for four packages' tests. Such a failure is now kept under a lock of its own and counted once the instruments are made, so the count stays every failed export's.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O1 merged with stage 3a's last changes and run under systemd, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O1 was written beside decisions 24 to 27 and the AppArmor profiles, and then merged with them and run on the Ubuntu 24.04 virtual machine of §15's notes (arm64, systemd 255, AppArmor 4.0.1, the three profiles enforced). What the merge and the machine settled:

- **`ariesd status` and the one protocol version (decision 25).** The upgrade's probe is welcomed whatever protocol it speaks, because it sends no request and the binary that runs it is the old one. `ariesd status` sends a request and reads a result, so it is held to the protocol like any client: one from another version is refused with `version`. Both names stay reserved, the bridge refuses both to every key, and every other reserved name is refused before the hello.
- **Trace context (decision 27)** rides the request's context from before the dispatch, and `observeRequest` measures the same stretch after it. A comment marks where slice O2 starts its span from `TraceFrom`. No span is built.
- **Claims ended by an archive (decision 24) are counted once,** as a release with the reason `archived`, whether the store was open, was opened for the archive, or was opened as it is at the daemon's start (`ReleaseArchived`, which now measures the stores it opens). A store opened at the start without migrating is opened beside the registry and adds nothing to `aries.store.open.duration`.
- **The openings a daemon makes before it listens are measured now.** `OpenProjects` is where an upgrade's migrations run, and the first build of this slice measured only the openings a connection causes, so `migrated="true"` would hardly ever have been seen.
- **The archive lock.** `Server.archiving` is held across the control store's archive write and nothing else. What this slice added takes no lock while it holds another of the server's: the check of Dolt holds its own mutex for a ping, the count of open stores is read by the SDK's collection through the registry's mutex, and every recording is made after the server's or the registry's mutex is released, or under the keys file's or the connection counter's, which no collection takes.
- **An export cut short by the stop is not a failure.** The reader cancels an export that is under way when the daemon stops, and the first build logged and counted that as a collector's failure. It is now neither: the last export follows it.
- **The status report's text** carries the keys held back (decision 26), with its tests, in `internal/statustext`.
- **Under systemd.** `Type=notify`: `Started ariesd.service` is logged within a millisecond of `ariesd serving`, and systemd's debug log shows `READY=1`, `WATCHDOG=1` a minute later and `STOPPING=1` at the stop. `WatchdogSec=120`: a healthy daemon was left alone for 130 seconds; a frozen one (`SIGSTOP`) was killed with `SIGABRT` 120 seconds after its last notification, left its goroutines' stacks in the journal, and was started again. `TimeoutStartSec`: a start that never said it was ready was ended when the time ran out (run with 20 seconds). `systemd-analyze verify` accepts the unit. The 76 checks of §15's run passed again with all of it on, across a cold boot, with nothing in the kernel log.
- **The race with Dolt at boot is not removed by `Type=notify`**, since it is Dolt's unit that systemd counts as started before it listens. The unit's state is now true through it: `activating` until the start that serves. The same race follows every restart of Dolt's unit, a crash included, because `Requires=` has systemd stop ariesd with Dolt and start the two together; so `/readyz` reports a Dolt that hangs or cannot be reached, and a Dolt that restarts takes the listener down with ariesd for about five seconds. Having ariesd wait for Dolt at its start, now that the unit says when it is ready, would remove the failed start; it is not built.
- **AppArmor.** `ariesd-serve` gained one rule, write to `/run/systemd/notify`: without it the daemon serves and the unit never counts as started. The listener and a collector on the machine needed nothing new. A collector whose name needs DNS, and HTTPS, need three lines the shipped profile leaves out and its comments, its README and the guide give for `local/ariesd-serve`; each was shown to be needed and to be enough against receivers on the machine. `ariesd status` runs under the bridge's profile, by the binary's path, since it needs only the config file and the socket; root, to whom that profile gives no capability, runs it as the `aries` user, and the refusal says so.
- **Memory.** Idle, a minute after its start, ariesd's resident memory was 13.9 MB before this slice, 20.3 MB with it and nothing configured, and 22.1 MB with metrics pushed and scraped (22.2 MB five minutes later). Of the 20.3 MB, 14.3 MB is the binary mapped from its file, against 8.5 MB before; the memory that is the process's own went from 5.4 MB to 6.0 MB, and to 6.4 MB with metrics on.
- **govulncheck** with Go 1.27.2, which the repository now pins, reports nothing: the standard-library findings of the note above, the two this slice had made reachable among them, are gone.
- **Still not run:** amd64; a real OpenTelemetry Collector or hosted backend (a few lines of Python received the pushes, over HTTP and over TLS with a private authority); the unit's start timeout at its real ten minutes; a migration long enough to need it.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O2: traces, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O2 of §16.8 is built: a tracer in `internal/telemetry`, exported with OTLP over HTTP; the spans of §16.4, from the caller's trace context (decision 27) through the request into the store; `telemetry.principals` with the stand-in key and `aries admin principal whois` (decision 32); a trace context for each tool call of `aries mcp`; and the ids of the server's span on its log lines. The log schema, `event`, `req`, the audit stream and OTLP logs (O3), Dolt's figures (O4) and the harness (O5) are not built. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** Two modules, the ones decision 28 approved for traces: `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/exporters/otlp/otlptrace` and `.../otlptrace/otlptracehttp`, both v1.47.0; the tracer itself, `sdk/trace`, is a package of a module `ariesd` already linked, and no other module's version changed. With the release flags, and measured against main after slice O4 merged, `ariesd` went from 20,824,224 to 21,414,048 bytes for linux/amd64 (2.8% more) and from 19,910,642 to 20,439,602 for darwin/arm64, and from 35 third-party modules linked to 37. `aries` links the same 12, and none of the SDK: built from this tree with only the packages its own new code is in taken back to main's (the `whois` command, the per-call context, the new op, the status line), it is the same file as main's, byte for byte, on linux/amd64 and darwin/arm64. Its own new code adds 12,288 bytes on linux/amd64.
- **A span is made in one package, which decides what it may carry.** The server, the store's hook and the commands see `telemetry.Span`, a type of Aries's own. Its name is an op the server knows or one of eleven fixed names; its attributes are ten fixed keys, each with a rule for its values; its status is a code. It takes no error, since an error's message quotes what it refused, and it has no events and no links. Anything else it is given is dropped or recorded as `unknown` or `other`. So "no span carries an issue's title" is not a habit of the callers': a test sends every op with marked text in every string of its arguments, through a real server and store, and searches the spans in process and the bytes posted to a collector.
- **The caller's `tracestate` is not put on a span.** Decision 27 has the request carry it, and it does. The SDK would copy it from the remote parent onto every span, and export it; it is the caller's text, and the server calls nothing it could pass it on to. A span takes the trace id, the parent's span id and the sampled flag.
- **The store's hook is a second, smaller interface,** `store.Tracer`, beside `Observer`: one method, `Begin(ctx, stage, attempt)`, which returns the context for the stage's work and a func the store calls with how the stage ended (`ok`, `retry` or `error`; never the error). The stages are an attempt of a write, the wait within it, a Dolt commit and the migrations of an open. `Observer` is unchanged, and so are the figures of `aries admin status` and the O1 metrics. The store still imports nothing of telemetry.
- **The wait for the writer** is the span around the start of the write's transaction on the store's one writer connection: the wait for the connection, and the round trip that begins the transaction, which cannot be told apart without changing how a write takes its connection. It is inside the attempt's span, since each attempt waits again.
- **A read has one span, the request's.** No span is made for each SQL statement: §16.5's "a trace ends at ariesd's span around the SQL it ran" is the request's span for a read, and the attempt's for a write.
- **Background spans are one trace for each tick,** not a root each as §16.4 has it: the reaper's span is the root, and the clock check, the reread of the control store, the keys file's write, the access sweep and the store's writes are beneath it, so that a slow tick shows which part was slow. Each is a root when it runs on its own, and the keys file's write after an `aries admin` change is beneath that request's span. The clock check is one more than §16.4 lists (decision 37 came after it).
- **A handshake's span** covers the handshake and the note of the session in the registry that follows it, and has the opening of the project's store beneath it when this connection caused it. It is a root: a hello brings no trace context. `store.open` is a span only on a server of many projects, where the server opens stores; the one store of a server of one project is opened by `ariesd serve` before there is anything to parent it, and shows as `store.migrate` alone.
- **Failed means the server's failure.** A request answered `unavailable` marks its span failed, as OpenTelemetry's conventions have a server mark only its own errors; a refusal is the span's `result` and no failure. An attempt that lost to another transaction has the outcome `retry`; one that ended in any other error has `error`, which is most often the request's own refusal, so an attempt is never marked failed, and a commit, a migration or a wait that errs is.
- **People.** The stand-in is `p_` and the first 16 hex digits of HMAC-SHA-256 of the principal's name under a key of 32 random bytes; a session's is `s_` and the same of the session id, with a prefix inside the HMAC so that a session named for a principal is not that principal's stand-in. With `name`, a session id that is not 1 to 255 printable ASCII characters with no space is left out, since it is the client's text. Only a request's and a handshake's span say who.
- **Where the key lives.** `telemetry: principals_key:`, by default `telemetry/principals.key` under the home directory of the user ariesd runs as: `/var/lib/aries/telemetry/principals.key`. A directory of its own, because the unit's `ProtectSystem=strict` leaves the home read-only and `ReadWritePaths` works on directories: the only other writable one, `.ssh`, is sshd's, is not writable on a server of one project, and holds `authorized_keys`, which the daemon must never be able to write. `StateDirectory=` would have made the whole home writable. The operator makes the directory and names it in a drop-in, as for the keys file; the AppArmor profile `ariesd-serve` allows the one file, read and write, through a new tunable. The file is created with `O_EXCL`, never replaced, and holds 64 hex digits.
- **A key that cannot be made never stops the daemon** (changed 12 Oct 2026, on review, before this slice merged). As first built, a start that needed the key and could not make it failed with a fix, as a keys file that cannot be written does. That was wrong for telemetry: §16's goal is that it never fails a request, and a daemon that will not start fails them all; without the keys file nobody can sign in, but without this key the server works and only the labelling of people in spans is affected; and an operator who had set `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` for metrics as slice O1's guide says would have had a server that refused to start after an upgrade, since that variable now turns traces on too. Now, with `principals: pseudonym`, a key that cannot be read or made for any reason (no directory, a read-only file system, the AppArmor profile, a file others can read, a file that holds no key, no home directory to put the default path under) leaves spans saying nothing about who, exactly as `omit` does. No key is ever made up in memory, since stand-ins that change at each start and that `whois` cannot resolve are worse than none, and the name is never sent in its place. An explicitly set `principals_key` is treated as the default path is. `name` and `omit` need no key and are unaffected.
- **It says so in three places.** The log has it at error level when it is found, as short lines: what happened, the key's file, `cause:`, then each `fix:` on a line of its own, so that `systemctl status`, which cuts a long line, shows them. The status report (`aries admin status`, `ariesd status`) gains how people appear in spans, beside the clock's lines: by stand-ins, by name, left out by the setting, or `TRACES SAY NOTHING ABOUT WHO` with the cause; in JSON it is `server.traces` (`people`: `pseudonym`, `name`, `omit` or `unavailable`; `cause`, `key_file`, `damaged`), which joined protocol 5 as a result field. And `whois` answers `unavailable`, `this server has no key for stand-ins:` and why, in place of `not_found`, which is kept for a stand-in that is nobody's.
- **A key file that is there and cannot be trusted is its own case.** One that others can read, or that does not hold a key, is not a key that could not be made: someone may have read or changed it. The daemon still starts and still names nobody; the log and the status say exactly that (`others can read it`, `CANNOT BE TRUSTED`), and the fix is to replace the file, which makes earlier stand-ins unlinkable, or, for the mode alone and if nobody can have read it, to tighten it. ariesd does not delete or repair the file itself.
- **It retries, at the reaper's tick.** Every 30 seconds a daemon that wants the key and has none looks again: it reads the file, or makes it. So making the directory where the daemon may write, loading the profile's rule, or repairing the file is all an operator does; one line, `spans name people by stand-ins from now on`, says when it took. A cause that changes is logged again; one that stays is not. A unit's new `ReadWritePaths` still needs a restart, because systemd applies it when the unit starts. A running server thus goes from no stand-ins to stand-ins without a restart, and never the other way: once it has the key it keeps it in memory.
- **Deleting the key file takes effect at the next start,** since the daemon holds the key in memory: stop, delete, start. The new key gives every principal a new stand-in, and `whois` answers that an old one is nobody's.
- **Start refusals are printed a line at a time.** `ariesd` prints an error that stops it as its cause and then each `fix:` on its own line, as the clock's refusal already was, so that the refusal of an `OTEL_*` value it cannot honor is not one long line that `systemctl status` cuts before its fix. A test holds each of those to lines of at most 200 bytes.
- **`whois` tries the key against every principal** of the control store and every server admin the config file names, which is at most the `principals` limit of them and the config file's admins. It is the op `principal.whois`, a read, for server admins only, and joined protocol 5 while no release speaks it (`internal/proto/version.go`). A session's stand-in is not resolvable. A server of one project has no control store and so no `whois`; its operator has `authorized_keys` to read the names from, or `principals: name`.
- **The variables are read by Aries,** as O1's are: the endpoint for traces and its own, the headers, the protocol, the timeout, `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER` (`otlp` or `none`), `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` with its argument (the SDK's six samplers; any other is refused at start), and the four `OTEL_BSP_*` variables of the batching, with the queue held to 65,536. `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=none` now turns off metrics alone. The resource is made once, so metrics and spans carry one `service.instance.id`.
- **Dropped spans are counted exactly, which the SDK does not do.** Its batching processor drops a span its queue has no room for and keeps the count to itself (it reports it only through an experimental self-observability option, to the process's global meter). So the bound is enforced one step earlier, in a processor of Aries's in front of the SDK's: a span is passed on only while fewer than the queue's size are waiting, which leaves the SDK's queue never full, and one that is not is counted. `aries.telemetry.dropped{signal="traces"}` is every span lost: at that bound, or in an export that failed. For `signal="metrics"` it still counts failed exports, which lose nothing.
- **The exporter's retries are left on for traces,** unlike metrics: a span that is not exported is gone. They end with the export's timeout. A failed export is counted and logged through a second sampler like O1's, one for each signal, so that one's recovery does not speak for the other's.
- **A stop cuts short what it cannot finish.** The SDK's processor, told to stop with a deadline, returns at the deadline and leaves its last exports running for as long as their own timeout. Aries's processor cancels them when the deadline passes, waits for them to end, and counts their spans as dropped, so a stop leaves no goroutine behind.
- **Log lines.** With traces on, a request's line carries the trace id and the id of the server's own span, sampled or not, in place of the caller's span id that decision 27's build logged; with traces off it is as that build left it. The lines of a handshake and of background work carry the ids of the span they were logged under, which for a line inside the sweep or the keys file's write is that span, in the tick's trace, not the tick's root. Nothing of O3 is built: the ids are added to the lines there were.
- **Trace context for each tool call.** The MCP Go SDK (v1.8.0) hands a request's `_meta` to a tool's handler as a map and knows nothing of trace context. The MCP specification reserves `traceparent`, `tracestate` and `baggage` in `_meta` for it from its 2026-07-28 version, after OpenTelemetry's convention for MCP; before that it was the convention alone. `aries mcp` reads the first two at each call, checks them as a request frame is checked, and puts the context on the Go context of the call (`client.WithTrace`), which the connection sends in place of its own. A call that brings none, or a malformed one, sends the environment's; the renewals between calls always do. `baggage` is not read. No tool or schema changed, and the wire did not: the context rides `tp` and `ts`.
- **What a request costs.** Starting and ending a write's three spans takes 14 ns and allocates nothing with traces off, 0.6 µs and 16 allocations when the trace is not sampled, and 2.0 µs and 30 allocations when it is (Apple M4 Max, Go 1.27.2). Through a server and a real store, a read took 0.35 to 0.54 ms and a write 5.2 to 6.1 ms in all three modes: the spans are below what Dolt's own variation hides, and show only as allocations, 107, 123 and 155 for a read.
- **How many spans.** One for a read; three for a write, and two more for each retry; three for a handshake, twelve when it opens the store; one for a Dolt commit; and for the reaper's tick four on a server of one project, or five and four for each open store. The default of §16.4, to follow the parent and otherwise sample everything, is kept: at a tracker's traffic it is tens of spans a second. An idle server with 100 stores open sends about 400 spans every 30 seconds for the reaper alone, which is what `parentbased_traceidratio` is for, and the guide says so.
- **Under systemd and AppArmor.** Run on the Ubuntu 24.04 virtual machine of the notes above (arm64, systemd 255, AppArmor 4.0.1, the three profiles enforced), with a Python receiver on the machine, and with slice O4's config (`dolt_metrics_url`) in place. With the guide's one variable and nothing else the daemon starts under `ariesd-serve (enforce)`, serves, and posts spans with no stand-in and no name; the log's error lines, the status report and `whois` give the cause, `it cannot be made: no such file or directory`. With the directory made and no restart, the next tick's cause is `read-only file system`. With the unit's `ReadWritePaths` and a restart, under main's profile: `permission denied`, and `DENIED … operation="mknod"` for the file in the kernel log, which is what the profile's new rule is for. With this slice's profile loaded and no restart, the next tick made the key (mode 0600) and logged that stand-ins began; spans then held one, and `whois` resolved it for a server admin. With the file made mode 0644 and a restart: no stand-in, `CANNOT BE TRUSTED` in the status; after `chmod 600` and no restart, the same stand-in again. The 76 checks of §15's run passed with traces on, with no denial and nothing dropped. The first build of this slice, which refused to start without the key, was run on the same machine earlier the same day: a restart kept the key; with the file deleted the next start made another, the same person had another stand-in and the old one resolved to nobody; another user could not read the key, nor could the `aries` user under the bridge's or the operator's profile; and one tick of the reaper, with one store open and its first prune, was eleven spans. This slice's first commit says the profile's rule was not run under AppArmor; it has been, as this says.
- **Dolt's metrics are read without a span** (after the merge with slice O4, 12 Oct 2026). O4's reader (`internal/server/doltwatch.go`) fetches Dolt's metrics endpoint every 15 seconds for the status report. It is left out of the traces on purpose: it touches no store and serves no request, its failures are already logged and shown in the status, and a span for each read would add two to the four spans an idle server of one project sends every 30 seconds for nothing an operator would look up in a trace. No span name was added for it, and nothing of Dolt's answer reaches a span.
- **A real Collector.** An OpenTelemetry Collector (0.162.0, in Docker, on the developer's machine) received and decoded the spans and metrics of a daemon started as `serve` starts it: the handshake's, a request's under the trace id of the `traceparent` it was sent, the attempt and the wait beneath it, with the attributes above and no title, name or `tracestate`. That was one run by hand; the tests use a receiver of their own, and the harness of §16.7 is not built.
- **The soak, with a dead collector.** The short soak, and one of two minutes (23,815 requests), were run by hand with metrics and traces both sent to a closed port: every invariant held, the requests were no slower (248 a second against 223 with telemetry off, on the same seed), and the log had one warning for each signal. The soak does not do this by itself yet; that is O5's.
- **Still not run:** amd64; a hosted backend; traces over HTTPS or to a collector by a name that needs DNS, which the profile's three optional lines are for and which were run for metrics only.
- **Left for the later slices.** O3: the ids reach a line through a logger made for it at each place (`withIDs`), not through the log's handler, which the schema will want to do once for every line; `req` is not built, and the request's span id is the nearest thing to it today; `telemetry.principals` governs spans only, and exported log records are to follow it. O5: the smoke test's check that a trace arrives whose root is the test's `TRACEPARENT`; the soak's cross-check of spans against the event log; and a dashboard and an alert on `aries.telemetry.dropped{signal="traces"}`.

**As built (the status report does not wait for Dolt, 12 Oct 2026).** With Dolt stopped by `SIGSTOP`, `ariesd status` waited its 30 seconds and failed with a read timeout: the report counted each open store's sessions one store after another, each within the request's 60 seconds. The counts now run at once under one deadline of 2 seconds, the bound of `/readyz`'s own check of Dolt, so the report takes that long at most with all 64 stores open; a count that does not come back is `sessions_unknown` in the result, with the cause as text, and `unknown` in the table with a line that says why, never 0. It is a result field, so the protocol is unchanged. Nothing else on the report's path reads Dolt or holds a lock across a read of it: the control store's lists and counts, the registry, the clock, the keys file and Dolt's own figures are read from memory. Measured with Dolt frozen: 30.0 s and an error before, 2.0 s and the report after. **Left as it is:** a new connection still waits on Dolt before its first request is read, to note the session in the registry (up to the request timeout, 62 s measured for `status` on a new connection to an open store) or to open the project's store (60 s, then `unavailable`), so `aries admin status`, which connects for each command, is prompt only where a connection is already open; `ariesd status` is the command for a server whose Dolt hangs. The local status connection starts no span, as before.

**As built (a new connection does not wait for Dolt before its first request, 12 Oct 2026).** What the note above left as it was is changed, since `aries` connects for each command and `aries admin status` is what an admin runs from their own machine when the database is in trouble. (1) **Store open.** The note of the session in the project's registry is made beside the read of the first request, not before it. Every request waits for it, for as long as the request may wait at all, so no request reads or writes a store for a session the registry has not been told of, and a failed note is logged and does not fail the request, as before. For every op but `status` that is the request's timeout. `status` counts its asker's session, so it waits too, but its 2 seconds on Dolt now cover the wait for the note and its own reads together: with Dolt frozen it answers in 2 seconds with the sessions unknown, and otherwise it counts its asker as before. A request that finds the note failed makes it again, within its own time, so a session is noted by its first request that Dolt answers; before, it stayed out of `aries who` until it renewed a claim. The handler waits for the note before it returns, so the goroutine never outlives the connection or its hold on the store: a connection that closes with its note still being made, which only one that asked nothing can, gives it 2 seconds to finish, so that a session which said hello and left is still listed when Dolt answers, and then cancels it. The `handshake` span now ends at the welcome; the note's write is beneath it in the same trace and may end after it. (2) **Store closed.** A connection is to a project, and its project's store is opened in the handshake; the report opens no store. Rather than change how a connection binds to a project, the handshake gives Dolt 2 seconds to answer a ping of the control database before it opens a store, and refuses with `unavailable` when it does not: `the server's database does not answer, so project "…" was not opened`, with `ariesd status` on the server as the fix, and `dolt.check_failed` in the log. The ping is the handshake's own, not the one `/readyz` caches for 5 seconds, and is made only when a store would be opened. Not done: welcoming such a connection with no store, to answer `status` alone, or opening the store at the first request that needs it; either makes a session without a project, which the connection limits, the tracking of whose connection is whose and every admin op assume it has. (3) **Any other request** on a new connection is refused with `the server timed out on …` after one request timeout, where it was two (the note, then the request). No protocol change. Measured with Dolt frozen, the last with a request timeout of 4 s: `status` on a new connection to an open store 62 s before and 2.0 s after; to a closed store, 60 s and `the server could not open project` before, 2.0 s and the refusal above after; `list` on a new connection 8.0 s before and 4.0 s after.

**As built (stage 3b, slice O3: the log schema, `event`, `req`, the audit stream and OTLP logs, 12 Oct 2026).** Slice O3 of §16.8 is built: every line ariesd logs names an event from one list and has only fields from one list; a request's id is on every line logged for it and in its refusal; the ids reach a line through the log's handler; the control store's access changes are also log lines with `audit=true`; and with `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` the lines also go out over OTLP, under `telemetry.principals`. `telemetry.audit_log` is a setting now. The harness (O5) is not built. Where the build differs from the above, or settles what it left open:

- **What it cost.** Two modules: `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/sdk/log` v1.47.0, which is the Logs SDK and is at the SDK's own version, and `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/exporters/otlp/otlplog/otlploghttp` v0.23.0, the exporter §16.2 calls experimental and which is still below version 1. `go.opentelemetry.io/otel/log`, the Logs API, was already linked through the SDK and is now named in `go.mod`. No other module's version changed. With the release flags, and against main after slice O2, `ariesd` went from 21,414,048 to 21,852,320 bytes for linux/amd64 (2.0% more) and from 20,439,602 to 20,882,402 for darwin/arm64, and from 37 third-party modules linked to 39. `aries` links the same 12 and none of the SDK; it is the same size to the byte on linux/amd64 and 16 bytes larger on darwin/arm64, for the one line it prints (below).
- **No `slog` bridge.** §16.4 says the records go out "through the `slog` bridge", which is `go.opentelemetry.io/contrib/bridges/otelslog`. It is not used: it copies every attribute of a record, and what a record may carry has to be decided field by field (below), which is forty lines over the Logs API. That is one module fewer than §16.2's table counted.
- **The lists are a package, `internal/logs`,** on the standard library alone, so that the store can import it: the fields (`schema.go`), the events (`events.go`), the request's id and the handler. A test parses every log call in `cmd/ariesd`, `internal/server`, `internal/store`, `internal/telemetry`, `internal/sdnotify` and `internal/doltmetrics` and fails unless its message is a string literal, its first field is `"event"` with a constant of the list, and every field it names is in the schema; a second holds `docs/server.md` to both lists. A call that passes a slice of fields is checked by the tests that read the lines themselves: a run with marked text in every argument of every op, whose every line must name a listed event and have only listed fields.
- **77 events, 86 fields.** §16.4's three examples are in the list as it gives them (`keys_file.write_failed`, `project.open_failed`, `conn.cap_refused`). Every line has an event, not only those "worth an alert": a line without one would be the one that turns out to matter. A request's line is `request.done` at debug or `request.refused` at info; the server's own failure is `request.failed`.
- **Fields that changed their names.** `ms` on a request's line is `dur_ms`, as §16.4 has it. Where a line named a principal who is not the line's own, the field is now `subject` (a key held back from the keys file, the lines about a new member's keys), so that `principal` always means whose connection, request or claim it is. `key` is `fingerprint` (a configured admin's key) or `path` (the stand-in key's file); the sampler's `key` is `principal`; `by` on the clock's step line is `step`.
- **The lines about the stand-in key are one event with fields,** not messages built from the cause and the fix: `msg=cause cause="…"`, `msg=fix fix="…"`, a line each as before. A message has to be fixed text for the rule about exported records to mean anything.
- **`req` is 16 hex digits, chosen at random by the server for each request.** It is on the context, so every line logged with the request's context has it: the request's own, the store's failure, a part of the answer that could not be read. It is `req` on the refusal (`proto.Error`), a result field, so there is no protocol change: an older client ignores it and an older server sends none. `aries` prints it as a third line, `req:`, only when the server itself failed the request (`unavailable`), since any other refusal says what to do and needs no log; `--json` carries it whenever it is there; and `aries mcp` gives it to the agent to pass on, for `unavailable` only and only when it is 16 hex digits, since it is the server's text in what the agent is told. A line outside a request has none.
- **The ids come from the handler** (`logs.Handler`), once: it reads the request's id and the span's ids from the context of the call and adds them after the line's own fields. `withIDs` and the request line's own reading of its span are gone; a call site logs with its context (`log.ErrorContext(ctx, …)`) and names neither. With traces off the ids are still the caller's, as decision 27's build had them. The schema is flat: a group opened on the logger qualifies the keys that follow and nests nothing.
- **The audit stream is read from the event log,** not written beside it. After each `aries admin` write is answered for, and at each tick of the reaper, the daemon reads the control store's events past the last it wrote a line for and writes one for each that changes access, in the log's order. So a line cannot say what the log does not, a change made by `ariesd control init` beside the daemon has its line within a tick, and the one thing the daemon keeps is a number in memory. It starts at the newest event there is when the daemon starts: a restart repeats nothing, and what was written while no daemon ran has no line. Keeping the position across restarts would have needed a file or a row of its own, a second thing to be wrong; the event log is there for whoever needs every event.
- **An audit line** has `event` (`audit.` and the event's op, ten of them), `audit=true`, `seq` and `at` (the event's own), `principal` and `session` (the actor), `op`, `target` as the event log names it, and the target's parts where it has them: `project`, `subject`, `fingerprint`, `role`. A key's event holds the key's text in its before and after; a line takes only the principal and the role from those. It carries no `req`: the read after one request can find another's event, and a line that named the wrong request would be worse than one that named none; `seq` finds the event. An event with no actor, and `clock.step`, are not audit lines.
- **`audit_log: false`** writes none. It is in `telemetry:` as §16.3 has it, though the lines are on stderr and need no telemetry.
- **What an exported record carries is decided in `internal/telemetry`, by each field's kind in `internal/logs`,** as a span's attributes are: the message as the body, the level, the event as the record's event name, and the fields that are ids, codes, numbers, times or fixed words, each only in its own shape (a project that is not a UUID is left out, an op the server does not know is `unknown`). `trace_id` and `span_id` are the record's own, from the span on the context. **An `err` is never exported, nor any other text**, under any setting: an error's message quotes what it refused and can name a person, and "exactly as spans do" leaves no room for it. §16.4's "the same records" is therefore the same lines with fewer fields; the journal has the rest, found by `req`.
- **`telemetry.principals` governs every field that names a person,** not only `principal` and `session`: `by`, `subject`, `admin`, `created_by` and `added_by` too. They are the span's stand-ins, from the same key, so exporting logs alone makes the key, and without the key a record says nothing about who and never a name. So an exported audit record names the actor and the subject by stand-ins by default. §16.4 says the setting "does not change the journal or the audit stream": that holds for the stream on stderr, and the copy that leaves the machine follows the setting, which is the reading that never sends a name the operator did not ask to send.
- **Off unless asked for.** `OTEL_LOGS_EXPORTER=otlp` turns it on; with it unset, an endpoint exports metrics and traces and no logs, unlike those two signals, which an endpoint alone turns on. `otlp` with no endpoint is refused at start with a fix, as is any other value. The variables are read by Aries as the others are: the endpoint, headers, protocol and timeout for logs, and the four `OTEL_BLRP_*` of the batching, with the queue held to 65,536.
- **Dropped records are counted exactly,** as spans are and for the same reason: the SDK's batching processor overwrites the oldest record of a full queue and tells only its own logger. A processor of Aries's in front of it passes a record on only while fewer than the queue's size wait, and counts the rest on `aries.telemetry.dropped{signal="logs"}`, with the records of an export that failed. A stop cuts short what it cannot finish and counts it.
- **The line that says a log export failed is itself exported.** It cannot feed itself: it is sampled, once and then every ten minutes. The lines of the stop are not exported: the export is taken off the logger before telemetry stops.
- **What a line costs.** A line that is exported takes 2.7 µs and 32 allocations more on the goroutine that logged it; with logs not exported the handler adds nothing that allocates (Apple M4 Max, Go 1.27.2). The requests of the dead-collector test were no slower with every line, at debug, exported to a closed port or to a listener that never answers, and the log had one warning.
- **The driver's logger.** `go-sql-driver/mysql` logs through each store's logger now (`dolt.driver`, at warn, with what it said in `detail`), by the connector's own setting, not the package's global one, so two stores do not share it. A test restarts Dolt under an open store and reads the line.
- **Dolt's metrics.** The reader's lines are `dolt_metrics.reading`, `dolt_metrics.read_failed` and `dolt_metrics.read_recovered`.
- **The listener's HTTP server** logs through `telemetry.listener_error`, with what it said in `detail`: its messages are the standard library's and are not fixed text.
- **Text is still the default format.** §16.1 says "JSON lines on stderr … as now"; the default was and is `log_format: text`, and the schema is the same in both. The guide gives both.
- **Found on the way, and left.** The status report still says `traces:` for how people appear, though the line now holds for exported log records too: `status.go` and `localstatus.go` were left alone for a change being made to them at the same time. A line logged by a store's background committer, or by the driver, has no request to name, so it carries no `req` even when a request caused it.
- **Not run:** amd64; a real OpenTelemetry Collector or a hosted backend receiving the log records (the tests use a receiver of their own, and read the bytes posted); the daemon under systemd and AppArmor with logs exported, which needs no rule the traces did not; a SIEM reading the audit stream from the journal.
- **Left for O5:** the smoke test's check, with a Collector, that an access change produces its audit record there (the smoke test now checks the line in the server's log, with no Collector); an alert rule on `telemetry.export_failed`, `keys_file.write_failed` and `project.open_failed` by name, and on `aries.telemetry.dropped{signal="logs"}`; the collector configuration that filters the journal for a third party, which §16.4 promises the guide; and the soak's cross-check, which could hold the audit lines to the control store's log as the unit test does.

**As built (the HTTPS listener's metrics, 16 Oct 2026).** T1 ([transport.md](transport.md#as-built-16-oct-2026-slice-t1)) added the listener with no instrument; six are added to §16.4's, each with fixed values only, and none with an address, a URL, a header or a key: `aries.https.connections` (up-down, the TCP connections the listener holds), `aries.https.connections.refused` (`reason`: `untrusted`, `limit`), `aries.https.upgrades` (`result`: `ok`, `refused`; `reason`: `none`, `origin`, `auth_failures`, `bad_request`), `aries.https.key_proofs` (`result`: `ok`, `failed`, `invalid`, `url`, `unknown_key`, `bad_proof`; T2 added `disabled`), `aries.https.tls_handshakes.failed`, and `aries.https.cert.reloads` (`result`: `ok`, `failed`). `aries.limit.refusals` gains `https_conns_per_server`, `https_conns_per_addr` and `auth_failures_per_minute`. A TLS handshake that failed is told from `http.Server.ConnState`: a `*tls.Conn` closed with its handshake incomplete, so net/http's error text, which is the peer's, is still never read. A key proof is counted before its refusal or its welcome is written, so a client that read either finds it counted. The alert rules gain `AriesTLSCertReloadFailing` (the guide already said to alert on `tls.cert_reload_failed`), and `AriesConnectionCapReached` the listener's cap; the dashboard gains a panel. No span is added for the upgrade or the proof; the handshake's span begins after the proof, as before.

**As built (stage durations, 21 Oct 2026).** Three more histograms, in `CycleBuckets`, recorded through the `Observer` after the commit on the store's clock: `aries.issue.queue.duration` (`project`, `type`) at an issue's first `claim.take` ever, from its creation or its `defer_until` if later; `aries.issue.landing.duration` (`project`, `type`) at the closing pass, from the last handoff with state `done`; and `aries.gate.wait.duration` (`project`, `kind`) at a gate's pass or waiver, from its `gate.add` or last `gate.rearm` event. The queue time is not measured from when the issue became ready: ready is derived (rule 7), and computing it would mean reading the close events of every blocker, so an issue that waited on a blocker counts that wait. Each is one indexed query in the write that already holds the issue.

**As built (the work, 21 Oct 2026).** Two instruments and a dashboard show the work, asked for on 17 Oct 2026. `aries.issue.cycle.duration` (histogram, seconds; `project`, `type`) is recorded once when an issue closes, from its first start (the first `claim.take` event) since it was last opened to the close, on the store's clock (`wtx.now`): `setStatus`, which every close passes through, reads the start with one query served by `events_target` and notes the time, and the store tells its observer after the commit through `Observer.IssueCycled`, as it does for usage. A close of an issue never started notes nothing; a reopen and a later close note again from the next start after the reopen. Its buckets are `CycleBuckets`, one minute to 14 days, by a single view that gives every other histogram `Buckets`, since two matching views would each make a stream. `aries.issues` (gauge; `project`, `status`) is read at collection: one `SELECT status, count(*) … GROUP BY status` for each open store, run in turn under 3 seconds each, never opening a store, and a failed count leaves that project out of the collection. Chosen over counts kept in the writes because the table is the truth and nothing can drift or need rebuilding at open; the price is one status-index scan for each open project at each scrape. It is the first gauge with attributes that is watched, so `Telemetry.WatchSeries` reads it and `Telemetry.Watch` refuses an instrument with attributes. `type` and `status` are the store's closed lists, no text. `deploy/observability`'s `dashboard-work.json`, "Aries work", has issues in progress, open and blocked by project, closes a day, the mean and p50 and p90 of start to close by type over the window, and the token panels of the server dashboard, which stay there too.

**As built (outcome counters, 21 Oct 2026).** `aries.issues.closed` and `aries.issues.reopened` (counters; `project`, `type`, `reason`) count what happens to an issue once it closes (productivity-metrics.md §6, F1). `setStatus`, which every close and reopen passes through, notes each in the write (`wtx.noteOutcome`) and the store tells its observer after the commit through `Observer.IssueClosed` and `IssueReopened`, as it does for usage. The reason is classified in the store from what the event records and never passed on: a close is `forced` if its event records `acceptance_overridden`, `checks_overridden` or `reviews_overridden`, else `landed` if the closing pass (`wtx.closingPass`) is the one closing, else `other`; a reopen is `check_failed` if the event's reason is `ReasonCheckFailed` (a revert), else `other`. A person's close whose reason merely says "landed" is `other`. A gate's own issue (type `gate`) is not counted: its closes and reopens follow its passes and rearms. The attribute's list is fixed in `internal/telemetry`, and a value outside it is `other`. "Aries work" gains closes a day by reason and, for the revert rate, the reopen rate and the forced share, a panel for the ratio beside one for its counts.

**As built (live claims and the ready queue, 21 Oct 2026).** `aries.claims.held` and `aries.issues.ready` (gauges; `project`) show how much of the work's slots are in use, F4 of [productivity-metrics.md](productivity-metrics.md). Both are watched like `aries.issues` (`Telemetry.WatchSeries`, one store after another under 3 seconds each, never opening a store, a failed count leaving the project out). Claims held is `SELECT COUNT(*) FROM claims WHERE principal IS NOT NULL AND expires_at > now`, as `ActiveClaims` counts: an issue in progress awaiting landing, or whose lease ended, holds no claim and is not counted, which is why `aries.issues{status="in_progress"}` overstates the work being done. Ready is `Store.ReadyCount`, the ready query's condition (`readyWhere`, shared with `Ready`) counted, with no path ranking. It is a recursive query over the whole project, so the server keeps each open project's count for at most a minute (`readyCache`): a scrape reuses it, and the one that finds it older reads again. Chosen over deriving it on writes because ready depends on dependencies, parents, deferral and the clock (`defer_until`), which a write cannot cheaply keep right, and the table stays the truth; the price is a count up to a minute old, which an idle-slot panel does not need finer. The benchmarks `Scale/…/scrape/ready`, `scrape/claims` and `scrape/status` (`ARIES_BENCH_LARGE=1` for 50,000 issues) measure each. The work dashboard's last panel draws both against a constant, `vector(4)`, the coordinator's slot count, which the harness test accepts as the one query that selects no series.

**As built (the ready count's cost, 22 Oct 2026).** At 50,000 issues the first form of the count took 1.2 s of Postgres a read, against 24 µs for claims and 4.5 ms for statuses (`ARIES_BENCH_LARGE=1 go test -run '^$' -bench 'Scale/issues=50000/scrape' ./internal/store/`, darwin/arm64, Postgres 18.6). The query was already count-only; the cost was the plan. `readyWhere` held an issue back with three `NOT IN` subqueries, and the planner, which cannot estimate a recursive CTE, left the `deferred` one as a subplan rescanned for each of the 17,502 open issues. Measured on the same project, one read each: three `NOT IN`, 1.26 s; one `NOT EXISTS` for each set, 3.0 s (a nested-loop anti join on `blocked`); one `NOT IN` over the three sets' union, 3.6 s; `EXCEPT` of the sets from the candidates, 35.5 ms; one `NOT EXISTS` over the union, 35 ms, which is what `readyWhere` is now (31 ms in the benchmark). The condition is shared, so `ready` went from 375 ms to 114 ms and `start`'s ranking gains the same. Not done, with the reasons: a stored count, which rule 7 forbids (a write cannot cheaply keep it right through dependencies, parents and `defer_until`, and the clock moves it with no write); a longer cache, which would only hide 1.2 s; dropping the gauge above a size, which loses the gauge where it matters. The budget is under 50 ms for one read at 50,000 issues, read once a minute: under 0.1% of a core for each project.

**As built (tokens by model, 16 Oct 2026).** `aries.usage.tokens` (counter; `project`, `kind`, `model`) counts the tokens of each usage record the store takes, once: the store's usage write reads which rows it inserted (`RETURNING`, so a record the store already had is not one), and after the commit tells its observer through `Observer.UsageAdded`, as it does for a gate, so a batch sent again, a write replayed under its idempotency key and a rerun after a lost race count nothing more. `kind` is `input`, `output`, `cache_write` or `cache_read`; `cache_write_1h` is a part of `cache_write` and no kind of its own. Only tokens are counted: cost is computed on read from prices that change, and stays with `aries cost` (decision 92). `model` is held to rule 2 twice. The store names a model only when the project's price table has a price for it, at any time, and says `(other)` for every other, which no reported model can be, as one starts with a letter or digit; and `internal/telemetry` takes a model as a value only after `AdmitModel`, which `internal/server`'s observer calls for the priced ones, and which refuses a name of the wrong shape and any past 64 (`MaxModels`), so a store or a caller that was not so careful still adds no series. A model that gains a price later is counted by name from then on, and what was counted before it stays under `(other)`. A harness that reports a turn or a session as well as each request would be counted twice; Claude Code's hook reports requests alone. `deploy/observability`'s dashboard gains two panels, tokens a second and tokens in the last day, each by model; there is no alert, since how many tokens a team uses is its own business. No span is added: the usage request's span carries its op and result. The names are in `internal/telemetry/testdata/names.golden`.

## 17. Identity: certificates, handles and provisioning

Designed 12 Oct 2026, from the investigation of §15.6. **Proposed, not decided, and nothing here is built.** The questions for the maintainer are in §17.12. It answers two concerns: that one public key for each machine, added by an admin, will not scale or fit a company's sign-on; and whether people's names should be hidden. Signing in to Aries itself, which §17.10 leaves out, is researched in [access-research.md](access-research.md) (13 Oct 2026; research only).

**Sign-in for v1.0.0 is designed in [identity.md](identity.md)** (15 Oct 2026, proposed): the identity of slice I2 as tables, invitations, expiring keys, `aries login`, service principals with workload tokens, and the Claude Desktop extension, in slices N0 to N8 after the Postgres port's P1 and P2 (decision 83). It is a document of its own because this one is long and this section is a separate design, for certificates, that v1.0.0 does not include. It reuses §17.2's model and §17.14's clocks; where the two differ on sign-in, identity.md is the plan.

In short: a principal stops being a set of keys and becomes a permanent **handle** with, beside it and outside every history, a display name and the identity a company's certificate authority asserts. A short-lived SSH certificate becomes a second way in, through the file ariesd already writes, with no new sshd mechanism and no connection from Aries to the authority. The control store still decides everything. Membership follows a directory through a job that runs `aries admin`, not through what a certificate claims.

### 17.1 What was measured

All of it on 12 Oct 2026, on one Apple silicon Mac under Docker Desktop. The scripts and their output are not in the repository.

| Part | Version |
|---|---|
| sshd and the `ssh` client used against it | OpenSSH 9.6p1 (Ubuntu 3ubuntu13.19) on Ubuntu 24.04, in the Docker test server's image built from `main` that day |
| `aries` | built from `main` at `ea749e5` with Go 1.27.1 and `golang.org/x/crypto` v0.57.0; run on macOS against the container's port |
| `ssh-agent`, `ssh-add`, and `ssh-keygen` where it signed on the Mac | OpenSSH 10.3p1; an agent started for the tests on a socket of its own |
| The certificate authority | step-ca 0.30.2 with its `step` CLI 0.30.2, `smallstep/step-ca@sha256:a2b17872915c193259b75a5474c398326f41bd199f0842093e52cf4182bc8270` |
| Dolt | 2.4.2, the `dolt` command on a throwaway repository, not `dolt sql-server` |

step-ca was chosen for the experiment because it is open source (Apache-2.0), widely used, publishes an image that can be pinned, issues SSH user certificates, and has both an OpenID Connect provisioner and a simpler one (a shared secret, "JWK") that needs no identity provider to test with. That is a reason to test with it, not a recommendation: Aries would depend on no authority's API, only on OpenSSH certificates, which every such product issues. Only the JWK provisioner was run. **No OpenID Connect sign-in was run.**

In every test below the forced command named the principal and no key (`ariesd stdio --principal NAME`, reached through a small script that first recorded its environment), which today's daemon takes as a line of the hand-maintained file. So the daemon ran unchanged.

**1. `aries` and a certificate.**

| Setup | Result |
|---|---|
| The agent holds a key and its certificate (`ssh-add KEY` loads `KEY-cert.pub` itself); sshd admits the certificate only; unmodified `aries` | Admitted as the right principal. `aries` offered the bare key, which sshd refused, then the certificate |
| The agent holds the bare key only | Refused, as it should be |
| No agent; `key:` names the key, `KEY-cert.pub` beside it; unmodified `aries` | Refused: `aries` reads the private key and never looks for the certificate |
| The same, with `aries` patched to read `KEY-cert.pub` (`ssh.NewCertSigner`) | Admitted |
| The agent holds another principal's key; `key:` names alice's | **Signed in as the other principal.** `aries` offers every key of the agent, in the agent's order, before the `key:` file, and the first one sshd accepts decides who it is |
| The agent holds five or more keys ahead of the right one (5 decoys, then the key, then its certificate) | `Too many authentication failures`: sshd's `MaxAuthTries` is 6 and every offer it refuses counts. With four decoys the certificate is the sixth offer and is admitted. `aries` reports this as `ssh handshake … failed` with the fix `check server.host and server.port`, which is wrong |
| One key is in the store for bob and has a certificate for alice; the agent lists the key, then the certificate | Unmodified: signed in as bob. With certificates offered first: as alice |
| An expired certificate | sshd: `Certificate invalid: expired`. `aries`: `refused your SSH keys (tried 2)`, with a fix about sending a public key to the admin, which is also wrong |

The patch, kept outside the repository, is about thirty lines in `signers` (`internal/client/conn.go`): read `KEY-cert.pub` beside a `key:` file; with `key:` set, offer only that key (the agent's signers are kept only where they are the same public key); offer certificates before bare keys. One thing cost a wrong first attempt: a signer from `agent.NewClient(...).Signers()` holds an `*agent.Key`, so a type assertion to `*ssh.Certificate` never sees an agent's certificate. The key's bytes have to be parsed.

**2. A real authority, the whole path.** A person asked step-ca for a certificate (`step ssh certificate alice@example.com KEYFILE`); it came back valid 90 seconds, the lifetime set on the provisioner, with key id `alice@example.com`, principals `alice` and `alice@example.com`, a random 64-bit serial, no critical options and the five default extensions. The unmodified `aries` used it from the agent and the issue it created is recorded under `alice`. After it expired, the same command was refused. `step ssh renew` does not renew a user certificate (`sshpop certificate must be a host ssh certificate`): a person signs in again, which gave a new certificate, on the same key, with a new serial, and `aries` was admitted again. What Aries would rely on in a certificate:

| Field | Seen | Use |
|---|---|---|
| Principals | The authority's choice. With the JWK provisioner the *requester* chooses them: a request for key id `bob@example.com` with principals `alice@example.com` and `root` was signed | The only field sshd matches. Whoever can make the authority sign a principal is that person |
| Key id | Free text, the authority's | sshd logs it. Audit only, never authorization |
| Serial | Random per certificate from step-ca; whatever `-z` says from `ssh-keygen` | Names one certificate, for revoking and for the log |
| Validity | Enforced by sshd at authentication only (below) | The window |
| Signing key | The authority's | Which authority |
| Critical options | `force-command` and `source-address` are honored by sshd; an unknown one is refused (`is not supported`) | Aries sets none and needs none. A certificate whose `force-command` differs from the line's command is refused by sshd; one equal to it is admitted. `source-address` that does not match: `Refused by certificate options` |
| Extensions | `permit-pty` and the rest by default; an unknown extension is admitted and reaches the forced command | Ignored: the line's `restrict` already removes them. A certificate with none (`-O clear`) is admitted |

The authority's own revocation (`step ssh revoke SERIAL`) changed nothing at sshd: the certificate was admitted until it expired. A host certificate offered as a user's, and a certificate from another authority, were refused.

**3. What identifies a connection.** With `ExposeAuthInfo yes`, sshd gives the forced command `SSH_USER_AUTH`, the path of a file (mode 0600, the account's) holding `publickey TYPE BASE64`: **the whole certificate**, from which the key id, serial, validity, principals and signing key are read. It is there with `restrict` on the line. A client that sent `SetEnv SSH_USER_AUTH=…` did not change it, with `ExposeAuthInfo` on or off (off, the variable is simply unset); a client's `LC_*` variable did arrive, since Ubuntu accepts those. sshd passes the forced command nothing else about the certificate: which principal matched is known only from the line's own `command=`.

| Tried | Result |
|---|---|
| A certificate expires while its connection is open (`aries watch`, 100 seconds past expiry) | The connection stays open and still receives pushed events. sshd checks validity once |
| A principal is disabled while its certificate is valid and its line still in the file | Today's daemon, unchanged: the open connection closed at once, and the next sign-in passed sshd and was refused by the daemon, `alice is disabled on this server` |
| The line is removed from the file | The next sign-in is refused by sshd; nothing happens to an open connection |
| `RevokedKeys` naming a file that is missing, or that is not a key list | **Every key is refused, the break-glass key in `authorized_keys` included** |
| `RevokedKeys`: a KRL made by `ssh-keygen -k` revoking a serial, a range of serials, or a key id | That certificate is refused; the same person's other device and other people are admitted |
| `RevokedKeys`: a text file holding one device's public key, or its certificate | The key and every certificate on it are refused, a certificate issued afterwards too |

**4. Groups.** A certificate carried a person's principal and a group's. sshd takes **the first line of the file that matches any of the certificate's principals**: the file's order decides, not the certificate's. The forced command is not told which matched, but reads the whole certificate from `SSH_USER_AUTH`, so a line for a group could still learn the person. A line naming two principals separated by a comma matches nothing.

**5. Agents.** A launcher that held the provisioner's secret minted a one-time token for `ci-nightly@agents.example.com` (`step ca token --ssh`); the agent, holding only the token, made a key and exchanged it for a certificate; the patched `aries`, with no ssh-agent, signed in with the key and certificate files, and its issue is recorded under `ci-nightly`. The token was refused a second time, refused for another principal (`principals does not match`), and refused for a lifetime over the provisioner's maximum.

**Three more findings that shape the design.**

- **`cert-authority` lines work in an `AuthorizedKeysFile`, and so in the file ariesd writes.** With neither `TrustedUserCAKeys` nor `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile` configured, a line `cert-authority,principals="alice@example.com",restrict,command="…" CAKEY` admitted alice's certificate and ran its command, refused a certificate for anyone else, and gave the command the same `SSH_USER_AUTH`. It worked appended to `aries_keys` too, until ariesd's next tick rewrote the file. Where both mechanisms could admit a certificate, the `authorized_keys` line was the one used.
- **A line with no `command=` is a shell.** A principals-file line that was only `restrict alice@example.com` ran the command the client asked for.
- **A certificate authority trusted for the whole machine opens the `aries` account, today.** With `TrustedUserCAKeys` set outside the `Match` block, as a company that already uses SSH certificates has it, and the guide's block as it is, a certificate from that authority whose principal is the account's name, `aries`, was admitted with no forced command. `TrustedUserCAKeys none` in the block refused it and left the `cert-authority` lines working; `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile none` and `AuthorizedPrincipalsCommand none` are accepted there. This is a gap in the server guide's block whatever is decided here, of the kind `AuthorizedKeysCommand none` already closes.

**Dolt and forgetting.** A row deleted from a Dolt table is still read back from history (`AS OF`, `dolt_history_…`), and its bytes are still in the repository's files after `dolt_gc`, full or not. A table named in `dolt_ignore` is left out of `DOLT_COMMIT('-Am', …)`, which is how the store commits: it has no history, and after its row was deleted and `dolt_gc` run the bytes were gone from disk (before the collection they were still there). It keeps its rows across processes, in the working set.

### 17.2 The model

A principal is one row of the control store, as now. What changes is what its columns mean and what hangs beside it.

| Thing | What it is | Changes? |
|---|---|---|
| **Name** (the handle) | What Aries calls the principal, everywhere: the forced command, every project's event log, claims, assignments, inboxes, memories, usage, hours. The existing `principals.name`, pattern unchanged. `alice` on a small team; a generated handle, `u-` and eight characters (`u-7f3a2c9d`), where names are to be hidden | Never, and never reused: a reused handle would inherit a stranger's history |
