| **External identity** | The subject an authority asserts: the certificate principal sshd matches, such as `alice@example.com`. Unique on the server. Optional: a principal with none has keys only | Yes (a new address, another authority), with no trace in any project |
| **Display name** | How people see the principal: `Alice Example`. Optional, not unique | Yes |
| **Kind** and **owner** | `person` or `agent`; for an agent, the principal that answers for it (§17.6) | Owner yes |

The name is the only one of the three that is ever written into a project's database or the control store's event log. The other two are personal data in the plain sense, and live in one table that has no history (§17.7).

| Place | Shows |
|---|---|
| A project's event log and every table in it | The name. No project schema changes |
| The control store's event log | The name. An identity change records that it happened, who made it and which fields are now set, never their values |
| `aries` printing to a person (`who`, `list`, `show`, `history`, the board, `cost --by person`) | The name, with the display name beside it where the caller may see it (§17.7): `u-7f3a2c9d (Alice Example)`. `--json` prints names only |
| MCP results | The name only. A display name costs tokens, and a tool result is sent to a model provider, which is one more place a person's name would be kept |
| Telemetry (§16.4) | `telemetry.principals` applies to the name: a pseudonym of it by default, the name itself, or nothing. Never the display name or the external identity |
| The journal and the audit stream (§16) | The name |
| The file ariesd writes for sshd | The external identity, in a `principals="…"` option. It is rewritten without it when the identity is removed |
| sshd's own log | The certificate's key id and serial, which are the authority's choice and usually an address. Outside Aries's control; the guide says so |
| `aries admin principal list` and `show` | All three, to those who may see them |

### 17.3 Certificates, a second way in

Keys stay exactly as they are (§15.2). A certificate is another kind of line in the same file, for the same account, on the same port.

**The line.** For each active principal that has an external identity, and each authority the server trusts, ariesd writes:

```text
cert-authority,principals="alice@example.com",restrict,command="/usr/local/bin/ariesd stdio --principal u-7f3a2c9d --cert" ecdsa-sha2-nistp256 AAAA…
```

after the key lines, by principal and then authority, so an unchanged store still gives identical bytes. An external identity is restricted to letters, digits and `._@+-`, 255 at most, so that it needs no quoting inside `principals="…"` and cannot be a pattern. Every line has a `command=`: the writer refuses to produce one without, since such a line is a shell (§17.1).

This is preferred to `TrustedUserCAKeys` with an `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile`, which was also measured and also works, because it adds no file, no writer and no sshd directive to what decision 22 built, and because `aries admin status`, the write-on-change and the stale-file property carry over unchanged. Its cost is that the trust in an authority is written by the `aries` account, not by root in sshd's configuration. That account already writes every key line, so nothing is lost.

**Settings** (`ariesd.yaml`, the operator's file, read at start; nothing over the protocol names an authority, as nothing over it names a server admin):

```yaml
certificates:
  authorities:               # public keys of the authorities trusted to name principals; two while one is rotated
    - ecdsa-sha2-nistp256 AAAA…
  max_lifetime: 24h          # a certificate valid for longer is refused by the daemon
  drop_at_expiry: true       # close a connection when its certificate expires
  expiry_grace: 60s
```

With no `authorities`, no certificate line is written and `--external` is still stored: a server can record identities before it trusts an authority.

**sshd.** The guide's block gains three lines, and the Docker image the same:

```text
Match User aries
    AuthenticationMethods publickey
    DisableForwarding yes
    PermitTTY no
    AuthorizedKeysFile .ssh/authorized_keys .ssh/aries_keys
    AuthorizedKeysCommand none
    TrustedUserCAKeys none
    AuthorizedPrincipalsFile none
    ExposeAuthInfo yes
```

The two `none` lines close the gap of §17.1 and belong in the guide now, with or without certificates. `ExposeAuthInfo yes` is what lets the daemon see the certificate.

**The bridge and the handshake.** `ariesd stdio --principal NAME --cert` reads the file `SSH_USER_AUTH` names, takes the `publickey` line's certificate, and puts it in the bridge frame (a new field; the bridge frame is not client protocol and is read leniently, §15.2). It reads nothing else from its environment. With `--cert` and no such file, it refuses with `auth`, and the fix says to add `ExposeAuthInfo yes`. The daemon, before a connection slot or a store is taken and again as the connection is recorded, refuses unless:

- the certificate parses, is a user certificate of at most 16 KiB, and its signature verifies under one of `authorities`;
- the store holds, for an active principal of that name, an external identity that is among the certificate's principals. This is what keeps the file a cache (§15.2): a stale line gets past sshd and no further;
- none of its other principals is the external identity of a *different* principal of the server. step-ca's default certificate carried `alice` and `alice@example.com`; if both were identities here, sshd would pick whichever line came first, so the daemon refuses the certificate rather than guess;
- now is within its validity, and its validity is no longer than `max_lifetime`;
- neither its serial nor its key is revoked (§17.4).

It then records on the connection, beside the principal and the project: the authority's fingerprint, the serial, the key id, the key's fingerprint and the expiry. What it cannot check is that this certificate is the one that authenticated this connection: that rests on sshd writing `SSH_USER_AUTH`, as a key's fingerprint rests on sshd choosing the line. The signature check means a file's content cannot invent a certificate.

**Moving a server, person by person.** A principal may have keys, an external identity, or both.

1. The operator adds the authority to the config, the three lines to sshd, and restarts.
2. `aries admin principal set NAME --external alice@example.com`: alice's certificate now works, beside her keys.
3. Once she has signed in with it (`aries admin cert list` shows the connection), `aries admin key remove` each of her keys. She is now certificate-only: a new laptop is nothing, and leaving is the authority no longer issuing.
4. Optionally, `certificates.keys: admins` (or `none`) makes ariesd write key lines only for the principals an admin marks, for the machines that cannot get a certificate.

Decision 19 extends: nobody sets their own external identity, since an agent holding a developer's credentials could point their principal at an identity it controls. Who may set another's is the rule for keys (§15.3): a server admin, or a project admin with reach.

**The first admin, break-glass, and the authority being down.** Nothing changes for the first admin: a bare key in the hand-maintained `authorized_keys`, which sshd reads first. It is also the way in when the authority is down, and the reason ariesd writes no `RevokedKeys` file (§17.4). Aries never contacts the authority, so its outage stops nobody who holds a valid certificate; it stops renewal. Connections then end as certificates expire, so `max_lifetime` is both the revocation window and how long a team survives the outage. A server admin should keep one key in `authorized_keys`.

**`aries admin` gains:**

```text
aries admin principal add [NAME] [--display D] [--external ID] [--kind agent --owner NAME] [--key FILE]
aries admin principal set NAME [--display D] [--external ID] [--owner NAME]     "" clears a field
aries admin principal show NAME | forget NAME
aries admin cert list [NAME]            connections that signed in by certificate: key id, serial, key, expires
aries admin cert revoke --serial N | --key FINGERPRINT [--until TIME]
aries admin cert revoked | unrevoke ID
```

`principal add` with no NAME generates a handle. `status` gains the authorities' fingerprints, and its BY HAND column is joined by KEY and CERT.

### 17.4 What identifies a connection, and how access ends

A key's fingerprint is replaced by what §17.3 records: the authority, the serial, and the fingerprint of the key the certificate certifies. The serial names one sign-in; the key names one device, for as long as the person's tool keeps the key (step-ca's `--sign` reuses it; a tool that makes a new key each sign-in gives a device no lasting name, and then only the principal can be cut off).

| To end | Do | Takes effect |
|---|---|---|
| A person's access | `principal disable`, as today | At once: connections closed, claims released (decisions 20 and 21), the line gone from the file. Measured with a certificate and today's daemon |
| A person's access, by doing nothing in Aries | The authority stops issuing | When the last certificate expires: at most `max_lifetime`. With `drop_at_expiry`, the connection too |
| One device or one sign-in | `aries admin cert revoke --key …` or `--serial …` | At once: matching connections closed, the next handshake refused |
| A membership | `member remove`, as today | At once |
| An authority | Remove it from `authorities` and restart | At the restart |

- **Revocations live in the control store** (a table, an event each, a cap of 10,000 under rule 18). One by serial is kept until the certificate it names would have expired and is then pruned, which is bookkeeping (rule 7, `wtx.quiet`); so that list is bounded by the lifetime. One by key lasts until `--until`, or until it is removed.
- **ariesd writes no `RevokedKeys` file.** sshd refuses every key while that file is missing or malformed, the break-glass key included (§17.1), so a failed write would lock out the people who could repair it. Revocation is the daemon's, at the handshake, like every other decision of the store. An operator whose company publishes a KRL may name it in sshd themselves.
- **The daemon closes a connection when its certificate expires** (`drop_at_expiry`, after `expiry_grace`), because sshd does not. Without this a long `aries mcp` session would outlive its sign-in by days. The client reconnects, with whatever certificate it then finds. Claims are the store's and are untouched: a lease the client no longer renews expires as it always has.
- **The window.** A certificate whose principal is active and unrevoked works until it expires, whatever the authority has since learned. Aries does not read the authority's revocations (step-ca's had no effect at sshd). The lifetime the authority issues, capped by `max_lifetime`, is the whole of that window: 8 to 16 hours for people and an hour or less for agents are the usual choices (general knowledge, not measured).

### 17.5 Groups

A line for a group principal can admit a connection, and the daemon could learn the person from the certificate (§17.1, 4). The design does not use this. **Membership stays a row in the control store, and a certificate's other principals are ignored.** A company's groups reach Aries through provisioning (§17.8), which turns them into the same rows an admin would write.

What membership by a certificate's group would cost:

| Rule today | With membership read from a certificate |
|---|---|
| The control store decides access | The authority does, at each sign-in. `member list` no longer says who can reach a project |
| Every grant and revocation is an event (rule 7) | Joining or leaving a group writes nothing. The audit trail of access is the directory's |
| Losing access releases claims at once (decision 21) | Leaving a group is seen only when a certificate expires and the next one lacks the group: no moment to release at, and up to a lifetime late |
| A project admin's reach is computed from memberships (§15.3, decision 17) | Reach would depend on certificates not yet presented |
| Disabled wins (decision 20) | Still holds, if every person keeps a row |

If groups are wanted in Aries itself later, the form that keeps these rules is a group as a control-store object (a named set of principals with a role on projects), written by admins or by the provisioning job, never read from a credential.

### 17.6 Agents

**An agent a person runs at their desk** signs in with that person's certificate, from their ssh-agent, as their principal, as it does with their key today. Its sessions are told apart by session id and harness. Nothing changes, except that the credential now expires overnight.

**An unattended agent** (CI, a cloud sandbox, a Bearings worker) is a principal of its own, as the server guide already advises and decision 8 requires:

- `kind: agent`, with an `owner`: the principal that answers for it. Its handle is what every event of its work carries, so its work is attributable to it; the control store says who owns it.
- Its external identity is one the authority issues only to that workload, such as `ci-nightly@agents.example.com`. It gets a certificate by exchanging an identity its platform already gives it (a CI system's OpenID Connect token, a cloud instance's identity document, a Kubernetes service account) at the authority. That exchange is the authority's feature and was not run; what was run is its shape, a one-time token for a certificate (§17.1, 5), in which the token fixed the principal and could not be used twice.
- It holds no long-lived secret. The key is made for the run, the certificate lasts the run (an hour or less), and `aries` reads both from files (`key:` and `KEY-cert.pub`), since such a machine has no ssh-agent. This needs the client change of §17.9.
- **Which launch it was** (the run, and who or what started it) belongs in the certificate's key id, which the authority writes. The daemon records the key id and serial for the connection and writes them to the audit stream with the session id (§16), so a session's work in a project's log joins to the launch. An extension is not used for this: sshd admits unknown extensions, but an authority's template has to add them, and the key id is already logged by sshd.
- Where no authority can issue to a workload, a key of its own remains the way, as today.

This is what makes decision 8's "distinct agent identities" cheap: an identity is a row, made by the provisioning job or by `principal add --kind agent`, with no key to distribute. `kind` is also where decision 8's narrower default rights attach; those rights are that decision's work, not this section's.

### 17.7 Hiding who a person is

§15.6's three depths. The first is §16.4. This is the second, and an honest account of the third.

**The schema** (one control migration, `0002`, described, not written):

- `principals` gains `kind` (`person` by default), `owner` (empty for a person) and `source` (empty, or the principal of the provisioning job that created the row, §17.8). The states gain `forgotten`.
- A new table `principal_identities`: `name` (primary key), `display_name`, `external_id` (unique where set), `updated_by`, `updated_at`, `write_id`. **It is named in `dolt_ignore`,** so Dolt commits no version of it (§17.1). It is written through `Store.write` like any table, in the same transaction as its event, and the event (`identity.set`, `identity.clear`) carries the target and the names of the fields set, no values.
- The index gains the map from external identity to name, for the handshake.

**Who may see the mapping.**

| Caller | Sees |
|---|---|
| A server admin | Every principal's display name and external identity |
| A project admin | Both, for principals in a project they administer |
| A member | The display names of principals they share an active project with, if `directory: members` (the default). With `directory: admins`, names only |
| A principal | Its own |
| An agent over MCP | Names only, always |

A new read, `names`, takes up to 200 names and returns the display names the caller may see; `aries` asks it once per command, on the connection it already has, when it prints to a person, and keeps nothing on disk. So a member sees `u-7f3a2c9d (Alice Example)` in `who` and `list`; an admin sees the address as well in `aries admin principal list`. A mention is typed as `@u-7f3a2c9d`, or as a display name that `aries` resolves when it is unique among what the caller can see; the text stored holds the handle.

**Forgetting a person:** `aries admin principal forget NAME`, a server admin's. In one control write it disables the principal if it is not already (so decisions 20 and 21 apply: connections closed, claims released), removes its keys, its memberships and its row of `principal_identities`, and sets its state to `forgotten`, which is final: it cannot be enabled, and its name is never given out again. What that leaves behind, which the guide must say plainly:

- The handle, in every project's log, with everything it did and when. If handles were generated, nothing in Aries now says whose it was.
- Anything typed about or by the person: titles, comments, handoff notes, memories. Aries cannot find a name in free text.
- The session's machine name, which every event carries and which often contains a person's name (`alices-macbook`). A setting, `record_machine: name | hash | off`, should come with this work.
- The comments of keys already removed (`alice@laptop`), which are in the control database's Dolt history. A server that hides names should add keys without comments, or use certificates.
- The deleted identity row's bytes, until Dolt next collects garbage; and every backup taken before.
- Outside aries: sshd's log (key ids), the telemetry backend (pseudonyms, which deleting the key file of §16.4 unlinks), agent transcripts, git history.

**An existing server's names.** Names already written stay: `alice` is a handle that happens to be a name, and nothing breaks. An existing principal can be given a display name and an external identity at once. To stop adding to a name-like handle, an admin creates a new principal with a generated handle, moves the memberships, and disables the old one; the old history stays under the old name, and Aries records no link between the two. Only the third depth removes the old name.

**The third depth: erasing a name already written.** What it would take, honestly:

- A principal's name is text in about a dozen tables of every project (the event log and its JSON before and after states, claims, the agents registry, inboxes, handoffs, acceptance, issue paths, usage, hours, plans, memories, and the issues and comments themselves). Rewriting it means editing the event log, which principle 3 and every replay test treat as append-only.
- Rewriting the rows erases nothing by itself, because Dolt keeps every earlier version (measured, §17.1). The history has to go too: in practice, the project's current state exported into a new database with no history, the old database dropped and collected, and every backup aged out. That loses the second audit trail (§2) for everyone in the project, not only for the person.
- It is the opposite of what an audit log is for, which is the usual ground for keeping one.

When it is needed, in the terms of obligations a company commonly has (this is not legal advice, and a company's own counsel decides): when a handle still identifies the person, as a name-like handle or a handle on a team of three does, and an erasure duty applies that no retention ground outweighs; or when a contract requires a customer's data gone at the end of an engagement, which is better met by **deleting the project** than by rewriting it. `aries admin project delete` does not exist; archiving is all there is. It would be the cheaper thing to build first. Pseudonymous records generally remain personal data while a mapping exists, which is the reason the mapping here can be destroyed without touching any history. Two further things an enterprise will ask about and this design only notes: `aries cost --by person` and hours are per-person measurement, which some works agreements restrict; and what is sent to processors (telemetry, model providers) is covered by §16.4 and by keeping display names out of MCP.

### 17.8 Provisioning

Who is a member is a separate question from how they prove who they are. It is answered by a job the company runs, from anywhere `aries` can reach the server, on a schedule: it reads the directory and runs `aries admin`. ariesd gains no HTTP endpoint (decision 9), and no knowledge of any directory.

```text
directory ──(the company's own query: SCIM, LDAP, an export)──> desired.yaml
desired.yaml ──> aries admin apply -f desired.yaml [--dry-run] [--prune] ──ssh──> ariesd
```

`desired.yaml` lists principals by external identity, each with a display name, a kind, and the projects and roles it should have. Only producing that file is specific to a company; the guide gives an example that builds it from a CSV. What `aries admin` needs so that the job is safe to rerun and safe to leave alone:

- **Idempotent verbs.** `principal add` for a principal that exists as asked, `member add` for a membership that exists, and a removal of what is absent all succeed and write nothing. Several control writes already behave so (§15.1); the rest are made to.
- **Lookup by external identity** (`--external`), since the job never knows a handle. A new person gets a generated one.
- **`apply`**: read the current state, print or perform the difference. `--dry-run` performs nothing. `--json` prints one document either way: what was added, changed, disabled and left alone, and what was refused and why. Exit 0 only if nothing was refused.
- **Guards against a bad export.** Nothing is disabled or removed without `--prune`. With it, a run that would disable more than `--max-remove` principals (10 by default) does nothing and exits 1: an empty file from a failed directory query must not lock out a company. A leaver is disabled, never forgotten; forgetting is a person's decision.
- **A principal for the job with only the rights it needs:** a new server-level role, `provisioners:` in the config beside `admins:`. A provisioner may create principals, set their display name and external identity, and add them as plain members to the projects the config lists for it; it may disable, and change, only principals whose `source` is itself. It may not add a key, grant an admin role, touch a server admin or a principal someone else created, or create or archive a project. So a stolen job credential can admit people the authority will vouch for, to listed projects, and no more. It cannot sign in as anyone: it sets identities, and a certificate still has to come from the authority. The job is an agent (§17.6) and gets its own certificate or key.
- **Rate.** Control writes are limited to one a second with a burst of 30 (§15.4), so a first run for 500 people takes about eight minutes. That is acceptable for a job; a batch op is a later optimization, not a need.

A directory's groups become memberships here, in the file, where the mapping is visible and reviewable, and every resulting change is an event with the job's principal on it.

### 17.9 The client

Independent of everything above, and useful today, since a `cert-authority` line written by hand into `authorized_keys` already works with today's daemon (§17.1).

- **Which credential.** With `key:` set, `aries` offers only that key: its certificate first (the file `KEY-cert.pub` beside it, or the agent's certificate for the same key), then the bare key. The agent is still used to sign when the key file has a passphrase. With no `key:`, it offers the agent's certificates first, then its bare keys, in the agent's order. A certificate is tried before a bare key of the same person because it is the newer, shorter-lived proof, and because a key that is also in the store for another principal must not win by accident (§17.1). `key:` may also be a `SHA256:` fingerprint, which selects that key from the agent, for a person whose agent holds many.
- **A certificate that has expired is not offered,** and if nothing else gets in, the refusal says so: when it expired, and to sign in again. A committed, optional `server.sign_in_hint` in `.aries.yaml` is quoted in that fix (`run: step ssh login`), escaped like all server text. It is words for a person, never a command `aries` runs: a command in a committed file is code execution from a cloned repository (§2).
- **`Too many authentication failures`** becomes an `auth` refusal that names the cause and the fix (`set key:`), in place of today's advice to check the host and port.
- **`aries whoami`** prints the principal the server took this connection for (the welcome already carries it), its display name, how it signed in (key fingerprint, or the certificate's key id, serial and expiry), and the server. `prime` and the MCP notices gain one line when the certificate expires within ten minutes. These read new fields of the welcome, which a client ignores if it does not know them.
- **`aries` does not obtain certificates.** The company's tool does. `aries` only finds what that tool left in the agent or beside the key.

### 17.10 What this does not solve

- **Signing in to Aries itself in a browser, a device-code flow, or SCIM pushed by the directory.** Each needs an HTTPS endpoint (decision 9 defers it). Here the authority does the single sign-on and Aries sees only its certificate; the directory is pulled by a job.
- **The second factor.** Aries cannot tell whether one was used. It trusts the authority's policy.
- **An authority that signs what it should not.** Whoever can make it sign a principal is that person; the JWK provisioner signed any principal its secret's holder asked for. Trusting an authority is trusting everyone who can configure it.
- **Instant removal with no action in Aries.** Without `principal disable` or the job, a leaver has until their certificate expires.
- **The authority's own revocations,** which Aries does not read.
- **Anonymity.** A handle hides a name, not a pattern of work, and not what people type.
- **Machines that cannot reach SSH,** such as hosted sandboxes behind HTTP proxies ([bearings.md §3.14](bearings.md#314-unattended-operation)).

### 17.11 Plan

Each slice leaves `main` working, and every one is off until configured. The protocol is being reset to a single version on another branch; until that lands, "protocol" below means a change under rule 10 (a new op or argument, refused by an older server), and after it, whatever that branch makes of such a change. New fields of the welcome and of results need none.

| Slice | Delivers | Protocol |
|---|---|---|
| I0 | The guide's `Match` block and the Docker image gain `TrustedUserCAKeys none` and `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile none`; the smoke test offers a certificate naming `aries` from a globally trusted authority and expects a refusal | None. Documentation and the image |
| I1 | The client of §17.9: the certificate beside `key:`, one key when `key:` is set, certificates first, the two refusals reworded, `aries whoami` | None |
| I2 | Control migration `0002`; `display_name`, `external_id`, `kind`, `owner`; `principal add` arguments, `principal set` and `show`; the `names` read and the CLI's display; `directory:` | New ops and arguments |
| I3 | Certificates in the server: the settings, `cert-authority` lines in the keys file, `--cert` and the bridge frame's field, the handshake's checks, the connection's record, `drop_at_expiry`, `status`; the image and smoke test with a throwaway authority; the guide | None for clients: the bridge frame is not client protocol, and the welcome only gains fields |
| I4 | `cert list`, `revoke`, `revoked`, `unrevoke`; the revocation table and its cap | New ops |
| I5 | `principal forget` and the `forgotten` state; `record_machine`; the guide's account of what forgetting leaves | A new op |
| I6 | Provisioning: idempotent verbs, `--external` lookup, `apply` with `--dry-run`, `--prune` and `--max-remove`, the `provisioners:` role and `source` | New arguments; the role is the server's |
| I7 | Agents: `kind: agent` in the audit stream with the certificate's key id and serial; with decision 8's work, the narrower rights | With decision 8 |

I0 and I1 can go now. I2 is worth having without certificates. I3 needs I2 for the external identity.

**Since this was written (12 Oct 2026).** I0's configuration is done: the guide's `Match` blocks and the Docker image set `TrustedUserCAKeys none` and `AuthorizedPrincipalsFile none` (§15.2, "A globally trusted certificate authority"); the smoke test does not yet offer a certificate that must be refused. Of I1, the maintainer has decided that a configured `key:` is the only key `aries` offers and that `aries whoami` says who you are; that is being built with the fixes for the security review of stage 3a. Decision 26 has sshd read the generated file before `authorized_keys`, which the `cert-authority` lines of §17.3 would follow. The rest of this section is undecided: it is a design for the maintainer to accept, change or put aside, and its questions are in §17.12.

### 17.12 Decisions for the maintainer

1. **Do certificates become a way in?** Recommended: yes, a second way beside keys on the same server, never required. *Cost of yes:* slices I2 to I4, and a mode to test, document and secure, though it reuses decision 22's file. *Cost of no:* keys only, and the concern of §15.6 stands. *Cost of certificates only:* small teams need an authority to try Aries.
2. **Which sshd mechanism?** Recommended: `cert-authority` lines in the file ariesd writes. *Cost:* the trust in an authority is written by the `aries` account. *The alternative,* `TrustedUserCAKeys` and a principals file ariesd writes: root owns the trust, at the price of a second generated file, two more sshd directives, and a second stale-file case.
3. **Who names an authority?** Recommended: the operator, in the config file, only. *Cost:* a restart to rotate. *The alternative,* `aries admin authority add`: a server admin could mint a way in as anyone, where today decision 19 at least stops them adding their own key.
4. **May membership follow a group in a certificate?** Recommended: no; groups arrive through provisioning as rows (§17.5). *Cost of no:* a company must run the job. *Cost of yes:* the table of §17.5.
5. **Is the second depth of hiding wanted, and are handles the default?** Recommended: build it (I2, I5); names stay free by default, with `principal_names: generated` for servers that want handles. *Cost of generated by default:* every small team reads `u-7f3a2c9d` until it sets display names.
6. **Where does the mapping live?** Recommended: a table named in `dolt_ignore`, pending one check that ariesd's locked-down account may write `dolt_ignore` and that the server collects garbage. *Costs:* it is outside the backup tag `ariesd upgrade` makes, so the guide must say how it is backed up; and a deleted row's bytes last until a collection. *Alternatives:* a 0600 file beside the telemetry key (no SQL, a second store to keep consistent), or an encrypted column whose per-person key is what gets deleted (history keeps only ciphertext; more machinery).
7. **Who sees display names?** Recommended: members who share a project, by default, with `directory: admins` to restrict. *Cost of admins only by default:* a team cannot tell who holds an issue.
8. **Does the daemon close a connection when its certificate expires?** Recommended: yes by default. *Cost:* every long session reconnects once a lifetime, and an authority outage ends sessions as certificates lapse. *Cost of no:* a session outlives its sign-in without limit.
9. **Should `key:` mean only that key?** Recommended: yes, with certificates offered first. *Cost:* someone whose `key:` file is wrong and who has been getting in by their agent stops getting in, with a refusal that says why. It also fixes signing in as the wrong principal, which happens today.
10. **What is an unattended agent's identity?** Recommended: a principal of its own for each worker, `kind: agent` with an owner, the launch in the certificate's key id. *Cost:* more rows (cheap with provisioning), and the audit stream is needed to join a session to a launch. *The alternative,* one principal for a fleet: fewer rows, and decision 8's concern returns.
11. **A provisioner role, or does the job run as a server admin?** Recommended: the role. *Cost:* a third tier to explain and test. *Cost of an admin:* a stolen job credential is total control.
12. **Does ariesd ever write sshd's `RevokedKeys`?** Recommended: no. *Cost:* revocation depends on the daemon running, which every sign-in already does.
13. **Is erasing a name from history (the third depth) wanted?** Recommended: not now; build `project delete` first if a contract requires data gone. *Cost of not:* a server that already has name-like handles cannot remove them from its logs.


### 17.13 Not tested

- An OpenID Connect sign-in at any authority, a workload identity exchanged for a certificate, and any authority other than step-ca.
- Everything of §17.3 to §17.9 as code: only the sshd behavior they rest on, the client patch, and today's daemon behind a line that names no key.
- `cert-authority` lines at scale (thousands of lines in the file), and sshd's time to scan them.
- `dolt_ignore` and `dolt_gc` through `dolt sql-server` as ariesd's locked-down account; whether a backup or a clone carries an ignored table (the one attempt gave no output either way).
- ssh-agent on Windows, FIDO (`sk-`) keys with certificates, and an RSA authority.
- OpenSSH other than 9.6p1 as the server. 9.8 and later penalize an address for failed authentications (`PerSourcePenalties`), which a client that offers many keys would meet sooner.
- Whether `aries mcp` reconnects by itself when the daemon closes its connection, which `drop_at_expiry` relies on.
- systemd, the providers' images and SELinux, as for the rest of §15.
- The clock (§17.14): no certificate was presented to an sshd whose clock had been set back or forward, no connection was held across its certificate's expiry on a suspended machine, and no revocation was pruned. What §17.14 says of sshd reading the wall clock is general knowledge of OpenSSH, not a measurement.

### 17.14 Clocks

Recorded 12 Oct 2026 at the maintainer's instruction (decision 37). Three clocks touch a certificate, and only one of them decides anything.

| Clock | Decides | Does not decide |
|---|---|---|
| The authority's | The validity window it writes into the certificate, which is signed: nobody who holds the certificate can edit it | Whether the certificate is accepted on a given day |
| The server's | Whether now is inside that window: sshd at authentication, and the daemon at the handshake and when it drops a connection at expiry (§17.3, §17.4) | |
| The person's own | Nothing. A certificate is not made valid, or kept valid, by the clock of the machine that presents it. Setting a laptop's clock back does not revive an expired certificate, and does not delay a revocation | |

So the exposure is the server's clock, and the certificate design must say what a wrong one does.

- **The server's clock is set back.** sshd reads the wall clock, so an expired certificate is valid again for as long as the clock stays behind its expiry, and Aries cannot change what sshd admits. The daemon's own checks need not follow: its check of the window at the handshake should use the store's time, which does not go backward (decision 37), so a certificate that the daemon has seen expire stays expired for the daemon however the clock is then set. That holds for a running daemon and, through the high-water mark, across a restart; and with `clock_behind: stop` a daemon whose clock is behind its history does not start at all.
- **Revocations and pruning.** §17.4 prunes a revocation by serial once the certificate it names has expired. With a clock that can go back, that is too early by exactly the amount it goes back: the certificate is valid again and its revocation is gone. A revocation should be kept a margin past the certificate's expiry (the certificate's lifetime again, capped by `max_lifetime`, is the natural margin), and "expired" for pruning should be read on the store's time, never the wall clock, so that a clock set back prunes nothing early and a pruned revocation's certificate is one the daemon already refuses as expired.
- **Dropping a connection at its certificate's expiry** (`drop_at_expiry`) needs both clocks, because either alone can be defeated. A timer on the monotonic clock is not moved by setting the wall clock, but does not run while the machine is suspended, so a connection would outlive its certificate by the length of a suspend. A check of the wall clock is not fooled by a suspend, but is by a clock set back. The design is therefore a monotonic timer set at the handshake, and a check of the expiry against the store's time on each request, the earlier of the two closing the connection.
- **`max_lifetime`** is checked from the certificate alone (its two signed times), so no clock enters it.

What the certificate design can rely on once it is built, from decision 37 as built (§7): a time that does not go backward in a running daemon or across a restart, shared by every store of the server, against which expiry and pruning can be read; a detected and reported step, so that an operator learns of a clock that moved; and a refusal to start with the clock behind history. What it cannot rely on: sshd's own check, which follows the wall clock; a jump forward, which expires certificates early (the safe direction, and an outage); and anything about the authority's clock, which Aries never sees. If the authority's clock is ahead, its certificates are not yet valid at the server; if behind, they expire early.

This subsection is part of the proposal, like the rest of §17: it changes no decision of §17.12 and builds nothing.

## 18. Working without the server: the stage 5 design

Designed 12 Oct 2026, from §8 and what stages 1 to 3b built. **Decided 13 Oct 2026 (§18.11; §14, decisions 39 to 45), and nothing here is built.** Where this differs from §8, it says so and why; §8 stays as the plan it was.

In short: stage 5 is built in three layers, each useful without the next. An **outbox** makes sure nothing an agent or a person meant to write is lost when the server cannot be reached. A **cache** lets a session keep the context of the work it already holds. **Conflicts** are parked on the server, with base, mine and theirs, when an operation written against an old state meets a newer one. The first cut is narrower than §8: no claims are made offline, the cache holds what a session read and not a copy of the project, and there is no hybrid logical clock.

### 18.1 What "offline" is, here

§8 was written with a laptop on a plane in mind. For an AI coding agent that is the rare case: most models are themselves reached over the network, so a session with no network does no work at all. The cases that happen are these, in the order they are expected:

| Case | How long | What still works |
|---|---|---|
| The server is restarting or upgrading, or Dolt is slow | Seconds to minutes | The model, git, the session |
| The path to the server is down: a VPN, an IAP token, a firewall, sshd | Minutes to hours | The model, git, the session |
| The server is down | Hours | The model, git, the session |
| No network, with a model that runs on the machine | Hours to days | An open-source model run locally, git |
| No network, a person at the CLI | Hours to days | Reading, writing notes and issues |

In the first three the session goes on producing work, and today what it tries to write is refused: a handoff, the `finish` of an issue, a comment, a memory, token usage. `aries mcp` redials and retries with the same idempotency key (§7, As built), which covers seconds; past that the write is gone when the session ends. That loss is the first thing stage 5 fixes, and it is the larger part of the value: a handoff that did not arrive is context the next session pays for again.

So the design is judged by the server being unreachable while work continues, for up to a day, and it must not be wrong for longer. It is not judged by how much of the tracker can be used on a plane.

### 18.2 Principles kept, and what they rule out

- **One authority (principle 2).** The client never applies an operation to its own copy as if it had happened. What is queued is shown as queued. There is no merge between clients, and no client-side derivation of ready or blocked from a partial copy.
- **Fail closed and loud (principle 6).** Every answer from the cache says it is from the cache and how old it is, on stdout for a person and in the result for an agent. An operation that was queued returns that, never `{id, rev}` as if written. An outbox that cannot be written refuses the operation: nothing is queued in memory alone.
- **Every claim is fenced (principle 4), and time comes from the store (rule 19, decision 37).** A client's clock decides nothing. A queued operation carries when the client says it was made, which the server records as a claim and bounds; the event's time is the server's, at replay.
- **Non-invasive (principle 8).** One local store for each project on a machine, under the user's data directory, shared by every worktree and session. Nothing in the working tree.
- **Small binaries (rule 4).** `aries` is 11.9 MB today (linux/amd64, release flags) and links 12 modules. §2 names SQLite. Release builds have no cgo, so that would be a Go translation of SQLite, which is several megabytes and dozens of packages of generated code nobody here will audit. §18.4 proposes not to use it.

### 18.3 The outbox

**What may be queued.** An operation is queued only if replaying it later cannot do harm that the person or agent did not ask for:

| Queued when the server cannot be reached | Never queued: refused, with the reason |
|---|---|
| `create`, `comment`, `handoff`, `remember`, `usage`, `hours.log`, `label.add`, `label.rm`, `dep.add`, `accept` | `start`, and anything else that takes a claim (§18.6) |
| `update` of fields, with the `rev` it was based on | `close`, `reopen`, `forget`, `dep.rm`, `hours.delete`: they undo or end something, on a state the client cannot see |
| `finish`, as its parts (below) | Every admin op, and `renew` (the client's own chore) |

`finish` closes an issue, writes the handoff and files discovered work. Offline it is queued whole and replayed whole, and it succeeds only if the session still holds the claim at the epoch it had; otherwise its handoff is kept as a comment and its discovered issues are still created, as §8's "close vs. edit" rule has it, and the session's inbox says so.

**What an entry holds.** The op and its arguments exactly as they would have been sent; an idempotency key, made when the operation was first attempted; the `rev` and claim epoch it was based on, where the op has them; the session and machine that made it; the client's time; and a sequence number, which is the order of replay for that principal on that machine. It holds no secret: the same secrets lint that the store runs on memories runs before an entry is written.

**Replay.** On the next successful connection, before the request that caused it, the client replays the outbox in order, one operation at a time, each with its key. The server answers each as it would any request, plus two outcomes that are new: `parked` (§18.7) and `stale` (too old, §18.5). An entry leaves the outbox when the server has answered it, whatever the answer; a refusal is moved to a `refused` list the person can read, retry by hand or drop (`aries outbox`), and is never retried by itself. A connection lost mid-replay resumes at the same entry with the same key. Replay never blocks the session's own request for longer than a bound; what is left goes on in the background of `aries mcp`, and at the next command for the CLI.

**Who it runs as.** The principal is whoever's key authenticates at replay, as always. An entry written under one principal is not replayed by another: the outbox is keyed by server, project and principal. If access ended meanwhile, the entries are refused as `forbidden`, kept in the refused list, and nothing is lost from the machine.

**Bounds (rule 18).** An outbox has caps on entries, on bytes and on age, with defaults in the client and server-side caps on what one replay may bring. Past a cap, the operation is refused with a fix (`aries outbox`, or reach the server), not queued.

**Many processes.** A machine may run hundreds of sessions. Appends are serialized by a lock file held for the write only; replay is done by one process at a time under a second lock with a lease, so a session that dies mid-replay does not hold the rest back.

### 18.4 The local store, without SQLite

The outbox is an append-only file of NDJSON entries, synced on each append, with a second file of what has been answered; compaction rewrites both under the lock. It needs no index: it is read in order and is small by its caps.

The cache (§18.6) is a directory of files, one for each thing cached, named by a hash of what it is, written whole and renamed into place. Both are mode 0600 in a 0700 directory, as the token-usage state already is (`internal/capture`).

This is standard library only and adds nothing to the binary. What it gives up is querying: there is no offline `list` with filters over the whole project, and no offline search. §18.6 argues the first cut does not need them. If the maintainer wants a full local copy (§18.11, question 2), an embedded store is needed and the choice is between a small pure-Go key-value store and translated SQLite; a spike would measure both for size, module count and behavior with many processes on Linux, macOS and Windows before either is chosen, as stage 0 did for Dolt.

### 18.5 Idempotency over a long gap

Stage 3 built idempotency keys for `create`, `finish`, `comment` and `handoff`, later `remember` and `hours.log`: per principal, kept on the operation's last event, and a key older than 24 hours is refused. An outbox changes two things.

- **Every queued op needs a key.** `update`, `label.add`, `label.rm`, `dep.add`, `accept` and `usage` gain an `idem` argument. That is a new argument field, so the protocol goes to 6 (rule 10), with 5 still accepted; a server at 5 is never sent a queued op it cannot deduplicate, so against an old server the client queues only the ops that server can.
- **The window.** A key that is replayed after its window must not be applied twice and must not be mistaken for a clash. The server keeps keys for a setting's length, proposed 7 days by default, and refuses an entry older than that as `stale`, with the fix that the person replays it by hand from `aries outbox` if they still want it. The client refuses to queue past the same age. The window is a server setting because it is the server's table that grows.

### 18.6 The cache, and why claims are not made offline

**What is cached.** What a session was given: the result of `prime`, of `start` and `show` for the issues the session holds or looked at (with acceptance items, the last handoff and the memories that came with them), the session's inbox, and `digest`. Each is stored as the server returned it, with when. That is the context a session needs to go on with work it has, and to write a good handoff.

**What is not.** The rest of the project. `ready`, `blocked`, `list`, `who` and `claims` are answers about what everyone is doing now; from a cache they would be wrong in the way that makes two agents take the same work. Offline they are refused, saying when the server was last reached, except that `ready` and `list` may be shown from the cache to a person who asks for it by flag, marked stale on every line.

**Reads while connected are unchanged.** The cache is written on the way past and read only when the server cannot be reached, or cannot be reached within a short bound. It is never served in place of a server that answers.

**No offline claims, in the first cut.** §8 allows a provisional claim that loses on return. The cost of that rule is exactly what Aries exists to prevent: two sessions doing the same issue for hours, one of whose work is then a comment. An agent that cannot reach the server may finish what it holds and may file new issues; it may not start something new. This is the largest departure from §8 and is question 1 of §18.11.

**Work a session holds.** A lease is renewed by the client (principle 5); while the server cannot be reached, renewals fail and the lease runs out on the server's clock, after which the reaper returns the issue to open and another session may take it. Two things soften that. `aries away DURATION`, from §8, extends the caller's leases before a planned absence, within a cap the server sets. And a `finish` or `handoff` replayed after the lease ran out is not thrown away: if nobody else took the issue it applies, with an event that says the lease had lapsed; if somebody did, it is kept as a comment and both sessions' inboxes say so.

**What a cache means for access.** A cache is a copy of what a person could read, on their machine, and it stays there after their access ends: Aries cannot take back what it has shown. The cache is deleted by the client at the first connection that is refused as `forbidden` for the project, and `aries cache clear` removes it. It never holds another principal's private memories, because the server never sent them. This goes in the security model.

### 18.7 Conflicts, parked on the server

A conflict is an operation that was written against one state and arrives at another. Only `update` (and the edit inside a `finish`) can be one; every other queued op is an addition, which merges.

- **Detection.** `update` already carries `rev` and is refused when it is stale. A replayed `update` instead asks the server to compare field by field against the state at that `rev`, which the event log gives (principle 3): a field the op changes that nobody changed since applies; a field somebody did change is a conflict.
- **Parking.** The parts that apply are written. Each field in conflict becomes a row in a new `conflicts` table of the project's database: the issue, the field, base, mine and theirs, who and when, and the operation it came from. The server's value stays until someone resolves it. Parking is a write like any other: through `Store.write`, with a `conflict.park` event.
- **Resolving.** `mine`, `theirs`, or new text; a normal operation, guarded like any change to the issue, with a `conflict.resolve` event. A conflict nobody resolves is closed by a later change to the same field, with a note in the issue's history, and conflicts older than a setting are reported by `digest`.
- **Who is told.** The author's inbox, and the claim holder's when it is someone else. `prime` gives the count.
- **The rules of §8's table**, as kept: additions merge; different fields both apply; the same scalar field parks; status and claim, the server wins; close wins over edit, with the edit kept as a comment; delete wins over edit, with the edit kept on the tombstone. **Changed:** long text parks whole in the first cut, with no automatic three-way merge of hunks. A merge of text that is wrong is worse than a question, there is no diff3 in the standard library, and the resolver can show a diff. Merging hunks that do not overlap can follow once there are real conflicts to measure it against.
- **The recommendation** of §8 ("theirs is newer and from the claim holder; take theirs") is kept as text computed at read time from who holds the claim and the order of the two changes. It is advice, and never applied by itself.
- **A proposal** is an agent's or a person's suggested resolution, kept on the conflict's row with who made it and why, with a `conflict.propose` event. It is a write like any other and is guarded like a change to the issue. A later proposal replaces an earlier one. It is never applied by itself either: a person accepts it, which is a `resolve` that names the proposal, or resolves otherwise, which discards it (decision 42).
- **Bounds.** Open conflicts for each issue and for each principal are capped; past the cap the op is refused with a fix, not parked.

### 18.8 What people and agents see

- **CLI.** `aries outbox` (list, retry, drop), `aries conflicts` and `aries resolve ID --mine|--theirs|--text|--accept`, `aries away DURATION`, `aries cache clear`. A queued write prints `queued (server unreachable since …)` and exits 0, because it was accepted for delivery; `--no-queue` makes it fail as today, for scripts that must know. `--json` says `"queued": true`.
- **MCP.** A queued tool call returns one terse line saying so, and what the agent should do: carry on. The agent is never asked to manage the outbox. Conflicts reach an agent through `prime` and the inbox. An agent may propose a resolution to a conflict, and a person approves it (decision 42): the proposal is `mine`, `theirs` or new text, with a reason, stored with the conflict, and it changes nothing until a person accepts it with `aries resolve ID --accept`, or resolves another way. There is no `resolve` tool over MCP. Resolving is a judgment between two people's intent, and what the agent adds is its reading of the two sides. F4 settles how the agent reads base, mine and theirs within the result cap, and whether proposing is a new tool or an argument of one there is, against the schema cap of rule 12.
- **Status.** `aries whoami` and `prime` say when the server was last reached and how many operations wait.

### 18.9 What changes on the server

- Protocol 6: `idem` on the ops of §18.5; a replay marker on a request (the client's sequence and time), so the server knows to park and not refuse; the outcomes `parked` and `stale`; the ops `conflicts`, `conflict.propose`, `resolve` and `away`.
- A migration for `conflicts`, and one for the idempotency window if the key's table needs it.
- Events: `conflict.park`, `conflict.propose`, `conflict.resolve`, and the client's claimed time on a replayed operation's event, bounded by a setting as `clock_client_tolerance` bounds it today, and marked when it is out of bounds.
- Limits: the idempotency window, replayed operations for each connection, open conflicts.
- Telemetry and logs: counters for replayed, parked and stale operations, with events in the log schema.

### 18.10 Plan

| Slice | Delivers | Gate |
|---|---|---|
| F0 | Measurements: how often and how long sessions lose the server today (from the logs of real use), and the local store's behavior with 100 processes on three systems | The numbers, and the choice of §18.4 confirmed or changed |
| F1 | The outbox for the ops that have keys today, replay, `aries outbox`, the refused list | A session's handoff written during an outage arrives once, after restarts and dropped connections |
| F2 | Protocol 6: keys on the remaining ops, the window, `stale` | A replay after 24 hours applies once; one past the window is refused and kept |
| F3 | The cache of a session's own context; cached answers marked | `prime` and `show` of held work answer with the server down, and say so |
| F4 | Conflict parking and resolving, inbox, `digest` | Two edits of one field park; two of different fields both apply |
| F5 | `aries away`, and a late `finish` after a lapsed lease | Partition tests: the soak with the client cut from the server for minutes at a time, and its event log still equal to what the sessions were told |

F1 alone is worth releasing. F3 and F4 do not depend on each other.

### 18.11 Decisions

The maintainer answered the seven questions on 13 Oct 2026; they are decisions 39 to 45 of §14, which has his words. Six stand as proposed, and the fourth was changed.

1. **Offline claims.** None in the first cut: a session finishes what it holds and files new work, and starts nothing new without the server. §8 allows provisional claims.
2. **How much is cached.** What a session read, in files, with no new dependency. §8's copy of everything a person can see, which needs an embedded store in `aries`, is not built.
3. **The idempotency window.** 7 days by default, a server setting. It is how long an outbox entry can wait and still be delivered without a person.
4. **Conflicts over MCP.** Changed from the proposal, which only told agents of a conflict. An agent may propose a resolution, which is stored with the conflict, and a person approves it; there is no `resolve` tool over MCP (§18.7, §18.8).
5. **Long text.** Parks whole; no automatic merge of hunks until real conflicts show what it would save.
6. **A queued write exits 0,** with `--no-queue` for scripts.
7. **No hybrid logical clock.** With one authority the order is the server's, a client's order is its outbox's, and decision 37 already says a client's clock is a claim.

### 18.12 What this does not solve

- Two machines of one person, both offline, editing the same issue: both replay, and the second parks. Nothing reconciles the two outboxes with each other.
- A machine that is lost with a full outbox loses it. The outbox is durable on its disk, not replicated.
- An agent that works for a day against a stale picture of the project still wasted the day if the issue was closed meanwhile. The cache marks its age; it cannot know what changed.
- Bearings, the orchestrator, is a client like any other here. Whether it may queue is its design's question.

### 18.13 Not tested

Everything: this is a design. In particular, file locking with hundreds of processes on Windows, the cost of comparing a field against the state at an old `rev` on a long history, and how often real sessions lose the server at all, which F0 measures before anything is built.

**As built (stage 5, slices F0 and F1: the outbox, 13 Oct 2026).** F0 measured the local store of §18.4 with 100 and 500 processes on Linux and macOS (`spike/outbox/RESULTS.md`), and F1 is built on it: `internal/outbox`, the queue; `internal/offline`, which joins it to a connection; and their use by `aries` and `aries mcp` ([When the server cannot be reached](../cli.md#when-the-server-cannot-be-reached)). F1's gate passes in the test stack: a handoff, a comment, a create and the finish of a held issue, written with the daemon stopped through the CLI and through MCP, each arrive once when it is back, also after a restart and dropped connections, and a write whose answer was lost with its connection applies once when replayed. F2 to F5 are not built: there is no cache, no conflict parking, no `aries conflicts`, `resolve` or `cache clear`, and the protocol is still 5. Nothing of it has run on Windows, where it builds. Where the build differs from §18.3, §18.4 and §18.8, or settles what they left open:

- **What is queued** is the six ops that have a key in protocol 5: `create`, `comment`, `handoff`, `remember`, `hours.log` and `finish`. §18.3's table also lists `usage`, `label.add`, `label.rm`, `dep.add`, `accept` and `update`, which wait for the keys of F2.
- **"Not reached"** is one test, `client.Unreachable`: a refusal coded `unavailable`. That is every way a dial fails to get through (TCP, the IAP tunnel, the SSH handshake, sshd refusing the session or the bridge, the bridge closing), the bridge's or daemon's `unavailable` at the handshake (the daemon is not running, Dolt does not answer), a connection lost before the answer, and a request the server answers `unavailable`. It is never a refusal of the client or the person (`auth`, which a host key mismatch and an unknown key are; `version`; `forbidden`), of the request, or `busy`; nor a machine without `gcloud` for a server behind IAP, which is marked apart. A write lost with its connection may have been applied, and is queued with the key it was sent with.
- **The lock** (F0). §18.3 has a lock file with a lease. A lock file made with `O_EXCL` and a lease stalled every process for the lease when its holder died and let several hold it at once; the locks are `flock` and `LockFileEx`, on files that are never the data, which the kernel frees. **The replay lock has no lease** for the same reason: a lease that can be taken over is what lets two replayers run.
- **An appender reopens the outbox under the lock, every time** (F0): one that kept it open across a compaction lost most of its entries with no error.
- **Replay is at-least-once** (F0): a replayer that dies between sending an entry and noting the answer sends it again. So nothing is queued without an idempotency key, and the answered file is not synced for each answer.
- **The outbox library's own choices** are in `internal/outbox/doc.go`: every open of its files is under the append lock, so that nothing has one open when a compaction renames over it; the caps (1,000 entries, 4 MiB, 256 KiB an entry); and the age cap of **24 hours**, not §18.5's 7 days, because today's server refuses a key older than that. An entry past it is not sent and goes to the refused list as `stale`, which is the client's word here and not yet a server outcome.
- **Bounds.** There are no server-side caps on what one replay brings (§18.3): a replayed entry is an ordinary request, under the limits every request has.
- **A replayed entry carries no marker** (§18.9): the server cannot tell it from a first attempt. So the event's session and machine are those of the connection that delivers it, not of the session that wrote it, which the entry keeps only on the client. When another session of the principal delivers a `finish`, the server sees that session close an issue the first one holds: it applies, since a claim is guarded by principal and fenced by the epoch the entry carries, and the first session gets a `claim.lost` item. §18.3's late `finish` (kept as a comment when the claim is gone) is F5's: today it is refused, and waits in the refused list.
- **`send`'s answer.** Applied: gone. A refusal: the refused list, never retried by itself. `unavailable`, a lost connection and `busy`: the entry stays pending and the replay stops there, so order holds; an entry the server keeps failing with `unavailable` therefore holds the ones behind it until it goes stale. A `finish` or `handoff` refused as `invalid` with its paths is sent once more without them, as when it is not replayed.
- **Replay** runs on a new connection before the request that opened it, for the outbox's budget of 5 seconds; the CLI leaves the rest to the next command, and `aries mcp` to a goroutine that takes the connection between tool calls, and to the renewal tick when the session is idle. The hooks neither queue nor replay. A replay that runs out of time mid-request closes the connection, as a canceled call does, and the caller connects again; so each background round of `aries mcp` that is cut off dials the next one's connection, and a round that sent nothing is not followed by another, so that a server too slow to answer one write in the budget is not redialed without end. A tool call made meanwhile waits for the round in progress at most, and then at most for one replay of its own.
- **Where** (§18.2 "under the user's data directory"). `$XDG_DATA_HOME/aries` or `~/.local/share/aries`, `~/Library/Application Support/aries`, `%LocalAppData%\aries`; `ARIES_DATA_DIR` overrides. Not the cache directory `internal/capture` uses: what an outbox holds exists nowhere else. One directory for each server and project, named by a hash of the pinned host key and the project id (the server is its key, not its address), and in it one outbox for each principal, named by a hash of the name, never by a key.
- **Whose** (§18.3 "Who it runs as"), decided by the maintainer on 13 Oct 2026. Offline, the client does not know which principal the server would take its key for. At each handshake `aries` records, for the server and project, the accepted key's public fingerprint, the principal and when (`seen.json`, with a version, several keys to a principal). With a key configured the write goes to the outbox of that key's recorded principal; with none, to that of the key accepted the last time none was configured, while it is still offered; otherwise nothing is queued. An outbox is replayed only on a connection whose principal is its own; when the server has moved a key to another principal the entries are left and the person is told. The directories are checked to be the user's own, 0700, before every use, and refused otherwise.
- **§18.8.** Built: `aries outbox` (list, `retry N`, `drop N`, and `stats`, which §18.8 does not have), the `queued (server unreachable since …)` line with exit 0, `"queued": true`, `--no-queue` and `ARIES_NO_QUEUE`, the MCP line, and the outbox's line in `prime`'s notices. Not built: "when the server was last reached" in `aries whoami` and `prime`; `aries outbox` says when it was last *not* reached. `aries away` predates this section.
- **Counts.** The outbox counts, per op, what it queued, delivered, refused, found stale and dropped, how long delivered entries waited, and the outages (`aries outbox stats`): F0 could not measure how often sessions lose the server from logs, and this measures it from now on. They stay on the machine.
- **Decided by the maintainer on 13 Oct 2026**, on six things F1 raised. (1) An entry the server keeps answering `unavailable` holds those behind it until it is stale: "Keep ordering for now." (2) When access has ended the handshake is refused `forbidden`, so nothing is replayed and the entries go stale; they should be marked refused as `forbidden`, as §18.3 has it. Agreed; not built here. (3) A server rebuilt with a new host key leaves its outboxes on disk where `aries outbox` does not look; it should list them. Agreed; not built here. (4) The queued line is on stdout, so `id=$(aries create …)` captures it, and scripts that need an id use `--no-queue`. Agreed. (5) The outbox is on for every `aries mcp`, with a replay of at most five seconds before the first call after an outage. Agreed. (6) `ARIES_DATA_DIR` must itself be mode 0700 or it is refused. Agreed.

**As built (stage 5, the follow-ups (2) and (3) of F1, 14 Oct 2026).**

- **(2) Access ended.** What proves it is a refusal of the handshake coded `forbidden` that names the principal it refuses: the server's welcome now carries `principal` on a `forbidden` refusal, as it does on a welcome, which a client that does not know it ignores, so no protocol change (`client.AccessEnded`). A principal disabled, one the control store has no row for, and one that is no longer a member of the project are refused so at the handshake, and stay refused until a person changes it. Every pending entry of that principal's outbox for the project then goes to the refused list with the server's code, message and fix, at once and without being sent (`outbox.RefuseAll`), and `aries` says so in one line on stderr; an agent's tool call is refused as before and its entries wait for the person in the same list. Nothing else marks an entry: `unavailable` and `busy` are the server's state; a project archived, or still being made, is refused `not_found` ("not served"), which `project.activate` mends, so its entries go on waiting; a key the server no longer holds is `auth`, a refusal of the key and not of the principal, whose other keys send the outbox; a `forbidden` answer to a request refuses that request alone, as replay already records; and a `forbidden` refusal that names nobody, from a server before this change, proves nothing about whose access ended, so against such a server entries still go stale. A replay in progress elsewhere, on a connection that was let in, wins: the refusal does not wait for it.
- **(3) A server rebuilt with a new host key.** `seen.json` now also notes the server's address as the config gives it at each handshake (host and port, and the IAP project, zone and instance), so that a directory names its project, its host key and where the server was. `aries outbox` lists every outbox of the same project under another host key, with what waits in it and that address, and `prime` has a notice; `aries outbox --host-key KEY` lists one, as `aries outbox` lists one's own. Nothing under an old key is ever sent by itself, even once the new key is pinned. `aries outbox retry N --host-key KEY` is the person's word: it moves entry N, waiting or refused, to the same principal's outbox under the current key with its op, arguments and idempotency key, added there before it leaves the old one, so a death between the two leaves it in both and its key makes the second sending harmless. It is refused unless the config names the server at the address the entry was queued for: an outbox from before the address was noted, or one for a server at another address, can only be dropped (`aries outbox drop N --host-key KEY`). A directory with no `seen.json` names no project, and is not found.

**As built (stage 5, slice F2: protocol 6, the window and `stale`, 14 Oct 2026).** F2's gate passes in the test stack (`internal/e2e`, `idem_test.go`): writes queued with the server away, and one the server applied whose answer was lost, are each applied once when sent 25 hours later, also after a restart; an entry 8 days old is not sent and waits as `stale`, and the write the server applied, retried by hand then, is refused `stale` by the server and is still applied once. Where the build differs from §18.5 and §18.9, or settles what they left open:

- **The ops** are §18.3's table, as `internal/outbox/entry.go` now has it: `update`, `label.add`, `label.rm`, `dep.add`, `accept` and `usage` take `idem` in protocol 6, and the outbox queues them with the six of F1. `dep.rm` takes none, and is never queued. An `update` is queued only with the `rev` it was based on: without `--rev` (or `rev` over MCP) it reads the rev from the server first, and with the server away it fails, saying to give it. A `label` call of several labels is a write, with a key, for each. An operation that changes nothing (a label the issue has, an item already ticked, records all stored before) records no event and so keeps no key: sent again, it is decided again on the state then. `usage` was already deduplicated by its records, but per session, and a replay comes on whichever session of the principal connects next; its key is the principal's, like every key.
- **Protocol 6, and a client of 6 against a server of 5.** `Proto` and `ProtoMax` are 6, `ProtoMin` 5. A server of 6 serves a client of 5, which sends no key on those ops, and sends it `conflict` where it sends a client of 6 `stale`. Rule 10's default, that a new client is refused by an old server with `version`, is what §18.5 changes: the client says hello speaking 6; a server of 5 refuses it with `version` and its range; the client says hello again speaking 5, on a new session of the same SSH connection, so the cost is one more session, not one more dial (`proto.Speak`, `client.Dial`). On that connection the six ops go without their key (`proto.ForProtocol`), and are not deduplicated. A server that accepts neither 5 nor 6 refuses as before.
- **What is queued against a server of 5.** `seen.json` notes the protocol the connection spoke at each handshake. With the server away a write is queued only when that protocol takes its op's key (`proto.Keyed`; a record that names none, from F1, is taken for 5), and otherwise fails, saying so. A replay sends an entry only on a connection whose protocol takes its key: one queued for a server of 6 that comes back at 5 is refused, unsent, with code `version`, and a person retries it when the server is upgraded. So a server of 5 is never sent a queued op it could apply twice.
- **The window** is `idem_window` under `limits:` in ariesd's config, 7 days by default, from 1 hour to 365 days, given to each store (`store.Options.IdemWindow`) and replacing protocol 5's fixed 24 hours (`IdemTTL`). A key used within it gives back its first result, whatever has changed since (an `update` whose rev has moved returns the result it had); past it, `stale`; the same key with other arguments, `conflict`, as before. **No migration:** keys are looked up by the unique index on (`principal`, `idem_key`) of migration 0008, which does not depend on the window, and they live on the events they stamped, which are history and kept anyway. Keys are never deleted, so a key is never applied twice however late it comes back, and the window bounds nothing the server stores; §18.5's "it is the server's table that grows" does not hold as built, and the Limits row says so. It is a setting all the same, as decided (decision 41), because it decides how long a write may wait on a machine and still be delivered without a person.
- **`stale`** is a new code, `proto.CodeStale`, for a key used longer ago than the window. Its message says when the key was used and that its request was applied then; its fix, that nothing is to be done if that was this request, and that a change still wanted is made again by hand, which sends a new key (`aries outbox` shows what the queued write was). Retrying the entry itself from `aries outbox` sends the same key and is refused again. Telemetry counts it as a result like any code. A client that does not know the code prints it, as it prints any. The outbox keeps its own `stale` for an entry it did not send.
- **The client's age cap** is 7 days, the default window, and not the server's own: the welcome does not carry the window (the frame belongs to other work in progress), and a client cannot ask a server it cannot reach. That is safe whatever the server's window, because the server never applies a key twice: an entry sent later than the server's window either applies once, its key new to the server, or is refused `stale`, its key applied and the answer lost. The cap only decides when a person, and not `aries`, says that a write that old still stands.
- **Not built:** the replay marker of §18.9 (a replayed request is still an ordinary one), server-side caps on what one replay brings, `parked`, and the conflict ops, which are F4's.
- **An upgrade of `aries` with a full outbox.** Entries written by a client of 5 are F1's six ops, keyed in both protocols, and a client of 6 sends them as they are, with their keys. It sends entries up to 7 days old that the old client would have marked stale after a day: one the server never had applies; one it applied within its window gets the first answer; one older, `stale`, and kept. Entries the old client had already marked stale stay refused until a person retries them. Going back to a client of 5 with entries of the new ops on disk: it reads and sends them as they are, key included. A server of 6 takes the key and applies each once; a server of 5 refuses the unknown argument as `invalid`, and the entry is kept as refused, not applied. A client of 5 marks anything older than a day stale. A client of 5 that rewrites `seen.json` drops the protocol and address it does not know; the next handshake of a client of 6 notes them again, and until then it takes the server for 5 and queues only F1's six ops. In no order of upgrades is a write applied twice or dropped without a person deciding.
- **An upgrade of the server** from 5 to 6 keeps every key: the column and the index are the same. Keys used 1 to 7 days before now give their results back where protocol 5 refused them. From 6 back to 5, which no migration prevents, keys protocol 6 stamped stay on their events, and clients of 6 step down and send the new ops without them.

**As built (stage 5, slice F3: the cache of a session's own context, 14 Oct 2026).** F3's gate passes in the test stack (`internal/e2e`, `cache_test.go`): with the daemon stopped, `aries show` and `aries prime` of the issue a person holds, and `aries prime --hook`, answer from the cache and say so, as do `prime` and `show` over MCP for the issue an agent's session holds; the next session's hook finds the prime the agent read; `ready` is refused with when the server was last reached and a fix; another principal using the same machine and data directory is never shown the copy; the server is asked again once it is back; and a handshake refused as access ended deletes the cache. No protocol change and no new dependency. Nothing of it has run on Windows, where it builds. Where the build differs from §18.4, §18.6 and §18.8, or settles what they left open:

- **What is kept** (`internal/offline/cache.go`): the answers to `prime`, whole; to `show`, by the issue alone, the latest answer whichever text it asked for; to `start`, kept as a `show` of the issue it took (the issue, its claim, acceptance items and last handoff; the memories that came with it are not kept with it, and `prime`'s are); and to `inbox` and `digest`, by all their arguments. §18.6's "the issues the session holds or looked at" is every `show` answered, within the caps. Each answer is kept as the server returned it, with when, by the client's clock, which decides only the age shown and the cap on age (rule 19 is the store's). The CLI keeps them, the SessionStart hook's prime among them, and so does `aries mcp`, with or without `--no-queue`: the cache is `mcpserver.Options.Cache`, apart from `Offline`.
- **Whose, not which session's.** A cache is a principal's for a server and project, as an outbox is, and is chosen the same way: written for the principal of the connection the server let in, and read while the server is away for the principal `seen.json` names for the keys `aries` would offer. §18.6 says "a session's"; a principal's sessions on one machine share one cache, so a new session's hook finds what the last one read. `prime` from the cache carries the current session's id.
- **The files** (§18.4, and F0's section 6): a directory `cache-HASH` for each principal beside the outboxes, named by a hash of the principal's name, with a file naming the principal; in it one file for each answer, named by a hash of what it answers, holding a version, that key, the time and the answer, written to a temporary file and renamed into place with the time as its own modification time, never synced. A file that does not parse, is of another version or key, or whose answer does not decode is read as absent. Each write sweeps temporary files older than a minute that a writer that died left. Directories are checked to be the user's own, as the outbox's are, read and write; one that is not is refused with the outbox's fix.
- **Bounds.** For each principal: 500 answers, 16 MiB, 1 MiB for one (a larger one is not kept, and removes the older answer it would have replaced), and 7 days, the outbox's age cap. Each write removes what is past them, oldest first, by modification time. They are constants of `aries` (`offline.CacheOptions`, whose zero value takes them), not settings: what grows is on the person's own disk, and §18.3's outbox caps are constants too.
- **Shown only when the server is not reached** (`client.Unreachable`, as F1 defined it), and only by `show`, `prime`, `inbox` and `digest`: a read that is part of another command (an `update` without `--rev` reads the rev) never gets the cache. §18.6's "or cannot be reached within a short bound" is not built: the bound is the 75 seconds after which `aries` gives up on an answer (§7, As built), and nothing shorter.
- **What it says** (§18.2, §18.8).
