On the CLI one line on stderr, `aries: cached: aries cannot reach its server, so this is this machine's copy from TIME (AGE old), and may be out of date`, so that standard output stays the command's own: §18.2 says stdout, and F1's queued line is on stdout, but a cached answer is the command's output and a script that reads it should get it unchanged. With `--json`, `"cached": true` and `cached_at` in the one document, and nothing on stderr. Over MCP the same text as one more content line after the result, as the inbox's line is. `prime` says it as its first notice instead, in its JSON, its text and the hook's context alike, and is held under its budget with it.
- **`prime` from the cache has no ready issues**, though it was kept with them: nothing can be started without the server (decision 39), and §18.6 refuses `ready` offline for the reason that applies to prime's top five as well. Its text says so in place of the ready list, and its next step is to carry on with the work in progress. Its notices are said afresh: the outbox's, and not the version warnings of the connection it was kept from.
- **Refused offline:** `ready`, `blocked`, `list`, `who`, and the op `claims`, which no command or tool calls on its own. The refusal keeps the code and message of the failure to connect, adds that it is not answered from the copy and when the server was last reached, and its fix adds that `prime` and `show` answer meanwhile (for an agent: carry on with the work it holds). When the server was last reached is a new file, `reached`, beside `missed`, written at each handshake at most once a minute. F1's "not built: when the server was last reached in `aries whoami` and `prime`" stands for `whoami`; `prime` from the cache says how old it is.
- **The flag for a person to see `ready` and `list` from the cache, marked stale (§18.6), is not built.** Their answers are not kept at all, only `prime`'s top of `ready`, so there is nothing to show; building it means keeping `list` with its filters and cursors, which is more than F3's gate asks, and the maintainer asked to keep moving on features. It can follow if people ask for it.
- **Access ended** (§18.6): the first handshake refused as `forbidden` naming the principal (`client.AccessEnded`, F1's follow-up) deletes that principal's cache of the project, in the CLI, in a SessionStart hook (which says nothing of it), and in `aries mcp`, and the CLI says so on stderr in one line. Other principals' caches on the machine stay. A `forbidden` answer to one request, a project archived, and a key the server no longer holds delete nothing, as for the outbox. A machine that never connects again keeps its cache: an answer past 7 days is not shown, and its file goes at the next write.
- **`aries cache`** lists, for each principal, how many answers are kept, their size and when, and when the server was last reached; **`aries cache clear`** deletes every principal's cache of this server and project on the machine, and leaves the outboxes. Neither connects. Caches under a host key the server no longer has are not listed or cleared by them.
- **Windows.** Go opens files there without `FILE_SHARE_DELETE`, so a rename over an answer another process is reading should fail (F0, section 6); a cache write that fails is dropped, and costs a later answer, not the command. Not run.

**As built (stage 5, slice F4: conflicts, 14 Oct 2026).** F4's gate passes in the test stack (`internal/e2e`, `conflicts_test.go`): an agent holds an issue and renames it; its person, with the server away, edits the title and body from the rev they read; the replay applies the body and parks the title; both sessions are told; the agent reads the conflict in `show` and proposes; the person approves the proposal with `aries resolve --accept`. Store tests hold each rule of §8's table as kept, parking, each way of resolving, a later change closing a conflict, the caps, and a merge rerun after losing to a concurrent write. No new dependency. Where the build differs from §18.7 to §18.9, or settles what they left open:

- **Detection is asked for, by a replay alone.** `update` gains `park` (protocol 7), which the outbox adds when it sends a queued update; a live `update` at a stale rev is refused `conflict`, as before. `park` is the whole of §18.9's replay marker: no other op carries one, and a replayed request carries no client time.
- **The state at a rev, from the event log.** The one event of each write that changes an issue's row now records the rev the write left (migration 0016, `events.rev`). The fields changed since a base rev are the keys of the before states of the stamped events after the last one at or below it, which also gives each field's value at the base, and who changed it last and when. A base older than the issue's first stamped event, which is every event written before the migration, cannot be compared: the update is refused `conflict`, as before. A base value longer than 8 KiB is the event log's shortened copy (`EventTextMax`); mine and theirs are whole.
- **The rules.** A field applies when nobody changed it since the base, when the other change set the same value, or when it is back at the base's. Otherwise: title, body, design, acceptance, notes, priority, type, owner, parent and account park; status and assignee keep the server's, and are listed in the result's `dropped`; declared paths, a set replaced whole, keep the server's when an `issue.paths` event of declared paths follows the base, also `dropped`. An issue closed since the base takes nothing, and the update is kept as a comment listing what it would have set (cut at 64 KiB). Delete against edit is not built: nothing deletes an issue. `finish` carries no field edit, so it is never compared; a late `finish` is F5's.
- **Guarded whole.** A merge is guarded as an update is (rule 5): on an issue another principal holds it is refused `forbidden`, and waits in the outbox's refused list, rather than parked. The holder's inbox item is therefore for an admin's replay, and a holder that is another session of the author's principal hears through the author's item, which is for every session. The times, flags and metadata no client sends are refused in a merge.
- **Parking** writes a row of `conflicts` (migration 0017) and a `conflict.park` event with base, mine and theirs for each field. There is no new outcome on the wire: the answer is `update`'s, with `parked` (field and conflict), `dropped` and `comment`. The outbox records it as delivered and counts it as parked, says so after the replay (`delivered 1 (1 parked conflicts for a person to resolve: `aries conflicts`)`) and in `aries outbox stats`.
- **Resolving** is the op `resolve`: mine, theirs, text, or accept. A new value goes through the same checks, guard and `issue.update` event as an update, followed by `conflict.resolve`, which names the choice and whether it accepted a proposal; theirs records `conflict.resolve` alone. A conflict already resolved is refused `invalid`. **A later change** to a field, by any write of the issue's row (an update, an import, the resolution of another conflict on it), closes its open conflicts, each with a `conflict.resolve` of choice `superseded`.
- **Proposals and MCP** (decision 42). The op `conflict.propose` keeps the proposal on the row with who and why, replacing an earlier one, with a `conflict.propose` event, guarded as a change to the issue. Over MCP it is an argument of a tool there is: `comment` with `conflict` and `propose` (`mine`, `theirs` or the new text), its body the why. It costs about 46 estimated tokens, paid for by moving `update`'s "empty clears" into its description and trimming `handoff`'s and `digest`'s, so the set is about 2,696. A text that is exactly `mine` or `theirs` cannot be proposed as text over MCP. An agent reads the sides in `show`, which gives an issue's open conflicts, each side cut to fit the result cap. Nobody is told of a proposal; `aries conflicts` shows it.
- **The recommendation** is from who holds the claim and who made each side: theirs when the holder made it, mine when the holder replayed it, and otherwise to compare them. The order of the two changes is not used: a replayed update carries no client time, so when the edit was made is not known.
- **Who is told, and `prime`.** The author's item is for every session of its principal; the holder's, for the holder's session. `prime` counts the open conflicts its principal parked or that are on issues assigned to it, from `inbox`'s new `conflicts`.
- **`digest`** lists, as old conflicts, those open longer than `conflict_age` (under `limits:`, a day by default), whenever they were parked; `--by` names the author.
- **Bounds.** `conflicts_per_issue` (20) and `conflicts_per_principal` (100, the author's), under `limits:`; past either the update is refused `invalid` and nothing of it applies, with a fix to resolve the open conflicts and retry the entry.
- **Protocol 7**, with `ProtoMin` raised to 6 by rule 10: a server of this build refuses a client of 5, and a client of 7 is refused by a server of 5 alone. A client of 7 steps down to a server of 6 as F2's did to 5, and sends `update` there without `park`. A machine's record of a server that names no protocol, from F1's client, is still taken for 5.
- **Not built:** §18.9's counters and log events for parked operations; a resolver's diff (`aries conflicts N` prints each side whole, for a person to diff). **Not measured:** the cost of the comparison on a long history; it reads the issue's events after the base through `events_target`.

**As built (stage 5, slice F5: `aries away`, the late `finish`, and the partition tests, 14 Oct 2026).** F5's gate passes in the test stack: the soak in its partition mode (`ARIES_SOAK_PARTITION=1`, [CONTRIBUTING.md](../../CONTRIBUTING.md#with-the-network-cut)) cuts agents off from the server for 2 to 30 minutes of its clock at a time while they work, and holds every write they were told was queued to their outboxes and the event logs; four 10-minute runs with `-race` pass, seeds 11 to 14, each with about 220 cuts, 10,000 queued writes, 8,000 to 9,000 replays, 70 to 130 finishes and handoffs kept as comments and 220 to 260 applied after their lease lapsed, as do short runs, seeds 1 to 4. Earlier runs found a fault in `aries mcp`, mended below, and one in the soak's own replay, which did not count an update kept as a comment. e2e tests (`internal/e2e`, `late_test.go`): a person goes away for 3 days and their claim of an hour holds against another principal for two of them and lets go after; an agent finishes with the server away, past its lease, and the finish applies with `claim.lapsed` when nobody took the issue, and is kept as a comment, its discovered issue filed and both told, when another principal did. No new dependency. Where the build differs from §18.3, §18.6, §18.8 to §18.10, or settles what they left open:

- **`aries away` predates this section** (§7, As built: `renew` with `all`, from the session `cli` alone, up to 7 days), and is kept as it was on the wire: there is no `away` op (§18.9 lists one), so no protocol change for it. What changed: the cap is a server setting, `away_max` under `limits:`, 7 days by default, from 1 hour to 30 days (`store.Options.AwayMax`), and a longer one is refused `invalid` with a fix naming it. It extends every claim of the principal, in every session, to at least now plus the duration, where it extended only a claim with less than half the duration left, so that a person going away for 4 hours with 3 left kept 3; a claim within a minute of it is left as it is, since `aries away` sends each claim's paths in a renew of its own a moment later. Each claim it extends records a `claim.away` event, with the claim as it was and its new expiry: going away is a person's act and history, where an agent's renewals are bookkeeping (rule 7). It is guarded as a change to the claim, which passes, the claims being the principal's own. The reaper and a forward jump of the clock (decision 37) honor the lease as any other; tests hold both. **No `--back`**, and `aries away` with no duration is a usage error as before: a lease is never shortened, so a person back early finishes or hands off what they are done with. **No MCP tool**: going away is a person's act, refused from any session but `cli`, and the schema set has no room (rule 12).
- **A late `finish` or `handoff`** (§18.3, §18.6). Protocol 8 adds `park` to `finish` and `handoff`, which the outbox adds when it replays them, as it does to `update` since F4; the server then calls `ReplayFinish` or `ReplayHandoff` (`store/late.go`). `park` is excluded from what an idempotency key stands for, so a finish whose answer was lost and is then replayed gets its first answer. The outcomes:

  | When it arrives | What the server does |
  |---|---|
  | Its claim still live, at its epoch | Applies, as live |
  | Its claim lapsed, reaped or not, and nobody took the issue since | Applies, with a `claim.lapsed` event first, whose before state is the claim (with holder and expiry while the reaper had not ended it, the epoch alone once it had) and after state the op; the result says `lapsed` |
  | A later epoch was taken since (by anyone, another session of the writer's principal among them), another principal holds it now, or, for a `finish` or a releasing `handoff`, the issue was closed | Kept: a plain comment by the writer saying what it was, the epoch it was made at and why it did not apply, with its reason, note and fields; an `issue.kept` event (op, epoch, why, comment, reason, note); a `finish`'s discovered issues filed, discovered from the issue; a `conflict` inbox item for the writer's principal, every session, and one for the session that holds the issue, naming the comment. Nothing else of it applies: no close, no ticks, no paths, no handoff to its `to` nor mentions. The result names the `comment` |

  A write that names no epoch, as `aries finish` without `--epoch` sends, is kept only when another principal holds the issue or it was closed, and is marked lapsed when the claim that lapsed was its own principal's, which for a reaped claim the reaper's `claim.expire` of that epoch says. A claim its holder let go, by a releasing handoff, did not lapse. **The fence is unchanged for a live write** (principle 4): without `park` a stale epoch is refused `conflict` and an issue another principal holds `forbidden`, as before, and nothing is written; the one change to a live write is that one applying after its lease lapsed records `claim.lapsed` too, since that is what happened. Tests hold each outcome, each refusal of a live write, and a replay sent again giving its first answer.
- **The outbox counts a kept write apart** (`outbox.Outcome.Kept`, `Report.Kept`): delivered, and said so after the replay, `delivered 2 (1 kept as a comment, as the issue moved on while it waited: `aries inbox` says why)`. F4's `update` kept as a comment because the issue was closed is counted so too, where F4 counted it as parked; `aries outbox stats` keeps one column, `parked`, for both.
- **Protocol 8**, with `ProtoMin` raised to 7 by rule 10: a server of this build refuses a client of 6, and a client of 8 is refused by a server of 6 alone. A client of 8 steps down to a server of 7 and sends `finish` and `handoff` there without `park`, which that server refuses as before when the issue moved on; the entry waits in the refused list, and `aries outbox retry` keeps it as a comment once the server is upgraded (`TestClientStepsDownToProtocol7`, which replaces F4's test of a server of 6 that this client no longer speaks). The results' `lapsed` and `comment` need no version.
- **The partition tests** (§18.10). How a client is cut: by its dials. A cut agent's `aries mcp` dials fail as an unreachable server's do (`unavailable`, which `client.Unreachable` takes for not reached), and the connection it had fails its next request without sending it, as a dropped one does, which `aries mcp` then closes; a dial under way when the cut comes is closed as it completes. So whatever the timing, a request made while the agent is cut reaches nothing, and what the check holds the log to is what the agent was told. Each agent is connected for 2 to 30 minutes of the store's clock and cut for as long, alternately, as its seed chooses; its leases lapse when the cut is longer than the 15 minutes of an agent's lease. The check, `partitionFaults`, is a pure function with a table test (`TestPartitionFaults`) that it catches a write lost, silently dropped from the outbox, applied twice by its key or by its markers, still waiting at rest, refused with no reason, refused yet applied, told it was kept but applied or the reverse, and a replay no session queued. It goes by idempotency keys (each write's key read from the outbox as it is queued, the events stamped with it), markers and the server's answers, never a clock. The replay of each log checks the rest by the log's order: that no epoch went to two sessions, as before, that `claim.lapsed` names the claim the issue is still under, nobody having taken it, and a lease that had run out, and that `issue.kept` came only where the issue had moved on. One refusal is allowed in the log: an entry refused unsent because its principal's access had ended (F1's follow-up) whose earlier attempt applied and lost its answer; the person's retry gets the first answer.
- **Found by the partition soak, and mended: a replay that was busy was not tried again on the same connection.** `aries mcp` replays on each new connection and then in the background while rounds make progress; a round that found another process replaying the same outbox, or sent nothing within its budget, was not followed by another until the connection dropped. In a 10-minute run an outbox of a few hundred writes then waited minutes with none sent. Each renewal now runs a round on the connection it has while something waits (`TestRenewSendsWhatABusyReplayLeft`).
- **Not built:** §18.9's replay marker beyond `park` (a replayed request still carries neither the client's time nor the session that made it, so its event's session is the delivering connection's); an `away` op; telling a person's other sessions that a claim was extended; deciding whether a write with no epoch was overtaken by another session of its own principal.

## 19. Search by meaning: a design for vectors

Designed 12 Oct 2026, from §10 and [database.md](database.md#vectors-later). **Decided 14 Oct 2026 (§19.8; §14, decisions 46 to 51), and nothing here is built.** One question, where the vectors live, waits on V0. It is planned for stage 7; this section says how it could be built sooner and smaller, and what would have to be measured first.

In short: ariesd runs no model. An operator points it at an embedding endpoint, by default one on the same machine, and ariesd asks it for vectors in the background, after a write and never during one. The vectors are derived data: kept beside the database and not in it, and rebuilt from the text at any time. Search is a plain comparison in Go. Every use falls back to what Aries does today when the endpoint is off, slow or behind, so nothing an agent or a person does can fail because of it.

### 19.1 What it is for, in order of worth

| Use | Today | What a vector adds |
|---|---|---|
| Memories in `prime` and `start` | Pinned, then linked to or sharing a label with the caller's issues, then newest (§6) | The memories that are about the work, whatever they were tagged. Every session pays for `prime`, so a better ten is fewer tokens spent finding the rest |
| The duplicate check at `create`, and similar closed issues in `show` | Shared words in titles of the 2,000 most recently closed issues (§7, As built) | Issues that say the same thing in other words, and open ones |
| Search | `list` filters; no search by text | `aries search TEXT` and `recall` by meaning |

The first is the reason to build it early: it bears on cost for every session. The third is the only one that needs something new on the wire.

### 19.2 The endpoint, not a model

`aries` and `ariesd` stay as they are: no model, no runtime for one, no new dependency (rule 4). A server setting names an HTTP endpoint that takes text and returns vectors in the request and response shape most model servers share, which a local server (Ollama, llama.cpp, a text-embeddings server) and the hosted ones all speak. ariesd calls it with the standard library.

- **Off by default.** With no endpoint set, nothing below exists and Aries behaves as it does now.
- **Loopback by default.** An endpoint that is not on the loopback interface is refused unless `embeddings.allow_remote: true` is also set, because it sends the text of every issue, comment and memory to another machine, and for a hosted model to another company. Memories pass the secrets lint (`internal/secretscan`); issues and comments do not, and the guide says so beside the setting.
- **A token**, where the endpoint needs one, comes from the 0600 environment file, never the settings file or argv (rule 2), as decision 34 has it for telemetry.
- **The model's name and dimensions** are settings. A vector is kept with the model that made it, and one made by another model is never compared with it.

### 19.3 Embedding happens after the write

A worker in the daemon, one for each open project, reads the project's event log from where it last stopped, as the audit stream reads the control store's (§16, slice O3), and for each issue, comment or memory that changed asks the endpoint for a vector of its text, in batches. Text whose hash is unchanged is skipped. Long text is cut at a bound.

- **No write waits for a vector, and none fails for want of one.** The endpoint is not in the write path at all.
- **Behind is normal.** A new or changed item has no vector for a moment, or for as long as the endpoint is down. Every use says what it covers and falls back for the rest (§19.5).
- **A dead endpoint costs nothing.** Calls are bounded in time and in number at once; failures back off, are counted and logged with an event, and `aries admin status` says how far behind each project is.
- **What is embedded follows visibility.** A vector carries its item's scope. A private memory's vector is compared only for its owner, by filtering before scoring, never after.

### 19.4 Where the vectors live

Proposed: **in files beside the database, not in Dolt.** One file for each project and model under ariesd's state directory, mode 0600, appended to and compacted, and read into memory when a project's first search needs it and dropped when its store closes for idleness.

- A vector is derived from text the event log already holds. It is not history, so it needs no event (rule 7), no place in backups, and no migration: delete the file and the worker rebuilds it.
- Kept in Dolt, every re-embedding would be a write through the project's single writer and a row in Dolt's history, which is most of a Dolt database's size, for data that can be recomputed. The memory measurements of stage 3a and the collector's behavior under writes (§16.5) both argue for keeping avoidable writes out.
- At 768 dimensions, a vector is 3 KB as float32 and under 1 KB as int8. A project of 50,000 items is about 40 MB in memory as int8. A cap on items for each project and on projects with vectors loaded bounds the total (rule 18).
- The cost: a second place for state on the server, which the AppArmor profiles and the guide must name, and a rebuild after a restore from backup.

database.md proposed the `embeddings` table in Dolt, and a Dolt vector index past about 50,000 rows. This is question 1.

### 19.5 Search, and falling back

Comparison is exact cosine over the project's vectors in Go: tens of milliseconds at the sizes above, with no index to tune. Past the cap, the oldest closed issues are left out, as today's scorer leaves out all but the newest 2,000.

- **Ranking for `prime` and `start`** needs no query from a person: the query is the caller's in-progress issues, whose vectors are already there, so the endpoint is not called during the request. Pins still come first. Memories with no vector yet are ranked as today and merged in.
- **The duplicate check** compares the new title and body once the worker has embedded them, so `create` returns at once with today's word-overlap matches, and a better match found a moment later reaches the author's inbox. `create` never calls the endpoint.
- **Search by text** is the one use that embeds at request time. It is bounded (proposed 500 ms); past the bound, or with the endpoint down, it answers by word overlap and says so.
- **Results say how they were found**, by meaning or by words, and over how much of the project.

### 19.6 What changes

- Settings: `embeddings.endpoint`, `model`, `dimensions`, `allow_remote`, and limits for items, text length, batch, concurrency and the search bound.
- No migration, if §19.4 stands.
- The protocol: none for ranking and the duplicate check. A `search` op, and a query on `recall` if it has none that fits, are new and raise the protocol (rule 10); they can come last.
- MCP: no new tool for ranking or duplicates, so the schema budget is untouched. Whether agents get `search` is question 4.
- Telemetry and logs: items embedded, behind, failed; the endpoint's latency; events in the log schema. Exported records carry no text, as now.
- Docs: the server guide, the security model (what an endpoint is sent, and that vectors are as sensitive as the text they came from), and the AppArmor profiles.

### 19.7 Plan

| Slice | Delivers | Gate |
|---|---|---|
| V0 | A measurement, with no server change: on a real project's export, how often today's ranking and duplicate check miss what a person would call related, against two local models and one hosted | Numbers that say whether to go on, and with which default model |
| V1 | The endpoint setting, the worker, the files, status and telemetry | Vectors appear for a project, survive a restart, rebuild after deletion; a dead endpoint changes no request's result or timing |
| V2 | Memory ranking in `prime` and `start` | The measurement's gain holds in the test stack, within the token cap |
| V3 | The duplicate check and similar issues | A reworded duplicate is found; `create` is no slower |
| V4 | `search`, and `recall` by meaning | Protocol bump; falls back within the bound |

V0 decides whether any of the rest is built. If word overlap already finds what matters at the sizes real projects have, the answer is to leave it.

### 19.8 Decisions

The maintainer answered the six questions on 14 Oct 2026; they are decisions 46 to 51 of §14, which has his words. Five stand as proposed. The first is left to V0, which is to time both places: search costs the same in either, and they differ in loading a project's vectors, in writes through its single writer, in Dolt's history and memory, and in what a restore from backup brings back. The questions as they were put follow.


1. **Where vectors live.** Proposed: files beside the database, rebuilt at need. database.md proposed a table in Dolt.
2. **Pull it forward.** Proposed: V0 now, since it costs nothing and decides the rest; V1 and V2 after the observability and stage 5 work in flight; V3 and V4 stay with stage 7.
3. **Remote endpoints.** Proposed: allowed, behind an explicit setting, with a warning at start naming what is sent.
4. **Search over MCP.** Proposed: not at first. Agents gain from better `prime` and `start` without a new tool.
5. **A default model.** Proposed: none named until V0. The guide then recommends one that is openly licensed and runs on a CPU.
6. **One setting for the server, or one for each project.** Proposed: the server's, with a project able to opt out. A portfolio (the unit above a project) may want its own; that waits for portfolios.

### 19.9 What this does not solve, and what is not tested

- It does not search across projects. Each project's vectors are its own, as its database is.
- It does not work offline: the client holds no vectors (§18).
- A person who could read an item can recover much of its text from its vector. The files are protected as the database is, and a remote endpoint sees the text itself.
- Not tested: everything. In particular the claim that 50,000 vectors compare in tens of milliseconds on the smallest server the guide sizes, the memory of many projects with vectors loaded beside Dolt's, and whether any model beats word overlap on real Aries data, which is V0.

## 20. Coordination across agents and projects: the stage 6 design

Designed 15 Oct 2026, from §3, §4, §7, §13 and what stages 1 to 5 built. **Decided 15 Oct 2026 (§20.11; §14, decisions 66 to 77), and nothing here is built.** Two answers differ from the proposal below: cross-project edges include another project's issues (decision 74), and the MCP budget rises to about 3,000 tokens for agent tools (decision 76); each section says where. Where this differs from the rows of §3, §4 and §7, it says so and why; they stay as the plan they were.

In short: stage 6 is six parts, ordered by the failure each prevents and built so that each is useful without the next. **Gates** are issues of a new type that only a named person, or a CI job with a principal of its own, can close; everything the blocked derivation already does then applies to them unchanged. **CI reaches the server by running `aries`**, as any client does: no listener, no polling, no webhook. **Locks** are leased, fenced, first-in-first-out mutexes and semaphores for a merge train, a deploy or a shared resource, held by a client that renews them. **Graphs**: one write creates a whole plan or formula run, idempotently; templates are files in the repository that `aries` reads; waves and parallelism are computed by the client over one new read. **Cross-project** dependencies start as named capabilities a project ships, mirrored into the projects that wait on them. **Reservations** of paths, tied to a claim, come last, and only if rework shows they are needed. ariesd gains no runner, no template engine, no outbound call and no port; the MCP tool set gains about 20 estimated tokens, at the last slice.

### 20.1 What each part is for, in order of worth

| Part | The failure it prevents | What prevents it today | Who needs it first |
|---|---|---|---|
| Human gates | An agent starts an epic's children before a person approved the plan; an agent "unblocks" work a person held back | Nothing: `blocked` and `deferred` are statuses any agent's `update` sets back to `open` | Every team; Bearings §3.13 (plan approval) |
| CI gates and outcomes | A dependent starts on code that is closed but not merged, or merged red ("closed is not landed", Bearings §3.13) | Nothing: `close` is the agent's own assertion | Every team with CI; Bearings B3; enterprise review E8 |
| Locks | Two merges or two deploys at once; a crashed holder of bd's merge slot blocks everyone forever (§7) | CI concurrency groups, inside one CI system only | Bearings' merge train (§3.5) and governor (§3.7); scripts that deploy |
| Graphs (pour, `graph`, swarm) | A retried or crashed instantiation leaves a duplicate or half a checklist; a release checklist retyped by hand each time | Per-call idempotency keys; children filed under a gated parent (§20.6) | Bearings' formula runner (§3.10); repeated procedures |
| Cross-project capabilities | Work in one project waits on another team's feature, and only a person remembers to look | A comment | Servers of several projects |
| Reservations | Two agents edit the same files and one redoes its work at merge | Worktrees, and `ready` ranking down issues whose paths overlap held ones (§12, As built, files to issues) | Nobody yet: Bearings decision 5 waits for its conflict log |

Swarm is the least of these: a view, not a guard, and it rides the graph read. The first two rows are governance as much as coordination, which is why they come first.

### 20.2 Principles kept, and what they rule out

- **One authority, and the event log is the truth (principles 2, 3).** Every gate passed, lock granted and capability shipped is an event in a log; ready and blocked stay derived at read time. A dependency on another project is derived from the depending project's own log (§20.7), not from a read of another database during `ready`.
- **Every claim is fenced (principle 4).** A lock is a claim on a name: an epoch that only rises, a lease on the store's clock (rule 19), and the reaper. A reservation lives and dies with the claim it belongs to and has no lease of its own.
- **The harness does the chores (principle 5).** `aries lock run NAME -- CMD` takes, renews and releases a lock around a command; nothing asks a model to renew a lock.
- **Fail closed and loud (principle 6).** A dependency on something the server cannot resolve blocks and says so; it is never read as satisfied. Every refusal names the holder or the approvers and the next step (rule 11).
- **MCP stays small (rule 12).** Agents see gates, locks and capabilities in the results they already get (`show`, `blocked`, `ready`); none of the new verbs is an MCP tool. Passing a gate, like resolving a conflict (decision 42), is a person's act.
- **Small binaries, one way in (rule 4, decision 9).** No new dependency, no listener, and no call from ariesd to anything but Dolt and, when configured, a collector.
- **What this rules out:** a gate runner that polls anything; webhooks into ariesd; a template language on the server; ephemeral issues that are deleted; a lock or a reservation queued offline.

### 20.3 Gates

**A gate is an issue of type `gate`,** with a row in a new `gates` table, keyed by the issue, for what is not an issue's: its kind, who may pass it, its subject and its evidence. What it holds back depends on it with an ordinary `blocks` edge.

| Option | For | Against |
|---|---|---|
| **A. A gate is an issue (recommended)** | The blocked derivation, `blocked`'s "by what", history, comments, the inbox, event push and cross-project edges work unchanged; a descendant of a gated parent is blocked already (`blockedCTE`), so one gate on an epic holds its whole plan; bd's gates were issues too, so import maps | A new issue type, left out of `ready`, refused by `start`, and guarded on `close` |
| B. A `gates` table, and a blocker kind the derivation joins | Gates apart from issues in lists | A second blocker kind in `blockedCTE`, `blocked`, `show`, the board and the soak's replay; no history or comments of its own |
| C. A new issue state | None | A state is one issue's; a gate is shared by many issues |

**Kinds.** Of §3's five (human, timer, gh, issue, capability), two remain kinds:

| Kind | Passed by | Spec |
|---|---|---|
| `human` | `aries gate pass G` by a principal the gate names, or by a project admin when it names none | `approvers`: up to 10 principals |
| `ci` | `aries gate pass --check NAME` from a CI job, by a principal the gate names (§20.4) | `check`: a name such as `build` or `landed`; `by`: the principals that may report it; `subject`: the issue whose branch the check runs on |

The other three are not kinds. A **timer** is `defer_until`, which the schema and `ready` already honor and only the wire lacks (`create` and `update` gain it, and `aries update --defer-until`), derived at read time with no runner.

**Dates on an issue** (added 15 Oct 2026, at the maintainer's request: "a maintenance upgrade at a specific date"). The schema already has `defer_until` (not before) and `due_at` (due by), and nothing on the wire sets either. G0 puts both on `create` and `update` (`aries create --defer-until DATE --due DATE`, `aries update --defer-until DATE --due DATE`, an empty value clears one, and the same two arguments on the MCP tools only if they fit the budget, else CLI only), shows them in `show` and `list`, and makes them noticed with no runner: `digest` and `prime` list the issues that became ready since the reader last asked because their `defer_until` passed, the issues due within a window (a setting, a day by default) and those overdue, each computed at read time from the store's time (rule 19). A deferred issue still drops out of `ready` until its date, as today. A date is a claim about the calendar, not a lease: nothing ends, expires or is reassigned when it passes. A maintenance window is then an issue deferred to the window's start and due at its end, behind a human gate if a person must approve it first. An **issue** gate is a `blocks` edge, which exists. A **capability** gate is a dependency on a capability (§20.7). So there is no gate runner: what is time is derived, and what is an event arrives as a request.

**Rules.**
- `ready` and `start` leave gates out, as they leave out templates. `close`, `finish`, an `update` of status and `reopen` on a gate are refused `invalid`, with a fix naming `aries gate pass` and the approvers. Only `gate.pass` closes a gate and only `gate.rearm` reopens one, each by those the gate names or an admin, in one write with `issue.close` or `issue.reopen`. Anyone else is refused `forbidden`, naming the approvers.
- `gate.fail` records a failed check with its evidence (a URL and a commit, 1 KiB at most), leaves the gate open, and tells the subject's holder or assignee (inbox kind `gate.failed`). `gate.waive`, by an admin, passes a gate with a reason, as an acceptance item is waived.
- `aries gate add --blocks ID [--kind human|ci] …` creates the gate and its edge in one write. A gate cannot be a descendant of what it blocks: the cycle check already refuses that, a parent link being an edge.
- Who is told: the approvers when a gate is added (`gate.pending`), and the assignees of the issues it held when it passes (`gate.passed`), which is how a waiting agent learns, through its inbox line.
- An agent sees a gate as a blocker in `blocked` and `show`, with its kind and approvers, in the result alone. No MCP tool creates or passes one.
- **Who approves is a principal, not a person.** Until agents have identities of their own (decision 8, §17.6), an agent holding its person's key can run `aries gate pass` in a shell. As with `aries away`, the server accepts a human gate's pass only from the session `cli`: a speed bump, not a wall (§7, As built, authorization and holds). Whether the gate's creator may pass it is decision 3.
- **bd import** maps bd's `gate` issues, today tasks labeled `bd-type:gate`, to `human` gates with no approvers.

### 20.4 How CI results reach the server

Decision 9 keeps SSH the only transport; ariesd calls out to nothing but Dolt and, when configured, a collector, and listens on loopback alone (decision 29). So the question is who carries a check's result to the server.

| Option | What it adds | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **1. CI runs `aries` (recommended)** | A CI job, as a principal of its own (`ci-example`, a member of the project), runs `aries gate pass --check build --from-branch --sha "$SHA" --evidence "$RUN_URL"`; `--from-branch` takes the issue from the branch name (`internal/gitx`, as `aries start --branch` names it). A failure runs `aries gate fail`. The job on the default branch after a merge passes `landed` | CI needs a path to sshd (a public port, or the IAP transport with `gcloud` signed in in the job), and a key in its secret store until workload sign-in (decisions 52 and 56, access-research §7) gives it one that lasts the job. The CI principal has a member's rights until decision 8's narrower ones |
| 2. ariesd polls the host's API | Results without touching CI | The daemon's first call to a vendor: a token on the server (rule 2); DNS and certificate rules in the shipped AppArmor profile, which has them today only in `local/`; an adapter per host (GitHub, GitLab, …) in ariesd, against §16.1's rule that nothing in ariesd names a vendor; rate limits; repository settings per project; results a poll late |
| 3. A webhook receiver | Results the moment the host sends them | A listener another machine reaches, which decisions 9 and 29 exclude for ariesd. As a separate process it holds a key and calls the server as `aries` does, so it is option 1 run by someone else, plus TLS and a webhook secret |

With option 1 the server side is one op whoever carries it: a relay (option 3) is a client, built outside ariesd if anyone asks, and needs no server change. The guide gets a workflow for GitHub Actions and one for GitLab CI. **What a pass is worth** is what the CI principal's key is worth: whoever can change the workflow can pass the gate (§20.13).

### 20.5 Locks

**For** a merge train (one lock for each target branch, Bearings §3.5), a deploy (one for each environment), a shared resource (a staging database, an API with a rate limit, as a semaphore), and Bearings' governor (a semaphore of the agents one provider may run at once, §3.7). Not for issues: a claim is the lock on an issue.

**API** (protocol ops and `aries lock`; no MCP tool):

| Op | Does |
|---|---|
| `lock.acquire NAME [capacity] [lease] [wait]` | Granted at once if fewer than capacity hold it and nobody waits; else, with `wait`, joins the queue and returns its place; else refused `busy`, naming the holders and when their leases end. Granted, returns `{epoch, expires_at}` |
| `lock.renew NAME EPOCH` | Extends the caller's hold, or its place in the queue; quiet, as claim renewals are |
| `lock.release NAME EPOCH` | Ends the hold, and grants the next waiter in the same write |
| `lock.leave NAME` | Leaves the queue |
| `locks [NAME]` | Holders, waiters and epochs; a read |

- **Semantics.** A lock is a name in the project (`[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._/-]{0,63}`), made by its first acquire with its capacity (1 by default, at most 64); a later acquire naming another capacity is refused. A holder is (principal, session, machine), as a claim's. The epoch rises by one at each grant and is the **fencing token**: `renew` and `release` with another epoch are refused `conflict`, and `aries lock run` gives the command `ARIES_LOCK_EPOCH` for a resource that can check it.
- **FIFO with waiter expiry.** Waiters are served in the order they joined. A waiter keeps its place by renewing (a 2-minute lease, renewed every 30 seconds by `aries lock run` or Bearings), so a waiter that died leaves the queue at its lease and never holds the lock up. A grant goes to the earliest waiter whose lease is live, and is pushed to its session as an inbox item (`lock.granted`).
- **Leases and the reaper.** A hold's lease is 15 minutes by default and at most 24 hours (`MaxClaimLease`). The reaper ends lapsed holds (`lock.expire`), drops lapsed waiters and grants the next, on its 30-second tick; a lapsed hold holds nothing even before the reaper runs, as a claim's, so an acquire finds it free. A clock jump forward is settled for locks as for claims: the settle adds it to every live hold and wait (decision 37). Lost access (decision 21) ends the principal's holds and waits with its claims, each with its event, and the holder's session gets `lock.lost`.
- **Relation to claims.** The same machinery, separate rows: a lock is tied to no issue, and holding one changes no issue.
- **Scope.** A project's, in its database and through its writer. A lock shared by projects (one staging environment for several repositories) would live in the control store; deferred until asked.
- **Events.** `lock.acquire`, `lock.wait`, `lock.grant`, `lock.release`, `lock.expire`, `lock.leave`, targeting `lock:NAME`, which never parses as an issue id, so the board's issue push does not carry them. Renewals are quiet (rule 7).
- **Offline,** never queued (§18.3): `aries lock` refuses at once when the server cannot be reached, so a script fails before its critical section, not inside it.

### 20.6 Graphs: molecules, formulas and swarm

**What of bd's to keep.**

| bd | Here |
|---|---|
| Formula (a workflow with variables) | A file in the repository, `aries pour FILE --run KEY --var k=v`. `aries` parses it; the server sees only the graph it yields |
| Proto (a template epic), `cook` | A template issue (`template`, a column since migration 0001 that `ready` already leaves out) with its children and edges, poured by `aries pour --from ID`. `cook` is not a step: the client compiles at pour time |
| Pour, molecule | One `graph.create` write: the issues, parents, edges and labels, by local references, all or nothing |
| Bond | Edges between graphs: `dep`, or `pour --after ID` |
| Wisp, squash, burn | **Dropped.** An issue that expires and is purged is history deleted, and nothing deletes an issue. The `ephemeral` flag bd import sets stays, and nothing purges it |
| Distill (an epic back into a formula) | Deferred; client-side over `graph`, if asked |

**On the server, two ops.**
- `graph.create`: up to 200 issues and 1,000 edges, with local references (`@build`) given new ids in the write, the cycle check run over the result, one `issue.create` event an issue and one `graph.create` naming the run. It takes an idempotency key, as `create` does, and is queued offline as `create` is. A **run key** (`--run release-1.4`), unique in the project, is kept in a small `graph_runs` table: a second pour of the same formula and run key returns the first run's root whoever pours it, so two Bearings machines with different principals cannot start one run twice, which a principal's idempotency key cannot promise.
- `graph ROOT`: a read of the open descendants of an issue, or of a label's issues, with their status, priority and blocking edges among them and to outside, at most 2,000 issues, in one snapshot.

**Templates in the repository, recommended.** A formula changes with the code it drives, is reviewed in its pull request, and needs no catalog, no versions and no template language in ariesd. A run's root records the formula's name, its `version` and the file's SHA-256 in `metadata`, so a run says what it came from. Template issues in the store are kept for bd parity only; they are poured by the client over `graph`, so they cost the server nothing more.

**The line with Bearings.** Aries instantiates and derives: a formula's steps advance because closing one makes the next ready, with gates where a step needs a person or CI, and no runner. Bearings' formula runner (§3.10) decides which ready step goes to which agent, cancels a run and bounds its spend. A team without Bearings runs a formula from `ready`, step by step.

**Swarm** is computed by the client from `graph`: wave 1 is what is ready now, wave *n* what waits only on earlier waves, maximum parallelism the widest wave, and what is blocked from outside the set is listed apart. `aries swarm ID` prints it, and `aries graph ID --format mermaid|dot|text` the graph (§4's Port row). One function in a shared package serves `aries` and Bearings' dispatcher. It is not an MCP tool.

**Agents' plans need none of this at first.** A planning agent that files an epic's children one `create` at a time can race an agent that starts child 3 before its edge to child 2 exists. Gating the epic first (§20.3) closes that: the children are blocked through their parent until a person passes the gate, and by then every edge exists. So `graph.create` matters for formulas more than for agents, and needs no MCP tool at first.

### 20.7 Cross-project dependencies

Each project is its own database with its own writer (decision 11), so no transaction spans two. §15.5 item 5 said the edges "become reads across databases, checked against the reader's memberships"; this refines it.

| Option | How `ready` in project A learns about B | Cost |
|---|---|---|
| A. Read through | `ready` in A reads B's database during its query | A's log no longer explains A's ready; a slow, closed or archived B slows or breaks A's every `ready` |
| **B. A mirror (recommended)** | The target's state is kept on the edge in A (`deps.metadata`), written by A's writer with an event when it changes; ready reads A alone | A relay and a reconcile (below); at most a reaper tick late when the relay was missed |
| C. Capabilities (bd's `ship`) | Edges name a capability B publishes, not B's issues | Coarser than an issue; but B chooses what it says, and A's members learn nothing else of B |

**Recommended: C, on B's mechanism; edges to another project's issues wait.**
- **A capability** is a row of the control store (`capabilities`: project, name, state `pending` or `shipped`, who and when, `rev`, `write_id`), with `capability.add`, `capability.ship` and `capability.unship` in the control log. Any member of the owning project ships it: `aries ship NAME`. It joins the control store's in-memory index beside projects, roles and keys, so reading it costs nothing (§15.1, As built).
- **An edge** to it is a `deps` row in A whose target is `cap:PROJECT/NAME`, made by `aries dep add ID cap:…`. The blocked derivation counts such a target as unclosed unless the edge's mirrored state is `shipped`, so an edge in any other state blocks.
- **The relay.** When the daemon commits a ship or unship, it writes the new state onto the edges of each open project that has one, as `ariesd`, with a `dep.target` event and an inbox item to each dependent's assignee (`dep.shipped`). Each store keeps its `cap:` targets in memory, loaded at open and kept by its own `dep` writes. **The reconcile:** at a store's open and on each reaper tick, edges whose mirrored state differs from the index are written the same way, so a relay lost to a crash, or a store closed at the time, costs a tick and never a wrong answer for long.
- **Visibility.** A capability is visible to the members of its project. Linking to one needs membership in both projects: the person who bridges them agrees to tell A. A link to a capability the caller cannot see is refused `not_found`, so the refusal is no oracle. Once made, the edge shows the target's name and state to every member of A: one bit, on a bridge's say. A capability removed, or an owner archived, shows `unknown` and blocks; `aries dep rm` frees it.
- **Decided otherwise (decision 74): edges to another project's issues are built in stage 6 too,** on the mirror (option B), showing A only whether the issue is open or closed, with the same rule for linking (a member of both projects) and the same refusal for an issue the caller cannot see. As first proposed: edges to another project's issues (`B/ISSUE`) would use the same mirror, reading B's store in the relay and the reconcile instead of the index, and would show A only open or closed. Deferred until someone asks: capabilities answer "my work waits for their feature", and an issue edge shows B's backlog to A.
- **Cross-project gates** follow: a gate in A that waits on B is an edge to B's capability, or a gate whose approvers are B's people.

### 20.8 Reservations

**What a reservation guards, and what it cannot.** Aries sees no file writes. A reservation guards only what passes through aries: another session's reservation of overlapping paths is refused with the holder named, and `ready` and `start` treat reserved paths as held. An agent that edits a reserved file is not stopped; it meets the conflict at merge, as today. So a reservation adds exclusivity among those who ask, beside what files-to-issues already does without being asked: rank down work that overlaps held issues' paths.

**The form recommended, when it is built.**
- **Tied to a claim.** A reservation names the issue and the claim's epoch, and lives exactly as long as that claim: no lease, nothing to renew, ended with the claim by `finish`, a releasing `handoff`, the reaper and lost access. Rows of ended claims are pruned as bookkeeping.
- **Paths, not globs.** A file, or a directory prefix ending in `/`, checked by `proto.CheckPath` and matched as declared paths are. §7's `reserve <glob>` would need a glob matcher on the server and an index no arbitrary pattern allows.
- **Exclusive first.** Shared reservations conflict only with exclusive ones and add a mode to every check; deferred.
- **The seam is `loadHeld`** (§12, As built, files to issues): a live reservation joins the held set with its issue, so `ready`'s `overlaps`, `start`'s choice and `show` cover it unchanged. The one new check is in the reserving write: an overlap with another claim's reservation is refused `conflict`, naming the holder, the issue and the paths.
- **API.** `start` gains `reserve: [paths]` (protocol and MCP, about 20 estimated tokens), and `aries reserve ID PATH…` adds to a held issue's. Bearings reserves before dispatch through the protocol (§3.4).
- **Offline,** never queued: it is a claim.

### 20.9 What changes on the server

- **Ops,** each a protocol change (rule 10): `gate.add`, `gate.pass`, `gate.fail`, `gate.waive`, `gate.rearm`; `defer_until` and `due_at` on `create` and `update`; `lock.acquire`, `lock.renew`, `lock.release`, `lock.leave`, `locks`; `graph.create`, `graph`; `capability.add`, `capability.ship`, `capability.unship`, `capabilities`; `cap:` targets on `dep.add` (a new value, which an older server refuses as `invalid`); `reserve`, and `reserve` on `start`. Each slice that adds any raises `Proto` and `ProtoMax` by one and keeps `ProtoMin` one back, as stage 5 did, unless the maintainer folds unreleased versions as decision 25 did.
- **Migrations.** Project set: `gates`; `locks`, `lock_holders` and `lock_waiters` (normalized, where §3 has a `queue[]`); `graph_runs`; `reservations`. Control set: `capabilities`. The `gate` type needs none.
- **Events:** `gate.add`, `gate.pass`, `gate.fail`, `gate.waive`, `gate.rearm`; the six `lock.*`; `graph.create`; `dep.target`; `reservation.add`; `capability.add`, `capability.ship`, `capability.unship` in the control log.
- **Inbox kinds:** `gate.pending`, `gate.passed`, `gate.failed`, `lock.granted`, `lock.lost`, `dep.shipped`.
- **The reaper's tick** gains lapsed locks and waiters and the capability reconcile. Nothing else runs on a timer.
- **Limits** (rule 18), each a `limits:` setting with a row in `docs/server.md`, refused `invalid` or `busy` with a fix:

  | Limit | Default |
  |---|---|
  | `locks` (names in a project) | 200 |
  | `lock_capacity` | 64 |
  | `lock_waiters` (one lock's queue) | 100 |
  | `lock_waits_per_principal` | 20 |
  | `gate_approvers` | 10 |
  | `open_gates` (a project) | 1,000 |
  | `graph_issues`, `graph_edges` (one `graph.create`) | 200, 1,000 |
  | `graph_read` (issues one `graph` returns) | 2,000 |
  | `capabilities` (a project) | 100 |
  | `reservations_per_principal` (paths) | 500 |

- **Telemetry** (§16.4), attributes from fixed lists only: `aries.locks` (counter, `event`), `aries.lock.wait.duration` (histogram), `aries.gates` (counter, `kind` and `event`), `aries.capability.relays` (counter, `result`), `aries.reservations` (counter, `event`). A lock's or a capability's name is a person's text and goes on no metric or span (rule 2). Log events (`lock.expired`, `gate.failed`, `capability.relay_failed`) join `internal/logs`.
- **AppArmor:** nothing, on the recommended path. Option 2 of §20.4 would add network and certificate rules to the shipped profile.
- **Offline** (§18.3):

  | Queued | Never queued |
  |---|---|
  | `graph.create` (an addition, with its key); `dep.add` to a capability | Every `lock.*` and `reserve` (claims); every `gate.*` (a decision on a state the client cannot see, as `close` is); `capability.ship` (a person's act); `graph` and `locks` (reads, refused offline as `ready` is) |

- **MCP cost,** against the budget of about 2,700 estimated tokens, which the 24 tools spend to about 2,696 (8,086 bytes):

  | Slice | Schema change | Estimated tokens |
  |---|---|---|
  | G0 to G4 | None. Gates, locks and capabilities reach agents as fields of `show`, `blocked` and `ready` results, and `cap:…` is a value of `dep`'s `depends_on` | 0 |
  | G5 | `start`'s `reserve` | about 20 |
  | Not proposed: a `gate`, `lock` or `reserve` tool | About 70 to 110 each, as `close` (75) and `label` (96) are | 210 to 330 |

  So stage 6 needs no decision to raise the budget: about 20 tokens at its last slice, paid for by trimming a description (`inbox`'s, 74 tokens, can say less). A tool for each verb would need it raised to about 3,000.

### 20.10 Plan

| Slice | Delivers | Gate |
|---|---|---|
| G0 | Human gates: the `gate` type and `gates` table, `aries gate add`, `pass`, `waive`, `rearm` and `list`, the guards, the inbox items; `defer_until` and `due_at` on the wire, shown in `show` and `list`, and the newly ready, due soon and overdue in `digest` and `prime` | An issue behind a human gate is never in `ready`, and no MCP tool or CLI command of a non-approver starts it or closes or reopens the gate; an approver's pass from `cli` makes it ready and pushes the event to a watching board; anyone else is refused, naming the approvers |
| G1 | CI gates: the `ci` kind, `--check`, `--from-branch`, `gate.fail` with evidence, `landed`; the guide's workflows for GitHub Actions and GitLab CI | e2e: a CI principal passes the `build` gate of the issue its branch names and the dependent becomes ready; a principal the gate does not name is refused; a failure tells the subject's holder. The Docker test server runs the workflow's commands (`smoke.sh`, not CI) |
| G2 | Locks: the tables, ops and reaper's part, `aries lock run`, the `lock.granted` push, the clock's settle, lost access | A soak of sessions contending for locks of capacity 1 and 3 through dropped answers, dropped connections, daemon restarts and clock jumps: the log's replay never shows more holders than capacity, grants follow the order of live waiters, epochs only rise, and a killed holder's lock is granted within a tick of its lease |
| G3 | `graph.create` with run keys, `graph`, `aries pour`, `aries graph`, `aries swarm` | A formula pours whole or not at all; the same run key twice, by two principals, makes one run; its steps become ready as earlier ones close, with no runner; waves are golden-tested |
| G4 | Capabilities: the control table, `aries ship`, `cap:` edges, the relay and the reconcile | An issue waiting on another project's capability becomes ready when a member of that project ships it, at once with its store open and at open with it closed, with an event in its own log and an inbox item; a non-member cannot link to it; an archived owner blocks as `unknown`; the soak across two projects holds every mirror to the control log |
| G5 | Reservations, **only if** Bearings' conflict log or users show rework at merge | Two sessions reserve overlapping paths and the second is refused, naming the first; a reservation ends with its claim however the claim ends; MCP within budget |

G0 alone is worth releasing: a person can hold agents back and know they stay held. G1 turns gates from assertions into outcomes. G2 and G3 are what Bearings B3 waits for. G4 waits for a server of several projects to ask, and G5 for evidence.

### 20.11 Decisions for the maintainer

The maintainer answered the twelve questions on 15 Oct 2026; they are decisions 66 to 77 of §14, which has the answers. Ten stand as proposed. The ninth (decision 74) brings edges to another project's issues into stage 6, on the mirror of §20.7 (option B), beside capabilities. The eleventh (decision 76) raises the MCP budget to about 3,000 tokens for a gate tool, dates on create and update, a cross-project link and, later, reservations. The questions as they were put follow.

1. **Gates are issues** of a new type with a side table, not a blocker kind beside issues. Recommended (§20.3, A). Cost: three special cases (`ready`, `start`, `close`) and a type that lists show.
2. **CI reaches the server by running `aries`** as a principal of its own; no polling and no webhook in ariesd (§20.4). Recommended. Cost: CI needs a network path to sshd and, until workload sign-in, a stored key; the CI principal has a member's rights until decision 8 narrows them.
3. **Who passes a human gate.** Recommended: the principals it names, or the project's admins when it names none; only from the session `cli`; the gate's creator only when named. Cost: a person working alone names themselves.
4. **No timer, issue or gh gate kinds.** Recommended: timers are `defer_until` on the wire, issue gates are `blocks` edges, gh is the `ci` kind. Cost: none found.
5. **Locks are per project, first in first out with waiter leases, over the CLI and the protocol only.** Recommended. Cost: no lock shared by projects yet; an agent cannot take a lock itself, so Bearings or a script holds it for the agent.
6. **Formulas are files in the repository** read by `aries`; the server gets `graph.create` with run keys and the `graph` read; template issues stay for bd parity. Recommended. Cost: no catalog of formulas on the server; a formula's history is git's.
7. **Wisps are dropped,** with squash and burn. Recommended. Cost: bd users of wisps get ordinary issues.
8. **Swarm is computed by the client** over `graph`, with no server op and no MCP tool. Recommended.
9. **Cross-project starts with capabilities,** mirrored into the waiting project and visible to it as one bit through a member of both; edges to another project's issues wait for a request. Recommended. Cost: coarser than bd's `external:` edges.
10. **Reservations wait for evidence,** and when built are tied to a claim, paths and prefixes (no globs), exclusive only, set through `start`. Recommended. Cost: §7's row stays unbuilt until then.
11. **No rise in the MCP budget** for stage 6: about 20 tokens, trimmed elsewhere. Recommended.
12. **One protocol bump for each slice** that adds ops, `ProtoMin` one back. Recommended, as in stage 5; folding unreleased bumps, as decision 25 did, is the alternative.

### 20.12 What to cut, defer, or leave to Bearings and the client

| | What | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Cut | Wisps, squash and burn; `cook` as a step; timer, issue and gh gate kinds; globs; a gate runner | Each deletes history, or does what an existing part already does |
| Defer | Reservations (G5); edges to another project's issues; shared reservations; locks across projects; MCP tools for gates, locks or reservations; distill; a webhook relay | No failure seen yet needs them, or a cheaper part covers the common case |
| Leave to Bearings | The merge train's batching and bisecting (the host's merge queue does it, enterprise review §8); dispatching a formula's steps; sizing pools from waves; a run's budget | Policy over primitives; the server holds the primitives |
| Leave to the client | Parsing formulas, pouring templates, computing waves, drawing graphs, holding a lock around a command | One read and one write serve them; the rest needs no authority |

In all, ariesd gains a gate type with its table, a run key, a lock table with its part of the reaper, one read and, at G4, a control table with a relay. It gains no runner, no template engine, no outbound call and no port.

### 20.13 What this does not solve

- **Aries sees no file writes.** A reservation binds only those who ask Aries; worktrees and merges are still the safety net.
- **A fencing token protects only a resource that checks it.** A deploy script that ignores `ARIES_LOCK_EPOCH` can run on after its lease ran out, through a pause the lease did not see.
- **A CI pass is as good as the CI principal's key** and the workflow that holds it: whoever can edit the workflow can pass the gate. Required checks and branch protection stay the host's.
- **A human gate is passed by a principal.** An agent holding its person's key passes its person's gates until agents are principals of their own (decision 8).
- **A revert** re-arms a `landed` gate only if something runs `aries gate rearm`; work already begun on what it held is not stopped.
- **No change spans two projects atomically;** a mirrored capability is at most a reaper tick behind when its relay was lost.
- Git push fencing by claim epoch, budgets and autonomy windows (decision 8; enterprise review E3 and E13) are not stage 6's.

### 20.14 Not tested

Everything: this is a design. In particular: the blocked derivation with many gate issues at 50,000 issues (the query is unchanged, its data is not); how long a lock takes to pass on at the reaper's 30-second tick under contention; the reconcile's cost for a daemon that opens hundreds of projects at start; and whether a hosted CI runner can reach a server behind IAP with `gcloud` in the job.

### 20.15 As built

**As built (G0, human gates and dates, 15 Oct 2026).** Slice G0 of §20.10. Where the build differs from §20.3 and §20.9, or settles what they left open:
- **The model.** A gate is an issue of type `gate` and a row of `gates` (project migration 0018: the issue's id, `kind` (`human`), `approvers` as a JSON array, `outcome` (empty while open, else `passed` or `waived`), `decided_by`, `decided_at`, `reason`, `rev`, `write_id`). `gate.add` writes the issue, the row and the `blocks` edge in one write, with `issue.create`, `gate.add` and `dep.add` events. Only `gate.add` makes a gate: `create` or `update` to type `gate`, and `update` of a gate's type or status, are refused `invalid`. `gate.pass` and `gate.waive` close the gate's issue in the same write (`gate.pass`/`gate.waive`, then `issue.close`), and `gate.rearm` reopens it (`gate.rearm`, then `issue.reopen`), so the row and the issue never disagree.
- **What a gate holds.** An issue reaches an open gate by blocking edges to unclosed issues and by parent links: its own edge, an ancestor's, or a blocker's, transitively. That is wider than descendants alone, and matches what keeps an issue out of `ready`. `ready` and `start` leave gates out (`readyFilter`, `readyNow`). `start` of an issue a gate holds is refused for everyone, approvers included: pass the gate first. A `close` or `finish` of a held issue, a `dep rm`, and an `update` of `parent` that would end a hold, are refused unless the actor could release every gate it ends: an approver, or an admin, in the session `cli`. Setting a held issue's status back to `open`, the undo §20.1 names, is allowed and undoes nothing, since the hold is the graph's. A project with no open gate skips the walk (one count of `gates`).
- **Who.** Pass: the principals named; when none are, the project's admins (`Store.IsAdmin`, so in a server of many projects the control store's) other than its creator (decision 68). Waive: an admin, whoever the gate names, with a reason, which is required. Rearm: those named, or an admin. All three only from the session `cli`; refused `forbidden`, naming the approvers, with a fix that opens `wait for`, which `aries mcp` turns into "tell the user; never run aries gate yourself". Anyone may add a gate, an agent included.
- **Who is told.** `gate.pending` goes to each named approver. A gate that names none tells nobody: the store asks `IsAdmin` of one principal at a time and cannot list the control store's admins; `aries gate list` shows them. `gate.passed` goes to the assignees of the unclosed issues the gate held, at most 100, on a pass or a waive. The board gets `gate.pass` and `issue.close` through the event push, the gate's id being an issue id.
- **Reads.** `show` gives a gate's `gate` and an issue's `gates` (the open gates that hold it); `blocked` gives the `gates` among its blockers; the new `gates` op lists open gates, or with `all` every one. The CLI is `aries gate add|pass|waive|rearm|list`.
- **The agent tool** (decision 76): `gate`, with `add` (blocks, approvers, title) and `list`; and `defer_until` and `due` on `create` and `update`. The 25 tools measure about 2,876 estimated tokens (8,628 bytes) against the budget, raised from 2,700 to 3,000 in `TestTools`; G0 spent about 180 of the 300.
- **Dates.** `defer_until` and `due` on `create` and `update`, each a date (midnight UTC) or an RFC 3339 time from 1970 on, and an empty one clears; a replayed update merges both as fields (§18.7), parking a date someone else changed. `list` and `ready` carry them, `show` prints them. `digest` gains `ready` (ready now, with `defer_until` in its window), `due_soon` (unclosed, due within `due_soon`, a setting, a day by default) and `overdue`; the new `dates` op returns the three alone, and `prime` asks it and lists at most three of each. "Since the reader last asked" is the reader's own window: `digest`'s `--since`, and for `prime`, which keeps no record of when it was last asked, the last day. All are read from the store's time (rule 19); nothing is written when a date passes.
- **Protocol.** None of it raised the protocol: on decision 78 (§14), which replaced decision 77 for unreleased work, G0's ops and arguments joined unreleased protocol 8, and `Proto`, `ProtoMin` and `ProtoMax` stay 8, 7 and 8 (`internal/proto/version.go`). A server built from main before G0 refuses them as unknown, with code `invalid` and a fix to upgrade; `prime` goes without dates there.
- **Offline.** No `gate.*` op is queued, as §20.9 has it: an add sent later could hold back work started meanwhile, and a pass is a person's decision at the moment they make it, so none is made without a person present at a server that answers. `gates` and `dates` are refused offline, as `ready` is. A `create` or `update` with dates is queued as before.
- **Limits.** `gate_approvers` (10, at most 100) and `open_gates` (1,000, refused `busy`); the gates on one issue are its edges, which `deps_per_issue` already bounds. `due_soon` is a setting, not a cap.
- **Not built in G0:** the `ci` kind, `gate.fail` and evidence (G1); bd import's mapping of `bd-type:gate` issues to gates; the `aries.gates` counter and the gate log events of §20.9. Not measured: the walk that finds what holds an issue, and the date sections, which scan `issues` with no index on `due_at` or `defer_until`, at 50,000 issues.

**As built (G1, CI gates and outcomes, 15 Oct 2026).** Slice G1 of §20.10, on option 1 of §20.4: a CI job runs `aries` as a principal of its own, and ariesd polls, receives and calls nothing. Where the build differs from §20.3, §20.4 and §20.9, or settles what they left open:
- **The model.** Project migration 0019 adds `check_name`, `subject` and `report` (JSON, the last report) to `gates`, with an index on (`subject`, `check_name`). A `ci` gate names its check (`[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._-]{0,63}`), its subject and at least one principal, kept in `approvers` (§20.3's `by`; `--by` on the CLI). **A subject has one ci gate for each check**, so a job that knows its branch and its check finds one gate, and a second add is refused with a fix to `aries dep add` another issue onto the first gate. **A ci gate may hold nothing** (`blocks` is optional for it): it then records its check alone, which is how `landed` is kept on an issue nothing waits for. A gate cannot hold back its own subject, whose work could then never start. A ci gate tells nobody when added: its principals are CI jobs.
- **Who reports, and from where.** Only a principal the gate names, from the session `ci` or `cli`, never an agent's (`GateNotCI`, `forbidden`). **How a CI session is identified:** `aries` takes the session `ci` (`proto.CISession`) where `CI=true`, which GitHub Actions and GitLab CI set, unless `ARIES_SESSION` or a harness's variable names one (`client.DefaultSession`); like `cli`, it is the client's word, a speed bump and not a credential. **A person cannot pass a ci gate unless the gate names their principal**; an admin it does not name, its creator included, waives it as any gate (`cli` only, with a reason). **Human gates are unchanged:** decision 68, from `cli` alone, so the session `ci` does not pass one, and `gate.fail` on a human gate is `invalid`.
- **Pass, fail, rearm.** `gate.pass` with a check or evidence, and `gate.fail`, report by the gate's id or by (subject, check). A pass closes the gate with its report, `gate.pass` then `issue.close`, and tells the assignees of what it held (`gate.passed`), as G0's does; **a pass of a closed gate is a no-op**, so reruns of a job never fail. A failure records `gate.fail` with the evidence, leaves the gate open, and tells the subject's holder, in the holder's session, and its assignee if another (`gate.failed`, at most two items, under the sender's `notices_per_minute`). **A later pass closes the matter** and tells them too (`gate.passed` on the subject), once the last report was a failure. **Rearm on a new commit:** a failure of a passed gate reopens it (`gate.fail`, then `issue.reopen`), so what it held is held again; nothing compares commits, the last report is the truth. A waived gate takes no failure until rearmed. Rearm of a ci gate is by its principals or an admin, from `ci` or `cli`.
- **`--from-branch`.** The client sends the branch, and **the server reads the issue from it with the project's prefix** (`gitx.IDFromBranch`, so a hyphenated prefix works and `main` names nothing); the client reads the branch from the CI system's variables first (`GITHUB_HEAD_REF`, `GITHUB_REF_NAME` when `GITHUB_REF_TYPE` is `branch`, `CI_MERGE_REQUEST_SOURCE_BRANCH_NAME`, `CI_COMMIT_BRANCH`), since CI checks out a detached HEAD, then git. A branch that names no issue, and a subject with no gate for the check, are `not_found`, with fixes; `--optional` turns `not_found` into a note and exit 0, so one workflow serves every branch. `--issue ID` and `--branch NAME` name it directly.
- **Landed (enterprise review E8).** Landing means merged to the default branch, as the job on that branch reports after the merge: `aries gate pass --check landed --from-commit`, which takes the issue from HEAD's `Aries:` trailer, else the branch a merge commit's subject names (git's, GitHub's and GitLab's forms). `landed` is a check like any other; nothing on the server treats the name specially. `show` gains `checks`, an issue's ci gates in every state, and the CLI prints `closed, not landed` for a closed issue whose `landed` check is open, and `closed, landed` with the time once it passed. A squash merge names the issue only if its message holds the trailer.
- **Evidence and caps (rule 2, rule 18).** A report's `sha` (7 to 64 hex digits), `url` (`http` or `https`, with a host and no user or password) and `reason` (one line) are checked, run through the secrets lint (`internal/secretscan`) as a memory is, and capped together by `gate_evidence` (1,024 bytes, at most 4,096; §20.3 said 1 KiB). `gate_checks` (20, at most 100) caps the ci gates on one subject. The inbox line puts the commit, then the link, then the reason, and is cut at 200 bytes as every item is.
- **Protocol.** None raised: G1 joined unreleased protocol 8 as G0 did (`version.go`): the op `gate.fail`, `kind`, `check` and `subject` on `gate.add`, and `check`, `subject`, `branch`, `sha` and `url` on `gate.pass` and `gate.fail`. `gate.fail` is never queued offline: a CI job's report belongs to its run.
- **Agents.** No tool passes or fails a gate, and no schema changed: the tools measure about 2,876 estimated tokens (8,628 bytes), as after G0. `show` gives `checks`, a line each with the last report, dropped last when a result would pass its cap; the `gate` tool's `list` gives a ci gate's `check`, `subject` and `report`; a failure reaches the holding agent's `inbox` as `gate.failed`.
- **Workflows.** [docs/ci.md](../ci.md) has one for GitHub Actions and one for GitLab CI, with the key in the CI system's secret store as a file (decision 35's `ARIES_KEY_FILE`), actions pinned by commit, and §20.4's costs stated plainly. `deploy/docker/smoke.sh` runs their commands against the Docker test server.
- **Not built in G1:** the `aries.gates` counter and the gate log events of §20.9; an agent's `gate` tool adding a ci gate (a person or a script adds one). Not run: either workflow on GitHub's or GitLab's hosted runners, whether a GitLab *File* variable expands in `variables:` as the example assumes, and a hosted runner reaching a server behind IAP (§20.14).

**As built (G2, locks, 15 Oct 2026).** Slice G2 of §20.10, on decision 70: per project, first in first out with waiter leases, over the CLI and the protocol only. Where the build differs from §20.5 and §20.9, or settles what they left open:
- **The model.** Project migration 0020 adds `locks` (a name, its capacity, who made it), `lock_holders` (a hold: the lock, its epoch, the holder, `granted_at`, `expires_at`, and the lease its acquire asked for, which a renewal without one reuses), `lock_waiters` (a place: the lock, its order in the queue, the holder, `joined_at`, `expires_at`, and the hold's lease once granted) and `lock_epochs` (one row, the project's last epoch). **A lock's row goes once nobody holds or waits for it,** so the `locks` cap counts locks in use, not every name ever used; the next acquire of the name makes it again, **with a capacity of its own**: §20.5's "fixed by the first acquire" holds while the lock is in use. **Epochs are the project's:** each grant, of any lock, takes the next, so a lock's epochs only rise, across the removal of its row too, and an epoch names one grant in the project; they rise by one in the project, not in the lock as §20.5 has it, which a resource that remembers the highest epoch it saw does not notice. **Names** are `[a-z0-9][a-z0-9._/-]{0,58}`, 59 bytes rather than 64, since an event's target, `lock:NAME`, is a `VARCHAR(64)` and widening the column of every project's event log was not worth five bytes.
- **Holders.** A holder is (principal, session, machine). An acquire by one that already holds the lock, or waits for it, gets that hold, extended, or that place, renewed: a retried acquire whose answer was lost takes nothing twice. A renewal or a release names the epoch, and any session of the holder's principal may make it: a person releases from another terminal what a script took. Renewing with epoch 0 renews the caller's place, and once the place was granted it returns the hold: that is how a waiter that missed the push learns of its grant. Leaving the queue when one holds the lock instead, or neither, changes nothing and says which.
- **A lapsed hold holds nothing, before the reaper too,** as §20.5 says, and more: every write to a lock first ends what lapsed, a hold with `lock.expire` and a place with `lock.leave` whose after state says `lease expired`, and grants the free slots to the queue, in that one write. So a grant never finds more live holders than capacity, and a free slot never goes to an acquirer while a live waiter waits. A refused acquire keeps what that tidying did. **Events:** `lock.acquire` is a grant made at once to the acquirer; `lock.wait` a place; `lock.grant` a grant from the queue, recorded by whoever's write freed the slot (a release, the reaper, an acquire that found a lapsed hold), with the place in its before state; `lock.release` a holder's release, or with a `reason` an end for lost access; `lock.expire` a lapsed hold; `lock.leave` a place given up, lapsed or ended for lost access. All target `lock:NAME`, which no issue id matches, so a board's event push does not carry them (tested).
- **Inbox.** `lock.granted` goes to the waiter's session; `lock.lost` to a holder's session when its hold lapsed or its access ended, not when it released it. Neither counts against the sender's `notices_per_minute`, as `claim.lost` does not. Their items name no issue.
- **The reaper and the clock.** On each tick, after the claims' reap has settled any clock step, the reaper ends lapsed holds and places in every lock and grants the queues (`ReapLocks`); a tick with nothing lapsed is a read and takes no write. The settle extends every live hold and place with the claims, and `clock.step`'s after state gains `locks`, how many. **Lost access** ends a principal's live holds and places wherever its claims end: the admin's change, the tick's sweep, a store's opening, and an archived project at the daemon's start, whose one-query check (`HeldClaims`) now counts holds and places too; lapsed ones end as the reaper ends them.
